Welcome to Setting Captives Free - 365! A devotional committed to studying and teaching the four main things that will help you be safe now and happy later! 
These daily devotionals will take 15 minutes per day, so they are short and to the point. But remember this: 

    It's not the one who reads the most, but the one who meditates the most, who will become the choicest, sweetest, wisest, and strongest Christian. And it's not the knowing, talking, or reading person, but the doing person, who will ultimately be the happiest. "If you know these things, blessed and happy are you if you do them" (John 13:17). - Brooks, Thomas. Precious Remedies Against Satan's Devices: In Modern, Updated English (p. 15).

    No man who merely skims the book of God can profit from it. We must dig and mine until we obtain hidden treasure. - Charles Spurgeon quoted on X

Please watch this introduction, and then we'll begin our studying together:
Finally, these daily devotionals are packed full of Scripture! There are lots of references from God's Word imbedded in the text, and I would encourage you to hover your mouse over them all, as you read, so you can see God's Word in context. 

Day 1: The Promise of Freedom
The Promise of Freedom From Sin
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage:
"And she will have a son, and you are to name him Jesus, for he will save his people from their sins."
Matthew 1:21
Teaching:
Jesus' purpose for coming to earth is powerfully summarized in Matthew 1:21: He came to save His people from their sins. It does not say He came to save them in their sins. This distinction is crucial. Jesus didn’t just come to offer a temporary fix or to leave us trapped in the power of sin. Instead, He came to deliver us from the guilt, penalty, and control of sin (Isaiah 53:5-6; Romans 8:1-2). His death on the cross was a once-and-for-all act that destroyed sin’s grip over us (Hebrews 10:10, 14; Colossians 2:13-15).
Jesus promised that those who are freed by Him are truly free (John 8:34-36) because He knew He was going to the cross to be judged for your sin and mine (2 Corinthians 5:21) and to free us from the judgment, condemnation, and power of sin (Romans 6:6-7; Galatians 5:1). Now everyone who believes is like a prisoner who has been granted a full pardon, no longer fearing the judge's sentence (Psalm 103:10-12; John 3:16-18).
John Piper explains:

    The cross is the pivotal point of history where God’s judgment on sin is most clearly seen, and the mercy of God is most gloriously displayed. Jesus came to deal with our greatest problem—our separation from God due to sin. (John Piper, The Passion of Jesus Christ, p. 43)

At the cross, Jesus took the punishment we deserved, paying the price for our sins so that we could be reconciled to God (Isaiah 53:4-5; Romans 5:8-10). As a perfect sacrifice on the altar, He bore all the weight of our guilt (Hebrews 9:14; 1 Peter 2:24). Through His resurrection, He broke the power of death and sin, shattering them like glass underfoot (1 Corinthians 15:55-57; Romans 6:9-10).
And now we can live in the freedom He purchased for us, knowing that He defeated sin once and for all (Galatians 5:1; John 8:36; Colossians 1:13-14).
“But God raised him from the dead on the third day and caused him to be seen.”
Acts 10:40

Biblical Illustration: Manasseh
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
The story of King Manasseh of Judah provides a powerful illustration of how God’s freedom from sin works. Manasseh was one of the most wicked kings in Judah's history. He led the people into idolatry, practiced sorcery, and even sacrificed his own son to pagan gods (2 Kings 21:1-16). His reign was marked by evil deeds, and many people in Israel followed his example.
But when Manasseh was captured by the Assyrians and brought in chains to Babylon, he experienced a profound moment of repentance. He humbled himself and cried out to God for mercy. God responded by allowing him to return to his throne. 
Manasseh’s heart was completely changed. He tore down the idols, restored worship of the true God, and led the people in repentance (2 Chronicles 33:12–16). This radical transformation illustrates that no matter how deeply ensnared someone is in sin, God's grace has the power to set them free.
How does the transformation of King Manasseh give you hope for God's power to set anyone free from sin?

Day 1: The Promise of Freedom
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
If you feel trapped by a recurring sin or the weight of guilt, remember that Jesus offers you real freedom. Embrace the truth that you are not just forgiven but also empowered to overcome sin. This freedom is found in daily coming to the cross of Jesus Christ and surrendering to the Holy Spirit, who works in you to bring about lasting change. 
Every time you are tempted, remind yourself of the power of the cross. Jesus has already broken sin’s hold on you (Romans 6:6) and destroyed the power of Satan for you (1 John 3:8)—live in that freedom today.
John MacArthur reminds us: 

    True freedom in Christ is not freedom to sin, but freedom from sin. We are free to serve God with purity, joy, and devotion. (John MacArthur, The Freedom of the Christian, p. 56)

The cross and resurrection of Jesus mean that sin and death no longer have the final word in your life (Romans 6:9-10; 1 Corinthians 15:54-57). Like a shadow that vanishes at dawn, your sin is now gone for good (Psalm 103:12; Micah 7:19)! In Him, you are free indeed, as if a heavy weight has been lifted from your shoulders (Matthew 11:28-30; John 8:36).
The chains that once bound you have been shattered under the weight of Jesus' love for you (Isaiah 61:1; Galatians 5:1). You are like a bird released from its cage and set free to fly heavenward (Psalm 124:7; Isaiah 40:31). No longer a prisoner of fear, you are a conqueror, walking in the authority of the risen King (Romans 8:37-39; Matthew 28:18).
In Jesus, you are a new creation, the old is gone, the new has come (2 Corinthians 5:17). In Jesus, you have a new standing, a new heart, a new nature, a new life (Ezekiel 36:26; Colossians 3:9-10; Acts 5:20)! His resurrection is the dawn of a new day for you (Romans 6:4; 1 Peter 1:3). In Him, you are not a slave to sin, but are rather free indeed (Romans 6:6-7; John 8:36)!
“For we know that our old self was crucified with him so that the body of sin might be rendered powerless, that we should no longer be slaves to sin.”
Romans 6:6

Day 2: The Need for a Heart Change
The Need for a Heart Change
Awaiting feedback
Key Passage
"The heart is deceitful above all things and desperately sick; who can understand it?"
Jeremiah 17:9
Teaching
Jeremiah 17:9 underscores a fundamental truth about the human condition: by nature, all human hearts are deceitful and sick (Mark 7:21-23). Like a poison coursing through our veins, sin comes from our hearts and moves through our whole being, leaving us spiritually broken and lost (Romans 3:10-12; Isaiah 64:6).
This means that we cannot change our desires or overcome sin by willpower alone (Romans 7:18-19). Trying to clean ourselves up is like trying to clean a dirty window with a smear of mud—the harder we try, the more clouded our vision becomes. We need a complete heart transformation!
I have often described my own heart as wild horses running without reins, dragging me wherever they please (Romans 7:23-24). This is true of every human: our passions have been out of control, and we have not been able to tame them (Galatians 5:17; James 1:14-15).
I was reading the other day that conservationists can restore a scarred masterpiece to make it as perfect as it was originally. Similarly, through God’s Spirit replacing our old and dead hearts with new and living hearts, the image of God is restored in us (Romans 8:29, 1 Corinthians 15:49, 2 Corinthians 3:18, Colossians 3:9-10).
Timothy Keller writes: 

    The human heart is an idol factory. We can turn anything into an idol, and that idol then dictates our lives. That is why we need a heart transformation, not merely behavior modification. (Timothy Keller, Counterfeit Gods, p. 32)

God offers this most amazing solution to our wicked and deceptive hearts. The solution is the new heart. 

In Ezekiel 36:26, He promises to replace our heart of stone with a heart of flesh—one that is sensitive to His voice, responsive to His will, and able to love as He loves (Hebrews 8:10). This heart change happens through believing the gospel of Jesus Christ (Romans 1:16-17).
When we see Jesus on the cross, loving us to death and back (Romans 5:8), and we come to Jesus as a sinner (Luke 18:13-14), He gives us a new heart that is capable of desiring righteousness and living in obedience to God (2 Corinthians 5:17; Philippians 2:13). It’s as if the scales fall from our eyes (Acts 9:18), and we can finally see the beauty and the power of the cross (1 Corinthians 1:18). Our old, stony hearts are replaced with hearts of tender flesh, able to feel and respond to His love (Jeremiah 24:7).
This new heart is like a fully working compass, always pointing us toward what is good and true, and it operates in conjunction with God's Word which illuminates our path, showing us the way to go (Psalm 119:105). It is a wellspring, overflowing with the desire to follow Jesus and walk in His ways (John 7:38; Galatians 5:16).
I remember when the Spirit of God replaced my old stony heart with a heart of flesh (Titus 3:5-6). The chains that bound me were broken, and I was set free (John 8:36; Romans 6:18), and I began walking in a new direction (2 Corinthians 5:17). I now had a new heart that was a beacon, guiding me through the fog of confusion that sin had brought into my life (Proverbs 3:5-6).
The cross of Jesus provides the foundation for this heart transformation (1 Peter 2:24). Through His sacrifice, Jesus not only paid for our sins (1 John 2:2) but also made it possible for us to be born again with new hearts (John 3:3). His resurrection empowers us to live according to God’s will, following Jesus in obedience (Romans 6:4; 1 Peter 1:3). 
“I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit within you; I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh and give you a heart of flesh.”
Ezekiel 36:26

Biblical Illustration: David
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
The story of David's repentance after his sin with Bathsheba provides a vivid illustration of the need for a heart change. After David committed adultery and arranged for Bathsheba's husband, Uriah, to be killed in battle, God sent the prophet Nathan to confront him. David was initially blind to the seriousness of his sin, but when Nathan shared a parable (2 Samuel 12), David’s heart was pierced. He realized the depth of his sin and he cried out to God for mercy, saying, “Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me” (Psalm 51:10). His cry and prayer was for "a clean heart", a new spirit, which would produce right desires and holy living.
Why didn't David cry out for a group of men to hold him accountable? Or for a support group of people who had fallen like he had? Or for any of the other teachings that modern 12-step groups teach?
David understood that his outward actions were merely the surface ripples of a far deeper current—his heart was the underlying problem (Matthew 15:19). He didn’t crave a mere bandage for his wounds (Jeremiah 6:14); he longed for a new heart, one that would beat in harmony with God’s will (Ezekiel 36:26-27), one that would lead him to walk with God in righteousness (Psalm 23:3; Micah 6:8).
God, in His abundant mercy, did not merely offer some good information about David's problem, but the very transformation David needed—a heart of flesh instead of stone. And in this moment of grace, the chains of David's old nature fell away, he was given a new heart, and he became known as "a man after God's own heart" (Acts 13:22).

Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Like David, we must recognize that overcoming sin requires more than just outward change, or doing some steps to try to make ourselves better while we make amends—it requires a heart change (Psalm 51:10). This means we must go to God in repentance (Acts 3:19), come to Jesus at the cross (1 John 1:9), asking for a new heart, one that desires what He desires (Ezekiel 36:26-27).
If you’ve been stuck in a cycle of sin, don’t just try to manage the behavior (Romans 7:18-19); go to God and ask Him to create in you a clean heart (Psalm 51:10) and renew a right spirit within you (Psalm 51:12).
John Piper offers this profound thought: 

    The power of sin is broken when the heart is set free by the work of Christ. We must continually ask for God’s transforming grace to change our hearts so that we love what He loves and desire what He desires.  (John Piper, The Pleasures of God, p. 171)

The cross and resurrection of Jesus, and the subsequent giving of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost, make this heart transformation possible (Titus 3:5-6). Because of Jesus' finished work on the cross, we receive a new heart the minute we put faith in Him (Ezekiel 36:26; 2 Corinthians 5:17). So ask God to reveal the hidden roots of your sin—those dark seeds of desire that come from your heart (Psalm 139:23-24; James 1:14-15).  See Proverbs 4:23. Invite the Holy Spirit, the gentle yet powerful wind (John 3:8), to sweep through your life, uprooting what is toxic and planting the seeds of lasting transformation in their place (Galatians 5:22-23).
If you are already a believer in Jesus Christ, acknowledge that God has already given you a new heart (Ezekiel 11:19-20). You have the desire to follow Jesus (Luke 9:23), to walk in obedience to God (John 14:15), to live out your new life that was purchased for you at the cross (Romans 6:4). Your identity now is one who is washed clean from sin (1 Corinthians 6:11), and made new by the power of the Spirit (Romans 8:9-11). You no longer have a heart that is "deceitful and wicked" (Jeremiah 17:9) but rather one that is new and clean (Psalm 51:10).

Day 3: The Result of a Changed Heart
The Fruit of a Changed Heart
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage
"I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit within you; I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh and give you a heart of flesh."
Ezekiel 36:26
Teaching
When God transforms our hearts, the result is a changed life. The fruit of the Spirit (Galatians 5:22-23) becomes evident in our attitudes and actions. A heart that has been made new by the gospel will naturally produce love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control. These are not qualities we can manufacture through self-effort; they are the outgrowth of a heart that is aligned with God’s will and empowered by the Holy Spirit.
The promise of Ezekiel 36:26 is not merely a promise of forgiveness, but a declaration of radical transformation. When God gifts us a new heart, He carves out the old (Colossians 2:11) and breathes into us a heart that can truly love—both Him and others—a heart that mirrors His character like a clear reflection in still water. This renewed heart doesn’t just beat; it flourishes, bearing fruit in every corner of our lives, radiating the glory of God and showing the world the profound power of the gospel to reshape us from the inside out.
The cross of Christ and His resurrection are the foundation for this transformation. Through Jesus' death, we are crucified with Him (Galatians 2:20), and through His resurrection, we are given new life (Acts 5:20)—a life that produces the fruit of the Spirit.
"So we are buried with him by baptism into death, in order that, just as Christ was raised from the dead by the glory of the Father, we too might walk in newness of life.”
Romans 6:4

Biblical Illustration: Saul
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
One of the most striking examples of a changed heart in Scripture is the life of Saul of Tarsus, who became the Apostle Paul. Before his conversion, Saul was a fierce persecutor of Christians, known for his zeal to destroy the church. “But Saul began to destroy the church. Going from house to house, he dragged off both men and women and put them in prison” (Acts 8:3).
However, when Jesus encountered him on the road to Damascus, Saul was blinded (a symbol of his past spiritual life), and Jesus asked, “Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting me?” (Acts 9:4). This divine encounter radically changed Saul’s heart. “Immediately, something like scales fell from Saul’s eyes, and he could see again. He got up and was baptized” (Acts 9:18).
After his conversion, Saul became Paul, one of the greatest apostles of the early church. The persecutor of the church became a preacher of the gospel. The terrorist became an evangelist. “For I am the least of the apostles and do not even deserve to be called an apostle, because I persecuted the church of God. But by the grace of God I am what I am, and his grace to me was not without effect” (1 Corinthians 15:9-10).
His life was a testimony to the transforming power of the gospel (1 Thessalonians 1:5). Oh how he loved to preach Christ crucified: “For I resolved to know nothing while I was with you except Jesus Christ and him crucified” (1 Corinthians 2:2). His life bore the fruit of the Spirit: love, faithfulness, patience, and joy (Galatians 5:22-23). The power of the cross and resurrection, the power of the Holy Spirit, had transformed him, making him a new creation in Christ. “Therefore, if anyone is in Christ, the new creation has come: The old has gone, the new is here!” (2 Corinthians 5:17).
Paul wrote to the Galatians:
I declare before God that what I am writing to you is not a lie. After that visit I went north into the provinces of Syria and Cilicia. And still the Christians in the churches in Judea didn’t know me personally. All they knew was that people were saying, “The one who used to persecute us is now preaching the very faith he tried to destroy!” And they praised God because of me.
Galatians 1:20-24
How does Paul’s transformation encourage you today?

Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Take a moment to look at your life today, as a farmer might examine a garden. Are you bearing the fruit of the Spirit, or does your soul seem as barren as a waterless desert? If you find yourself stagnating or returning to sinful bondage, ask the Holy Spirit to be the gardener who tills the soil of your heart (Ezekiel 36:34), nurturing the growth of good fruit where there has been drought. 
Jesus' death and resurrection for our sins is the very sunlight that powers this new life within us, and just as a tree cannot help but bear its fruit, our lives can be a living testament to His power, blooming in love, action, and speech.
Don’t strive to bear the fruit of the Spirit in your own strength. Instead, surrender to the Holy Spirit and ask Him to bear that fruit in your life. This happens as you study God's Word, fix your eyes on Jesus, look at the cross, and see how your old life is put to death on the cross with Jesus. The same power that raised Jesus from the dead (Ephesians 1:19-20) is available to you today to transform you from the inside out.
This is the value of the historically verifiable resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead, it shows the power that God has to rescue us! As Psalm 68:20 says, "Our God is a God who saves! The Sovereign LORD rescues us from death." 
Matthew Henry says:

    The resurrection of Christ is the ground of all our hope and the source of all our strength. By virtue of his resurrection, we are enabled to live a new life, and our old nature is crucified with him, that we may bring forth the fruit of holiness. – Matthew Henry, Commentary on the Bible, Romans 6:4
    

Day 4: The Cleansing Power
The Cleansing Power
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage 
"On that day a fountain will be opened to the house of David and the inhabitants of Jerusalem, to cleanse them from sin and impurity."
Zechariah 13:1


Teaching

The imagery of a cleansing fountain in Zechariah 13 points us to, and finds fulfillment at, the cross of Jesus Christ, where Jesus was pierced for our sins (Zechariah 12:10). Just as a morning rain clears the night’s fog, the blood of Jesus washes away the stains of our sin, leaving believers with only purity and righteousness. 
Jesus’ death and resurrection opened the way for deep, soul-level cleansing—providing us with the ability to be washed once (Titus 3:5), and then cleansed anew each day (John 13:10); not just once for salvation but daily for renewal and freedom. 
When we first believe the message of the cross, we lose our heavy burden of guilt, as Christian did in the best-selling book "Pilgrim's Progress": when he came to the cross his burden fell off his back. 
And by hearing and believing the message of the cross again and again, we are like a riverbed that is constantly washed and refreshed by a steady flow of water—and this is how the power of sin is broken in our lives. "For by that one offering he forever made perfect those who are being made holy" (Hebrews 10:14).
Charles Spurgeon reminds us of the constancy of this cleansing of the cross: 

    This fountain, we are told, is always open; what joy this is for the defiled! We need not wait for it to be opened, nor take our place by its closed doors; it is open and flows freely to cleanse. (Spurgeon, Morning by Morning). 

Spurgeon illustrates how the cross is a fountain, not a pond or a lake. A fountain is continually flowing, ever providing fresh water so that we can come to it repeatedly and wash. 
As we come to the cross again and again, we begin to see that we are truly sinful and defiled by birth and by practice, but that the blood of Jesus washed all our sin away. We also begin to see that we are oh so loved, so much so that Jesus traded places with us. In this way, He cleansed us from sin and gave us His righteousness. "God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God" (2 Corinthians 5:21). 
What amazing love, that Jesus, Almighty God, would die to heal us, save us, forgive us and rescue us. The Roman emperor Trajan, it is said, tore his clothes to bind up his soldiers' wounds. But how much better that Jesus poured out his blood and tore his flesh to bind up our wounds.
Tim Keller adds:

    The gospel says you are more sinful and flawed than you ever dared believe, but more accepted and loved than you ever dared hope (Keller, The Meaning of Marriage). 

Biblical Illustration: Peter
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
One of Jesus' key disciples, Peter, denied Jesus three times (John 18:15) the night before Jesus was crucified. After his betrayal, Peter was burdened by deep guilt and shame. However, after Jesus’ death and resurrection, Peter was restored and forgiven three times (John 21:15). This encounter is a picture of coming to the cross repeatedly—Peter’s shame was washed away, and he was given a new purpose, to minister the gospel to others. 
In the same way, when we approach Jesus at the cross, our guilt and shame are lifted off of us (Acts 13:39) and we are free to live out our calling with renewed purpose. This is because when we come to the cross, our guilt and shame are transferred to Jesus (2 Corinthians 5:21). We see Him dying as if He were a sin captive, held fast to the cross, even though He never sinned once, and as we see this our chains are broken! Sin no longer holds us captive, and we are free to move forward with purpose. 
This is repentance, turning from sin and coming to the cross daily. 
Charles Spurgeon says:

    Repentance grows as faith grows. Do not make any mistake about it; repentance is not a temporary penance to be gotten over as fast as possible: no, it is continuous and ongoing, the grace of a lifetime, like faith itself (Spurgeon, All of Grace).

What might keep us from coming to the cross for daily cleansing, as Peter did?

Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Today, take a moment to read through Isaiah 53:1-10. As you read through this Scripture, see the events of the cross and the fountain of cleansing it represents. Picture Jesus on the cross, bearing your sins, being pierced for your transgressions, and imagine the flow of His blood as a fountain washing you clean. 
Who has believed our message and to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed? He grew up before him like a tender shoot, and like a root out of dry ground. He had no beauty or majesty to attract us to him, nothing in his appearance that we should desire him. He was despised and rejected by mankind, a man of suffering, and familiar with pain. Like one from whom people hide their faces he was despised, and we held him in low esteem. Surely he took up our pain and bore our suffering, yet we considered him punished by God, stricken by him, and afflicted. But he was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; the punishment that brought us peace was on him, and by his wounds, we are healed. We all, like sheep, have gone astray, each of us has turned to our own way; and the Lord has laid on him the iniquity of us all. He was oppressed and afflicted, yet he did not open his mouth; he was led like a lamb to the slaughter, and as a sheep before its shearers is silent, so he did not open his mouth. By oppression and judgment, he was taken away. Yet who of his generation protested? For he was cut off from the land of the living; for the transgression of my people he was punished. He was assigned a grave with the wicked, and with the rich in his death, though he had done no violence, nor was any deceit in his mouth. Yet it was the Lord’s will to crush him and cause him to suffer and though the Lord makes his life an offering for sin, he will see his offspring and prolong his days, and the will of the Lord will prosper in his hand.
Isaiah 53:1-10 NIV
Reflect on the freedom from guilt that His death provides. Then, bring to mind any burdens of sin or guilt that you need to lay down at the foot of the cross, and thank Jesus for His complete forgiveness.
As Tim Keller says: 

    It is only when you see the cross as your only hope, that you are released to do everything in your life as a way to honor and serve the One who saved you freely at infinite cost to Himself (Tim Keller, Galatians for You). 

Viewing the cross this way motivates us to live each day in gratitude for the grace that cleanses us anew. Let's close out this day with a short clip of Billy Graham speaking on this subject:

Day 5: Washing and Renewal
Washing and Renewal
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage
He saved us through the washing of rebirth and renewal by the Holy Spirit.
Titus 3:5
Teaching
When we come to the cross the first time, we are saved from the penalty of our sin through the washing of rebirth, the experience of being born again and cleansed through Jesus' death and resurrection (Titus 3:5, John 3:3-5).
As we continue to come to the cross, day after day, we begin to experience freedom from the power of sin, because the cross is a continual source of renewal—cleansing us from impurities and refreshing our spirit (1 John 1:7, 2 Corinthians 4:16). This daily act of coming to the cross ensures that we’re not walking around with spiritual “dirt” on us, as Scripture reminds us: "If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just to forgive us our sins and to cleanse us from all unrighteousness" (1 John 1:9).
Coming to the cross daily and seeing all our sin washed away is like seeing the tide washing away footprints on the sand, giving the beach a blank slate. It makes all things new (Revelation 21:5), and we begin to gain the identity of one who is cleansed and set free, as if our sins were washed away by the ocean waters. “Who is a God like you, who pardons sin and forgives the transgression of the remnant of his inheritance? You do not stay angry forever but delight to show mercy. You will again have compassion on us; you will tread our sins underfoot and hurl all our iniquities into the depths of the sea" (Micah 7:18-19).
John Owen beautifully captures this idea when he writes: 

    "Christ is the spring and fountain of all spiritual life; in Him is our life hid, and from Him it is every day derived." (John Owen, In the 1965 edition published by Banner of Truth, this quote appears in Meditations and Discourses on the Glory of Christ.)
    
    Biblical Illustration: Naaman
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Naaman, a commander of the Syrian army, sought healing from leprosy and was instructed by the prophet Elisha to wash in the Jordan River seven times (2 Kings 5). Though initially resistant, Naaman obeyed and was completely cleansed. 
Think of how Naaman had to wash in the Jordan River, which was known to be a dirty and muddy river (2 Kings 5:12). It was an unlikely place for a person to be cleansed. This is one of the reasons why Naaman resisted washing in it.
When we come to the cross we see Jesus hanging in the dirt and filth of our sin. A cross, like the Jordan River, is an unlikely place for a person to be cleansed. This is one of the reasons our natural selves, and the culture we live in, resists coming to the cross for cleansing.
Tim Keller says:

    There is no message more contrary to the way the world understands life or more subversive to its values than the gospel," (50 Quotes From Tim Keller - The Gospel Coalition website).

The Jordan River was dirty and muddy, which required Naaman to humble himself and wash in it in order to be cleansed. But after he washed in the Jordan the Bible says "his flesh was restored and became like that of a little child, and he was clean" (2 Kings 5:14).
He was clean! 
This act of Naaman in washing over and over, seven times, is like when someone polishes a tarnished mirror. They must keep scrubbing until it reflects clearly again (James 1:23-25). Just as the mirror requires persistent attention to regain its brilliance, we must continually come to the cross where we experience double crucifixion (Galatians 6:14) where God's grace cleanses our hearts and minds (Titus 3:5; 1 John 1:7-9), restoring them to reflect His glory (2 Corinthians 3:18).
Why could Naaman's healing come only through God's way, not his own ideas of cleansing?
is not mentioned
Do not be deceived; neither the sexually immoral, nor idolaters, nor adulterers, nor effeminate, nor homosexuals, 10 nor thieves, nor the greedy, nor drunkards, nor revilers, nor swindlers, will inherit the kingdom of God. 11 And such were some of you; but you were washed, but you were sanctified, but you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and in the Spirit of our God.
1 Corinthians 6:9-11

Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Begin each morning by meditating on the cross and asking for the Holy Spirit’s renewal. Take a few minutes to reflect on areas of your life where you need a fresh start, and allow Jesus' blood to wash over those areas with His forgiveness and grace (1 John 1:7). Think of any habits or patterns that you want to change, and invite Jesus to cleanse and renew your mind as you start your day.
As you meditate on the cross, begin to see the amazing love of God for you (1 John 3:16), and rejoice in what He has cleansed you from (1 Corinthians 6:11), so that you might worship and obey Him throughout your day.
John MacArthur says:

    The cross is proof of both the immense love of God and the profound wickedness of sin. When we meditate on the cross, we see God's love on display, and we are compelled to respond in worship and obedience. (The Cross: The Atonement of Christ by John MacArthur.)

What difference might a daily time of “washing” make in your thoughts and actions?

Day 6: Freedom From Sin Through The Cross
Freedom From Sin Through the Cross
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage
Having canceled the charge of our legal indebtedness, which stood against us and condemned us; he has taken it away, nailing it to the cross.
Colossians 2:14
Teaching

When Jesus died on the cross, He took upon Himself the record of all our sins, nailing it to the cross so that they might be remembered no more (Hebrews 8:12, Colossians 2:14), and that we might walk in freedom from them (Romans 6:6-7). It’s like a debt collector tearing up the record of an insurmountable debt, declaring it paid in full and no longer owed.
This act wasn’t merely symbolic; it was a miraculous transaction that freed us from the debt of sin. When Jesus died on the cross, He declared victoriously, “It is finished” (John 19:30), and these exact words (which is one word in the Greek, “teleo”) were later found on a tax receipt showing that it had been “paid in full.”
Think of this: because of Jesus’ sacrifice, it’s as though a judge stepped down from the bench, took the guilty person’s place, and bore the punishment on their behalf, setting them free. "Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us, for it is written: 'Cursed is everyone who is hung on a tree'" (Galatians 3:13). "God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God" (2 Corinthians 5:21).
We are freed! What shocking love our Judge showed in becoming our Savior! "For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life" (John 3:16).
Charles Spurgeon once reflected on this truth and said: 

    You stand before God as if you were Christ, because Christ stood before God as if He were you.” (Spurgeon, Morning and Evening) 

What a profound exchange! This freedom from the condemnation, guilt, and debt of sin (Romans 8:1) allows us to live free, without the crushing weight of sin always pulling us down—just as Jesus said, "So if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed" (John 8:36). It’s like when God set the Israelites free from slavery: their yokes of bondage were broken, and they could stand upright and walk unhindered. "I broke the yoke of slavery from your neck so you can walk with your heads held high" (Leviticus 26:13).
This same freedom is ours in Christ, who invites us to exchange our burdens for His rest: "Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, and I will give you rest. Take my yoke upon you and learn from me, for I am gentle and humble in heart, and you will find rest for your souls. For my yoke is easy and my burden is light" (Matthew 11:28-30).
Let us live as those who are free, no longer bound by the yoke of sin or slavery, but walking confidently in the liberty Christ purchased for us. "It is for freedom that Christ has set us free. Stand firm, then, and do not let yourselves be burdened again by a yoke of slavery" (Galatians 5:1).
What does it mean to you that Jesus “canceled the charge” against you?

Biblical Illustration: Woman Caught in Adultery
Awaiting feedback
The woman caught in adultery (John 8:1-11) is a powerful example of freedom through Jesus. Brought before Jesus, she stood condemned, but He canceled the charges against her, knowing that He would soon take the guilt of her wrongs on Himself, and He silenced her accusers, then offered her complete forgiveness, telling her to “go and sin no more.” 
Jesus didn’t condemn her, but He also called her to a new life of freedom. This freedom from condemnation allowed her to leave her old life behind, showing that Jesus’ forgiveness and grace leads to life transformation (Titus 2:11-12).
John Piper writes:

    In this passage, Jesus doesn't excuse sin or minimize its gravity. He confronts it with truth and offers grace. He shows us that true condemnation comes from within, from a guilty conscience. And true freedom comes from outside, from the forgiveness of Christ. - John Piper, "Neither Do I Condemn You," Desiring God, March 6, 2011.


    Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Today, take a few moments to write down any lingering guilt or feelings of condemnation you carry. Bring these to the cross, knowing that Jesus has already canceled these “charges.” Reflect on the freedom Jesus gives you to “go and sin no more,” and ask Him to help you walk in that freedom. Remember that because of Jesus, you’re not only forgiven but empowered to live a new life.
How might daily remembrance of the cross change how you respond to temptation?


Day 7: The Bondage of Sin and the Freedom of the Spirit
The Power of the Spirit
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Scripture: 
So I say, walk by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the flesh.
Galatians 5:16
Teaching
The Apostle Paul introduces a profound truth in Galatians 5:16: walking by the Spirit naturally leads to freedom from gratifying the desires of the flesh. It’s like following a clear path through a dense forest, where each step guided by the Spirit steers us away from the thorns and snares of sin. “Your word is a lamp to my feet and a light to my path” (Psalm 119:105), illuminating the way forward.
We all know that there is a battle raging within us. “For I do not do the good I want to do, but the evil I do not want to do—this I keep on doing” (Romans 7:19). We have fought and failed numerous times. At one time, it seemed that I could only win the war for about 2 to 3 weeks, and then the devil would trample me. The devil works through our flesh to stir up ungodly desires, the purpose of which is to destroy us: “the thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy” (John 10:10).
The flesh represents our sinful desires—a part of us constantly at war with God’s Spirit (Galatians 5:17). This battle is like a raging storm within us, with the flesh and Spirit clashing like opposing winds. Yet when we walk by the Spirit, it’s as if the storm calms and the sun breaks through the clouds, signaling victory for righteousness. “The mind governed by the flesh is death, but the mind governed by the Spirit is life and peace” (Romans 8:6).
John Owen once warned, "Be killing sin or it will be killing you." This vivid imagery reminds us of the seriousness of this spiritual battle. Walking by the Spirit is not a passive endeavor but an active pursuit, relying on His power: “Not by might nor by power, but by my Spirit,’ says the Lord Almighty” (Zechariah 4:6).
Our flesh demands gratification, like a relentless hunger that is never satisfied, always urging us to consume more but leaving us unfulfilled and empty. "You will eat but not be satisfied; your stomach will still be empty" (Micah 6:14). We could replace that word "eat" with whatever our propensity is. "You will smoke but not be satisfied", "you will do drugs but not be satisfied", "you will watch porn but not be satisfied", "you will..."
The flesh offers fleeting pleasures, always promising happiness but the pleasure is only temporary, passing away shortly after it is gratified. “The world and its desires pass away, but whoever does the will of God lives forever” (1 John 2:17).
John Piper describes these fleeting pleasures of sin: 

    Sin gets its power by persuading me to believe that I will be happier if I follow it. The power of all temptation is the prospect that it will make me happier. - John Piper, "Future Grace: The Purifying Power of the Promises of God."

And we discover through it all that sin, though enticing and promising happiness, enslaves us. "Jesus replied, 'Very truly I tell you, everyone who sins is a slave to sin'" (John 8:34). Sin binds us with invisible chains that tighten with every indulgence, leading us deeper into captivity. “For a man is a slave to whatever has mastered him” (2 Peter 2:19).
True freedom comes only when Jesus Christ sets us free, just as a prisoner finds liberty only when someone outside the prison opens the door for them. "So if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed" (John 8:36). This freedom is not merely a release from bondage but an invitation to live in the fullness of life He offers: "I have come that they may have life, and have it to the full" (John 10:10).
The way this happens is that we learn how to walk by the Spirit (Galatians 5:16), to be led by the Spirit (Romans 8:14), to keep in step with the Spirit (Galatians 5:25). This is like traveling with a skilled guide through unfamiliar terrain—asking for His leading, trusting His direction at every turn, and matching His pace to safely reach our destination.
As Charles Spurgeon encourages:

    Without the Spirit of God, we can do nothing. We are as ships without wind. Charles Spurgeon, Sermon #2225, "The Power of the Holy Spirit."

Jesus Christ died on the cross to give us His Holy Spirit so that the Spirit would live inside of every believer (Ephesians 1:13). We now have power within us that we did not have as an unbeliever. And "greater is He who is in you, than he who is in the world" (1 John 4:4).  
How does walking by the Spirit differ from trying to overcome sin through your own strength?

Biblical Illustration: The Israelites in Egypt
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
The Israelites lived as slaves in Egypt, bound to Pharaoh's oppressive rule. Their physical captivity was real and severe, and it represents humanity's spiritual bondage to sin. 
When God delivered the Israelites, He redeemed them by the blood of a lamb and command them to leave Egypt; but He also provided a way through the Red Sea and led them by His presence—a cloud by day and fire by night (Exodus 13:21).
So you have these two truths connected together: redemption by the Lamb and leading by God's presence.
The Israelites' deliverance mirrors the freedom believers receive when they put their faith in the Lamb of God, Jesus Christ, who died to redeem us from slavery to sin. Then, as we learn to walk by the Spirit God leads us away from our bondage and provides the power to live free.
As we experience this new life, we can join our voices with the Israelites who sang this song after their rescue from Egypt:
“With your unfailing love you lead the people you have redeemed. In your might, you guide them to your sacred home" (Exodus 15:13). 
In what ways have you seen God lead you away from sin, just as He led the Israelites out of Egypt?

Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Freedom begins by choosing to “walk by the Spirit” daily. Here’s how:

    Invite the Spirit of God to lead you today: Begin each day in prayer, asking for His direct leading.
    Engage in the Word: Meditate on Scripture which will align your thoughts with God’s will (Romans 12:2). 
    Live at the foot of the cross: The cross and the Holy Spirit of God are interconnected. Note this connection in 1 Corinthians 2:1-5 and Galatians 3:1-5. 
    Remember the power of the resurrection of Jesus. The same power that rose Jesus from the dead lives in every believer and raises us out of the tomb of sin. "I also pray that you will understand the incredible greatness of God’s power for us who believe him. This is the same mighty power 20 that raised Christ from the dead and seated him in the place of honor at God’s right hand in the heavenly realms" (Ephesians 1:19-20).
    Yield to the Spirit: Consciously surrender areas of struggle to God throughout the day. Picture Him taking you by the hand, like the angel took Lot by the hand (Genesis 19:16) and led him out of a very impure city, led him to safety, and see Him leading you away from the sin that destroys.

What practical steps can you take today to rely on the Spirit rather than your flesh?


Day 8: The Conflict Within
The Internal Conflict
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Scripture: 
For the flesh desires what is contrary to the Spirit, and the Spirit what is contrary to the flesh. They are in conflict with each other so that you are not to do what you want.
Galatians 5:17
Teaching
Just as Rebekah had twins within her, and they struggled against each other (Genesis 25:22), so every believer experiences an internal struggle between the flesh and the Spirit within us (Galatians 5:17). You know what this is like; you've experienced it. It’s like a tug-of-war, with the flesh pulling us toward sin (Romans 7:23), while the Spirit draws us toward holiness (Romans 8:5-6).
In Mere Christianity, C. S. Lewis said: 

    Christians often think they are making no progress because they constantly feel the tug of sin. But the tug itself shows the Spirit is at work.

This conflict, this tug-of-war, is ongoing; it's never-ending (Romans 7:18-19). It can’t be resolved by sheer willpower or human strength (Zechariah 4:6). Victory does not come through trying but through dying to our flesh—like a seed falling to the ground and dying, bringing forth new life (Galatians 5:24). "Very truly I tell you, unless a kernel of wheat falls to the ground and dies, it remains only a single seed. But if it dies, it produces many seeds" (John 12:24).
Charles Spurgeon expounded on John 12:24, saying, 

    Death is the path to life. Christ’s death brought forth a harvest of souls, and in the same way, every believer must die to self if they would live to God. - The Sermons of Charles Spurgeon Volume 12, particularly in his reflections on surrender and grace in the Christian life.

In John 12:24, Jesus spoke of His upcoming death on the cross, showing that He would produce "fruit" through His death, that is, He would give life to many people (Romans 5:18-19). We, too, die to ourselves (Luke 9:23), die to our flesh (Galatians 2:20), and in so doing, the Spirit of God produces fruit in us (Galatians 5:22-23), and we overcome our sin struggle and have the victory (Romans 8:13). Samson illustrates overcoming through death as he, foreshadowing Jesus, died and brought victory for his people. "Thus he killed many more when he died than while he lived" (Judges 16:30). 
Tim Keller frequently used this metaphor: 

    True life comes only when we are willing to die to our own ambitions, comforts, and control. The paradox of the gospel is that losing yourself for Christ leads to finding yourself in Him. - Cited in "The Reason for God" and "Romans for You".

Victory comes not through the strength of our own efforts but through surrendering in weakness (2 Corinthians 12:9). As a flag raised on a battlefield signals the end of resistance, we surrender to the Spirit, and He wins the battle in us (Galatians 5:16). So, the way to rise is to sink (Matthew 23:12), the way to victory is to yield (James 4:7), and we conquer when we are conquered by the Spirit of God (Romans 8:37).
Take a moment and reflect on the following Scriptures, and see how they teach this truth:

    Exodus 14:14
    "The Lord will fight for you; you need only to be still."
    This verse emphasizes surrendering to God's power and ceasing self-reliant efforts.
    2 Chronicles 20:17
    "You will not need to fight in this battle. Stand firm, hold your position, and see the salvation of the Lord on your behalf."
    Jehoshaphat’s battle teaches us to depend entirely on God, not our strength.
    Zechariah 4:6
    "Not by might, nor by power, but by my Spirit, says the Lord of hosts."
    Victory comes through God’s Spirit, not human strength or strategies.
    Psalm 46:10
    "Be still, and know that I am God."
    Stillness and trust in God bring victory, as we surrender control to Him.
    Isaiah 40:29-31
    "He gives power to the faint, and to him who has no might he increases strength...but they who wait for the Lord shall renew their strength."
    Renewed strength comes through waiting on God, not personal striving.
    Matthew 11:28-30
    "Come to me, all who labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest...For my yoke is easy, and my burden is light."
    Surrendering burdens to Christ brings rest and true strength.
    John 15:5
    "I am the vine; you are the branches. Whoever abides in me and I in him, he it is that bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing."
    Abiding in Christ, not self-effort, produces fruit and victory.
    2 Corinthians 12:9-10
    "But he said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.’ Therefore I will boast all the more gladly of my weaknesses, so that the power of Christ may rest upon me."
    Paul teaches that God’s strength shines in our surrender and weakness.
    Galatians 2:20
    "I have been crucified with Christ. It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me."
    Surrendering self allows Christ to live and work through us.
    Ephesians 6:10-11
    "Finally, be strong in the Lord and in the strength of his might. Put on the whole armor of God, that you may be able to stand against the schemes of the devil."
    Victory in spiritual warfare comes through relying on God’s strength and armor.

These passages collectively remind us that surrendering our efforts (Proverbs 3:5-6), dying to our flesh (Galatians 5:24), and depending on the Lord (Psalm 37:5) brings victory by God's Spirit (Zechariah 4:6, Romans 8:13). 

    The reason Jesus told us to deny ourselves is because 'ourselves' is the biggest thing that gets in our way. Crucify your flesh! - JC Ryle, published on X.

Why is it important to recognize this internal conflict rather than ignore it?

Day 8: The Conflict Within
Biblical Illustration: The Israelites in the Wilderness
Consider the Israelites during their wilderness journey. After God miraculously delivered them from Egypt (Exodus 14:21-22) and parted the Red Sea, they, and the rabble that came out with them, repeatedly struggled with their fleshly desires (Numbers 11:4-6). Despite witnessing God’s power and provision, their flesh constantly pulled them toward rebellion (Psalm 78:40-41).
In Exodus 16, the Israelites grumbled about not having food, longing for the "meat pots" and bread of Egypt (Exodus 16:3). God, in His grace, provided manna from heaven (Exodus 16:14-15), yet many still struggled to trust Him fully (Deuteronomy 8:3). Some disobeyed His command by gathering more than they needed, only for it to spoil (Exodus 16:19-20).
Their conflict was clear: would they trust and obey God’s Spirit-led provision (Isaiah 55:2-3) or succumb to their fleshly cravings and rebellion (1 Corinthians 10:6-11)? 
This conflict illustrates our struggle as believers. Even after experiencing God’s salvation (Romans 6:4), the old desires of the flesh still tempt us to seek satisfaction outside of His will (Galatians 5:17). Just as the Israelites had to learn to rely on God daily for their sustenance (Matthew 6:11), we must depend on the Spirit daily (Galatians 5:25) and learn to walk by the Spirit to overcome the pull of the flesh (Romans 8:5-6).
Are there "meat pots of Egypt" in your life—things you’ve left behind but still long for in moments of weakness? How can you bring these desires to God?
How does the example of manna challenge you to depend on God for your daily needs rather than seeking satisfaction elsewhere?

Day 8: The Conflict Within
Practical Application
To address this internal conflict within us:

    Acknowledge Your Weakness: Admit areas where the flesh often wins.
    Confess and Repent: Bring those struggles to God in prayer.
    Yield and Surrender: Practice dying to yourself, surrendering to God, yielding to the work of the Spirit.
    Feed the Spirit: Strengthen your Spirit-led desires by meditating on the cross of Christ, worshiping the Lamb of God as in Revelation 5:6-14, spending time with other believers.

What specific action will you take today to feed the Spirit instead of the flesh?

Day 9: The Beautifying Work of the Spirit
The Beautifying Work of the Holy Spirit
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Scripture 
And you also were included in Christ when you heard the message of truth, the gospel of your salvation. When you believed, you were marked in him with a seal, the promised Holy Spirit.
Ephesians 1:13
Teaching
The Holy Spirit enters our lives the moment we believe in the gospel of Jesus Christ (Ephesians 1:13). The Holy Spirit’s entry is like a master craftsman moving into an empty, broken-down house, immediately beginning a beautiful renovation to make it a glorious temple for God (1 Corinthians 6:19-20). "And you are God's house" (1 Corinthians 3:9).
This Master Craftsman, the Holy Spirit, intends to fill you (Ephesians 3:19) and restore you to full glory, making you into the image of Jesus Christ (2 Corinthians 3:18, Romans 8:29). So, He sets about removing any rot, mold, or mildew (previous sin habits, Colossians 3:9-10), and bringing in new drywall, cabinets, and appliances (establishing new habits of righteousness, Ephesians 4:22-24).
William Gurnall makes this point very clear:

    "The Spirit’s work is not merely to cleanse the soul but to beautify it, that it may be a fit dwelling place for God."  - The Christian in Complete Armor, Part 2, Chapter 1: "Of the Holy Spirit’s Office in the Believer."

This "beautifying" of our lives is not accomplished by our own efforts. The Israelites who were bitten by the serpents in the desert (Numbers 21:4-9) were not restored through their own efforts, but rather by looking to an uplifted serpent on a pole. In the same way, it’s not through our own works or efforts that we are made new, but by faith in Christ’s finished work on the cross (John 3:14-16). 
The more we are exposed to the gospel, the more we discover that what Jesus did for us (dying to remove our sin), the Holy Spirit does in us (actual removing of sin in practice). Christ's work on the cross is the seed of salvation, and the Holy Spirit is the Gardener who nurtures it, bringing forth the fruit of righteousness, and making it a beautiful garden. "He has made everything beautiful in his time" (Ecclesiastes 3:11 KJV).
In Scripture, the Holy Spirit is pictured as "New Wine" (joy, vitality, and transformation) and as "Olive Oil" (consecration, healing, illumination), and He makes us "like a well-watered garden":
They will come and shout for joy on the heights of Zion; they will rejoice in the bounty of the Lord—the grain, the new wine and the olive oil, the young of the flocks and herds. They will be like a well-watered garden, and they will sorrow no more.
Jeremiah 31:12 NIV
We experience the power of the Spirit's ongoing beautifying work in us the same way we experienced Him at the beginning of our Christian journey: by believing the gospel message (Colossians 2:6). He opened our hearts to believe the gospel in the beginning (Acts 16:14), and as we keep in step with Him (Galatians 5:25), He leads us deeper into the gospel where we become more like Jesus (2 Corinthians 3:18).
To people who initially believed the gospel message (the Galatians), but then turned away from it in an attempt to beautify their lives by their own effort, the Apostle Paul wrote:
You received the Spirit because you believed the message you heard about Christ. How foolish can you be? After starting your Christian lives in the Spirit, why are you now trying to become perfect by your own human effort?
Galatians 3:2-3
In other words, we overcome sin and grow and mature the same way our Christian lives started: by believing the message we heard about Christ (Romans 10:17), that He died for our sins and rose from the dead on the third day (1 Corinthians 15:3-4). This is the real value of attending a church that is focused on preaching the gospel of Jesus Christ (1 Timothy 4:13). Not only does it bring in unbelievers and convert them (Romans 1:16), but it educates, grows, matures, and beautifies believers (Ephesians 4:11-13).
According to this teaching today, what role does the Holy Spirit play in your life?

Day 9: The Beautifying Work of the Spirit
Biblical Illustration: Pentecost
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
On the day of Pentecost, the disciples received the Holy Spirit after Jesus’ ascension (Acts 2:1-4). These men, previously timid, uncertain and fearful (John 20:19), became bold witnesses for Christ (Acts 4:13), performing miracles (Acts 3:6-8) and preaching the gospel fearlessly (Acts 2:14-41). They were changed into the bold and beautiful image of their Savior and Lord, Jesus Christ (2 Corinthians 3:18).
This transformation was not due to their efforts but because of the Spirit’s power within them (Acts 1:8). Similarly, our lives are raised up and beautified, and equipped for freedom and ministry through the work of the Holy Spirit (Ephesians 2:10). As we walk with Him (Galatians 5:25), we stop gratifying the lusts of our flesh (Galatians 5:16), and He renovates us and beautifies us, transforming us into the beautiful image of Jesus Christ (Romans 8:29).
And we all, who with unveiled faces contemplate the Lord’s glory, are being transformed into his image with ever-increasing glory, which comes from the Lord, who is the Spirit.
2 Corinthians 3:18
People who are outside of Christ do not have the Spirit's power (Romans 8:9), but those who are "in Christ" have the Holy Spirit (2 Corinthians 1:21-22). He is able to make dramatic changes in us (2 Corinthians 5:17), and He does so sometimes in marvelous and unexpected ways (Ephesians 3:20).
How have you experienced the Holy Spirit empowering you in unexpected ways?

Day 9: The Beautifying Work of the Spirit

Day 10: The Battle Within
Called to Battle
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage
For the flesh desires what is contrary to the Spirit, and the Spirit what is contrary to the flesh. They are in conflict with each other, so that you do not do what you want.
Galatians 5:17
Teaching
The Christian life is full of paradoxes. It’s like walking a tightrope between two mountains, balancing between rest and work. We are to rest in Christ's finished work on the cross (Hebrews 4:2-3) for our salvation, yet we are to work out our salvation with fear and trembling (Philippians 2:12-13). There are many more paradoxes, but people seem to fall off on one side or the other of this one.
Some people plant their flag on "resting in Christ" — like setting up camp on a mountaintop, admiring the view but never climbing down to engage in the valley battle. Their only knowledge seems to be what Jesus did for them in the gospel. They believe they are winning even though they are not consciously and specifically fighting sin, and they only want to hear about Jesus and His cross. 
Others have a high sin consciousness — it’s like they are carrying a heavy backpack full of burdens, trudging up a steep hill with no rest in sight. They know they are in a battle, and they know to work out their salvation, so they might seem flustered, ever striving, and exhausted in the fight. 
Both of these perspectives can produce ineffective Christians. We want to be balanced, always resting fully in the work of Jesus on the cross and always being careful to work out our own salvation and "fight the good fight of faith" (1 Timothy 6:12). 
Today, we want to examine the battle we are in, which will tell us that we have a fight on our hands. It’s not a peaceful garden stroll; it’s more like standing on a battlefield, armor on, sword in hand, knowing the victory is secure but the fight still rages on.
JC Ryle said:
The Christian life is not a walk in the park; it’s a battlefield (see Ephesians 6:10-20). The flesh and the Spirit are at war, pulling us in opposite directions. The flesh, like a crafty serpent (Genesis 3), tempts us to indulge in sinful pleasures, while the Spirit, as a gentle dove (John 1:32), calls us to live a life of holy obedience.
Dr. John MacArthur says:

    The conflict is relentless and intense because the flesh sets its desire against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh. This is the reality of the Christian experience. - John MacArthur, commentary on Galatians 5:17

Paul paints a vivid picture of this internal conflict, describing it not as a mere "struggle" but as a raging war that leads to captivity for the losing side. “For I see another law at work in my members, waging war against the law of my mind and making me a prisoner of the law of sin that is at work in my members” (Romans 7:23).
John Piper says:

    Paul does not describe this struggle to give us despair but to call us to war.  John Piper, sermon entitled "The War Within" 

Every Christian is like a piece of wood that is not fully dry, that has green parts, when it’s set on fire. While we can burn brightly for Jesus, there are still areas in our lives—like the damp or green parts of the wood—that can quench the flame. This is why it’s crucial to recognize that we are in a spiritual battle. If we’re not learning to fight, the ‘green,’ (unrefined parts of our flesh) can quench our zeal and passion for Christ and make us ineffective. This is why the church on earth is called "the church militant", we are Christian soldiers who fight against sin and who crucify our flesh daily.
The stakes of this spiritual battle are immense, just like a surgeon’s choice between removing a deadly tumor or letting it grow unchecked, which would mean the difference between life and death. As Paul emphasizes, “For if you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the misdeeds of the body, you will live” (Romans 8:23). So, this is clearly a matter of eternal life or eternal death.
Charles Spurgeon warned:

    Beware of thinking lightly of sin. Take heed how you trifle with that which will destroy you if you be found in it. - Charles Spurgeon, Morning and Evening

Let us remind ourselves that we are in a battle (Ephesians 6:12), that we have an internal conflict (Galatians 5:17), that we must be prepared to fight (1 Timothy 6:12), and that we need to learn the biblical strategies God gives us to win (2 Corinthians 10:3-5). We will study these strategies in the upcoming days (Psalm 119:105; Joshua 1:8).
"Dear friends, I warn you as “temporary residents and foreigners” to keep away from worldly desires that wage war against your very souls" (1 Peter 2:11). 
"Therefore, preparing your minds for action, and being sober-minded, set your hope fully on the grace that will be brought to you at the revelation of Jesus Christ. As obedient children, do not be conformed to the passions of your former ignorance, but as he who called you is holy, you also be holy in all your conduct, since it is written, 'You shall be holy, for I am holy.' (1 Pet. 1:13–16)
What are you currently doing to engage in this battle against the flesh?

Day 10: The Battle Within
Biblical Illustration: David
In 2 Samuel 11:1-4, we find David, a king called to lead his people in battle, choosing instead to remain in comfort and ease, like a soldier laying aside his armor in the midst of a war. This led to his moral downfall. David’s lack of vigilance highlights a critical truth: when we let our guard down, we become vulnerable to sin. David’s story warns us that lack of vigilance in times of spiritual warfare can be deadly.
We must always remember that before we became Christians we were at war with God (Colossians 1:21, James 4:4) and at peace with our sin. Now that we have been justified by faith we are at peace with God (Romans 5:1), and must be at war with our sin.
What was David supposed to be doing instead of remaining in Jerusalem? See 2 Samuel 11:1-4
 How can lack of awareness of the battle you are in make you more susceptible to sin?

"Work at living in peace with everyone, and work at living a holy life, for those who are not holy will not see the Lord" (Hebrews 12:14).

The first step in fighting the flesh is recognizing the battle (Romans 7:23), like a soldier hearing the distant drumbeat of war and preparing to don their armor (Ephesians 6:11-13). Ask God to make you a real student of His Word (2 Timothy 2:15), a lover of the gospel of Jesus Christ (Romans 1:16), vigilant in prayer (Colossians 4:2), and disciplined in your daily life (1 Timothy 4:7-8). Having now freed us from our shackles, He has bound us to follow Him and obey Him (Matthew 16:24-25). This means we fight sin and follow Jesus (Hebrews 12:1-2), we die to ourselves and we live for Him who died for us (Galatians 2:20; 2 Corinthians 5:15).

    A holy violence, a conflict of warfare, a fight, a soldier's life, a wrestling, are spoken of as a characteristic of the true Christian. -  J.C. RYLE quoted by David S. Steele in Be Killing Sin: The Art of War on the Battlefield of Faith

Day 11 Victory Through the Cross
Jesus Triumphed At The Cross
Key Passage
And having disarmed the powers and authorities, he made a public spectacle of them, triumphing over them by the cross.
Colossians 2:15
Have you ever wondered why Scripture tells us that Jesus Christ triumphed in His death (Colossians 2:15)? And what that truth has to do with overcoming sin in our lives?
Well, it’s clear that Jesus’ death is our death to sin (Romans 6:6-7), that Jesus’ resurrection is our resurrection to new life (Romans 6:4; 1 Peter 1:3), and that His victory over sin and Satan is our victory over sin and Satan (1 Corinthians 15:57). So when we read about Jesus’ victory below, understand that Jesus did all that He did for you and for me (Romans 5:8), and that we were considered by God as being "in Christ" in whatever He did and accomplished (2 Corinthians 5:21; Ephesians 2:4-6)!
At the cross, Jesus secured victory over sin, Satan, and death for us (Colossians 2:15). His triumph wasn’t through physical might but through sacrificial love and obedience to the Father’s will. “And being found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to death—even death on a cross!” (Philippians 2:8).
This ultimate act of humility and strength is as if a mighty warrior disarmed his enemy not by striking him but by allowing himself to be struck. And in so doing, He broke the chains of bondage for all who were captive. "He has sent me to proclaim freedom for the prisoners and recovery of sight for the blind, to set the oppressed free, to proclaim the year of the Lord’s favor" (Luke 4:18-19).
The cross is our source of victory and the ultimate reminder that we fight from a position of triumph, not defeat. Don’t be afraid of the battles you must fight, for the cross is a banner raised high over a battlefield, proclaiming a victory already won. “But thanks be to God! He gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ” (1 Corinthians 15:57).
Through His death, Jesus destroyed, for you, the one who held the power of death. “Since the children have flesh and blood, he too shared in their humanity so that by his death he might break the power of him who holds the power of death—that is, the devil” (Hebrews 2:14). In looking at the cross and resurrection of Jesus, you see that there is absolutely nothing to fear in death. "...and free those who all their lives were held in slavery by their fear of death" (Hebrews 2:15).
Jesus came to die, and in dying and rising from the dead He killed death for us, assuring us of eternal life. “For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life” (John 3:16).
Our precious Lord Jesus Christ, our General in battle, bore the punishment for our sins, securing peace with God for us (Romans 5:1). He took the blows that would have been ours, and in so doing, He built a bridge of reconciliation where there was once a chasm of separation. “But he was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; the punishment that brought us peace was on him, and by his wounds we are healed” (Isaiah 53:5).
The victory of the cross gives us confidence to face any challenge and fight any battle, knowing we will overcome. “No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us" (Romans 8:37). Our General has won the war for us, at a great cost to Himself, and now we are fighting our battles knowing we will win too. "As He was, so are we in this world" (1 John 4:17).
But the cross does more. It stands as a testament to Jesus' love and power, proclaiming the ultimate defeat of sin and death. One day our mortality will put on immortality, and we will be able to ask, "Where, O death, is your victory? Where, O death, is your sting?” (1 Corinthians 15:55). And it reminds us that His grace is sufficient for us, even, and especially in, our weakness. “But he said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.’ Therefore, I will boast all the more gladly about my weaknesses, so that Christ’s power may rest on me” (2 Corinthians 12:9).
How does knowing Jesus has already won the battle over sin, death, and Satan encourage you in your battle against the flesh?

Day 11 Victory Through the Cross
Biblical Illustration: The Battle with the Amalekites
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
In Exodus 17:8-13, we read the account of the Israelites in battle against their enemies, the Amalekites. God told Moses to go to the top of a hill, holding up the staff of God. As long as he held the staff up, the Israelites prevailed, but when his hands dropped, the Amalekites gained the upper hand. 
To ensure victory, Aaron and Hur supported Moses, one on each side held up his arms, and the outcome was: "So his hands held steady until sunset. As a result, Joshua overwhelmed the army of Amalek in battle" (Exodus 17:12-13).
There they were, three men on a hill, the one in the middle winning the victory for those fighting below. The victory came at "sunset", as darkness came over the land. 
This story showed how the victory for the Israelites came through the strength of the one in the middle of two others—Moses. Just as Moses’ obedience and the help of his companions secured Israel’s victory, we find our victory through Jesus, the man in the middle (between two thieves), who died for us and rose to intercede for us. Jesus’ sacrifice on the cross is like Moses in the middle with hands raised—He won the battle for us as darkness came over the whole land (Mark 15:33).
As you fight your battles with the flesh, temptation, and the allurements of the world, remember that you are assured victory because Jesus won the battle! “But thanks be to God! He gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ” (1 Corinthians 15:57). “In this world you will have trouble. But take heart! I have overcome the world” (John 16:33).

    Moses represents the mediator, Jesus Christ, in that he stood between God and the people, and it was through his intercession that the victory was won. Moses' hands lifted in prayer remind us of Christ's work on the cross. Jesus, in His suffering, held out His arms as the ultimate mediator. The victory over Amalek came not by human might, but by God's intervention, showing that Christ alone can bring victory over sin and death. - Charles Spurgeon, commentary on Exodus 17. 

For further reading, this is an excellent message on Christ our Mediator by Charles Spurgeon! 
P.S. Some wonder why we include so much emphasis on the gospel of Jesus Christ. Why not just give steps and practical application so we know exactly what to do to win the battle?
This illustration in Exodus 17 should make the answer clear: as Moses lifted his hands, Israel prevailed in the battle, but when his hands lowered, Amalek prevailed (Exodus 17:11). The victory wasn’t in Israel’s strength or strategy but in what Moses did on the hill, and God’s power working through those who were fighting the battle. In the same way, Jesus won the battle for us, and we must know that so that we fight from a position of strength and victory.
In our battle against sin, the cross of Christ—the blood He shed for us—is everything! That’s where all the power is! "...for our gospel did not come to you in word only, but also in power and in the Holy Spirit and with full assurance; just as you know what kind of men we proved to be among you for your sake.” (1 Thessalonians 1:5).
How does Moses' victory on the hilltop point to our dependence on Jesus' victory on the cross?
And let us run with endurance the race God has set before us. 2 We do this by keeping our eyes on Jesus, the champion who initiates and perfects our faith. Because of the joy awaiting him, he endured the cross, disregarding its shame. Now he is seated in the place of honor beside God’s throne.

Day 11 Victory Through the Cross
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
To win your battles, see yourself as having died to sin with Christ. “Since we have died to sin, how can we continue to live in it?” (Romans 6:2). He died on the cross, and you died with Him (Romans 6:6-8). He was buried in a tomb; you were buried with Him (Romans 6:4; Colossians 2:12). Therefore, when temptation knocks on your door, see yourself as dead, knowing a dead person cannot answer a knock on a door.
Jesus was raised from the dead; you were raised with Him. “In the same way, count yourselves dead to sin but alive to God in Christ Jesus” (Romans 6:11). You are now alive to God and live for righteousness (1 Peter 2:24). Pray for strength to resist the enemy (James 4:7) and align your heart with Christ’s finished work (Hebrews 12:2).
Also, just as Aaron and Hur helped Moses by holding up his hands during the battle, we are called to support one another in the spiritual battle, offering prayer and encouragement to fellow believers (Exodus 17:12; Galatians 6:2).
Meditate on Colossians 2:13-15 daily this week: “When you were dead in your sins and in the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made you alive with Christ. He forgave us all our sins, having canceled the charge of our legal indebtedness, which stood against us and condemned us; he has taken it away, nailing it to the cross. And having disarmed the powers and authorities, he made a public spectacle of them, triumphing over them by the cross.”
Write down one area of your life where you need to apply the victory of the cross.
