Day 12: Stand and Fight
Put On the Whole Armor of God
Key Passage
Therefore put on the full armor of God, so that when the day of evil comes, you may be able to stand your ground, and after you have done everything, to stand.
Ephesians 6:13
Several truths emerge when reading through the biblical passage on spiritual armor (Ephesians 6:10-18). First, we understand that our "armor" is Christ Himself. Scripture tells us to "put on the Lord Jesus Christ" (Romans 13:14).
Think of it this way: In Ephesians 6:10-18 the apostle Paul does not say that for sexual sins and drunkenness we should put on purity and sobriety, or for lying and greed, we should put on honesty and giving, but rather, for everything we "put on the Lord Jesus Christ," as if to say that Jesus Christ is our armor. Until Christ is put on, we are unarmed, naked, and exposed to the enemy's tactics (Isaiah 61:10; Galatians 3:27).
William Gurnall stated this clearly:

    It is not a man's morality and virtues that will repel a temptation when it is sent with a full charge from Satan's cannon...it is, rather, the man in armour, that is in Christ. - Gurnall, William. The Christian in Complete Armour (p. 22). Kindle Edition. 

Second, we are to "do everything to stand." This means we must take sin seriously, recognizing that “our struggle is not against flesh and blood, but against the rulers, against the authorities, against the powers of this dark world and against the spiritual forces of evil in the heavenly realms” (Ephesians 6:12; 2 Corinthians 10:3-5). To stand firm, we put on the full armor of God: truth, righteousness, the gospel of peace, faith, salvation, and the Word of God (Ephesians 6:14-17; Isaiah 59:17).
"Do everything to stand" means fighting against sin with God's power (Ephesians 3:20-21; Zechariah 4:6), trusting in God's plan (Psalm 32:8; Proverbs 3:5-6), and leaning on God’s promises: “No temptation has overtaken you except what is common to mankind. And God is faithful; He will not let you be tempted beyond what you can bear. But when you are tempted, He will also provide a way out so that you can endure it” (1 Corinthians 10:13; Psalm 34:19).
"Do everything to stand" includes dependence on God, knowing that “it is God who works in you to will and to act in order to fulfill His good purpose” (Philippians 2:13; 2 Peter 1:3-4). Through His power, we resist sin and remain steadfast: “Be on your guard; stand firm in the faith; be courageous; be strong” (1 Corinthians 16:13; Galatians 5:1).
Third, the clear purpose for putting on the whole armor of God is "so that when the day of evil comes, you may be able to stand your ground, and after you have done everything, to stand" (Ephesians 6:13; Matthew 24:13). Clearly, "standing," not falling, is the result of being fully suited for battle.
Scripture is clear that the wicked "will not stand in the judgment" (Psalm 1:5; Malachi 3:2) but will blow away with the wind, like chaff, fleeting and weightless, when a gust carries it off a threshing floor (Job 21:18; Isaiah 17:13). Believers, who are clothed in Christ, are like trees rooted deep by streams of water (Psalm 1:3; Jeremiah 17:7-8). They will not only stand firm in the judgment but also remain steadfast in their everyday lives (Colossians 1:23).
This steadfastness does not mean that we never fall. Indeed, “the righteous fall seven times, yet they rise again” (Proverbs 24:16; Micah 7:8). But when we fall, we “approach God’s throne of grace with confidence” (Hebrews 4:16), thank Him for His abundant forgiveness (1 John 1:9; Psalm 103:12), and ask for wisdom to develop a plan against the falls (James 1:5; Proverbs 2:6-7).
What does it mean to put on the armor of God and to stand firm?

Day 12: Stand and Fight
Biblical Illustration: Jesus' Temptation
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
In Matthew 4:1-11, Jesus demonstrates how to stand firm against Satan’s attacks. Armed with the Word of God, He countered each temptation from Satan with Scripture. This teaches us the importance of preparation, reliance on God’s Word, and standing firm when under attack.
Jesus won this battle for us! God the Father sees every believer as being “in Christ,” so when Jesus defeated Satan, His victory is counted as ours. It’s like a victorious general sharing the spoils of war with his people—His triumph becomes their triumph, and His victory banner flies over all who belong to Him.
As Paul writes, “In all these things we are more than conquerors through Him who loved us” (Romans 8:37). For 40 years Israel failed in the wilderness, wandering aimlessly like a ship lost at sea, battered by winds of doubt and disobedience. But for 40 days Jesus triumphed in the wilderness, navigating every temptation like a captain steering through a storm to bring His people safely to shore.
We must remember, He triumphed for us! What Jesus accomplished when He lived, when He was tempted, and when He died and rose again, He did on our behalf. It reminds me of a climber scaling a mountain—not for his own sake, but to fasten a rope and secure a way up for those who would follow.
And through Christ’s victory, we are not only forgiven but also empowered to walk in newness of life (Romans 6:4). His resurrection life flows through us like a spring of living water, refreshing dry and weary souls, enabling us to rise and walk in the light of His grace.
Friend, it can be challenging to recognize that, as Christians, we are called to engage in a spiritual battle and resist the desires of our flesh. Sometimes, we focus so much on the grace of the cross that we forget Jesus also invites us to take up our cross daily and follow Him (Luke 9:23). When we lose sight of this balance, we risk misinterpreting the gospel as permission to indulge our earthly desires, and this leads to stumbling. We may stumble, not because we reflect too deeply on Christ's sacrifice, but because we overlook His call to actively “put to death, therefore, whatever belongs to your earthly nature” (Colossians 3:5).
Some may feel so weighed down by their struggles that it’s as if their sin has become a constant shadow, following them everywhere and shaping how they see themselves. They resemble prisoners who have been set free but are still clinging to their chains, unsure how to leave the cell that has felt like home for so long.
Even the very recognition that we need to go to war against our socially acceptable sins, to put to death our selfish desires, and to "do all to stand" is truly a miracle of God. It’s like Ezekiel’s vision of the dry bones: only the breath of God can bring life and call us to rise from the grave of complacency (Ezekiel 37:5-6). Only by the Spirit’s power will we “throw off everything that hinders and the sin that so easily entangles” and “run with perseverance the race marked out for us” (Hebrews 12:1). Let us enter this battle for holiness, fixing our eyes on Jesus, the author and perfecter of our faith.
Do you see the need to fight against the devil and your own flesh? Please explain:

Day 13: Know Your Enemy
Satan's Tricks and Traps
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key Passage
Put on the whole armor of God, that you may be able to stand against the wiles of the Devil.
Ephesians 6:11 
Teaching
In this battle that you and I are in, it is crucial to know our enemy, and especially to understand the "wiles," or manipulative actions that Satan uses to deceive us, and what his purpose is in doing so (2 Corinthians 2:11; 1 Peter 5:8). The truth is that life is like walking through a dense forest filled with hidden snares—each step requires vigilance to avoid being caught in a trap (Proverbs 4:26-27; Psalm 119:105).
Satan uses many tricks to divert us from following Christ, to deceive us, his purpose being to destroy us (John 10:10; Revelation 12:9). He has most likely trapped you in the past as he has me, or is plotting a trap for you right now, just like a predator silently stalking the next victim (Job 1:7; Luke 22:31).
In Ephesians 6:11 the Greek word for "wiles" (or "devices", "designs", etc.) is an amazing word. See if you can spot your enemy's plans for you, in the unfolding of this word (2 Timothy 2:26; James 1:14-15).
First: It means traps that are set behind someone, sneaky attacks that catch you off guard. It highlights the tactics or ambushes of that old cunning Serpent, who, like a snake in the path, bites the heels of travelers, and spreads his poison to the head and the heart (Genesis 3:1; Genesis 3:15; Psalm 91:13). The word signifies an ambush or war strategy, where the enemy attacks a person by surprise (Ephesians 4:27; 1 Thessalonians 5:3).
Second: It means traps set to catch someone on their path; a person walks along, not thinking about it, and suddenly they are set upon by thieves or fall into a pit, etc. (Proverbs 7:21-23; Psalm 57:6). This reminds us to be watchful and alert because our adversary is seeking to devour (1 Peter 5:8; Matthew 24:42-44).
Third: It signifies an ambush, circumvention, or going around, as those who seek after their prey (Psalm 10:9; Micah 7:2). This is your enemy and my enemy (John 8:44; Revelation 12:17)!
Our enemy can transform himself into an angel of light, like a good pastor, theologian, or seminary professor. I could name some that you'd recognize, and you could name some too. Their error is always toward the gospel of Jesus Christ.
Julian the Apostate was a Roman emperor who ruled from 361 to 363 AD. He professed faith in Christ but through cunning and deception, he led more people away from Christ than all the cruelty of his persecuting predecessors ever could. In the same way, Satan causes greater harm when he disguises himself as a sheep or when he comes as an "angel of light" (2 Corinthians 11:14; Matthew 7:15; 2 Thessalonians 2:9-10), rather than directly as a wolf or wearing horns and carrying a pitchfork, saying, "Hi, I'm Satan, and have I got a pack of lies for you!"
From @JCRyle, posted on X:
Subtly or not, Satan aims to get you into a trap or snare. Notice 2 Timothy 2:26: "And that they may recover themselves out of the snare of the devil, who are taken captive by him at his will." 
The Greek word translated as "taken captive" means to be captured alive; it’s a war term, meaning to be taken alive like soldiers in battle or birds caught in a trap. Satan has traps for the wise and the foolish, for genuine believers and hypocrites, for brave people and cowards, for the rich and the poor, the old and young, and so on. 
The Greek word here translated as "recover themselves" means to wake up. The Apostle Paul refers to someone asleep or drunk, who needs to be awakened and brought back to their senses and escape their trap. 
Satan's purpose is to take us captive, making us a slave to sin and ultimately to destroy us or our testimony (John 10:10; 2 Timothy 2:26). This happens by us giving ear to his lies, which are designed to lead us right into a trap (Genesis 3:4-5; John 8:44).
Happy are those people who are released and rescued from Satan's powerful traps! They are like a bird that has finally been set free from a cage, soaring into the open sky with joy and relief (Psalm 124:7; Isaiah 61:1). This is precisely why Jesus came to this earth: to set captives free and to destroy the works of the devil (Luke 4:18-19; 1 John 3:8; Hebrews 2:14-15).
For he has rescued us from the dominion of darkness and brought us into the kingdom of the Son he loves, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins.
Colossians 1:13-14
What is Satan's main desire for you, and how does he accomplish it?

Day 13: Know Your Enemy
Biblical Illustration: Samson
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
For the full biblical account please read Judges 16:1-30.

    Situation: Delilah repeatedly pressed Samson to reveal the secret of his strength. He eventually gave in and revealed it was his obedience to God in not cutting his hair.
    Trap: Delilah betrayed Samson, leading to his capture by the Philistines who put out his eyes and made him their slave. He was now trapped and powerless to escape on his own. 
    Outcome: Though trapped and blinded, Samson called upon the Lord in his final moments and God enabled him to achieve victory over his enemies. In raising his right hand and putting it on the right pillar of the temple, and raising his left and putting it on the left (Judges 16:29), he became a physical picture of Jesus dying on a cross. Then Samson destroyed the enemy through his death (Judges 16:30), bringing victory for his people, and this pictured Jesus Christ's victory over the enemy for us at the cross (Colossians 2:15).

Application: Giving in to persistent temptation always leads to a trap, with Satan's goal for us being death, but God’s mercy is always available for all who call on Him (Romans 10:12).
A modern-day illustration:
Imagine navigating a bustling city to reach a vital destination—symbolizing your walk with Christ. Along the way, a master thief has studied you, setting traps tailored to your habits and weaknesses. Perhaps it’s a tempting shortcut, a distraction, or a false sense of safety, each designed to trap you.
Thankfully, your GPS—God’s Word—sends warnings: “Danger ahead! Reroute!” But the thief creates interference with distractions and doubts, hoping you’ll miss the guidance and fall into his trap. His goal isn’t just to delay you but to enslave you in sin and pull you away from Christ.
The good news? Jesus is able to rescue you from traps (Psalm 25:15)! Once rescued, God equips you with His armor: salvation, righteousness, His Word, His local church, faith, truth, and gospel sharing, all to protect and guide you.
If Delilah represents Satan, and Samson represent you, how does Satan trap you and how do you win?

Day 13: Know Your Enemy
Practical Application

If you are caught in a trap, cry out to Jesus—as if you were calling out for a lifeguard to dive into the stormy waters and pull you from certain drowning. "In my distress, I called to the LORD; I cried to my God for help. From his temple he heard my voice; my cry came before him, into his ears" (Psalm 18:6).
Jesus shed His blood and gave His life to release you and set you free. "In him we have redemption through his blood, the forgiveness of sins, in accordance with the riches of God’s grace" (Ephesians 1:7). "So if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed" (John 8:36).
Charles Spurgeon wrote:

    Let us think how dear we are to Christ, how much we cost him, how precious we are in his sight. Can he leave us? Can he be unkind to us? Let us reflect upon the way in which the Lord has hitherto always appeared for us in times of difficulty, and rescued us in days of jeopardy. - Quoted on X by "The Daily Spurgeon"

"What does it mean to you personally that Jesus hears and responds to your cries for help?"

Day 14 Our Identity in Christ
Child of God
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
Key passage
For He chose us in Him before the creation of the world to be holy and blameless in His sight. In love He predestined us for adoption to sonship through Jesus Christ, in accordance with His pleasure and will—to the praise of His glorious grace, which He has freely given us in the One He loves. In Him, we have redemption through His blood, the forgiveness of sins, in accordance with the riches of God's grace.
Ephesians 1:4-7
Teaching
God's eternal plan was to make us His children through Jesus Christ. It's like a builder drafting blueprints for a magnificent home before construction begins; before the foundation of the world, God chose us to be holy and blameless, adopting us into His family (Ephesians 1:4-5).
Yes, God chose us before we were born (Jeremiah 1:5). And just as children adopted from an orphanage are no longer under the authority of that orphanage, we who believe, as God’s adopted children, are no longer under the power of sin or Satan (see John 8:44); the heavy yoke that enslaved us has been lifted (Matthew 11:28-30). Now, as beloved children of God, we are redeemed through the blood of Jesus Christ (Ephesians 1:7), forgiven of all sin (Colossians 1:13-14) and made right with God through faith (Romans 5:1).
If we want to overcome Satan's temptations, we must know who we are as children of God (1 John 3:1). If you remember, the first temptation Satan presented Jesus was an attempt to get Him to doubt His identity. At Jesus' baptism, God the Father declared, "This is My Son whom I love; with Him I am well pleased" (Matthew 3:17), and in his first temptation to Jesus, Satan said, "If you are the Son of God, tell these stones to become bread" (Matthew 4:3-4).
Every believer faces similar temptations, causing us to question our relationship with God as His child (Galatians 4:6-7). It's like Satan puts a smudge on a mirror, and our own sin adds more smudges, and combined they distort the reflection of who we truly are. Satan knows that we live out of our identity; that is, we act according to who we see ourselves to be, and if he can distort or deny our identity, he's practically won the battle (2 Corinthians 10:4-5).
Believer: keep your identity clear: you are a child of God, purchased and adopted by Jesus Christ, like an orphan welcomed into a loving family and given a new name (Isaiah 62:2). "But as many as received Him, to them He gave the right to become children of God, to those who believe in His name" (John 1:12). "He predestined us for adoption to Himself as sons through Jesus Christ, according to the purpose of His will" (Ephesians 1:5). You are a child of God, a child of the King (1 Peter 2:9), and whatever Satan is offering to you is beneath you.
We need to remind ourselves often of not only what Jesus has done for us, but of who we are in Him (Colossians 3:1-3). One time I heard a father say to his child, "You are a Marshall (last name), and Marshalls don't do that!" He was trying to instill identity into his child, knowing that all people live according to their identity.
All throughout Jesus' life He referred to God as "My Father" (John 5:17, Matthew 11:27, Matthew 26:39). But at His death, while He had your sin and mine on Himself, while He was under the wrath of God, He cried out not "My Father, My Father" but rather "My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?" (Matthew 27:46, Psalm 22:1). Jesus was forsaken by His Father that you and I might be accepted by God (2 Corinthians 5:21), and that we might call God "Father" (Romans 8:15, Galatians 4:6).
Know who you are, know that you are God's child both by adoption and redemption (Titus 3:5-7). Then you will repel temptations when they come to you. Like a delivery truck driver that tries to make a delivery to the wrong address, when Satan brings you a temptation, you know that it doesn't belong to you, for you are God's child (James 4:7). Keep your identity very clear so that you will not keep on sinning (1 John 5:18).
According to Ephesians 1:4-7, why did God choose us?
1. Because of our good works
2. To be holy and blameless
3. To display His glorious grace
4. Both 2 and 3

Day 14 Our Identity in Christ
Biblical Illustration: Israel
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 6 months ago
In the Old Testament, we see the concept of adoption in God’s relationship with Israel. God spoke: “I said, ‘You are my people,’ and they said, ‘You are our God’” (Jeremiah 31:33). Despite their rebellion, God chose Israel as His own, delivering them from slavery in Egypt (Exodus 6:7).
In the same way, you and I were purchased out of the world, bought by the blood of Jesus (1 Peter 1:18-19), and adopted into the family of God (Ephesians 1:5). This understanding helps us live according to our royal blood, just as a prince would live knowing the throne belongs to him. We live according to the purchase price; the cross tells us that we are priceless treasures bought at a great cost (1 Corinthians 6:19-20). And we live according to our true identity, like a child who has finally discovered his true name and place in the world.
The very fact that God calls us His children is designed to prove His love for us: "See what great love the Father has lavished on us, that we should be called children of God! And that is what we are!" (1 John 3:1). Now we are to live in His love (John 15:9), rejoice in His love even as God rejoices in His love for you (Zephaniah 3:17), tell others of His love (2 Corinthians 5:20), and live in the identity that we are children of God (Romans 8:16-17).
How does understanding our adoption into God’s family influence the way we live our lives?

    Reject lies that you are unworthy or unloved or condemned (Romans 8:1).
    Embrace God’s forgiveness and grace daily (Psalm 103:12).
    Walk in freedom and joy, reflecting your Father’s love to others (Matthew 5:16).

Day 15: Our Identity in Christ
You Have a New Heart!
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit in you; I will remove from you your heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh. And I will put my Spirit in you and move you to follow my decrees and be careful to keep my laws.
Ezekiel 36:26-27 NIV
Earlier in this devotional we talked about the need to have a new heart. Today, we will pivot to the truth that all Christians have received a new heart! We initially had a heart that was "deceitful above all things and beyond cure" (Jeremiah 17:9), but through Jesus' death on the cross and resurrection from the dead, through the giving of the Holy Spirit and our faith in the gospel, we received a new heart.
Ezekiel, an Old Testament prophet, prophesied of this profound transformation that was coming in the future (Ezekiel 36:26). This promise, fulfilled through Christ and the gift of the Holy Spirit, means believers have had a spiritual heart transplant and now have a new identity in Christ.
Just as a caterpillar sheds its cocoon to become a butterfly, we emerge as new creations (2 Corinthians 5:17). We leave our old self, our old stony and dead hearts behind, and the Spirit enables us to live in obedience and love, reflecting our restored relationship with God where we are dead to sin and alive to God (Romans 8:10).
Though we still have flesh, so we can still be tempted and trip up with sin, our new hearts hate it when we fall and we love Jesus more than sin (Romans 7:19-20). This inner struggle, and our rising again when we fall, shows the reality of our new hearts longing to walk in righteousness (Proverbs 24:16).
This new heart, given by God, brings with it new desires and new affections (Psalm 40:8). And it is this new heart that leads us to say, as Paul did, "Thanks be to God, who delivers me through Jesus Christ our Lord!" (Romans 7:25).

When temptation hits, remind yourself that you have a new heart, one that does not love or respond to sin, one that loves Jesus and is happiest when it is not sinning. Trust God's promise was fulfilled for you: "I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit within you; I will remove your heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh" (Ezekiel 36:26).
You should view yourself as a new creation in Christ: "Therefore, if anyone is in Christ, he is a new creation; the old has passed away, behold, the new has come" (2 Corinthians 5:17). Because of this, you love different things than you used to, like someone who once craved junk food but now finds true satisfaction in a feast of wholesome food. You now delight to live for Jesus who died for you (2 Corinthians 5:15).
This is the new heart and the new identity that Jesus died to purchase for you (Hebrews 10:16). Don't belittle His death by claiming you still have an old stony dead heart but live with the truth that He bought a new heart for you by shedding His blood to cover your sin (1 Peter 1:18-19) and giving you the Holy Spirit to live within (John 14:17).
According to Ezekiel 36:26-27, what is the result of receiving a new heart and spirit?
Freedom to indulge our flesh
The ability to follow and obey GodCorrect
A perfect understanding of God’s plans
How does this passage challenge your understanding of what it means to be a follower of Christ?

On the Sabbath we went outside the city gate to the river, where we expected to find a place of prayer. Although she worshiped God, it wasn’t until Paul preached the gospel that her heart was truly opened. This divine act of grace transformed Lydia, leading her to receive Christ and immediately express her faith through baptism. Her new identity in Christ was evident as she showed hospitality and became a supporter of the gospel mission.

Having a new heart and new spirit means our lives should reflect our new identity in Christ. The Holy Spirit works within us to produce the fruit of the Spirit (Galatians 5:22-23) and to empower us to live free from habitual sin (Romans 6:11-14).

Day 16 Is That Christian Music?
Christians Have Clean Hearts
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
And they sang a new song, saying: “You are worthy to take the scroll and to open its seals, because you were slain, and with your blood you purchased for God persons from every tribe and language and people and nation."
Revelation 5:9
Christian songs are about Christ! They help us to freedom by reminding us of Jesus' love for us, how He purchased us with His own blood. In heaven we will be singing about the worth of Christ, the cross of Christ, the blood of Christ, as shown in Revelation 5:6-14. 
Today let's talk about music, and see the need to exercise discernment about the music we play and sing.
In the last lesson, we rejoiced that God replaces the hearts of all believers, removing the heart that is deceitful and dead and replacing it with a new and clean heart. “I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit in you; I will remove from you your heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh” (Ezekiel 36:26). He literally takes our hearts that are as lifeless and corrupted as a broken, rusted machine and replaces them with one that is new and clean. “If anyone is in Christ, the new creation has come: The old has gone, the new is here!” (2 Corinthians 5:17).
Unfortunately, some songwriters have not gotten the good news yet, and they write songs that make it seem like we are still under the Old Covenant, where we had old and foul hearts instead of new and clean hearts. It is as if they are still under the Law of Moses:“For the law was given through Moses; grace and truth came through Jesus Christ” (John 1:17). 
“This grace was given us in Christ Jesus before the beginning of time, but it has now been revealed through the appearing of our Savior, Christ Jesus, who has destroyed death and has brought life and immortality to light through the gospel” (2 Timothy 1:9-10). Some Christian songs seem to be stuck in the shadows, unable to proclaim the dawn of the gospel that has arrived in Christ. 
In order to live in freedom, the songs that we sing matter. We need to get good at discerning whether the music we hear on the radio, that claims to be Christian, or the music we hear at church, is really Christian music. “Dear friends, do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits to see whether they are from God, because many false prophets have gone out into the world” (1 John 4:1). 
Old Covenant music is like walking into a room still lit with dim, flickering candles when the bright light of day is already shining through the windows. “For God, who said, ‘Let light shine out of darkness,’ made his light shine in our hearts to give us the light of the knowledge of God’s glory displayed in the face of Christ” (2 Corinthians 4:6).

Music can have a profound effect on our hearts, minds, and actions (Colossians 3:16). To listen and sing songs that are rooted in the Old Covenant, not founded in the redemption of Jesus Christ, is to reinforce the darkness of shadows (Hebrews 10:1), as if Jesus had not come and changed everything through the gospel (Galatians 3:24-25). Day 17: Our Identity in Christ: Loved and Chosen
Loved and Chosen by God
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
Even before he made the world, God loved us and chose us in Christ to be holy and without fault in his eyes. God decided in advance to adopt us into his own family by bringing us to himself through Jesus Christ. This is what he wanted to do, and it gave him great pleasure.
Ephesians 1:4-5 NLT
Teaching
In Ephesians 1:4-5, Paul writes that God “chose us in Him before the creation of the world to be holy and blameless in His sight. In love, He predestined us for adoption to sonship through Jesus Christ.” Similarly, 1 Peter 2:9 calls believers “a chosen people, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, God’s special possession.”
We don't earn Christ or His grace by our overcoming sin, or any other "good works" we do, but rather He is given to us by God’s grace, because God loves us (Ephesians 2:8-9; Titus 3:5-7). This is called the doctrine of "election" (Ephesians 1:4-5). We elect government officials to serve; similarly, God elects us to serve Him, but His election of us is based on His love for us, not our merits (Deuteronomy 7:6-8; Romans 9:11-13).
After we receive Christ, we come to know that we are children of God by His choice, by His adoption, by His payment, and because of His love (Ephesians 1:4-5; Galatians 4:4-7). As John 1:12 declares, “Yet to all who did receive Him, to those who believed in His name, He gave the right to become children of God.”
He simply loved us and chose to make us His children, sending Jesus to this earth to pay for our adoption, bringing us into His royal family (Romans 8:15-17; 1 John 4:9-10). It’s as if we are handed a royal robe that we could never afford, that wraps us in righteousness, dignity, and belonging. “I delight greatly in the Lord; my soul rejoices in my God. For He has clothed me with garments of salvation and arrayed me in a robe of His righteousness” (Isaiah 61:10).
We are chosen, adopted, loved by God, and clothed in righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21; Colossians 3:12-14; 1 Peter 2:9-10).
Knowing who we are in Jesus liberates us from the need to find worth in worldly accomplishments, relationships, fantasy life, or anything else on this earth (Philippians 3:7-8; 1 John 2:15-17).
When we see ourselves as God sees us—His cherished children—we are freed from sin’s grip, which often enslaves us through lies about our identity (Galatians 4:7). Indeed, this freedom is why Jesus came to this earth for us, as He said in John 8:36, “So if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed.”
He freed us by His death on the cross (Romans 5:8-9; 1 Peter 2:24), by showing us our identity in Him (Ephesians 1:3-4; Colossians 3:1-4), by the exhortations of Scripture (2 Timothy 3:16-17; Psalm 119:105), and by the work of the Holy Spirit in us (John 14:26; Romans 8:14-16).
The other day, I saw on social media a bird that clearly felt trapped by—get this—a mirror! The way it was acting, it must have believed its own reflection was an enemy to fight. Talk about not knowing your own identity! We are like that bird when we are mastered by sin, not knowing who we are in Christ, confused, and upset (James 1:23-24; 2 Peter 1:9).
A believer's correct identity is as a child of God, loved and chosen by Him, clothed in righteousness and royalty. Galatians 3:26-27 reminds us, “So in Christ Jesus you are all children of God through faith, for all of you who were baptized into Christ have clothed yourselves with Christ.” We see the purchase that Jesus made, and it fills our hearts with love for God, who would go so far and pay so much for people so undeserving. And to know this liberates us from any other wrong identities.
As 2 Corinthians 5:17 declares, “Therefore, if anyone is in Christ, the new creation has come: The old has gone, the new is here!”
Do you know that you are loved and chosen by God? What thoughts do you have about this truth?

Day 17: Our Identity in Christ: Loved and Chosen
Biblical Illustration: The Prodigal Son
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
The story of the prodigal son in Luke 15:11-32 illustrates this truth. The younger son squandered his inheritance, believing his life came from possessions and pleasure. But when he returned to his father, broken and repentant, he discovered his true identity as a beloved son. The father didn’t shame him but embraced him and celebrated his return.
Tim Keller taught:

    God’s love and forgiveness can pardon and restore any and every kind of sin or wrongdoing. Tim Keller, The Prodigal God, Chapter 2

This story reminds us that even when we fail, our identity in Christ remains secure (Romans 8:38-39; 2 Timothy 2:13). God’s love is not contingent on our performance but on His unchanging character (Malachi 3:6; James 1:17).
When we grasp this, we are motivated to turn from sin, not out of fear but out of love for our heavenly Father (1 John 4:18-19), and gratitude that Jesus died to forgive us and remove our sin far from us (Isaiah 53:5-6; Colossians 1:13-14). "He has removed our sins as far from us as the east is from the west" (Psalm 103:12).
How does the parable of the prodigal son reveal the security of our identity in God's unchanging love and forgiveness?

Day 17: Our Identity in Christ: Loved and Chosen
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
David Steele, in the book, "Be Killing Sin" quotes Sinclair Ferguson. Note the power of what Ferguson is saying here:

To live out your identity in Christ, begin each day reminding yourself of what Jesus did for you, and who you are in Him. Memorize verses like Ephesians 1:4-5 or 1 Peter 2:9, and pray, thanking God for His love and adoption, for His grace and redemption. 
When tempted by sin, pause and ask, “Does this align with my identity as God’s child, purchased by the blood of Jesus?” Often, the reminder of who you are will give you strength to resist.
Additionally, share this truth with others. Encourage fellow believers who may feel trapped by shame or guilt, reminding them how Jesus took their shame and guilt, so they have none. "Surely he has borne our griefs and carried our sorrows... upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed" (Isaiah 53:4-5). "There is therefore now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus" (Romans 8:1).
Remind them of their true identity in Christ: "Therefore, if  is in Christ, he is a new creation. The old has passed away; behold, the new has come" (2 Corinthians 5:17). "But you are a chosen race, anyone royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people for his own possession, that you may proclaim the excellencies of him who called you out of darkness into his marvelous light" (1 Peter 2:9).
This mutual encouragement strengthens the entire body of Christ "Rather, speaking the truth in love, we are to grow up in every way into him who is the head, into Christ, from whom the whole body, joined and held together by every joint with which it is equipped, when each part is working properly, makes the body grow so that it builds itself up in love" (1 Peter 4:15-16).

    The Gospel changes your identity:  From lost to found. From orphaned to child of God. From wrecked to redeemed. From empty to filled w/ the Spirit. From captive to free. From broken to new creation. From bankrupt to co-heir. From aimless to ambassador. From dead to alive. - Shane Pruitt quoted on X.

What steps will you take this week to remind yourself of your identity in Christ?

Day 18: Our Identity in Christ: Forgiven and Redeemed
Day 18: Redeemed and Forgiven
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
In Him we have redemption through His blood, the forgiveness of sins, according to the riches of His grace.
Ephesians 1:7
He has delivered us from the power of darkness and conveyed us into the kingdom of the Son of His love, in whom we have redemption through His blood, the forgiveness of sins.
Colossians 1:13-14
These passages highlight two foundational truths of the gospel: redemption and forgiveness. The understanding of these two concepts has been used by God to release untold numbers of people from the shackles of sin.
Redemption means being set free by the payment of a ransom—like a prisoner whose shackles are unlocked by a costly payment—and here that ransom is the blood of Christ, shed on the cross for our sins (Matthew 20:28; 1 Peter 1:18-19; Isaiah 53:5-6). This theme of ransom and redemption is woven throughout Scripture, beginning with God’s deliverance of Israel from Egypt as a foreshadowing of the ultimate redemption through Christ (Exodus 6:6; Psalm 111:9).
Forgiveness is God’s gracious act of removing the penalty of sin, much like a debt being erased from the ledger, leaving the account clean (Psalm 103:12; Micah 7:18-19; Acts 10:43). The prophets foretold this forgiveness, and Jesus fulfilled it through His death and resurrection, establishing the new covenant in His blood (Jeremiah 31:34; Luke 22:20; Hebrews 9:22).
Together, these verses remind us that salvation is not earned but is a gift of God’s abundant grace (Romans 3:24-25; Titus 3:5-7; 2 Timothy 1:9), and the love of God is at the heart of this grace. But it's more than salvation, it's also freedom from sin's bondage! 
It was out of love that God came in the form of a human to rescue humans from the raging flood of sin (John 3:16; Philippians 2:6-8), from God's curse and condemnation (Galatians 3:13; Romans 8:1), from our own sense of unworthiness (Isaiah 61:10; Ephesians 2:4-5), and to bring us into the eternal kingdom of His Son (2 Peter 1:11; Revelation 1:5-6; John 14:2-3).
John Calvin wrote:

    His righteousness covers your sins—His salvation extinguishes your condemnation; He interposes His worthiness and so prevents your unworthiness from coming before God. -
    John Calvin quoted by Holcomb, Justin S. God with Us: 365 Devotions on the Person and Work of Christ (p. 36). Baker Publishing Group. Kindle Edition. 

This truth echoes the Old Testament promise in Isaiah 53:5: “But He was wounded for our transgressions, He was bruised for our iniquities; the chastisement for our peace was upon Him, and by His stripes we are healed.” It was always God’s plan to provide redemption through the sacrifice of His Son. In the New Testament, Romans 3:24-25 affirms this: "Being justified freely by His grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus, whom God set forth as a propitiation by His blood, through faith."
When it comes to desiring to live free from habitual sin, it is extremely important to think of ourselves as those who have been redeemed and forgiven. We simply take God at His Word when He says, "I have blotted out your transgressions like a cloud and your sins like mist; return to me, for I have redeemed you” (Isaiah 44:22). This verse tells us of God's work and our response: He made our sins vanish like a cloud or morning mist, and our response is to come to Him, to return to Him based on our purchased redemption.
We are not trying to work our way out of sin, nor step our way to forgiveness; rather, we have been rescued by Jesus and forgiven by God. “He has delivered us from the domain of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of his beloved Son, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins” (Colossians 1:13-14). This needs to be our identity: delivered, transferred, redeemed and forgiven!
Imagine you are in a prison cell with the door unlocked, yet you remain sitting inside, unsure if you're truly free. Then someone comes to you with undeniable proof: a legal document signed by the highest authority declaring your pardon and release. That's exactly what happened! Jesus, the highest Authority, has declared this truth: “The Spirit of the Lord God is upon me, because the Lord has anointed me... to proclaim liberty to the captives and the opening of the prison to those who are bound” (Isaiah 61:1).
Only when you embrace this truth and see yourself as a free person will you confidently step out of the cell and live in freedom.
When battling habitual sin, as we all do, it’s vital to see yourself as one who has already been redeemed and forgiven through Christ. Paul reminds us, “For freedom Christ has set us free; stand firm therefore, and do not submit again to a yoke of slavery” (Galatians 5:1).
You’re not striving to earn freedom or forgiveness; you’ve already been set free. Living in that identity empowers you to walk out of the "cell" of sin and live in the liberty Christ has provided. Jesus Himself assures us, “So, if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed” (John 8:36).
How does understanding your identity as someone already redeemed and forgiven by Christ change the way you view and respond to habitual sin?

Day 18: Our Identity in Christ: Forgiven and Redeemed
Biblical Illustration: The Nation of Israel
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
The story of Israel’s deliverance from Egypt (Exodus 12) beautifully illustrates redemption. God commanded the Israelites to sacrifice a lamb and spread its blood on their doorposts, protecting them from the angel of death. This act of deliverance foreshadows Christ, the Passover Lamb of God (1 Corinthians 5:7), whose blood redeems believers from the bondage of sin and death.
The New Testament reinforces this in John 1:29, where John the Baptist declares: “Behold! The Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world!” Just as God brought the Israelites out of Egypt to lead them into the Promised Land, Jesus has rescued us from the dominion of darkness, bringing us into His kingdom of light, as described in 1 Peter 1:18-19: “You were not redeemed with corruptible things, like silver or gold, but with the precious blood of Christ, as of a lamb without blemish and without spot.”
What parallels do you see between the blood of the Passover lamb and the blood of Christ?

Day 18: Our Identity in Christ: Forgiven and Redeemed
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Redemption and forgiveness call for a response of gratitude and obedience. Live as one who has been set free from the power of sin!

    Confess sins, resting in the assurance of God’s forgiveness: "If we confess our sins, He is faithful and just to forgive us our sins and to cleanse us from all unrighteousness" (1 John 1:9).

    Remember the high cost of redemption by cherishing and boasting in Christ’s sacrifice: "As for me, may I never boast about anything except the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ. Because of that cross, my interest in this world has been crucified, and the world’s interest in me has also died" (Galatians 6:14).
     
    Share this good news with others, helping them understand the freedom and forgiveness found in Jesus: “Go therefore and make disciples of all nations" (Matthew 28:19-20). 

Think of a "darkness" in your life from which Christ has delivered you. How does that affect the way you approach God today?
grateful
Closing Thought: Through Jesus, we have been redeemed and forgiven—not by anything we’ve done, but by His love and grace alone. We are the prisoner set free, not because we picked the lock, but because the King Himself opened the door and welcomed us into His palace. We need to live by this identity, as if wearing a new name written on our hearts, and speak it out audibly to ourselves and others. As Psalm 107:2 says: “Let the redeemed of the Lord say so, whom He has redeemed from the hand of the enemy.”

Day 19: Our Identity in Christ: Your Old Self is Dead
Crucified With Christ
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
I have been crucified with Christ. It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. And the life I now live in the flesh I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me.
Galatians 2:20 (ESV)
Teaching
Paul’s declaration in Galatians 2:20 reveals a profound truth: believers are united with Christ in His death and resurrection (Romans 6:5-6). The implications of this truth are life changing, as we realize that not only did Jesus die for us (1 Peter 2:24), but that we died in Him (Colossians 3:3; 2 Timothy 2:11).

    To be crucified with Christ means what? It means the old man, the old self, the old nature, has been executed. It’s gone. It's no longer in charge. The new self is in union with Christ, sharing His life and righteousness. —John MacArthur, The Gospel According to Paul: Embracing the Good News at the Heart of Paul’s Teachings.

If you will see your old self, with its sinful inclinations, being crucified with Christ (Galatians 2:20), you will see that Jesus' sacrificial act of dying is what frees you from the bondage of sin (John 8:36). Whereas you used to be dead to God and alive to sin (Ephesians 2:1), now you are alive to God and dead to sin (Colossians 2:13). And dead people are free from sin (Romans 6:7).

    When Paul says, 'I am crucified with Christ,' it is as if he is saying, 'The nails that pierced Christ’s hands and feet have also pierced my own sinful nature.' It is not a theoretical crucifixion, but a real one, affecting every part of who I am." —Charles Spurgeon, Sermon No. 2519, "The Believer Crucified with Christ" (1897).

If you will see yourself being nailed to the cross with Jesus (Romans 6:6), believing the truth of God's Word (John 17:17), you will live differently. That's Paul's point in Galatians 2:20. He said he was "crucified with Christ" and that crucifixion affected the way he lives (2 Corinthians 4:10-11). He now lived by faith, relying on Christ's power instead of his own efforts (Philippians 4:13; 2 Corinthians 12:9).

    "The Christian life is not just a matter of improvement but a matter of crucifixion. Christ does not merely tidy up our old self; He puts it to death and brings forth something entirely new."
    —C.S. Lewis, Mere Christianity.

The story of Abraham paying tithe to Melchizedek shows the greatness of Jesus’ High Priesthood. Scripture explains that Levi, though not yet born, paid tithe to Melchizedek through Abraham because Levi was “in” Abraham’s body (Hebrews 7:9-10). This simple illustration teaches a profound truth: God accepts a required payment through the body of another.
Why is this good news? Like Levi, we were "in" Jesus when He lived in perfect obedience (Romans 5:19), when He died to pay for our sins (1 John 2:2), and when He rose from the dead (Romans 6:4-5). God accepts Jesus’ perfect life, death, and resurrection on our behalf.
Because we are “in Christ,” God sees us as having lived perfectly (Philippians 3:9), paid for our sins entirely (Colossians 2:13-14), and risen to new life eternally, never to die again (Romans 6:9-10). Just as Levi paid tithe through Abraham, we paid for our sins through Christ.
When you see yourself “in Christ,” your whole identity changes. Your thoughts, understanding, and life will be transformed! 
So never again say, "I still have my old man," or "my new me is fighting with my old me." Oh no, it isn't! If you are a believer, your old self is gone like a shadow that vanishes in the noonday sun. Say it to yourself often: "I have been crucified with Christ," meaning my old self is dead! Just as a caterpillar is no longer a caterpillar once it becomes a butterfly, your old nature has been completely destroyed and you are now a new creation. Holding onto the idea of your "old man" is like looking in a mirror and trying to see a reflection that’s no longer there—it’s gone, and it doesn’t define you, there's a new you created in true righteousness and holiness (Ephesians 4:24).
How does knowing Christ died for you and lives in you change the way you approach your daily life?

Day 19: Our Identity in Christ: Your Old Self is Dead
Free From Slavery!
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
In Romans 6, Paul uses the illustration of sin as a reigning King and a ruling Master, both of which have ultimate authority over their subjects (John 8:34). But that is true only as long as their subjects are alive (Romans 7:1).
We escape the reign of sin through the redemptive work of Christ on the cross, and our being crucified with Him. "In the same way, count yourselves dead to sin but alive to God in Christ Jesus. Therefore, do not let sin reign in your mortal body so that you obey its evil desires" (Romans 6:11-12).
Someone who has escaped the rule of an evil king no longer needs to offer himself to serve that king (Colossians 1:13-14); he's no longer under the authority of that king (Romans 6:14). Even so, we are told "Do not offer any part of yourself to sin as an instrument of wickedness but rather offer yourselves to God as those who have been brought from death to life; and offer every part of yourself to him as an instrument of righteousness" (Romans 6:13).
If a servant dies, he is no longer under the authority of his previous master. You have died in Christ so, "sin shall no longer be your master, because you are not under the law, but under grace" (Romans 6:14). 
So, through the cross of Christ, we have escaped sin's reign and sin's mastery over us!
What happens to the "old self" when we are united with Christ? 
It slowly gets better
It is crucified and rendered powerlessCorrect
It is unchanged.

Day 19: Our Identity in Christ: Your Old Self is Dead
Practical Application

Likewise, my brothers, you also have died to the law through the body of Christ, so that you may belong to another, to him who has been raised from the dead, in order that we may bear fruit for God.
Romans 7:4
Living by faith in the truth that we have died with Christ (Romans 6:8) results in dying to self (Luke 9:23), much like a seed that has fallen to the ground must die before it can sprout (John 12:24). This process involves relying on the Spirit to bear fruit (Galatians 5:22-23), as a tree depends on sunlight and water to grow (Psalm 1:3).
For example, when you are tempted to react in anger to an undesirable circumstance, it’s like standing at a crossroads—pause and pray for the Spirit's help and remember you are dead to that old way of responding and you have new life with Christ (Ephesians 4:26-27). When temptation knocks, see yourself nailed to a cross and buried in a tomb, because you died with Christ.
To close this day out, let me say this to you in love: you're dead, now live like it!

Day 20: Our Identity in Christ: You Are Risen From the Dead
Raised From The Dead
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
But God is so rich in mercy, and he loved us so much, that even though we were dead because of our sins, he gave us life when he raised Christ from the dead. (It is only by God’s grace that you have been saved!)  For he raised us from the dead along with Christ and seated us with him in the heavenly realms because we are united with Christ Jesus.
Ephesians 2:4-6
The heart of the gospel is that God, in His mercy and love, brought us from death to life through Jesus Christ (Ephesians 2:4-5). It’s like dry bones coming to life, as Ezekiel saw in his vision, when God breathed His Spirit into what was lifeless and made it live again (Ezekiel 37:1-10). Just as God breathed life into Adam at creation (Genesis 2:7), He breathes new life into us through His Spirit (John 3:5-6; Romans 8:11). Jesus said, “I am the resurrection and the life. The one who believes in me will live, even though they die” (John 11:25), showing that in Him, what was once dead is made alive again (Colossians 2:13).
John Piper emphasizes the miraculous nature of this transformation: 

    We were dead. We were not in need of a mere moral improvement or a spiritual boost. Dead people do not make decisions to follow God. Dead people need resurrection. That is what God did for us when he raised Christ from the dead and joined us to him.  - Sermon, God Made Us Alive Together with Christ

Before salvation, we were spiritually dead, unable to respond to God or live in His ways (Ephesians 2:1; Colossians 2:13). This spiritual death, which is like a body being disconnected from the Head (Colossians 1:18), showed up in many ways: bondage to food or drugs, alcohol or prescription meds, porn, lust, greed, dishonesty (all these kept me in bondage), and all the works of the flesh (Galatians 5:19-21). These had power over us, as if they were our master and we their willing slaves (Romans 6:16-18).
Spurgeon vividly describes this state: 

    Man is so spiritually dead that he neither wills to come to God, nor does he come to Him. He chooses sin, and he will not renounce it. He does not want God, nor is he willing to yield to Him. Thus, it takes a mighty power, even that same power which raised Christ from the dead, to bring a dead soul to life in Christ.”  - The Bondage of the Will, Martin Luther)

But through Jesus’ sacrificial death and resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:3-4), and through the work of the Holy Spirit (Titus 3:5; Romans 8:11), God made us alive with Him (Ephesians 2:4-5). This is not because of our works but entirely by His grace (Ephesians 2:8-9). 
Being "raised up" and "seated with Christ" signifies a new identity: we are connected to Jesus and united to Him forever (John 15:5; Ephesians 2:6). We are no longer dead slaves, under the power of sin (Romans 6:6-7), but we are children of God (Galatians 4:6-7), raised from the dead, secure in His eternal kingdom (Philippians 3:20-21). 
We were transferred from the domain of darkness into the kingdom of His beloved Son (Colossians 1:13), where we are now heirs with Christ (Romans 8:17) and part of His family forever (John 1:12). Like Mephibosheth, we who were once "dead dogs" because of our sin (2 Samuel 9:8), are now invited to sit at the King's table, where the tablecloth of grace covers our broken condition (Ephesians 2:8-9). Just as David showed kindness to Mephibosheth for Jonathan’s sake (2 Samuel 9:7), so God, in His mercy, has shown us grace through Jesus Christ (Titus 3:4-7).
John MacArthur explains the transformative power of this transfer: 

    “When you were saved, you were moved from the kingdom of darkness and placed into the kingdom of light. You are no longer under the tyranny of Satan but are under the rule of Christ, the King of kings, and Lord of lords.”  - Commentary on Colossians 1:13

What does it mean to be spiritually dead?
Separated from God
Alive to self and sin, dead to God
No ability to obey God's Word
All the aboveCorrect
How does knowing that God raised you with Christ impact how you see yourself today?

Day 20: Our Identity in Christ: You Are Risen From the Dead
Biblical Illustration: Lazarus
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
In John 11, we see a vivid example of ourselves when Jesus raises Lazarus from the dead. Lazarus was in the tomb for four days, utterly unable to respond to anything, much like all humans born dead in sins (Ephesians 2:1). Martha said of her brother, Lazarus in John 11:39 “by this time there is a bad odor, for he has been there four days" or in the KJV, "Lord, he stinketh!" That was us before Christ!
Yet, at the command of Jesus, “Lazarus, come out” (John 11:43), Lazarus rose to life. This miracle reflects what happens spiritually when we are saved (Ephesians 2:4-5). Just as Lazarus was helpless and required Jesus to call him out of death (John 11:44), so we are brought to life by the call of Christ through the gospel (2 Timothy 1:9; 1 Peter 1:3). Jesus’ own resurrection is the ultimate proof that He has the power to give life to the dead—both physically (Matthew 28:6; 1 Corinthians 15:20) and spiritually (Romans 8:11; John 5:21).
Why did Jesus allowed Lazarus to die?
To glorify Jesus by showing His power over deathCorrect
Because He was delayed
Because He was too busy to come when Lazarus was sick

Day 20: Our Identity in Christ: You Are Risen From the Dead
Practical Application

Because we have been made alive with Christ (Colossians 2:13), we must live as those who are alive (Romans 6:11). This means rejecting the sinful habits and patterns of our old dead life (Ephesians 4:22) and embracing our new identity in Him (2 Corinthians 5:17).
Spend time each day reminding yourself of the gospel: Jesus died for your sins (1 Corinthians 15:3), rose to give you life and justify you (Romans 4:25), and has seated you in heavenly places (Ephesians 2:6). Let this truth shape your actions, thoughts, and relationships (Philippians 1:27; Colossians 3:1-2).
When (not if) temptation comes, remember that you are no longer dead in sin but alive in Christ (Romans 6:13). The same power that raised Jesus from the dead lives in you (Romans 8:11), enabling you to walk in newness of life (Romans 6:4).
 Why is it important that we live in our new identity, that of being raised from the dead with Christ?
