Day 21: Our Identity in Christ: Seated in Heaven
Seated In Heaven
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
But because of His great love for us, God, who is rich in mercy, made us alive with Christ even when we were dead in transgressions—it is by grace you have been saved. And God raised us up with Christ and seated us with Him in the heavenly realms in Christ Jesus.
Ephesians 2:4-6
Since, then, you have been raised with Christ, set your hearts on things above, where Christ is, seated at the right hand of God. Set your minds on things above, not on earthly things. For you died, and your life is now hidden with Christ in God.
Colossians 3:1-3
Teaching
How would your life be different if you knew you were up and out of the reach of the devil, so that he had no authority over you whatsoever? Well through the gospel, we are not just forgiven; we are raised with Christ and seated in heaven. This means at least three important things:

    We share in Christ's victory. 
    We share in Christ's inheritance. 
    We share in Christ's intimacy with the Father (Romans 8:17). 

Let's look at some Scriptures that teach each of these:
First: We share in Christ's victory:

    1 Corinthians 15:57 – “But thanks be to God! He gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ.”
    Romans 8:37 – “No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through Him who loved us.”
    Colossians 2:15 – “And having disarmed the powers and authorities, He made a public spectacle of them, triumphing over them by the cross.”
    Revelation 12:11 – “They triumphed over him by the blood of the Lamb and by the word of their testimony; they did not love their lives so much as to shrink from death.”
    John 16:33 – “In this world, you will have trouble. But take heart! I have overcome the world.”

Second: We share in Christ's inheritance:

    Romans 8:17 – “Now if we are children, then we are heirs—heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ, if indeed we share in His sufferings in order that we may also share in His glory.”
    Ephesians 1:11 – “In Him we have obtained an inheritance, having been predestined according to the purpose of Him who works all things according to the counsel of His will.”
    Colossians 1:12 – “...giving joyful thanks to the Father, who has qualified you to share in the inheritance of His holy people in the kingdom of light.”
    1 Peter 1:3-4 – “Praise be to the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ! In His great mercy He has given us new birth into a living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead, and into an inheritance that can never perish, spoil or fade. This inheritance is kept in heaven for you.”
    Galatians 3:29 – “If you belong to Christ, then you are Abraham’s seed, and heirs according to the promise.”

Third: We share in Christ's intimacy with the Father.

    John 17:21-23 – “...that all of them may be one, Father, just as You are in Me and I am in You. May they also be in Us so that the world may believe that You have sent Me. I have given them the glory that You gave Me, that they may be one as We are one—I in them and You in Me—so that they may be brought to complete unity. Then the world will know that You sent Me and have loved them even as You have loved Me.”
    Romans 8:15-16 – “The Spirit you received does not make you slaves, so that you live in fear again; rather, the Spirit you received brought about your adoption to sonship. And by Him we cry, ‘Abba, Father.’ The Spirit Himself testifies with our spirit that we are God’s children.”
    John 14:23 – “Jesus replied, ‘Anyone who loves Me will obey My teaching. My Father will love them, and We will come to them and make Our home with them.’”
    Galatians 4:6-7 – “Because you are His sons, God sent the Spirit of His Son into our hearts, the Spirit who calls out, ‘Abba, Father.’ So, you are no longer a slave, but God’s child; and since you are His child, God has made you also an heir.”

All believers are spiritually seated in heaven with Christ (Ephesians 2:6). It's like sitting in the co-pilot's seat of an aircraft, above the turbulence and storms below, knowing that the journey's destination is already secured (Philippians 1:6) by the Captain of our salvation (Hebrews 2:10). Think of how that impacts our daily walk on earth. We know that our victory has been secured through Christ (1 Corinthians 15:57), our position is firm as an anchor in a storm (Hebrews 6:19), and we are out of the enemy's reach—he has no authority over us (Colossians 1:13).
Our heavenly identity transcends earthly circumstances (Colossians 3:1-2). Being "seated in heaven" is like stepping onto a high mountain peak, leaving the chaos of the valley far below, and finding both rest from striving (Hebrews 4:10) and a vantage point that reveals the bigger picture (2 Corinthians 4:18). Jesus is the Ruler on a throne, exercising authority over sin and death (Romans 6:14; Revelation 1:18), and we are in Him (John 15:4-5). It also means our perspective shifts—we focus on eternal realities (2 Corinthians 5:7; Philippians 3:20).
What does it mean to you personally to be "seated in heaven with Christ"?
A. Sharing Christ’s authority
B. Being secure in salvation
C. Having a position over the enemy
D. All the aboveCorrect

Day 21: Our Identity in Christ: Seated in Heaven
Biblical Illustration: Jospeh
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider the story of Joseph found in Genesis 41. After enduring slavery and imprisonment, Joseph was exalted to Pharaoh’s right hand. His new position gave him authority, provision for others, and a new identity. Similarly, Christ was exalted after His suffering (Philippians 2:8-9), and through His gospel, we share in His glory (2 Timothy 2:12). Like Joseph sitting on the right hand of Pharaoh, in the place of authority and power, all believers are seated with Christ at the right hand of God.
As Joseph’s exaltation gave him a vantage point to save his family, our being seated with Christ equips us to intercede, share the gospel, and live differently. This exalted position is not earned; it is a gift of grace (Ephesians 2:8).
In what ways does Joseph’s story reflect the believer’s identity in Christ?
How does Christ’s exaltation encourage you in times of suffering or temptation?

Day 21: Our Identity in Christ: Seated in Heaven
Practical Application
Being seated in heaven with Christ changes at least the following things:

    Perspective: Fix your mind on heavenly realities. Instead of focusing on earthly worries, remember that your citizenship is in heaven (Philippians 3:20). You belong there. This world is not your home. You are a temporary resident, just passing through. Keep that perspective.
    Behavior: As one seated with Christ, live as an ambassador for Him (2 Corinthians 5:20). This means showing grace, extending forgiveness, and seeking first His kingdom (Matthew 6:33).
    Hope: Rest in Christ’s completed work. When the world feels chaotic, remember you are secure in Christ, “hidden with Him” (Colossians 3:3). Believers are not those who must take drugs to manage their lives, nor alcohol to drown out the voice of guilt (which the cross did for us anyway), or who must "eat our emotions." We have hope, and hope has a Name, His Name is Jesus!

How can you set your mind on "things above" when earthly challenges arise? 
What practical steps can you take this week to live as an ambassador for Christ?

Day 22: The Danger of Presumption
The Danger of Clinging to the Cross and Sin
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
If, however, a man acts presumptuously toward his neighbor, so as to kill him by deceit, you shall take him even from My altar, that he may die.
Exodus 21:14
There is a danger for those who have been taught, rightly, to cling to the cross of Christ for both justification and daily growth: it can become a license for sin (Romans 6:1-2). This is when someone has intellectually grasped, and even rejoices in the accomplishments of Jesus in His life, death, and resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:3-4), but does not apply the gospel to their sin struggles (Galatians 5:16-17). This is destructive to the believer (Proverbs 14:12) and damaging to his or her testimony (Matthew 5:16).
This view of clinging to the cross while not fighting sin could be compared to holding tightly to a lifeboat while drilling holes in its bottom, forgetting that the refuge of the cross is also the death of sin (Romans 6:6-7). The mindset sounds like this: “We all stumble, but Jesus paid for it all, and I’m just resting in Him” (1 John 1:8-9; Jude 4), or "yeah, I have flesh, but so what, I don't give it any thought."
Charles Spurgeon warns against this mindset stating:

    To trifle with sin because grace abounds is the very essence of iniquity. Christ died for our sins not so that we might continue in them, but so that we might be delivered from them.  - Sermon: "Shall We Continue in Sin?".

While it is good theology to cling to the cross at all times, we must be careful that we do not twist our freedom in Christ into an opportunity for the flesh. Scripture warns us: “For you were called to freedom, brothers; only do not turn your freedom into an opportunity for the flesh, but through love serve one another” (Galatians 5:13).
It is very important to balance God’s mercy with His holiness (Psalm 85:10; Isaiah 6:3). The Old Testament sacrificial altar, which pointed to the cross (Hebrews 10:1-4), was a refuge, but it did not shield those who kept on sinning presumptuously (Numbers 15:30-31). “If, however, a man acts presumptuously toward his neighbor, so as to kill him by deceit, you shall take him even from My altar, that he may die” (Exodus 21:14).
A protective umbrella doesn’t shelter those who step out from under it (Deuteronomy 29:19-20; Hebrews 10:26-27).
John MacArthur warns:

    “Presumptuous sin is willful rebellion against God. Even under the Old Covenant, forgiveness required genuine repentance and not just an outward appeal to the altar.”  - The MacArthur Study Bible

This speaks to those who cling to the cross while taking sin lightly, resting in the accomplishment of the cross but neglecting to fight against their lusts and deceptions (Romans 8:13; Galatians 5:24). Examples in Scripture include King Saul (1 Samuel 15:22-23), Ananias and Sapphira (Acts 5:1-11), Demas (2 Timothy 4:10), and many in the Corinthian church (1 Corinthians 5:1-2; 1 Corinthians 11:29-30). Their words professed faith that Jesus is Lord (Matthew 7:21), but their lives indulged the flesh, leading to a miserable end (James 1:14-15).
The cross itself reveals the balance of God’s mercy and judgment (Romans 3:25-26). As Jesus hangs there, we see that God demands punishment for sin (Isaiah 53:5-6) yet loves us so deeply that He bore it Himself (John 3:16; 2 Corinthians 5:21). The cross is like a blazing fire: it offers warmth (Malachi 4:2), but it also consumes impurities (Hebrews 12:29). Its dual nature requires both reverence and respect (Hebrews 12:28), worship and fighting against sin (1 Peter 2:24; Colossians 3:5).
John Piper notes:

    The cross is not just the payment for our sin; it is the power to destroy sin in us. It is the evidence of God’s holiness and His commitment to purify His people. - Fifty Reasons Why Jesus Came to Die

At the cross, mercy and forgiveness were purchased at the highest cost (1 Peter 1:18-19; Isaiah 53:4-5). But repentance and faith are required (Mark 1:15; Acts 2:38; Luke 24:46-47).
Christians are like soldiers taking shelter in a fortress (Psalm 18:2; Proverbs 18:10) while still arming themselves against the enemy outside (Ephesians 6:11-13; 2 Corinthians 10:4) and preparing to fight (1 Timothy 6:12; 2 Timothy 2:3-4).
Or another way to look at it, we are those who are resting on the bosom of Jesus (John 13:23) which enables us to fight against the sin that crucified our Savior! Don't get stuck on the first part, continue on to the second! "For if you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the misdeeds of the body, you will live" (Romans 8:13).
See Genesis 4:7, Proverbs 4:23-27, Isaiah 55:7, Romans 6:12-13, 1 Corinthians 10:13, Galatians 5:16-17, Hebrews 12:1-2, 
What dangers do you see in clinging to the cross without fighting sin?

Day 22: The Danger of Presumption
Biblical Illustration: Joab
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
1 Kings 2:28-34 tells the story of Joab, a commander who sought refuge by clinging to the altar after committing treachery and murder. Like a man grasping at straws to save himself from drowning, Joab clung to the sacred altar, hoping it would shield him from the consequences of his sin. However, Solomon, following David's instruction, orders Joab's execution despite his desperate attempt to hold fast to the altar.
Joab's actions were premeditated and unrepentant, aligning with the principle of Exodus 21:14. His story illustrates that the altar, a sacred place, is not a hiding place for deliberate sin (1 Kings 2:28-34). God's justice could not be manipulated or evaded (Nahum 1:3; Galatians 6:7).
This account also underscores the need for discernment in distinguishing true repentance from self-preservation (2 Corinthians 7:10; Matthew 3:8). Just as a mask can conceal someone's face but not change their expression, so too can "hiding behind the cross" cloak a sinful heart without transforming it (Isaiah 29:13; Matthew 23:27-28). God's justice shines like a light in the darkness, exposing what is hidden (Luke 8:17; Hebrews 4:13) and refusing to let sin remain beneath a mask of religious talk (James 1:22; Titus 1:16).
In what ways might people today attempt to "cling to the altar" without addressing their sin?


Day 22: The Danger of Presumption
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
It is impossible for any believer to get free from sin in this world, and sin in their flesh, without fighting it and abstaining from it (Romans 7:18-19; 1 Peter 2:11). We will remain in bondage until we humble ourselves (James 4:6-7), seek the Lord to give us a plan to put to death the deeds of our flesh (Romans 8:13), and then fight with all His strength within us (Ephesians 6:10-11). "That’s why I work and struggle so hard, depending on Christ’s mighty power that works within me" (Colossians 1:29).
The two aspects of Christ's work are to crucify sin for us and to remove sin from us. The first was done once and for all, at the cross (Hebrews 10:10-12); the second is done all our lives, by His Holy Spirit (Galatians 5:16-17), as we learn to walk by His power.
God's justice, especially as it is seen at the cross, calls us to deal honestly with sin in our own lives (1 John 1:9). It warns us against using outward displays of religion, or merely Christian talk, to hide unrepentant hearts (Matthew 15:8; 2 Timothy 3:5). Instead, we are invited to come to God with genuine repentance (Psalm 51:17), trusting in Christ's sacrifice for forgiveness (Ephesians 1:7; Romans 5:8-9).

    In a word, Christ came primarily to preach good tidings to poor sinners. Yet He also brings terrible tidings to the unrepentant and unbelieving. — Thomas Doolittle (1632-1707), Captives Bound in Chains Made Free by Christ

What does true repentance involve?
Always talking about the cross, the blood of Jesus, the atonement He made.
Genuine acknowledgment of sin and turning away from it to Christ.Correct
Avoiding the topic of sin altogether.

Day 23: Praying in Agony
Being in Agony, He Prayed Fervently
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Verse: 
And being in agony, He prayed more earnestly; and His sweat was like drops of blood falling to the ground.
Luke 22:44
Teaching
There is one absolute essential need for those desiring to find freedom from sin: it is clinging to the Lord in prayer - consistent, resolute, determined prayer!
In Luke 22:44, we witness Jesus' intense agony in the Garden of Gethsemane as He contemplates the weight of bearing the world’s sins. His distress is so profound that His sweat is like drops of blood—a rare physical phenomenon called hematidrosis, associated with extreme stress, as foretold in Isaiah 53:3-5, where the prophet describes the suffering of the Messiah.
Such love Jesus has for you and me, to endure the torture of bearing our sin. It’s like a firefighter rushing into flames to rescue someone trapped and losing his life while saving them. John 15:13 reminds us, “Greater love has no one than this, to lay down one’s life for one’s friends.” Jesus bore the curse of God in our place, as Galatians 3:13 tells us, and He absorbed the full wrath of God like a lightning rod deflecting a deadly strike so that we might be spared, fulfilling what was prophesied in Isaiah 53:10: “It pleased the Lord to crush Him” for our iniquities. Oh, such love!
Think of this: "And being in agony, He prayed more earnestly." Despite His anguish, Jesus’ response is to pray more earnestly, surrendering His will to the Father: “Not My will, but Yours be done” (Luke 22:42, also recorded in Matthew 26:39).
This verse teaches us that Jesus’ agony was not due to fear of physical suffering alone but the spiritual burden of bearing our sins and enduring separation from the Father. Psalm 22:1 prophetically captures this moment with the words, “My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?” which were repeated by Jesus on the cross (Mark 15:34). Like a climber carrying the weight of an entire mountain on their back, pressing forward despite the crushing load, Jesus persisted through Gethsemane's anguish, clear up Mt. Calvary. Hebrews 12:2 tells us, “For the joy set before Him, He endured the cross.”
His fervent prayer demonstrates His dependence on God, as described in Hebrews 5:7, where Jesus is said to have offered prayers with loud cries and tears, and His commitment to fulfilling His redemptive mission—to save those He loves! Romans 5:8 reminds us of this profound truth: “But God demonstrates His own love for us in this: while we were still sinners, Christ died for us.”
Yes, when faced with carrying the sins of the world, bearing your guilt and mine, wearing our shame and owning our wrongs, Jesus felt the weight. He was in agony. His first response was to pray! "During the days of Jesus’ life on earth, he offered up prayers and petitions with fervent cries and tears to the one who could save him from death, and he was heard because of his reverent submission" (Hebrews 5:7).
When you face spiritual or emotional agony, what is your first response, and why?

Day 23: Praying in Agony
Biblical Illustration: Jacob
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider Jacob wrestling with God; you can read about it in Genesis 32:24-30. Like Jesus in the Garden of Gethsemane, Jacob faced a moment of intense struggle. Through the night, Jacob wrestled with God, refusing to let go until he received a blessing. This physical and spiritual wrestling mirrors the perseverance Jesus showed in prayer during His time of agony, as recorded in Luke 22:44, when He prayed more earnestly in the face of overwhelming sorrow.
Both Jacob and Jesus highlight the necessity of clinging to God during moments of distress. Jacob’s words, “I will not let You go unless You bless me,” remind us of the persistence God calls us to have in prayer (Genesis 32:26). Similarly, Jesus’ prayer, “Not My will, but Yours be done,” shows the importance of submission to the Father’s plan, as seen in Luke 22:42 and Matthew 26:39.
Their stories echo the message of James 5:16, which reminds us that “the prayer of a righteous person is powerful and effective.” Both men teach us that overcoming requires persistence, dependence on God, and complete submission to His will, trusting Him to provide what we need in His perfect timing.
How is Jacob's wrestling with God similar to Jesus’ prayer in Gethsemane?

Day 23: Praying in Agony
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
When we strive to overcome sin or endure trials, we may experience spiritual and emotional agony. In these moments, like Jesus, we are called to pray more fervently. 
As Charles Spurgeon once said, 

    "Prayer is the slender nerve that moves the muscle of Omnipotence."   - Charles Spurgeon Morning and Evening

Prayer connects us to God’s strength, aligns us with His will, and sustains us through our struggles. Practically, this means prioritizing prayer, especially when temptation or trials feel overwhelming. Instead of giving in to despair or self-reliance, let your agony drive you to your knees.
In moments of suffering and struggle, our prayers become a lifeline, a way to listen to God and align with Him and, as we pray God's Word back to Him, we find out what He wants us to do in any given situation.
There simply will be no overcoming our sin without specific praying, wrestling with God in prayer, and asking Him for His plan to implement in our lives so that we can experience the victory Jesus purchased for us on the cross. So, pray with honesty, persistence, and surrender, trusting that God hears and strengthens those who seek Him earnestly. "And without faith it is impossible to please him, for whoever would draw near to God must believe that he exists and that he rewards those who seek him" (Hebrews 11:6).
Through fervent prayer, we submit our struggles to God, allowing His will to take precedence over our desires and doubts.
May we, like Jesus, draw closer to God in prayer not only in struggle but in every season of life, trusting in His providence and His love for us.
When facing temptation, how can you incorporate more fervent prayer into your response?
a. Setting aside dedicated time for prayer 
b. Praying Scripture
c. Asking others to pray for you
d. All of the aboveCorrect

Day 24: Jesus Intercedes For You
Jesus Saves Sinners Through His Sacrifice
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage: 
Therefore he is able to save completely those who come to God through him, because he always lives to intercede for them. Such a high priest truly meets our need—one who is holy, blameless, pure, set apart from sinners, exalted above the heavens. Unlike the other high priests, he does not need to offer sacrifices day after day, first for his own sins, and then for the sins of the people. He sacrificed for their sins once for all when he offered himself.
Hebrews 7:25-27
In order to live in freedom in our daily lives, we absolutely must know the work of Jesus Christ and its effectiveness before God, our Father. 
Hebrews 7:25-27 teaches us at least 3 things:

    Christ saves completely
    Christ intercedes always
    Christ's sacrifice is sufficient

1. Christ Saves Completely
Jesus is not limited in His ability to save, He saves completely, or "to the uttermost." It's like a master craftsman who restores a shattered vase—not just gluing the pieces together but making it whole and stronger than before. 
Jesus saves us "completely," addressing both our sins and our struggles, leaving no part of us untouched by His grace. "Therefore he is able to save completely those who come to God through him, because he always lives to intercede for them" (Hebrews 7:25).
This truth teaches us that salvation is not partial or temporary but eternal and holistic. "And I give them eternal life, and they shall never perish; no one will snatch them out of my hand" (John 10:28; see also Isaiah 45:17). This means Jesus saves us to the end, keeps us forever, and we cannot perish! If you really know this, you gain a sense of security, knowing that Jesus has you! Nobody can take you from him, not even yourself!
The grace of Jesus Christ is a river that overflows its banks, saturating every dry and weary place in our souls. "Whoever believes in me, as Scripture has said, rivers of living water will flow from within them" (John 7:38; see also Ezekiel 47:9). This river goes to every low spot, every dry spot, every nook, and valley, crack and crevice in our lives, bringing its rich source of nutrients and refreshment.
When we feel overwhelmed by sin or life’s struggles, when we have fallen to temptation, when we are exhausted in the battle, we can rest in the assurance that Christ's work on our behalf is sufficient. On the basis of His own work, He invites us to "Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, and I will give you rest" (Matthew 11:28; see also Psalm 55:22).
He died for all our sins: past, present, and future, His purpose was to do away with them forever. "But now he has appeared once for all at the culmination of the ages to do away with sin by the sacrifice of himself" (Hebrews 9:26; see also Psalm 103:12). Do you really know that every single sin has been removed from you and put on Jesus? If you believe this, you know what real freedom is like!
2. Christ Intercedes always: 
Jesus "always lives to intercede for us" (Hebrews 7:25; see also Isaiah 53:12). This means that even now, in heaven, He is actively praying for us before the Father. "Who then is the one who condemns? No one. Christ Jesus who died—more than that, who was raised to life—is at the right hand of God and is also interceding for us" (Romans 8:34; see also Job 16:19).
Imagine having an advocate who never stops speaking on your behalf! Especially if you sin! As Scripture says, "If anybody does sin, we have an advocate with the Father—Jesus Christ, the Righteous One" (1 John 2:1; see also Zechariah 3:1-2). His intercession is not based on our performance but on His performance at the cross, where He cried, "It is finished" (John 19:30), declaring the full payment for our sins had been made. His perfect righteousness is the foundation of His prayers for us, and of our acceptance by God: "God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God" (Philippians 3:9).
This gives us confidence to draw near to God, even when we feel unworthy. "Let us then approach God’s throne of grace with confidence, so that we may receive mercy and find grace to help us in our time of need" (Hebrews 4:16; see also Psalm 34:17-18). Yes, it is specifically in our time of need that we can draw near and receive grace!
3. Christ's sacrifice is sufficient:
Unlike the Old Testament priests who offered sacrifices repeatedly: "Day after day every priest stands and performs his religious duties; again and again he offers the same sacrifices, which can never take away sins" (Hebrews 10:11), Jesus’ sacrifice was "once for all": "And by that will, we have been made holy through the sacrifice of the body of Jesus Christ once for all" (Hebrews 10:10). 
His death on the cross fully atoned for our sins: "But he was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; the punishment that brought us peace was on him, and by his wounds, we are healed" (Isaiah 53:5), making ongoing sacrifices unnecessary: "And where these have been forgiven, sacrifice for sin is no longer necessary" (Hebrews 10:18).
This truth frees us from trying to earn God’s favor through good works or penance. For it is by grace you have been saved, through faith—and this is not from yourselves, it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast" (Ephesians 2:8-9). The gospel reminds us that our sins—past, present, and future—are paid for in full. "When you were dead in your sins and in the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made you alive with Christ. He forgave us all our sins, having canceled the charge of our legal indebtedness, which stood against us and condemned us; he has taken it away, nailing it to the cross" (Colossians 2:13-14).
How does understanding that Christ saves completely, intercedes always, and sacrificed once for all impact your daily life and relationship with God?

Day 24: Jesus Intercedes For You
Biblical Illustration: The Good Shepherd
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Imagine a shepherd who finds a lost sheep caught in a thorny thicket on the edge of a cliff. The sheep is not just a little lost—it’s utterly helpless, unable to free itself, and in danger of falling to its death. The shepherd doesn’t merely guide the sheep partway to safety or bandage one wound while leaving others untreated. Instead, he rescues it entirely, lifting it onto his shoulders, returning it to safety, tending to every injury, and restoring it to the flock.
This is what Jesus does for us. When we come to Him, He doesn’t leave us half-rescued, struggling to finish the work ourselves. He saves us “to the uttermost” (Hebrews 7:25)—completely, wholly, eternally. He addresses every need of our souls, removing all our sins and clothing us in His perfect righteousness.
As the shepherd restores the sheep, Jesus restores us, making us new creations. His saving work is complete, leaving nothing undone: “Therefore, if anyone is in Christ, the new creation has come: The old has gone, the new is here!” (2 Corinthians 5:17).
"I am the Good Shepherd: the Good Shepherd lays down His life for the sheep" (John 10:10).
How does the image of Jesus as the Good Shepherd who rescues and restores completely deepen your understanding of His care for you?


Day 24: Jesus Intercedes For You
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Understanding these truths about Christ—His complete salvation, ongoing intercession, and sufficient sacrifice—helps us overcome sin by shifting our focus from self-effort to faith in Him. When we trust His finished work on the cross, we find strength to resist temptation, peace in struggles, and hope in trials.
Like the shepherd who rescues the lost sheep entirely, lifting it onto His shoulders and restoring it to safety, Jesus does not leave us to fend for ourselves. He saves us completely, addresses every wound, and makes us new creations. His intercession assures us that we are never alone, and His once-for-all sacrifice frees us from the burden of earning God’s favor.
Take time today to reflect on an area of your life where you feel overwhelmed, tempted, or burdened. Bring it to Christ in prayer, trusting in His ability to save completely, intercede for you always, and provide all the grace you need.
"Let us then approach God’s throne of grace with confidence, so that we may receive mercy and find grace to help us in our time of need." (Hebrews 4:16)
What difference does Christ’s complete salvation, ongoing intercession, and sufficient sacrifice make in the way you approach struggles, temptation, and trials?

Day 25: A Fountain Filled With Blood
There Is A Fountain Filled With Blood
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Today is different! It contains just one section, with one devotional writing that will knock your spiritual socks off! It should take less than 5 minutes to read, but the entire day to meditate on. It is from Greg Morse, a staff writer for Desiring God and a Bethlehem College and Seminary graduate. He and his wife, Abigail, live in Saint Paul with their son and three daughters. 
Here it is:

Without the shedding of blood there is no forgiveness of sins. —Hebrews 9:22
Christianity is unlike anything man can imagine on his own.
It is perhaps natural to imagine a religion that functions like a swimming pool — a hobby that may have benefits, but of course, swimming is not for everyone. Others might conceive of religion as a medicinal pool used to strengthen people who have sprained their hope and need some rehab to get them back on their feet.

    “Christianity offends a man before it can save him.”

But Christianity is different. It is not primarily a swimming pool to enjoy nor a hot tub to fix a midlife crisis. Christianity is about a pool filled with blood. It is graphic. It is gory. It is not a pristine pond found next to our manicured lawns. It is a crimson tide in which we must be submerged.
Drawn from His Veins
So, whose blood is it? Where it ought to have been the blood of God’s enemies, it was, almost unimaginably, the blood of his own Son. The God-man revealed to us as Jesus of Nazareth was not spared what others in God’s great story were.
The Red Sea did not part for him. The Father struck him with Abraham’s flint knife. He drowned in Noah’s flood. Daniel’s lions devoured him. The fiery furnace of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego consumed him. The spear that missed David impaled his Lord. He was cast overboard and swallowed by Jonah’s beast. He was crushed for David’s adultery, Abraham’s cowardice, Noah’s drunkenness. The squeals of every sacrificed animal that ever bled on the altar were in anticipation of his cry.
We, like all of God’s people since, were only spared because Christ was not.

    There is a fountain filled with blood, drawn from Immanuel’s veins.
    And sinners plunged beneath that flood lose all their guilty stains.

God passed over former sins until the day of reckoning came. The debt had accrued for God’s chosen. Man could not pay for his crime with money, time, or life change. “Without the shedding of blood there is no forgiveness of sins” (Hebrews 9:22). The wages of sin is death (Romans 6:23). And on a hill outside of Jerusalem, where Rome crucified criminals and burned their garbage, Jesus paid every awful debt we had, and he did so with the only acceptable currency: blood.
Plunged Beneath That Flood
Christianity is unlike any other religion. Not merely because it is true, but because it is beautiful. Yet it offends a man before it can save him. It tells him that he is dead in sin. It tells him that he’s a rebel. It tells him that unless he plunges himself underneath the flood of Christ’s blood by faith, he will die and never live. That his blood will be upon his own head forever.
But as plainly as it tells a man that he is condemned before God, it commands him to draw near and receive mercy.

    Seek the Lord while he may be found; call upon him while he is near; let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous man his thoughts; let him return to the Lord, that he may have compassion on him, and to our God, for he will abundantly pardon. (Isaiah 55:6–7)

God calls criminals near that he might have compassion on them. He threatens us with everlasting ruin — what your many sins deserve — but offers us everlasting fellowship with him, if we would turn from our evil and receive his crimson pardon. The Great Husband calls his adulterous bride to return to him and find complete forgiveness and unending love. No pity will be offered the one who insists on rejecting the blood-soaked offer of the cross.
You Would Not Forgive You
But what if you are the worst person you know? Why should you be confident to draw near to this God? Why should you have any hope to be an object of his love?
Not because your case isn’t as bad as you think, but because his greatness is higher than you can imagine. Because his thoughts are not our thoughts, and his ways are not our ways (Isaiah 55:8). Often, this verse is used to prove God’s omniscience. But it’s more specifically about his mercy and compassion towards repentant sinners. Read it in context:

    Seek the Lord while he may be found; call upon him while he is near; let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous man his thoughts; let him return to the Lord, that he may have compassion on him, and to our God, for he will abundantly pardon. For my thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways, declares the Lord. (Isaiah 55:6–8)

Why should you return to the Lord? Why should you go as you are to Christ and hope to be received? Because his grace is above your grace. His ways of mercy are not like ours. If you or I were God, the world would have been crushed ages ago. You would not forgive you, but he will. You wouldn’t pour out wrath on your own Son for rebels, but he did. This God displayed his love by sending his Son into the world to fill a fountain with his own blood. No man could conceive of it unless God revealed it.
Rejoiced to See

    The dying thief rejoiced to see that fountain in his day.
    And there may I, though vile as he, wash all my sins away.

One of the first to take the plunge under the flood of Christ’s blood in the Gospels is the dying thief who died beside him. Crucified at the same time as Christ, he initially began ridiculing him (Matthew 27:44). But after hearing the crowd, hearing Jesus’s words and his prayers, watching him die as the sun fled in shame, he, by the mercy of God, saw Jesus for who he was: the King of heaven (Luke 23:42). With his dying breaths, this criminal, stained in the consequences of his own sin and dying as a vile man with nothing to commend him, found the fountain being filled next to him. He trusted in those wounds and has now been with him for two thousand years.
Christianity is unlike any religion. The Father is unlike any god. Christ is unlike any savior. And the Spirit is unlike any helper. Seek the Lord while he may be found — because there is now a fountain filled with blood.
Please share your thoughts on this devotional today:

    Nothing ever delivers a man from the power of sin like a sight of the suffering Saviour. - Charles Spurgeon, quoted on X
    
    
Day 26: Jesus Came to Destroy Satan's Work
Jesus Destroyed Satan's Work For You
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage of Scripture
The one who does what is sinful is of the devil, because the devil has been sinning from the beginning. The reason the Son of God appeared was to destroy the devil’s work. No one who is born of God will continue to sin because God’s seed remains in them; they cannot go on sinning because they have been born of God.
1 John 3:8-9, NIV
Teaching
1 John 3:8 teaches us the power of the gospel: Jesus came to destroy the works of the devil, including sin's dominion over us (Romans 6:14). This was prophesied in the very beginning (Genesis 3:15). Therefore, to seek freedom from devil-inspired sin puts us in agreement with the very thing Jesus came to do (John 8:36).
Notice how this message was from the very beginning. After Adam and Eve sinned, God gave the message that an offspring of the woman would come and destroy the devil (Genesis 3:15). "And I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your seed and her seed; He shall bruise you on the head, and you shall bruise him on the heel" (Genesis 3:15).
This was a prophecy of Jesus Christ coming to destroy the works of the devil (Hebrews 2:14). We know this because it was to be "the seed of the woman" who would give a fatal blow to the head of the serpent, yet a woman does not have "seed," only a man does. This predicts the virgin birth of Jesus (Isaiah 7:14, Matthew 1:23) and how the Holy Spirit of God implanted Christ within Mary (Luke 1:35). It also predicts the cross where Jesus would die to destroy the devil:
"Because God’s children are human beings—made of flesh and blood—the Son also became flesh and blood. For only as a human being could he die, and only by dying could he break the power of the devil, who had the power of death. Only in this way could he set free all who have lived their lives as slaves to the fear of dying" (Hebrews 2:14-15, John 12:31, Colossians 2:14-15).
To be “born of God,” as described in 1 John 3:8-9, means to experience a spiritual rebirth through faith in Jesus Christ (John 3:3-7, Ephesians 2:4-5). This new birth, often called being "born again," is a work of the Holy Spirit (Titus 3:5), transforming a person from within (2 Corinthians 5:17). It is a fundamental change in nature, identity, and direction as we are set free from the law of sin and death (Galatians 2:20, Romans 8:1-2).
A New Identity: When you are born of God, you become a child of God, adopted into His family (John 1:12-13; Romans 8:15-16). You receive a new name and a fresh start, no longer defined by sin but by righteousness through Christ (2 Corinthians 5:21). Just as an orphan who is adopted into a loving family receives a new home and identity, so too does a believer enter God's family, embraced and cherished by Jesus, purchased at such a high price (1 Corinthians 6:19-20; Galatians 4:4-7).
A New Nature: God's "seed" (His Spirit and His life) comes to dwell in you (1 John 3:9). This is as if the soil of your heart, once barren and lifeless, is now planted with a divine seed that grows and flourishes, producing a life inclined toward righteousness and holiness (Ezekiel 36:26-27; Galatians 5:22-23). Sin no longer defines or controls you (Romans 6:14).
A New Power: Being born of God breaks the dominion of sin over your life. While believers still struggle with sin, they are empowered by the Holy Spirit to overcome it (Romans 8:11; 1 Corinthians 10:13; Philippians 2:13).
A New Direction: This transformation changes the trajectory of your life. Instead of living in rebellion against God, you now seek to obey and please Him out of love and gratitude (2 Corinthians 5:9; Ephesians 2:10; Titus 2:11-12). I once read of a river that flowed aimlessly into the wilderness, but a crew had been set up and a plan had been made to redirect the water to a fertile valley, bringing life and purpose wherever it went. That's what happens in our new birth (Psalm 1:3; John 7:38) as we are like that river; instead of flowing aimlessly along a path of sin we are redirected to produce fruit.
So, through the work of the Son for us in the gospel, and the work of the Spirit in us, we can overcome the sin that long held us captive (Romans 8:2; Galatians 5:1).
Questions for Reflection
Why did Jesus come into the world, according to this passage?
a. To perform miracles
b. To destroy the devil’s workCorrect
c. To give moral teachings
What does it mean to be “born of God”? 

Day 26: Jesus Came to Destroy Satan's Work
Biblical Illustration: Zaccheus
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider the story of Zacchaeus in Luke 19:1-10. Zacchaeus was a tax collector known for his sinful ways, including greed and dishonesty. However, when he encountered Jesus, everything changed. Zacchaeus repented, gave half his possessions to the poor, and repaid those he defrauded four times more. His transformation illustrates how Jesus destroys the works of the devil and brings new life to those who receive Him. 
Zacchaeus did not simply feel remorse; he acted in repentance, demonstrating that Christ’s power had truly taken root in his heart (Luke 19:8-9). His story reminds us that genuine faith produces a changed life (James 2:17; 2 Corinthians 5:17; Acts 26:20).
Questions for Reflection:
How did Zacchaeus’s actions show that Jesus had changed his heart?
What areas of your life need to reflect a similar transformation?

Day 26: Jesus Came to Destroy Satan's Work
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Have you been born of God by believing the saving message of the gospel of Jesus Christ (John 3:3-5; Romans 10:9-10)? To overcome habitual sin, we must see the cross of Christ (Hebrews 12:2-3) and rely on His victory (1 Corinthians 15:57; Colossians 2:13-15) and live in the new identity we have as God’s children (2 Corinthians 5:17; 1 John 3:1). This involves:
Confession and repentance: Acknowledging our sin and turning from it (1 John 1:9; Acts 3:19).
Abiding in God’s Word: Experiencing the renewal of our minds through the truth of God's Word (John 8:31-32; Romans 12:2; Psalm 119:11).
Walking by the Spirit: Depending on His strength to resist temptation (Galatians 5:16-17; Romans 8:13).
Receiving a gospel-centered, Spirit-given battle plan: We must seek the Lord for a battle plan daily that would bring us victory (Psalm 144:1-2). This plan will be an individual plan for each person to put to death that which belongs to our flesh (Colossians 3:5; Romans 6:11-14), and to walk that plan out in our lives by the power of the Holy Spirit (Ephesians 6:10-11; Philippians 2:13).
The gospel teaches us that Jesus' body was broken for us (Isaiah 53:5; Luke 22:19; 1 Corinthians 11:24) and through that experience, Jesus broke sin’s power (Romans 6:6-7; Hebrews 2:14-15). Our role is to walk in that freedom daily, trusting His Spirit to empower us (Galatians 5:1; 2 Peter 1:3).
How can you both rely on Christ’s victory over Satan and follow a battle plan to experience that victory in your own life?

Day 27: The Beautiful Benefits of Evil
Evil Can Look Good
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
She saw that the tree was beautiful and its fruit looked delicious, and she wanted the wisdom it would give her.
Genesis 3:6
Eve saw the tree that God had forbidden as "beautiful," its fruit as "delicious," and she believed eating it would grant her "wisdom." This highlights a crucial truth: all temptation presents benefits, which can blind us to the sin and evil behind it. The benefits are real— “forbidden fruit” can indeed seem desirable.
Consider a simple example: breaking the speed limit while driving. Speeding has caused countless deaths, yet it comes with a tangible benefit—reaching your destination faster. This is a real advantage. However, when we're in a hurry, we often ignore or downplay the dangers: the risk of harming ourselves or others, receiving a ticket, or for Christians, dishonoring the name of Jesus and disregarding the sacrifice of the cross. These consequences might cross our minds, but we quickly push them aside, focused only on the benefit of saving a few minutes. (If you are a believer in Jesus and you speed, please consider removing all Christian bumper stickers, church signs, etc. that you might have placed on your car, until you are able to comply with the laws of the land so that you don't ruin your Christian testimony.)
This pattern applies to any sin struggle. Reflect on your own struggles, and you can probably list the immediate benefits of giving in to temptation. And those benefits are real. The all-you-can-eat buffet offers a feast of pleasures. Pornography can appear as an enticing escape. Drugs promise relief from anxiety and depression. Drunkenness offers to numb the conscience and quiet guilt.
Yes, it's true, "Stolen water is sweet; food eaten in secret is delicious!'" (Proverbs 9:17). And if all we know is the "sweet and delicious" side of sin, i.e. the tangible benefits of it, then we are ignorant to the death it brings. "But little do they know that the dead are there, that her guests are deep in the realm of the dead" (Proverbs 9:18). 
The question everyone must answer for themselves: do the benefits of sinning outweigh the very real consequences of choosing sin? 
Why do the immediate benefits of temptation often blind us to the long-term consequences of sin, and how can we shift our focus to consider the true cost of our choices?

Day 27: The Beautiful Benefits of Evil
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Temptation often hides behind the mask of immediate benefits. Eve saw the forbidden fruit as beautiful, delicious, and a source of wisdom (Genesis 3:6), but like a fish looking only at the bait, she failed to consider the devastating consequences (James 1:14-15). Similarly, in our daily lives, sin entices us by emphasizing short-term gains while minimizing long-term costs. Recognizing this dynamic is essential for freedom and spiritual growth (1 Corinthians 10:13).
Take a moment to reflect on areas where temptation might be drawing you in. What benefits do you perceive? Maybe a harsh word feels justified because it provides immediate relief from frustration (Proverbs 15:1). Or maybe indulging in laziness seems appealing because it offers temporary comfort even though it stirs up anger (Proverbs 6:10-11). These benefits are real, but so are the consequences (Romans 6:23).
Now, consider the bigger picture. What might be lost if you give in and gratify your flesh? Relationships may suffer, guilt and shame might follow (Psalm 32:3-5), and most importantly, your walk with Christ will be hindered (Galatians 5:16-17). Combat temptation by weighing the fleeting benefits against the eternal consequences (2 Corinthians 4:17-18).
Ask God for discernment to see beyond sin’s facade and for strength to resist. Lean on His promises, knowing that the pleasures of sin are fleeting, but the rewards of obedience are eternal. The Bible says of Moses that "he chose to share the oppression of God’s people instead of enjoying the fleeting pleasures of sin" (Hebrews 11:25).
"No temptation has overtaken you except what is common to mankind. And God is faithful; he will not let you be tempted beyond what you can bear" (1 Corinthians 10:13).
Think about a time when you faced a temptation that seemed to offer a clear benefit. How did focusing on the short-term advantage influence your decision?
Looking back, how might considering the long-term consequences and seeking God’s perspective have changed your response?

Day 28: Jesus Took It All For You
Jesus Was Made Sin For You
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
As was stated in yesterday's devotional, many of us have experienced consequences of our sin (Romans 6:23). As we look back on some of these consequences, we can become very discouraged, seeing what happened because of our choices (Galatians 6:7-8). It can really hurt our hearts to consider, and can even tempt us to go back to sin through the feeling of hopelessness (Proverbs 13:12).
But here is where the gospel is really good news of great joy for all people (Luke 2:10)! Do you remember all the consequences of sin listed in yesterday's lesson? Here they are again, but this time I've shown how Jesus took each one of these on Himself (Isaiah 53:4-5), or reversed them at the cross (Colossians 2:13-14), thereby removing them from you eternally (Hebrews 10:14):
Separation from God
Genesis 3:23-24; Isaiah 59:2
Jesus experienced separation from God when He bore our sins:

    Matthew 27:46: "My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?"
    Jesus was forsaken by the Father as He bore the sin of the world, taking our separation upon Himself.

Death
Genesis 2:17; Ezekiel 18:4
Jesus took the penalty of death that sin demands:

    Romans 5:8: "While we were still sinners, Christ died for us."
    1 Peter 3:18: "For Christ also suffered once for sins, the righteous for the unrighteous, to bring you to God."

Curse upon Creation
Genesis 3:17-19
Jesus received the curse on Himself:

    Galatians 3:13: "Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us."
    He endured the effects of the cursed ground in His suffering and death.

Shame and Fear
Genesis 3:7-10
Jesus bore our shame:

    Hebrews 12:2: "He endured the cross, scorning its shame."
    Isaiah 53:3: "He was despised and rejected by men."

Judgment and Punishment
Numbers 14:33-34; 2 Samuel 12:10-12
Jesus bore the punishment for sin:

    Isaiah 53:5: "The punishment that brought us peace was on Him."
    Romans 8:3: God condemned sin in the flesh of Jesus.

Broken Relationships
Genesis 4:8; Genesis 3:16
Jesus reconciled relationships through His death:

    Ephesians 2:14: "For He Himself is our peace, who has made the two groups one and has destroyed the barrier."
    Colossians 1:20: "Through Him to reconcile to Himself all things."

Exile and Captivity
Leviticus 26:33; 2 Kings 17:6-23
Jesus took on the ultimate exile and brought us back to God:

    Hebrews 13:12: "Jesus also suffered outside the city gate to make the people holy."
    Ephesians 4:8: "He led captivity captive."

Divine Wrath
Genesis 6:5-7; Exodus 32:35
Jesus absorbed God’s wrath on the cross:

    Romans 5:9: "We have now been justified by His blood, how much more shall we be saved from God’s wrath through Him!"
    Isaiah 53:10: "Yet it pleased the Lord to crush Him."

Eternal Separation from God
Matthew 25:41-46; 2 Thessalonians 1:8-9
Jesus took eternal separation upon Himself so we wouldn’t have to:

    Hebrews 9:28: "Christ was sacrificed once to take away the sins of many."

Slavery to Sin
John 8:34; Romans 6:16
Jesus set us free from sin’s slavery:

    John 8:36: "If the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed."
    Romans 6:22: "But now you have been set free from sin."

Corruption of the Flesh
Galatians 6:8
Jesus took on human corruption in His body:

    1 Peter 2:24: "He Himself bore our sins in His body on the tree."

Loss of Fellowship with God
1 John 1:6
Jesus restored our fellowship with God:

    2 Corinthians 5:21: "God made Him who had no sin to be sin for us."
    Hebrews 10:19-22: Jesus opened the way to God.

Judgment and Wrath
Romans 2:5; Revelation 20:11-15
Jesus bore judgment and wrath for us:

    Romans 3:25: "God presented Christ as a sacrifice of atonement, through the shedding of His blood."

Spiritual Blindness
2 Corinthians 4:4
Jesus brought light to our blindness:

    John 9:39: "For judgment I have come into this world, so that the blind will see."
    Luke 4:18: "He has sent Me to proclaim recovery of sight for the blind."

Broken Relationships
James 4:1-2; Romans 1:29-31
Jesus restores relationships through reconciliation:

    2 Corinthians 5:18: "God, who reconciled us to Himself through Christ."

Grieving the Holy Spirit
Ephesians 4:30
Jesus sent the Spirit to restore fellowship:

    John 16:7: "Unless I go away, the Advocate will not come to you."

Loss of Peace and Joy
Romans 3:16-17; Isaiah 57:21
Jesus became our peace and joy:

    John 14:27: "Peace I leave with you; My peace I give you."
    Romans 15:13: "May the God of hope fill you with all joy and peace."

Consider that Jesus came to undo and reverse the curse and consequences of sin. What does that do to your heart as you contemplate this?

Day 28: Jesus Took It All For You
Biblical Illustration: Every Believer
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
"Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us—for it is written, ‘Cursed is everyone who is hanged on a tree’—so that in Christ Jesus the blessing of Abraham might come to the Gentiles, so that we might receive the promised Spirit through faith" (Galatians 3:13-14).
Sin brought devastating consequences: separation from God (Isaiah 59:2), guilt (Psalm 32:5), death (Romans 6:23), the curse of the law (Deuteronomy 27:26) and many more. But Jesus came to undo all that sin has done for every believer. On the cross, He bore the curse that was ours (Isaiah 53:5-6), fulfilling the law’s demands we could never meet (Romans 8:3-4). Through His sacrifice, He exchanged your curse for God’s blessing and your condemnation for His righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21).
Away with hopelessness (Romans 15:13)! Away with despair (2 Corinthians 4:8-9)! Away with the pull back into sin because we just can't get better (Hebrews 12:1-2). Can't get better? We're perfect (Hebrews 10:14)!
The gospel declares that Jesus not only redeems but restores. For every believer, He brings us back into fellowship with God (1 Peter 3:18), removes your guilt (Romans 8:1), grants you eternal life (John 3:16), and fills you with His Spirit (Ezekiel 36:26-27; Acts 2:38-39). Where sin brought death, Jesus brings life (John 10:10); where sin enslaved, Jesus sets you free (John 8:36).
This is grace: God gave His Son to undo sin’s power for you (Genesis 3:15) and renew you for His glory. By faith in Christ, you receive the Spirit and live as a child of God (Romans 8:14-16), walking in freedom and His abundant blessing.
According to Galatians 3:13-14 and the devotional, what did Jesus accomplish for every believer by becoming a curse for us?
 A. He abolished the law completely, so we may now sin without penalty.
B. He bore the curse of the law, granting us God's blessing and the promised Spirit through faith. Correct
C. He replaced the Old Testament with only New Testament teachings.
D. He removed all suffering and hardship from our lives.

Day 28: Jesus Took It All For You
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Imagine being trapped in a dark, locked room with no way out. That’s what sin does: it enslaves, isolates, darkens our understanding and condemns us under the curse of the law (Romans 6:16, Galatians 3:10). And try as we might, we're unable to find our way out. I was like this for many many years, maybe you were too. 
But Jesus, like a rescuer breaking down the door, came to free us by becoming the curse for us (Galatians 3:13). Through His death on the cross, He paid the penalty we deserved, fulfilling the demands of the law (Romans 8:3-4).
Picture a smudged mirror that distorts your reflection. Sin does that, obscuring the image of God in us. Soon, after enough sinning, we can hardly recognize ourselves. But Jesus came to cleanse and restore us, so we reflect His glory once more (2 Corinthians 3:18). He didn’t just remove the curse; He gave us the blessing of Abraham—life, righteousness, and relationship with God (Genesis 12:3, Galatians 3:14).
This good news isn’t just abstract theology; it’s deeply personal. For you and I as believers in Jesus, it means freedom from guilt and shame (Romans 8:1), the gift of the Holy Spirit to guide you (John 14:26), and eternal life in His presence (John 3:16). 
Like prisoners set free, let’s live as children of God, walking in His blessing, filled with His Spirit, and sharing this freedom with others (Romans 8:14-16).
How will you live differently today knowing Jesus has freed you from sin's prison?

Day 29: Jesus Took It All For You - Part 2
You Did It, Jesus Undid It
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
In yesterday's devotional, we saw how Jesus took all our sins and consequences on Himself and undid the curse that was on us due to sin.
It is important to know that you, as a believer, are entirely free from your past, to live out your freedom in your daily life. Just like a prisoner who has been fully pardoned and given a new identity, you are no longer bound by the chains of your former life (Colossians 1:13-14).
For this reason, in this devotional, I want us to see and rejoice in the truth that Jesus really did come to undo all that sin has done (1 John 3:8). 
We saw this truth yesterday in unequivocal statements from multiple Scriptures, but today, let's see it illustrated in the Old Testament, as if we were uncovering a hidden treasure that has been waiting there all along to reveal its riches (Luke 24:27).
Numerous "first words", or concepts, came into being as a direct result of sin. Words that had not been used before sin happened start appearing in Genesis chapter 3. For example "thorns" and "sweat" were not in the biblical record until Adam and Eve sinned, and both were a direct result of sin.
What words can you think of that are only used after the sin of Adam and Eve (see Genesis 3)?
death slavery separation
If we study Genesis 3 carefully we find many "first words" that were used as a direct result of sin. Here is a list of some of them:

    Curse – The ground was cursed because of sin (Genesis 3:17).
    Pain – Pain was introduced as a result of the curse, particularly in childbirth (Genesis 3:16).
    Thorns – Thorns and thistles began to grow as part of the cursed ground (Genesis 3:18).
    Sweat – By the sweat of his brow, Adam would now toil to eat (Genesis 3:19).
    Enmity – Introduced as a result of the hostility between the serpent and humanity (Genesis 3:15).
    Sword – A flaming sword was set to guard Eden, symbolizing judgment and separation (Genesis 3:24).
    Death – “For dust you are, and to dust you will return,” marking mortality as a consequence of sin (Genesis 3:19).

The consequences of sin are horrendous, shocking, and devastating. I have experienced the consequences of my sin in such painful ways, such devastating ways. We all do, to some degree or another. 
But good news, Jesus came! The reason He came was to destroy the works of the devil and reverse the curse for all who believe. 
So notice how these "first words" point directly to the good news:

    Curse – Jesus became a curse for us, redeeming us from the curse of the law:
     “Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us, for it is written: ‘Cursed is everyone who is hung on a pole’” (Galatians 3:13).
    Pain – Jesus bore unimaginable spiritual, physical and emotional pain on the cross for our redemption:
     “Surely he took up our pain and bore our suffering… he was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities” (Isaiah 53:4-5).
    Thorns – Jesus wore a crown of thorns, taking on the symbol of the cursed earth as He bore our sin:
     “The soldiers twisted together a crown of thorns and put it on his head. They clothed him in a purple robe” (John 19:2).
    Sweat – In the garden of Gethsemane, Jesus sweat drops of blood as He prepared to bear the weight of sin:
     “And being in anguish, he prayed more earnestly, and his sweat was like drops of blood falling to the ground” (Luke 22:44).
    Enmity – Jesus defeated the serpent and reconciled us to God, removing the enmity caused by sin:
     “The God of peace will soon crush Satan under your feet” (Romans 16:20).
    “And having disarmed the powers and authorities, he made a public spectacle of them, triumphing over them by the cross” (Colossians 2:15).
    Sword – Jesus faced the sword of God’s judgment, taking it upon Himself to reconcile us to the Father:
     “Awake, O sword, against my shepherd, against the man who is close to me!” (Zechariah 13:7).
    “It was the Lord’s will to crush him and cause him to suffer” (Isaiah 53:10).
    Death – Jesus conquered death by dying in our place and rising again to give us eternal life:
     “Since the children have flesh and blood, he too shared in their humanity so that by his death he might break the power of him who holds the power of death—that is, the devil” (Hebrews 2:14).
    “Where, O death, is your victory? Where, O death, is your sting?” (1 Corinthians 15:55).

Jesus took every consequence of the Fall upon Himself, and through His sacrifice, He reversed their effects for all who trust in Him. Your past and my past have been nailed to the cross. Now when Satan tempts us to despair, and tells us of the guilt within, upward we look and see Him there, He made an end to all our sin!


 How do the "first words" in Genesis 3 show the consequences of sin and the hope found in Jesus?
 
 Day 29: Jesus Took It All For You - Part 2
Biblical Illustration: Joseph
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Sometimes we see ourselves as cursed, and everything around us, all our life's circumstances seem to affirm this. 
Imagine Joseph, betrayed by his brothers, sold into slavery, and then falsely accused in Egypt. His life seemed cursed, riddled with pain, and marked by enmity and hostility from his own family members. Thorns of injustice pierced his every step, and sweat poured from his brow as he toiled in prison. Joseph's life appeared to embody the consequences of the Fall. Genesis 3 all over again. Yet, through his suffering, God was preparing a way of salvation.
Fast forward to Genesis 50:20, where Joseph stands before the very brothers who wronged him and says, “You intended to harm me, but God intended it for good to accomplish what is now being done, the saving of many lives.” Joseph's suffering became the means through which God saved others.
Joseph’s story foreshadows Jesus Christ, who also endured betrayal, pain, and the weight of the curse. Jesus wore a literal crown of thorns, bore the enmity of humanity's sin, and sweat drops of blood in agony. Like Joseph, He faced injustice but did so willingly, knowing God was working through His suffering to save not just many lives but all who would believe. Oh such love Jesus has for us!
Just as Joseph rose to power to provide bread for the starving, Jesus rose from the grave to become the Bread of Life. Where Joseph saved a nation from physical famine, Jesus saves the world from spiritual death. Joseph’s suffering and redemption illustrate Christ’s greater work: taking on the consequences of sin to bring about reconciliation and life.
Through our precious Jesus, the curse is lifted (Galatians 3:13), pain has a purpose (Romans 8:28), enmity is destroyed (Colossians 1:20), and death is defeated (1 Corinthians 15:54-55). Like Joseph, who saw God’s redemptive hand through his trials, we see in Jesus the ultimate fulfillment of God’s promise to redeem what was broken. 
And like Joseph, the curse is reversed in our lives too. We did it, Jesus undid it!
 How does Joseph's story reflect Jesus' work in reversing the curse and bringing redemption? 
 
 Day 29: Jesus Took It All For You - Part 2
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
When life feels like a field of thorns, remember that Jesus wore the crown for you. When your efforts leave you sweating and exhausted, trust the One who sweat blood to redeem your life. Jesus entered the curse of sin to free you from it—He transformed the sword of judgment into an open door of grace. When death whispers fear, look to the empty tomb and hear Christ’s victory shout. Whatever pain or struggle you face, lay it at the cross, where Jesus turned the curse into a blessing, despair into hope, and death into life eternal.
 How can you trust Jesus with your struggles today, knowing He has already borne the curse and transformed it into grace and victory?
 
 Day 30: Fighting Sin
Training To Fight
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
Blessed be the Lord, my rock, who trains my hands for war, and my fingers for battle; He is my steadfast love and my fortress, my stronghold and my deliverer, my shield and He in whom I take refuge, who subdues peoples under me.
Psalm 144:1-2
Teaching
Overcoming sin requires our active participation, i.e., our active fighting against it! A soldier on the battlefield doesn't win by sitting in the barracks, but by stepping onto the field and engaging the enemy. In Psalm 144, David recognizes that God is his strength and trainer in the battles that he fought: “Praise be to the Lord my Rock, who trains my hands for war, my fingers for battle. He is my loving God and my fortress, my stronghold and my deliverer, my shield, in whom I take refuge” (Psalm 144:1-2). This battle imagery applies to our fight against sin.
On Psalm 144:1, Charles Spurgeon writes:

    David was not a man of war by choice; he viewed his military skills as a gift and calling from God. Even the ability to handle weapons was seen as a gracious act of divine instruction. How much more should we depend on God for our spiritual warfare, seeing that the enemy is subtle, and our own strength is small! - Charles Spurgeon The Treasury of David

Sin is not a minor issue; it is a deadly enemy that seeks to enslave and destroy us, like a shadowy thief that sneaks in to steal, kill, and destroy (John 10:10). God Himself equips us for this fight, offering His strength, His Word, His body (the church), and the power of His Spirit. It’s like a king arming his soldiers before battle—without His provision, we would be defenseless. “Finally, be strong in the Lord and in his mighty power. Put on the full armor of God, so that you can take your stand against the devil’s schemes” (Ephesians 6:10-11).
The gospel reminds us that Jesus has already won the decisive victory over sin through His death for our sins and His resurrection from the dead on the third day. In this war the outcome has already been determined (1 Corinthians 15:57), yet we must still hold our ground and resist enemy attacks (James 4:7). We fight not to earn salvation but as freed people fighting to stay free, like prisoners who have been released but must refuse to return to their chains (Galatians 5:1), relying on Christ as our "fortress" and "shield." “The Lord is my strength and my shield; my heart trusts in him, and he helps me” (Psalm 28:7).
John Piper writes:

    The victory of Christ over sin is the root of our joy. When we fight sin, we do so as those who are already more than conquerors because of His finished work. The fight is not a fight for victory but from victory. - John Piper, Desiring God

Do you take sin very seriously, as a matter of life and death? Are you actively fighting against sin daily? 

Day 30: Fighting Sin
Biblical Illustration: Paul
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider the life of Paul, who described his spiritual battle in Romans 7:15-25. Paul admitted struggling with sin: "For I do not do the good I want, but the evil I do not want is what I keep on doing."  But notice how he fought this battle with sin: "So I run with purpose in every step. I am not just shadowboxing. 27 I discipline my body like an athlete, training it to do what it should. Otherwise, I fear that after preaching to others I myself might be disqualified" (1 Corinthians 9:26-27).  Yet with all of his training and fighting, he ultimately rejoiced in the gospel: "Thanks be to God through Jesus Christ our Lord!" (Romans 7:25).
Paul’s life illustrates that fighting sin is a daily battle, but it’s not fought in our strength. He acknowledged his own weakness, saying, “For I do not do the good I want to do, but the evil I do not want to do—this I keep on doing” (Romans 7:19). Yet, he relied on the grace of Jesus Christ, who "delivers" us from sin’s power: “Thanks be to God, who delivers me through Jesus Christ our Lord!” (Romans 7:25).
Paul also trusted in the cross of Jesus Christ, which crucified his old self: “We know that our old self was crucified with him so that the body ruled by sin might be done away with, that we should no longer be slaves to sin” (Romans 6:6). This reliance on Christ’s finished work transforms the battle, reminding us that the victory over sin has already been secured through His death and resurrection. Now we receive His grace and are taught by it. “For the grace of God has appeared that offers salvation to all people. It teaches us to say ‘No’ to ungodliness and worldly passions and to live self-controlled, upright and godly lives in this present age” (Titus 2:11-12).
How does Paul’s struggle with sin encourage you in your own fight?

Day 30: Fighting Sin
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Nobody has ever overcome sin without a serious fight. Yes, sometimes God simply delivers us from evil, as we are taught to pray (Matthew 6:13). But more often, he teaches us how to fight, gives us a daily battle plan to win, and strengthens us for the battle.
Here are three things to do, practically, to win the battle over sin:
Train Hard: Spend time daily in God’s Word and prayer, learning to wield the "sword of the Spirit" (Ephesians 6:17). It’s like a soldier sharpening his weapon before battle, preparing for whatever comes his way. Let Scripture shape your thoughts and actions.
Fight Hard: Identify your specific struggles with sin, confess them to God (1 John 1:9), and take practical steps to avoid temptation (Matthew 26:41). This is just like a gardener pulling out weeds, ensuring they don’t take over the garden of your heart (Proverbs 4:23). Avoid situations that fuel sin (1 Corinthians 10:13), and surround yourself with the intake of the gospel—like filling your cup with clean, refreshing water to quench your soul’s thirst (John 4:14). Listen to sermons by Pastor Tim Keller, John Piper, etc., and read good books, especially by the Puritans; in doing so you are collecting wise counsel with which to build a sturdy wall against the enemy (Proverbs 15:22).
Rest in the Gospel: Remember that Christ’s victory is your victory (1 Corinthians 15:57). When you fall, repent quickly and trust in God’s grace to lift you (Psalm 37:24, 2 Corinthians 12:9), just like a child reaching for their parent’s hand after a stumble. Preach the gospel to yourself daily: Jesus lived, died, and rose for you (Romans 5:8, 1 Peter 2:24, Romans 6:9-10) so that sin no longer reigns in your life (Romans 6:14), like the dawn breaking over the darkness of night, bringing light and hope to your path (Psalm 119:105, John 8:12).
What specific sin are you currently fighting, and what steps can you take to "train" for the battle?
How can you remind yourself of the gospel when you feel defeated by sin?
