Day 31: Grace to Repent
God's Grace is Required for Repentance
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
Come, let us return to the LORD. He has torn us to pieces; now he will heal us. He has injured us; now he will bandage our wounds. 2 After two days He will revive us, on the third day he will restore us, that we may live in his presence. 
Hosea 6:1-2
After we fall into sin, several things happen to us: the power of our spiritual selves is weakened (Psalm 31:10), the strength of grace fades (Revelation 2:4-5), the evidence that we are saved is blurred (2 Peter 1:9), fears and doubts arise as we ask ourselves, "Will God pardon this scarlet sin once more and show mercy to one so undeserving?"
Additionally, corruptions in our heart get the upper hand and become more confirmed (Jeremiah 17:9), our conscience becomes numb (1 Timothy 4:2), and many other things. These effects remind us of our utter dependence on God's grace to restore us to humility and repentance, for "a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (Psalm 51:17).
There are some medicines that induce vomiting, which is a terrible experience but sometimes necessary to restore health or even save life. Repentance is like the vomiting of our soul; it's very difficult and yet necessary to restore us ongoingly (2 Corinthians 7:10). God's grace is the medicine that induces us to vomit out the poison of sin (Titus 2:11-12).
When this medicine of grace is ingested, it raises us out of sin (Ephesians 2:5-6) and fills us with godly sorrow (Psalm 51:17), as we can see in Mary Magdalene who loved much and wept much because she was forgiven much (Luke 7:47-48).
Similarly, the Israelites in the Book of Hosea said, "Come, let us return to the LORD. He has torn us to pieces; now he will heal us. He has injured us; now he will bandage our wounds. 2 After two days He will revive us, on the third day he will restore us, that we may live in his presence" (Hosea 6:1-2). It was confidence in God's mercy and love, that He would heal them, bind up their wounds, revive their dejected spirits, and cause them to live in His presence, that worked their hearts to repent and return to Him.
In God tearing His people to pieces (Hosea 6:1), healing them, injuring and bandaging them, and restoring them on the third day (Hosea 6:2), they became a beautiful picture of the gospel itself. Jesus was torn to pieces for our sins (Isaiah 53:5), He was injured, wounded, beaten, and bloodied (Matthew 27:26-30) as He took the wrath of God for us (Romans 5:9) and suffered under the curse of God in our place (Galatians 3:13). But on the third day, He was raised from the dead (1 Corinthians 15:4), restored to life (Acts 2:24), and He ascended to live in the presence of His Father forever (Hebrews 9:24).
If you are giving in to sin repeatedly, beg God for grace to discipline and restore you (Hebrews 12:6-7) so that you might return to God and live in His presence (James 4:8).
 What does Hosea 6:1-2 reveal about God's role in our restoration and repentance?
 
 Day 31: Grace to Repent
Biblical Illustration: The Samaritan Woman
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
In John 4:1-42, we read about a Samaritan woman who was living a life marked by sin and shame (John 4:18). Jesus sought her out, revealing her sin (Psalm 139:1-3) and offering her "living water" (John 4:10), symbolizing eternal life and the satisfaction only He can provide (John 7:37-38).
His grace and revelation awakened her heart (Ephesians 2:8), leading her not only to repentance (Acts 3:19) but also to share the good news about Jesus with her entire village (John 4:28-30). This demonstrates that it was Christ’s initiative (Luke 19:10) and restoration that enabled her transformation, showing His power to redeem, and use even those society deems unworthy, for His purposes.
 How does Jesus’ interaction with the Samaritan woman illustrate His power to redeem and transform lives for His purposes?
 
 Day 31: Grace to Repent
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
The Bible teaches us that repentance is a gift granted by God, not a work of human effort but a response to His initiating grace. This understanding shapes how we approach our relationship with God, the way we view others, and how we live out the transformative power of His grace.

    Acts 11:18
    "When they heard this, they had no further objections and praised God, saying, 'So then, even to Gentiles God has granted repentance that leads to life.'” Repentance is explicitly described as a gift granted by God.

    2 Timothy 2:25
    "Opponents must be gently instructed, in the hope that God will grant them repentance leading them to a knowledge of the truth." This verse emphasizes that repentance is something God grants, an act of His grace.

    Romans 2:4
    "Or do you show contempt for the riches of his kindness, forbearance and patience, not realizing that God’s kindness is intended to lead you to repentance?" God's kindness, a manifestation of His grace, leads us to repentance.

    Titus 2:11-12
    "For the grace of God has appeared that offers salvation to all people. It teaches us to say 'No' to ungodliness and worldly passions, and to live self-controlled, upright and godly lives in this present age." The grace of God actively teaches and enables believers to turn from sin.

These Scriptures collectively demonstrate that repentance is not merely a human effort but a response to God's initiating grace. 
Application:

    Approach God with a heart of humility, acknowledging that every step you take toward righteousness is evidence of His grace to you. 
    Practice gentleness and reliance on God when engaging with those who resist the truth, trusting Him to work in their hearts. 
    Reflect on God’s kindness and respond with a willingness to grow and change.
    Actively pursue a self-controlled, upright, and godly life, relying on God’s grace to guide and strengthen you.

In embracing these truths, we align ourselves with God’s purpose, living as testimonies of His grace and bringing glory to Him through lives transformed by repentance.
How can recognizing repentance as a gift from God change the way you approach your relationship with Him and others?

Day 32: Actively Looking for Jesus
Searching for Jesus
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago

You search the Scriptures because you think they give you eternal life. But the Scriptures point to me!
John 5:39 NLT
There is one certain way to find freedom from habitual sin: search for, find, and fall in love with, the Person and work of Jesus Christ in the Scriptures. It’s like discovering a hidden treasure buried in a field, waiting to transform your heart. “The kingdom of heaven is like treasure hidden in a field. When a man found it, he hid it again, and then in his joy went and sold all he had and bought that field” (Matthew 13:44).
In John 5:39, Jesus spoke to people who searched the Old Testament Scriptures and sought to know the truth of God's Word but did not realize that all the Scriptures pointed to Jesus. “You study the Scriptures diligently because you think that in them you have eternal life. These are the very Scriptures that testify about me” (John 5:39). They were reading blindly like travelers holding a map but missing the destination it clearly marked.

    The Bible is not about you—it’s about Him. It’s not primarily a book of principles for how to live or a set of stories to inspire us. It’s a story about how God saves us through Jesus Christ. - Preaching: Communicating Faith in an Age of Skepticism. Tim Keller

Pastor Keller reminds us that the Scriptures must always be viewed as pointing to Christ, not as ends in themselves.
Everywhere we read, we should be looking for Jesus and should not rest until we find Him in the passage we are reading. “And beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he explained to them what was said in all the Scriptures concerning himself” (Luke 24:27). If we will read this way, our hearts will be loved over and over by the Holy Spirit. The Spirit’s role is to show Christ to us, so we can know Him (John 16:14), and to fill our hearts with His love (Romans 5:5). 
Have you been finding Jesus Christ in the Old Testament Scriptures?


Day 32: Actively Looking for Jesus
Biblical Illustration: An Ox Under a Heavy Load
Awaiting feedback
When reading their Old Testament Scriptures, the Pharisees did not see Jesus, and so they missed the point. It’s like reading a treasure map but never seeing the “X” that marks the spot. For example, when they read Leviticus chapter 1, they saw that animals were required for sacrifice. This taught them many things such as the holiness of God who demanded death for sin, and the mercy of God who allowed a substitute to die in the place of the guilty one. But they missed the main thing.
The main thing to see was that Jesus is the sacrificial Lamb who fulfilled all of the sacrifices. John the Baptist declared, "Look, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!" (John 1:29). The Pharisees missed this connection, and because of it, they remained in bondage to sin. Yet it is seeing this "main thing"—the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ—over and over, that sets us free. Jesus Himself said, "So if the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed" (John 8:36).
Seeing the love of Jesus poured out for us on the cross sets our hearts free—from blinding lust, from indulging greed, from bitterness and unforgiveness, from every kind of sin.
So, here’s how they should have read Leviticus 1:
Leviticus 1 introduces the burnt offering, symbolizing total surrender to God. This offering was completely consumed by fire, reminding us of Jesus’ complete surrender to the Father’s will. He offered Himself fully, holding nothing back, as He endured the full wrath of God for our sins. Hebrews 10:7-10 speaks of this total surrender: "Then I said, ‘Here I am—it is written about me in the scroll—I have come to do your will, my God.’" His life and death were a perfect, fragrant offering to God. "Christ loved us and gave himself up for us as a fragrant offering and sacrifice to God" (Ephesians 5:2).
The peace offering celebrated fellowship with God. Its portion was shared between the altar, the priest, and the worshiper, symbolizing peace and communion. Jesus, through His sacrifice, made peace between us and God. "For God was pleased to have all his fullness dwell in him, and through him to reconcile to himself all things…by making peace through his blood, shed on the cross" (Colossians 1:19-20). Because of Jesus, we are no longer enemies of God but invited into eternal fellowship and oneness with Him.
The sin offering and the guilt offering addressed specific transgressions and made atonement for sins. Jesus, the spotless Lamb of God, bore the full weight of our sins on the cross, taking our guilt and shame so that we might stand righteous before God. "God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God" (2 Corinthians 5:21).
Together, these offerings reveal the multifaceted beauty of Christ’s atonement. He surrendered fully, bringing us peace, bearing our guilt, and making us clean. Unlike the repeated sacrifices of old, Jesus’ work was once for all. "But when this priest had offered for all time one sacrifice for sins, he sat down at the right hand of God" (Hebrews 10:12).
The sacrifices of Leviticus weren’t just about ritual or tradition—they were a foreshadowing of the perfect, once-for-all sacrifice of Jesus Christ. If we read the Scriptures with this in mind, we will see Jesus in every offering (Leviticus 1:3-4), every law (Matthew 5:17), and every promise (2 Corinthians 1:20). We will see the cross of Christ, where all power and freedom flow from (1 Corinthians 1:18), and we will be changed by it (2 Corinthians 3:18). This is the way to freedom (John 8:36); it is to have a superior affection in our hearts for Jesus Christ (Philippians 3:8)!

We should rejoice in the completeness of His sacrifice! By faith in Jesus, we are fully forgiven, eternally at peace with God, and made new for His glory. Oh, praise Him with eyes open to see the cross, and hearts full of the love of God!


Day 32: Actively Looking for Jesus
Practical Application

When reading the Old Testament, look for Jesus in every passage. As you study the book of Leviticus, for example, ask how each offering points to Christ’s finished work on the cross. Recognize that Jesus is the ultimate sacrifice who fully surrendered, made peace, and bore your guilt. Meditate on how His once-for-all atonement brings freedom from sin’s power. Apply this truth by seeing your sin nailed to the cross. As you reflect on Jesus’ love poured out for you, let it fuel your daily surrender to Him. Read Scripture with a gospel focus, finding freedom and joy in Christ!
Read Exodus 23:1-5, and meditate on vs. 5. Where do you see the work of Jesus on the cross here?

Day 33: Exposing Tricks of Satan
Fleshly Confidence
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
There is a particular trick that Satan uses to lead people into sin, and that is to make us bold enough to take risks with situations that could lead us to sin (Genesis 3:1-5).
Satan tells you that you can walk by the prostitute's door, just don’t sin with her (Proverbs 7:6-10). It’s okay to sit down with those getting drunk, just don’t get drunk with them (Proverbs 23:29-35). You can admire Jezebel’s beauty (2 Kings 9:30), and you can flirt and play with Delilah, just don’t give in to them (Judges 16:4-19). You can look at and touch that gold bar like Achan did, just don’t steal it (Joshua 7:20-21), and you can tell a half-truth like Ananias and Sapphira, as long as you stick to your story (Acts 5:1-4).
This is a trap that many fall into, as I did in my past; it is called living by “fleshly confidence” (Jeremiah 17:5) rather than walking by the Spirit (Galatians 5:16).
What is the remedy for this fleshly confidence?
First, look often and long at the cross of Christ! Look at Jesus' body broken for you, and worship, knowing that He was broken on the cross to break the power of sin in your life (Romans 6:6-7), and to heal you from sin (Isaiah 53:5). 
Think often how Jesus came to save us from our sins (Matthew 1:21). He came to crucify our old selves that we might die to sin (Romans 6:6). And He rose from the dead in power that we might walk in newness of life (Romans 6:4).
Second, seriously consider the Scriptures that command us to avoid even the slightest appearance of evil. "Abstain from all appearance of evil" (1 Thessalonians 5:22). "Do not be wise in your own eyes; fear the Lord and shun evil" (Proverbs 3:7). 
Third, ask God for a true hatred of everything to do with sin "...save others by snatching them from the fire; to others show mercy, mixed with fear—hating even the clothing stained by corrupted flesh" (Jude 23). God commands us "Do not touch what is unclean, and I will receive you" (2 Corinthians 6:17).
The reality is we must learn to hate and avoid not only big and public and gross sins (adultery, murder, etc.) but also everything that carries even a hint or suspicion of sin. "Keep to a path far from her, do not go near the door of her house" (Proverbs 5:8). "Do not give the devil a foothold" (Ephesians 4:27).
Fourth, always have Scripture ready to combat temptations. 

    To Satan's lie that you can go near sin as long as you don't sin, use this passage: "can a man scoop fire into his lap without his clothes being burned? Can a man walk on hot coals without his feet being scorched?" (Proverbs 6:27-28). 
    To the lie that you can sit with those who are sinning, remember "Do not be deceived: 'Bad company ruins good morals'" (1 Corinthians 15:33). 
    To the lie that you can admire Jezebel's beauty, remember that "charm is deceptive, and beauty is fleeting" (Proverbs 31:30). 
    To the lie that you can flirt and play with Delilah, just don't give in to her temptations, remember "For the lips of an adulterous woman drip honey, and her speech is smoother than oil; but in the end she is bitter as gall, sharp as a double-edged sword" (Proverbs 5:3-4). 
    To the lie that you can look at and touch that gold bar like Achan did, just don't steal it, quote to yourself, "For the love of money is a root of all kinds of evil" (1 Timothy 6:10). 
    To Satan's whispers that you can tell a half-truth like Ananias and Sapphira, as long as you don't lie, use this passage: "Do not lie to each other, since you have taken off your old self with its practices" (Colossians 3:9).

What is one area of your life to which you can apply this truth today?

Day 33: Exposing Tricks of Satan
Biblical Illustration: the Naive Young Man
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Think through the progression of the "naive young man" in Proverbs 7, who first was "crossing the street near the house of an immoral woman, strolling down the path by her house" (Proverbs 7:8), then listened to her seductive words, and finally "followed her at once, like an ox going to the slaughter" (Proverbs 7:22). 
He only intended to go "near" and stroll "by," and he ended up caught in the trap of Satan and headed for death! "He was like a stag caught in a trap, awaiting the arrow that would pierce its heart. He was like a bird flying into a snare, little knowing it would cost him his life" (Proverbs 7:22-23).
Meditate on this progression of how sin works and compare it with the progression in James 1:14-15. Share your thoughts.

Day 33: Exposing Tricks of Satan
Practical Application
We are warned in Scripture, "Let anyone who thinks he stands take heed lest he fall" (1 Corinthians 10:12). Remember, the safest path is one far from sin’s doorstep, where we continually walk in the Spirit and not according to the flesh.
Maybe the better part of wisdom is for us to avoid all-you-can-eat buffets or movies that stir up fleshly desires, or substances that remove inhibitions. If you don't want to be burned, don't go near the fire. If you don't want to hear the bell, don't fiddle with the rope. Usually, we cannot conquer sins unless we avoid situations that lead to them. 
"Do not set foot on the path of the wicked or walk in the way of evildoers. 15 Avoid it, do not travel on it; turn from it and go on your way" (Proverbs 4:14-15).
Notice how Joseph did this exact thing when Potiphar's wife came after him: "And though she spoke to Joseph day after day, he refused to go to bed with her or even be with her" (Genesis 39:10). Compare what David said in Psalms 26:4-5.

    It's fair and just for God to let someone fall into a pit if they insist on dancing on the edge of it, and for someone to become a slave to sin if they won't avoid the situations that lead to sin. - Thomas Brooks Precious Remedies Against Satan's Devices*

Here is a space for you to pray about any ways you've been "going near" the door of sin and ask God for a plan to steer clear.

Day 34: Our Advocate
The Advocate For Sinners
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage:
My dear children, I write this to you so that you will not sin. But if anybody does sin, we have an advocate with the Father—Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours but also for the sins of the whole world.
1 John 2:1-2
1. The Problem of Sin
Sin is humanity's universal problem—a spiritual disease that infects every person without exception. It's your worst problem, and it's mine, just like a ship taking on water is every sailor’s worst nightmare. The Bible, including John's writings, has two specific purposes: first, to teach us how not to sin (Psalm 119:11), and second, to show us the sacrifice for our sins (1 John 2:1-2).
First, the Bible has a goal which the Apostle John states clearly: "I write this to you so that you will not sin" (1 John 2:1). The Bible teaches us about ourselves, our natural state (Ephesians 2:1-4), our deceptive and wicked hearts (Jeremiah 17:9), the pull of our flesh (Galatians 5:17). The Bible teaches us about Satan, the deceiver, and accuser (Revelation 12:10). It shows us his tricks, his schemes, his intentions, and his methods (2 Corinthians 2:11) so that we can be on guard against him (1 Peter 5:8). It teaches us about the world that is under the leadership of Satan (1 John 5:19), and how it seeks to draw us after the devil (James 4:4).
So, the Bible exposes all the problems of Self and of Satan and the world we live in, showing us how to navigate them all while avoiding sin, clothed in the armor of God (Ephesians 6:11-12). We will continue studying these things throughout the rest of this course.
Though John, and the Bible, writes to encourage believers to avoid sin, he acknowledges that sin is inevitable (1 John 1:8). Sin separates us from God (Isaiah 59:2) and brings guilt and shame (Genesis 3:7-10). Sin first deceives us and then it enslaves us (John 8:34); its purpose is to destroy us (James 1:14-15).
However, God, in His mercy, has provided a solution (Romans 6:23; John 3:16).
2. The Provision of an Advocate
When we sin, we are not left to rescue and ransom ourselves (Psalm 49:7-8) or defend ourselves before God. Jesus Christ stands as both our atoning sacrifice (1 John 2:2) and our Advocate—a legal representative who pleads on our behalf (Hebrews 7:25). He is uniquely qualified for this role because He is the Righteous One (Acts 3:14; 1 Peter 3:18). Unlike us, He lived a sinless life (Hebrews 4:15) and now intercedes for us before the Father (Romans 8:34; 1 Timothy 2:5).
3. The Propitiation for Our Sins
Jesus is not only our Advocate but also the atoning sacrifice (propitiation) for our sins. His death on the cross satisfied God's righteous wrath against sin. 
This is how Jesus Christ "propitiated," or removed God's wrath from us (1 John 4:10). Jesus Christ is our Propitiation (Romans 3:25)!
When you feel burdened by guilt, remember that Jesus has already paid the price for your sins. You don’t need to carry the weight of condemnation. Christ’s atonement covers you fully. His advocacy assures you that His death in your place carries weight with the Father, as Jesus advocates for you.
What does it mean that Jesus is the propitiation for our sins?
A) He simply overlooks sin, out of love, without any cost
B) He paid the penalty for sin and quenched the fury of God's wrathCorrect
C) He removes all consequences of our sin
How can you live in the freedom of knowing your sins are fully atoned for?

Day 34: Our Advocate
Biblical Illustration: Haman
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
In the Old Testament, Haman was a man who hated the Jews and plotted their destruction (Esther 3:5-6). He built a pole on which he sought to impale a much-loved Jew named Mordecai (Esther 5:14), and much like Hitler who would come after him, he sought to annihilate the entire Jewish race (Esther 3:13).
But the king at the time, Xerxes, was married to a Jewish lady named Esther (Esther 2:17), and when Haman's plot to extinguish the Jews was revealed, King Xerxes became furious (Esther 7:5-7)! He was so angry that he had Haman impaled on the same pole that Haman had built for Mordecai (Esther 7:9-10). Only then, after Haman was hung up on the pole, did the wrath of the king subside: "So they impaled Haman on the pole he had set up for Mordecai. Then the king’s fury subsided" (Esther 7:10).
Did you notice that when the man on the pole died the wrath of the king subsided?
Jesus Christ was nothing at all like Haman; indeed, He was the complete opposite—holy, spotless, undefiled, perfect in all His ways (Hebrews 7:26; 1 Peter 2:22). And yet when God put your sin and my sin on Him (Isaiah 53:6; 2 Corinthians 5:21), He was hung up on a cross-like pole (Galatians 3:13), and when that happened, God's wrath against us subsided (Romans 5:9; 1 Thessalonians 1:10). Jesus offered Himself as the perfect, final sacrifice (John 1:29; Hebrews 7:27). As He hung on the cross as the ultimate sacrifice (Hebrews 10:12; John 19:30), He had you on His heart (Isaiah 53:11; John 17:20-21)!
Through His once-for-all sacrifice, Jesus secured eternal redemption for us (Hebrews 9:24-26).
How does the story of Haman illustrate the way Jesus’ sacrifice on the cross satisfied God’s wrath and demonstrated His love?

Day 34: Our Advocate
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
We must learn how not to sin (Psalm 119:11; 1 John 2:1). A Christian is not just being "at rest" in Jesus' finished work (Hebrews 4:10), a Christian is actively "at war" against our lusts and our bent toward sin (Galatians 5:16-17; 1 Peter 2:11). 
We must learn the subtleties and the methods of Satan (2 Corinthians 2:11), and how our flesh wants to respond to him (James 1:14). We must learn his tactics and his schemes (Ephesians 6:11). We must learn how to access and utilize the provisions God has given us in His promises (2 Peter 1:3-4) and apply the power of the gospel daily (Romans 1:16).
But we must also not forget that when we do sin, we have an Advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the Righteous One (1 John 2:1-2), who propitiated God's wrath (Romans 3:25), like Hamaan's death assuaged the wrath of the king! Jesus died to remove God's wrath from us by taking it on Himself (Isaiah 53:4-5; 2 Corinthians 5:21), thereby making us acceptable to God (Romans 5:1-2; Ephesians 1:6-7).
Learn how to fight (1 Timothy 6:12), and by faith rest in Jesus' work for you (Hebrews 4:3)!
How can you actively fight against sin while resting in Jesus' finished work?

Day 35: Essentials - Church
Must Attend and Be Involved
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Passage
So when you are assembled and I am with you in spirit, and the power of our Lord Jesus is present, hand this man over to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, so that his spirit may be saved on the day of the Lord.
1 Corinthians 5:4-5
Teaching
There are some things that God has made non-optional for the Christian, and essential for spiritual growth, one of them is church attendance (Hebrews 10:25). 
I have pastored three churches over 15 years and during this time I've heard every imaginable excuse as to why people are not attending church. "My work keeps me from it," "I live too far away," "I don't agree with their theology," and on and on go the "plausible reasons," a.k.a. excuses, for why people don't attend church (Luke 14:16-20). But the gospel compels us to die to ourselves—just as Christ died for us—and live for Him (2 Corinthians 5:15). Our time, our pride, and our preferences, all take a back seat to His glory.
Most of the people using these excuses profess faith in Christ (James 2:17). Actually, most of them profess to understand the Bible better than the people who attend church. Why this pride? (Proverbs 16:18) Because there is something that happens in a real church that goes beyond knowledge and has the direct effect of knocking down our pride (1 Peter 5:5). When we understand what Christ did for us, we realize that He humbled Himself to the point of death, even death on a cross (Philippians 2:8), so how can we, then, allow pride to keep us from joining with others in worship?
Notice this Scripture where Paul is explaining what to do with a man who is sinning openly in the church: "So when you are assembled and I am with you in spirit, and the power of our Lord Jesus is present, hand this man over to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, so that his spirit may be saved on the day of the Lord" (1 Corinthians 5:4-5). 
If I were to ask you a simple question from this passage, "What happens when the church is assembled?" what would you answer? It's obvious: "The power of our Lord Jesus is present" (Matthew 18:20). There is a certain power that Jesus Christ reserves for those who assemble together, that goes beyond mere knowledge, emotion, or feelings. It is true spiritual power that manifests itself only in the gathering of the church (Acts 2:42-47). The power that raised Christ from the dead is at work among His people as they gather in His name.
This is why true believers love to assemble to hear the preaching and take part in the praying and praising. We simply don't want to leave! And one of my favorite promises in the Bible assures us that some day we won't have to: "The one who is victorious I will make a pillar in the temple of my God. Never again will they leave it" (Revelation 3:12). As opposed to some who never attend it, true Christians never want to leave it.
It is one thing to stay home and read our Bibles (2 Timothy 3:16-17), it is quite another to go to church with the desire to be changed (Hebrews 10:24-25). Reading our Bibles is beneficial, we do get knowledge, but knowledge alone can puff us up (1 Corinthians 8:1), but in the attendance is where we get grace (James 4:6), humility (Philippians 2:3-4), love (John 13:34-35), ability to serve others (1 Peter 4:10), and true spiritual growth (Ephesians 4:15-16). 
People who write into Setting Captives Free often articulate their struggles, sin-bondage, etc., and ask for help. One of the first questions our help desk asks them is, "Have you sought the counsel of your pastor or elders at church?" How do you think most of them answer? You're right if you said they respond, "I don't have a church." But the gospel is about community (John 10:16)—Jesus, in His final moments on earth, prayed for unity among believers (John 17:21). We can’t separate ourselves from the body of Christ without robbing ourselves of this unity and growth.
But it is the subtle pride, not the open and flagrant sin, that sneaks into people's lives who don't attend church (Proverbs 18:1). They most likely have convinced themselves that they understand Scripture better than their local church (Proverbs 12:15), therefore they don't agree with what is preached, and so they don't attend. Most of these people have found another preacher online who has taught them a lot, and they stay home and watch him instead of attending church. 
If you listen to these people for long, you will hear them repeat the same Scriptures, the same theology, the same understanding that they've had for years. What does this tell you? They aren't growing, they aren't changing, they aren't humbling themselves to go to church so they aren't being equipped for gospel ministry, and are not truly building up the body of Christ (Ephesians 4:11-16). In essence, they are trying to separate the Head (Christ) from the Body (the church) (Colossians 1:18; 1 Corinthians 12:12-14). Christ’s body, the church, exists to build each other up. His death and resurrection give us a new life that we live out together, in unity, with a humility that’s only possible because of His sacrifice for us (Romans 12:4-5).
How does regular church attendance help you grow spiritually and guard against pride?

    “There is no worship of God that is better than the hearing of a sermon.”  — Charles Spurgeon, quoted on X
    
 Day 35: Essentials - Church
Biblical Illustration: King Uzziah
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider King Uzziah (2 Chronicles 26). Uzziah started his reign well, seeking God and relying on the wisdom of the prophet Zechariah. As long as he sought the Lord, he prospered. But as Uzziah grew powerful, pride crept into his heart (2 Chronicles 26:16). He began to think he could bypass God's established order of worship. Instead of humbling himself and submitting to the priests as God commanded, he marched into the temple to burn incense (2 Chronicles 26:16-18) — a duty reserved only for the priests.
The result? God struck him with leprosy, and Uzziah lived in isolation until his death, cut off from the house of the Lord (2 Chronicles 26:20-21). His pride not only led to his downfall but also caused him to be separated from the very place where God’s presence dwelled.
Uzziah’s story mirrors what happens when believers isolate themselves from the church today. Pride leads them to think they don’t need the church or that they know better than those God has placed in leadership. But just like Uzziah, pride leads to separation, isolation, and spiritual decay. God’s power and presence are found in the assembly of His people, not in solitary pride.
What does King Uzziah's downfall teach us about the dangers of pride and disobedience?

Day 35: Essentials - Church
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Uzziah’s downfall shows that pride blinds us to our need for God's design: the local church. Ask yourself, “Have I been avoiding church for personal reasons?” Perhaps you think you're too busy, or you don’t agree with everything taught. 
God calls us to humble ourselves, submit to spiritual leaders, and prioritize gathering with His people (Hebrews 13:17, Ephesians 4:11-13). Spiritual growth happens in community, not in isolation. Don’t let pride keep you from the power and presence of Christ found in the local church. Go with a heart ready to listen, ready to be changed, ready to serve, and be transformed by His grace. 
There are no perfect churches, but only churches where God has designed for His people to gather and exercise their spiritual gifts for the building up of His people. 
Can you identify any personal reasons or pride that might be keeping you from fully engaging with your local church?

Day 36: The Kingdom of God is Power
Power For Real Change
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Key Verse
For the kingdom of God is not a matter of talk but of power. 
1 Corinthians 4:20
In a world filled with opinions, debates, and endless streams of information, words can lose their impact. People talk about change, hope, and goodness, yet information is not transformation.
Here’s your text with additional metaphors woven in:
The Apostle Paul, in his letter to the Corinthians, draws a stark line between mere talk and true power (as does James in James 1:22). He reminds us that the kingdom of God is not about eloquent speeches or intellectual knowledge. It’s about the life-changing power of God working in and through believers, rooted in the gospel of Jesus Christ—His death on the cross for our sins and His resurrection from the dead (Romans 1:16). This power is what sets captives free from sin, wrong thinking, and destructive habits. It’s like a key that unlocks prison doors; mere words can describe freedom, but only the power of the gospel can set a prisoner free.
12-step groups and worldly, humanistic, man-centered teaching can give us knowledge, but the kingdom of God is not mere knowledge, not mere talk—it is spiritual power! It’s like thunder compared to an echo; one shakes the ground, the other merely repeats the sound. It's compared to, and illustrated by, the power it took to create the world, as stated in 2 Corinthians 4:6-7:
"For God, who said, 'Let light shine out of darkness,' made his light shine in our hearts to give us the light of the knowledge of God’s glory displayed in the face of Christ. But we have this treasure in jars of clay to show that this all-surpassing power is from God and not from us."
It took power to create the world, but we have "all-surpassing power" that works in us!
It’s easy to fall into the trap of knowing theology, discussing doctrine, or even leading Bible studies without experiencing real transformation. But the essence of the kingdom of God is not about how well we can articulate our beliefs to each other but how powerfully God’s Spirit changes our lives through the gospel’s message (2 Timothy 3:4-5). Without this power, it's like polishing the outside of a rusty car—the appearance might improve, but the underlying issues remain. Real change happens from the inside out.
Jesus Christ died for our sins, was buried, and rose again on the third day (1 Corinthians 15:3-4). This truth is the very power that enables us to live differently, break free from sin traps, change from the inside out, and walk in freedom.
Many people remain trapped in patterns of sin such as bondage to substances, pornography, bitterness, or unforgiveness. These are chains that cannot be broken by knowledge, self-discipline, or willpower. The power to overcome them comes from the gospel and the work of the Holy Spirit. "For we know, brothers and sisters loved by God, that he has chosen you, 5 because our gospel came to you not simply with words but also with power, with the Holy Spirit and deep conviction" (1 Thessalonians 1:4-5).
This power is the same that raised Christ from the dead (Ephesians 1:19-20). It’s the power that enables us to overcome sin (Romans 6:4), forgive others (Colossians 3:13), love our enemies (Matthew 5:44), and live selflessly (Philippians 2:3-4). Without this power, our faith becomes mere talk, and our lives remain unchanged. True Christianity manifests in a transformed heart and a new way of living that reflects the gospel’s transformative message (Galatians 2:20).
What distinguishes mere knowledge of the gospel from experiencing its transformative power in daily life?

Day 36: The Kingdom of God is Power
Biblical Illustration: Mary Magdalene
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
Consider Mary Magdalene. Before encountering Jesus, she was possessed by seven demons (Luke 8:2). Her life was one of torment and bondage, a stark picture of humanity’s enslavement to sin (Ephesians 2:1-3). But when she met Jesus, everything changed. Jesus cast out the demons, freeing her from her bondage, and she became one of His most devoted followers (Mark 16:9).
Mary was present at the crucifixion (John 19:25) and was the first to witness the resurrected Christ (John 20:11-18). Her transformation was not just about being healed; it was about being empowered by the reality of Jesus’ victory over sin and death (Romans 6:9-11). She went from a life of despair to becoming a witness of the gospel, proclaiming the risen Christ to the disciples (Matthew 28:1-10). Her story is one of true freedom—a captive set free by the power of the gospel.
Mary’s life exemplifies the kingdom of God’s power. Her encounter with the risen Lord gave her a new identity and purpose (2 Corinthians 5:17). Her transformation was a testimony of the power of Christ’s death and resurrection working within her, turning a once broken life into a powerful witness for the gospel (Matthew 28:7). Today, many people are similarly bound by spiritual chains—addictions, anger, anxiety, or bitterness. But just as Jesus freed Mary, He can free us when we look at the cross, receive Jesus' love, rely on His power, and implement a battle plan that He gives to us, personally.
 How does Mary Magdalene’s transformation illustrate the power of the gospel to bring true freedom?
 
 Day 36: The Kingdom of God is Power
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 5 months ago
The same power that transformed Mary Magdalene is available to us today through the gospel (Romans 8:11). But how do we access it?

    Believe in the Gospel – The power to live differently starts with faith in Jesus Christ. Believe that His death paid for your sins and that His resurrection gives you new life (John 3:16). Only the gospel can break the chains of sin.
    Seek God through Prayer – Power comes through connection with God. Spend time in prayer, asking the Holy Spirit to fill you and empower you to live out your faith (Luke 11:13; Philippians 4:6-7). Pray specifically for freedom from sinful patterns and wrong thinking.
    Apply God’s Word – Don’t just read the Bible; live it (James 1:22-25). When you read about forgiveness, actively receive it and actively forgive others. When you learn about generosity, receive Jesus' generosity in making Himself poor to make you rich (2 Corinthians 8:9), and practice giving. The power is in the doing (Matthew 7:24). God’s Word is a tool to renew our minds and change our behavior (Romans 12:2).
    Rely on the Holy Spirit – Stop trying to live the Christian life in your own strength. Instead, surrender to the Spirit’s leading (Galatians 5:16). He empowers us to do what we cannot do on our own (Acts 1:8). The Spirit is the one who breaks chains and sets captives free (Isaiah 61:1).
    Attend a gospel preaching church regularly - (Hebrews 10:24-25). Turn from all excuses (Luke 14:18-20) and go to church to be changed (2 Corinthians 3:18) and to serve others (Galatians 5:13).

The kingdom of God is not a matter of talk but of power—power rooted in the gospel of Jesus Christ. Let’s move beyond words and embrace the transformative power of God’s kingdom in our lives. By relying on Jesus’ death and resurrection, we can walk out of sin traps and experience true freedom. As followers of Christ, we are called to proclaim this message of freedom to others, helping to set captives free by pointing them to the One who breaks every chain (John 8:36).
How can you actively rely on the Holy Spirit this week to experience freedom and transformation in a specific area of your life?

Day 37: Lies = Slavery
Locked Up In Lies
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
You belong to your father, the devil, and you want to carry out your father's desires. He was a murderer from the beginning, not holding to the truth, for there is no truth in him. When he lies, he speaks his native language, for he is a liar and the father of lies.
John 8:44
Teaching
In John 8:44, Jesus speaks to the religious leaders who reject His message, exposing a harsh reality that lies enslave us. Satan is called the father of lies, and when we believe his deceptions, we walk the path of captivity. 
Lies distort God's truth (Romans 1:25), twist our perceptions, blind our minds (2 Corinthians 4:4), and lead us into sin (James 1:14-15). It reminds me of a carnival mirror that bends and warps our reflection. What we see isn’t reality, but if we trust that distorted image, we can live a life of illusion.
Satan's goal is to entangle us in falsehoods that corrupt our thoughts and behaviors, making us prisoners of sin (2 Timothy 2:26). He is like a fisherman casting a line — the bait is a lie, and once a fish swims toward it, then takes the bait, it is trapped, unable to free itself, and dies.
Sin begins with a lie: "You need this to be happy," "God will forgive you," "You're under grace, not law", and finally, "You're beyond redemption." Lies like these, seep into our minds like water dripping onto stone, slowly carving out channels where the truth once resided. They take root in our hearts. When we believe them, we act on them. The result? Spiritual bondage.
Proverbs 14:12 warns us: "There is a way that appears to be right, but in the end it leads to death." It's like following a trail through the woods that looks promising, only to realize it leads to a cliff’s edge.
The antidote to lies is truth (Ephesians 6:14), and truth comes from God's Word (John 17:17), as it is focused on the gospel of Jesus Christ. Just as sunlight dispels darkness, the light of God's truth exposes the lies, showing us the way to freedom and life.
Reflection Questions:
What lies have you believed about myself, God, or others?

Day 37: Lies = Slavery
Biblical Illustration: Eve
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
The first recorded lie in Scripture comes from Satan in the Garden of Eden. In Genesis 3, the serpent deceives Eve by questioning God's command: "Did God really say...?" He twists the truth, making her doubt God's goodness and trustworthiness, and promises her life instead of death "You will not surely die" (Genesis 3:4).
Eve believed the lie that God was withholding something good from her. She took the forbidden fruit, thinking it would make her wise like God. But instead of finding freedom, she and Adam became captives of sin. The lie led to spiritual death, separation from God, and conflict within their relationship.
We see that Satan's tactics haven't changed. He still whispers doubts and half-truths, trying to lure us away from God’s Word and into spiritual captivity. 
Look at how our culture, worldwide, has become a culture of lies. The "Father of Lies" has taught that truth is subjective (I have "my truth" you have "your truth"), there are many ways to salvation, sexual perversity is "an alternative lifestyle", men can become women and women can become men, we should "accept ourselves as we are" (body positivity), murdering an unborn child is a woman's right over her own body, and the lies go on and on. This is because Satan is "the god of this world" (2 Corinthians 4:4) and "the ruler of this world" (John 12:31) and has blinded the minds of unbelievers to the gospel.
What lies did Eve believe in the Garden?
How did believing those lies affect her relationship with God?

To break free from sin’s captivity, we must confront the lies we've believed with the truth of God's Word. Jesus said, "Then you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free" (John 8:32). The key to freedom is not willpower or self-help, but truth.
We combat lies by renewing our minds with Scripture. When Satan whispers, "You' fell again, you're no longer acceptable" we respond with Romans 8:1: "There is now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus." When we feel unworthy of God's love, we hold to 1 John 3:1: "See what great love the Father has lavished on us, that we should be called children of God!"
Let God’s truth be your anchor, let the gospel dispel all lies, and you will find freedom from the chains of sin. The truth will set you free.

Day 38: Counteracting Lies
God's Word Counteracts Lies
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Today, I wanted to get us thinking about how to counteract the lies of our culture, of the devil, and of the world we live in that is in bondage to lies. So, I am listing below some lies that the world has fully embraced and then giving the biblical truth that sets us free.
Maybe you've believed some of these as I have, and ended up in captivity because of it. We need to recognize lies for what they are and replace them with the truth.
Lie #1: "Follow your heart. You are your own guide."
The world says: Truth comes from within. Trust your emotions, desires, and instincts.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“The heart is deceitful above all things and desperately wicked; who can understand it?” — Jeremiah 17:9
“Trust in the Lord with all your heart and lean not on your own understanding.” — Proverbs 3:5-6
💡 Your old heart isn't a compass; it's confused and easily deceived. Instead, follow God's Word, which never leads astray (Psalm 119:105; John 17:17).
Lie #2: "Your worth is based on your accomplishments and appearance."
The world says: You need to be successful, attractive, and impressive to have value.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“God shows His love for us in that while we were still sinners, Christ died for us.” — Romans 5:8
“For you formed my inward parts; you knitted me together in my mother's womb.” — Psalm 139:13-14
💡 Your worth is found in being made by God (Genesis 1:27; Psalm 139:13-14) and redeemed by Christ (1 Peter 1:18-19; Ephesians 1:7), not in worldly achievements (Philippians 3:7-8) or fading outward beauty (Proverbs 31:30).
Lie #3: "You only live once, so live for yourself."
The world says: Life is short. Pursue pleasure and live for the moment - be immoral, gorge yourself on food, get drunk, tell lies to get ahead, etcetera, etcetera. 
📖 Biblical Truth:
“For what does it profit a man to gain the whole world and forfeit his soul?” — Mark 8:36
“Whoever finds his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for My sake will find it.” — Matthew 10:39
💡 Life isn't about indulging yourself (Galatians 5:13; 1 John 2:15-17) but laying down your life for Christ (Matthew 16:24-25; Romans 12:1). Eternal life is the reward (Romans 6:22-23; Titus 1:2), purchased for us when Jesus laid down His life for us (John 10:11; 1 John 3:16).
Lie #4: "If it feels good, it must be right."
The world says: If it feels right, do it. Your feelings are your truth.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“There is a way that seems right to a man, but its end is the way to death.” — Proverbs 14:12
“Abstain from the passions of the flesh, which wage war against your soul.” — 1 Peter 2:11
💡 Feelings are fleeting (Proverbs 28:26; Jeremiah 17:9). God's truth is eternal (Isaiah 40:8; Matthew 24:35) and leads to life (John 8:31-32; John 14:6).
Lie #5: "You are enough."
The world says: You have everything within you to overcome and thrive. God is a crutch that you don’t need and the gospel is a fairy tale.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“Apart from Me you can do nothing.” — John 15:5
“My grace is sufficient for you, for My power is made perfect in weakness.” — 2 Corinthians 12:9
💡 We aren't enough on our own (Romans 3:10-12; 2 Corinthians 3:5)—but Christ is enough for us (2 Corinthians 12:9; Colossians 2:9-10). His power works through our weakness (2 Corinthians 12:9-10; Philippians 4:13). His gospel has freed an untold number of people down through the ages (Revelation 7:9)!
Lie #6: "Truth is subjective."
The world says: You have your truth, and I have mine. There’s no absolute right or wrong.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“I am the way, and the truth, and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.” — John 14:6
“Sanctify them in the truth; Your Word is truth.” — John 17:17
💡 Truth isn't subjective; it’s found in Christ (John 14:6; Ephesians 4:21) and revealed in Scripture (John 17:17; 2 Timothy 3:16-17).
Lie #7: "Love means accepting everything."
The world says: If you love someone, you must affirm every choice they make.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“Love does not rejoice in wrongdoing but rejoices with the truth.” — 1 Corinthians 13:6
“Faithful are the wounds of a friend; an enemy multiplies kisses.” — Proverbs 27:6
💡 True love points people to the gospel of Jesus Christ and the truth of God's Word (1 Corinthians 13:6; John 15:13). It's willing to wound in order to heal (Proverbs 27:6; Hebrews 12:11).
Lie #8: "I deserve to be happy."
The world says: Your personal happiness is the highest goal.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“In this world, you will have trouble. But take heart! I have overcome the world.” — John 16:33
“Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied.” — Matthew 5:6
💡 God is more concerned with your holiness than your temporary happiness (1 Thessalonians 4:3; Hebrews 12:10-11). True joy is found in washing at the cross (Psalm 51:7-8; 1 John 1:7) and in learning how to walk with God daily (Micah 6:8; Galatians 5:16).
Lie #9: "God won’t give you more than you can handle."
The world says: You’re strong enough to face anything life throws at you.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“We were so utterly burdened beyond our strength that we despaired of life itself… But that was to make us rely not on ourselves but on God.” — 2 Corinthians 1:8-9
“Come to Me, all who labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest.” — Matthew 11:28
💡 God brings overwhelming circumstances to humble us (Deuteronomy 8:2-3; 2 Corinthians 12:7) and teach us to rely on Him (2 Corinthians 1:8-9; Proverbs 3:5-6).
Lie #10: "People are basically good."
The world says: Humanity is inherently good, and evil is a product of circumstances.
📖 Biblical Truth:
“None is righteous, no, not one.” — Romans 3:10
“For all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God.” — Romans 3:23
💡 People aren’t inherently good—we’re sinful and in need of a Savior (Romans 3:10-12; Romans 3:23). Jesus is the only solution (John 14:6; Acts 4:12). His death and resurrection are the proof (Romans 5:8; 1 Corinthians 15:3-4). If we're all good, why did Jesus need to die? (Galatians 2:21). When He rose again, He proved the power of the solution for you and me (Romans 4:25; 1 Peter 1:3).
What are some lies you have believed, and what were the consequences?

Day 38: Counteracting Lies
Biblical Illustration: Saul
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
The life of King Saul dramatically illustrates the error of believing lies. Notice how he believed each lie which led to his destruction:
Lie #1: "Follow your heart. You are your own guide."

    Example from Saul’s Life: When Samuel was delayed in offering the sacrifice, Saul followed his instincts rather than God’s instructions and offered the sacrifice himself. “So I forced myself and offered the burnt offering.” — 1 Samuel 13:12

    Result: His impatience and self-reliance led to God rejecting his dynasty. “Your kingdom shall not continue; the Lord has sought out a man after His own heart.” — 1 Samuel 13:14

Lie #2: "Your worth is based on your accomplishments and appearance."

    Example from Saul’s Life: Saul was chosen as king partly because he “looked the part”—a tall and handsome man who impressed the people. “There was not a man among the people of Israel more handsome than he.” — 1 Samuel 9:2

    Result: His physical stature couldn’t compensate for his spiritual flaws. God later reminded Samuel that He looks at the heart, not outward appearance. “For the Lord sees not as man sees: man looks on the outward appearance, but the Lord looks on the heart.” — 1 Samuel 16:7

Lie #3: "You only live once, so live for yourself."

    Example from Saul’s Life: Instead of obeying God fully, Saul prioritized his own desires. He spared King Agag and the best of the Amalekite livestock, and lied about it, despite God’s command to destroy everything. “I have obeyed the voice of the Lord... but the people took of the spoil.” — 1 Samuel 15:20-21

    Result: God rejected him as king for his partial obedience, which was disobedience. “To obey is better than sacrifice.” — 1 Samuel 15:22

Lie #5: "You are enough."

    Example from Saul’s Life: Saul had evidenced much self-sufficiency in his reign as king, but when facing Goliath, Saul did not step forward as Israel’s leader. Instead, he relied on others to fight his battles. This demonstrated his lack of faith in God’s sufficiency. “Let no man's heart fail because of him. Your servant will go and fight with this Philistine.” — 1 Samuel 17:32 (David’s words)

    Result: Saul’s inadequacy was evident, but David trusted in God’s strength, showing that dependence on God—not self-sufficiency—is key.

Lie #6: "Truth is subjective."

    Example from Saul’s Life: Saul repeatedly twisted the truth to justify his disobedience. He claimed partial obedience was enough, even when confronted by Samuel. “I have sinned yet honor me now before the elders of my people.” — 1 Samuel 15:30

    Result: Saul’s refusal to submit to God’s absolute truth led to his downfall.

Lie #8: "I deserve to be happy."

    Example from Saul’s Life: Saul became consumed with jealousy over David’s success and favor, believing he deserved the glory and praise of the people. “They have ascribed to David ten thousands, and to me, they have ascribed thousands.” — 1 Samuel 18:8

    Result: His pursuit of self-glory led to paranoia, bitterness, and ultimately his tragic death.

Conclusion:
Saul’s life is a vivid warning against believing cultural lies. Each time Saul trusted in himself, followed his own desires, or sought the world’s approval, he moved further from revealed will. His story reminds us that true life, identity, and fulfillment are found only in surrender to God’s will and reliance on His truth. Only the gospel of Jesus dispels all lies and confirms us in the truth as we daily put faith in it.
💡 Key Takeaway: Like Saul, we are born believing lies, tempted to believe them as we get older, and would believe them still if it weren't for Jesus coming to us and showing us the truth. Believing lies is why we ended up as captives, but unlike Saul, God has granted us repentance and led us to the cross where we found pardon and new life, and led us to return to God’s Word where we know the truth, and the truth sets us free.
What was the key reason for Saul's downfall as king?
His refusal to listen to the advice of his soldiers
His desire to expand Israel's borders
His reliance on his own instincts rather than God's instructionsCorrect

Day 38: Counteracting Lies
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
The lies of our culture can enslave us, but the gospel sets us free. Jesus said, “You will know the truth, and the truth will set you free” (John 8:32). True freedom comes when we reject the world’s deceptions and anchor our lives at the cross, and live in God’s Word. 
Spend time daily in Scripture, letting its truth expose lies and guide your heart. When tempted to follow your flesh or cultural norms, remember that Christ’s death and resurrection secure your identity, worth, and purpose. Trust in Him as the Ultimate Truth, the Absolute Truth and live in the freedom of the gospel.
How can you daily anchor your life in God's Word to guard against cultural lies and live in the freedom of the gospel?

Day 39: Confident Prayer: Praying God's Word
How To Pray Confidently
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage 
This is the confidence we have in approaching God: that if we ask anything according to his will, he hears us. 15 And if we know that he hears us—whatever we ask—we know that we have what we asked of him.
1 John 5:14-15
What a remarkable promise! God hears our prayers, and we know we have what we ask of Him!
But there’s an important condition: our prayers must align with His will. Think of God’s will as a well-trodden path in a forest, hedged in on both sides by God's Word (Psalm 119:105). Stray off the path, and you don't reach your destination. Stay on it, and you will find answered prayer left and right (John 15:7). Our confidence in prayer is rooted in walking this path, trusting that God's answers are always best—like a good doctor who prescribes the right medicine even when we think we need something else (Matthew 7:7-11).
Before I retired from pastoring, our church set up a prayer room where we could pray before each service on Sunday, from 9:15 AM to 10:00 AM. We got the name of this prayer room from Charles Spurgeon; he called his Sunday morning prayer room the "Boiler Room." It was the place where spiritual heat and light were generated, the place where the power came from (James 5:16). The room began filling up on Sundays as we sought to pray God's Word back to Him (Isaiah 55:11).
After a few months, we started noticing dramatic answers to prayer: we saw unsaved loved ones come to faith in Christ, healing, and reconciliation in relationships (Acts 2:42-47). This encouraged us to pray bolder prayers, rooted in Scripture, prayed in faith; so, we started praying for conversions and baptisms, based on Matthew 28:18-20. Within just a few months, people began coming to faith in Christ, and pretty soon we were baptizing around 10 people a week.
It is amazing to see how God has miraculous answers to prayers for us if we will learn to pray His Word, where His will is revealed, back to Him (John 14:13-14). It is an amazing connection we have with our Father, the Almighty God of the Universe who routinely does more than we ask of Him (Ephesians 3:20-21).
However, it's important to remember that sin disrupted this connection. Isaiah 59:2 reminds us, "Your iniquities have made a separation between you and your God, and your sins have hidden His face from you so that He does not hear." God turned a deaf ear to all humanity after Adam and Eve's fall, and a deaf ear to you and me through our sin (Genesis 3:22-24).
But Jesus came! (John 1:14) Through Jesus' death and resurrection, the barrier of sin was torn down, and the curtain in the Temple that kept people out of God's presence was torn down (Matthew 27:51). Jesus, our mediator, restored the broken relationship, making it possible for us to pray with confidence that God hears (Hebrews 7:25).
This truth, that God answers all prayers prayed according to His will, does not mean the answer is immediate, or even that the answer is always "yes". We don't know the entirety of the will of God. But what we do know is that praying His Word believingly is the best way to see our prayers answered, as God hears and answers all prayers that are prayed according to His will.
What is the basis of our confidence in prayer according to 1 John 5:14-15?
Our ability to pray eloquently
The urgency of our request
C) Praying according to God's will as revealed in God's WordCorrect

Day 39: Confident Prayer: Praying God's Word
Biblical Illustration: Elijah
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Consider Elijah on Mount Carmel (1 Kings 18:20-39). Surrounded by idol worshipers, Elijah confidently prayed for God to reveal His power. His prayer wasn’t random; it was rooted in God's will to turn Israel's hearts back to Him (James 5:17-18). The result? Fire fell from heaven, consuming the sacrifice and proving that God hears prayers aligned with His purposes (Psalm 34:17, 1 John 5:14-15).
Prayers that align with God’s will are like walking through an open door—access and blessing flow freely (Revelation 3:8). But selfish prayers are like pounding on a locked gate—it won’t budge, no matter how hard we try (Matthew 7:21).
What made Elijah's prayer on Mount Carmel so powerful?
The dramatic setting on Mount Carmel
His prayer aligned with God's will to turn Israel's hearts back to HimCorrect
The large number of people watching

Day 39: Confident Prayer: Praying God's Word
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Praying according to God's will is like tuning a radio. When we adjust our hearts through Scripture and the gospel, we tune into God's frequency. We begin to desire what He desires, and pray according to His will.
Here are some examples of praying God's Word back to Him, and as we do this praying we are assured He hears and answers us.
Being Set Free From Sin

    John 8:31-32
    "If you abide in My word, you are truly My disciples, and you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free."
    Prayer: Lord, help me abide in Your Word and discover the truth that sets me free, and help me to apply it.
    Romans 6:6-7
    "We know that our old self was crucified with Him so that the body ruled by sin might be rendered powerless, so that we may no longer be enslaved to sin, since a person who has died is freed from sin."
    Prayer: Father, thank You for the cross, and thank You that my old self was crucified with Christ. Teach me to live a crucified life and live in the freedom from sin that You have purchased and provided.
    Galatians 5:1
    "For freedom Christ has set us free; stand firm, therefore, and do not submit again to a yoke of slavery."
    Prayer: Jesus, thank You for setting me free by shedding your blood. Help me to stand firm and not fall back into the chains You broke for me.
    2 Corinthians 3:17
    "Now the Lord is the Spirit, and where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is freedom."
    Prayer: Holy Spirit, thank you for coming at Pentecost and for coming into my heart and life the moment I believed. May Your freedom flow in every area of my life.
    Colossians 1:13-14
    "He has delivered us from the domain of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of His beloved Son, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins."
    Prayer: Thank You, Father, for rescuing me from darkness when Jesus hung on the cross in darkness; and thank You for bringing me into the light as Jesus rose from the dead on the third day. Help me live the kingdom life I am now in, not the kingdom of death I came from.

Growing in Faith

    Hebrews 12:1-2
    "Let us also lay aside every weight, and sin which clings so closely, and let us run with endurance the race that is set before us, looking to Jesus, the founder and perfecter of our faith."
    Prayer: Lord, help me cast aside all that holds me back. Fix my eyes on Jesus and grow my faith through Him.
    2 Peter 3:18
    "But grow in the grace and knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ."
    Prayer: Lord, guide me and teach me to grow in grace and in the knowledge of Jesus. Teach me through Your Word and Spirit, and through others in the body of Christ.
    James 1:2-4
    "Count it all joy, my brothers, when you meet trials of various kinds, for you know that the testing of your faith produces steadfastness. And let steadfastness have its full effect, that you may be perfect and complete, lacking in nothing."
    Prayer: Father, help me see trials as opportunities to grow in faith. Strengthen my endurance so that I will mature in You.
    Psalm 1:2-3
    "His delight is in the law of the Lord, and on His law he meditates day and night. He is like a tree planted by streams of water that yields its fruit in its season."
    Prayer: Lord, thank you for Jesus Christ Who, alone, fulfilled this verse, and He did it for me. Now cause me to delight in your Word and make me like a tree planted by Your living water. Let Your Word nourish me, so I bear fruit for You.
    Philippians 1:6
    "And I am sure of this, that He who began a good work in you will bring it to completion at the day of Jesus Christ."
    Prayer: Thank You, Lord, for beginning this work of faith in me, leading me to the cross, and raising me from the dead of my sins. And thank you for the assurance that You will complete the work You started in me. Help me trust in Your faithfulness. 

Today, take time to pray with an open Bible. Let God’s Word shape your requests, just as Jesus modeled in the Lord’s Prayer: “Your will be done.”
What Scripture will you pray to God today, knowing He will hear and answer you?

Day 40: The Father Heart of God
God's Heart Revealed At The Cross
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
But while he was still a long way off, his father saw him and was filled with compassion for him; he ran to his son, threw his arms around him and kissed him.
Luke 15:20
The Bible makes it a well-known truth that to escape temptation and the pull of sin, we must be united with Christ, connected to God the Father, and purposely living in unity with the Trinity (John 14:23). Yet many people have a wrong view of God the Father, which keeps them from fully embracing their own "sonship" and experiencing the unity that is theirs by cross-purchased right (Romans 8:15-17; Galatians 4:6). This wrong view might develop for a variety of reasons, but a couple of common ones are:  1) their own earthly father and all his flaws (Psalm 27:10), or 2) viewing God the Father only as an Old Testament holy Deity who cannot stand sin (Habakkuk 1:13), which produces an unholy fear in them (1 John 4:18).
Those who escape the intense pull of sin have seen that 1) all humans are sinful (Romans 3:23; Ecclesiastes 7:20), so if their father wasn't all he should have been, they acknowledge that neither are they, themselves, all they should be, and they have extended grace to their father (Matthew 6:14-15). And 2) they have seen God's true heart revealed at the cross, as both a holy God who hates sin (Romans 1:18; 1 Peter 1:16), and loves His children (John 3:16). The cross shouts that God became man to pay for our sin so that we could be with Him forever (John 1:14; Philippians 2:6-8).
In today's devotional, we will deal with the first problem, the error of seeing our Heavenly Father like our earthly father, and in tomorrow's teaching, we will deal with the second problem, that of seeing God only as holy and righteous, a sin-hating God.
Jesus told a parable in Luke 15, which is known as the Parable of the Prodigal Son. It is primarily designed as a condemnation of the Pharisees' self-righteousness (as portrayed by the "older brother") but includes clear teaching on the heart of the Father toward us who have sinned. 
When the Prodigal son hit rock bottom, he came to his senses (Luke 15:17). Having squandered his inheritance on reckless living, he found himself longing for pig food and realizing the depth of his fall (Luke 15:13-16). Desperation led him to rehearse a confession: "Father, I have sinned against heaven and against you. I am no longer worthy to be called your son; make me like one of your hired servants" (Luke 15:18-19). But as he made the long journey home, he could not have imagined the welcome awaiting him. This welcome must come to you as the heart of your Father God to you.
The father saw him "while he was still a long way off" and ran to meet him. Filled with compassion, he embraced his broken, filthy son, silencing any rehearsed speech with kisses (Luke 15:20). Without hesitation, he restored him as a son, clothing him with the finest robe, placing a ring on his finger, and preparing a feast to celebrate his return (Luke 15:22-23). The father’s actions reveal the heart of God: loving, forgiving, and eager to restore (Micah 7:18-19; Ephesians 1:7). He doesn’t wait for us to clean ourselves up but meets us in our brokenness and welcomes us home through grace (Romans 5:8; Titus 3:5-7).
This is the gospel. Like the prodigal, we’ve all squandered the inheritance of God’s goodness through sin and ended up "a long way off" from our Heavenly Father (Ephesians 2:12-13). But God, in His great mercy, doesn’t leave us in the muck of our failure. Instead, through Jesus Christ’s life, death, and resurrection, He runs to meet us, wraps us in His righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21), and calls us His beloved children (1 John 3:1). Notice how the father affirms the sonship of the prodigal when he is speaking with the older brother: "For this son of mine was dead and is alive again; he was lost and is found" (Luke 15:24).
Yes, you and I were "dead in our sins and trespasses" (Ephesians 2:1-5), separated and estranged from our Father God, but God so loved us that He ran to us with flesh on (John 3:16; Hebrews 2:14-17), paid for all wrong that we have done, received us back to Himself with great joy (Zephaniah 3:17), and is now building a place for us that where He is, there we may be also (John 14:2-3).
Have you had a wrong view of your Heavenly Father? If so, how does the parable of the prodigal son correct that view for you?

Day 40: The Father Heart of God
Biblical Illustration: Ephraim
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Throughout Scripture, we see the heart of God revealed in His pursuit of the lost. Consider God’s words to Ephraim in Hosea 11:8: “How can I give you up, Ephraim? How can I hand you over, Israel? My heart is changed within me; all my compassion is aroused.”
Ephraim, representing the northern tribes of Israel, had repeatedly turned away from God. They pursued idols, rebelled against His commandments, and ignored His prophets. Despite their unfaithfulness, God’s heart ached for them. His compassion overflowed even as they deserved judgment. The imagery in Hosea captures a father torn by love: though justice demands punishment, mercy triumphs at the cross. God’s heart grieves over His wayward children, yet He remains steadfast, unwilling to give them up.
This is the Father heart of God. He does not abandon His children, even when they stray far from Him. His love is relentless, His compassion unchanging, and His desire is always for restoration. Just as the father in Luke 15 ran to embrace his prodigal son, so God longs to gather His people in His arms, to heal their wounds, and to celebrate their return.
What does Hosea 11:8 teach you about God’s character and His "Father heart" for His people?

Practical Application

Take time today to reflect on areas where you’ve strayed from God’s ways. Picture the Father running to meet you, embracing you with love and grace. Confess your sins to Him, knowing He is faithful to forgive and restore you.
Consider how you can reflect God’s heart to others. Who in your life needs to experience the Father’s love through you? A kind word, an act of forgiveness, or simply being present can reveal His compassion to someone who feels far from Him.
How does experiencing God’s forgiveness and love inspire you to love and forgive others?

Day 41: The Father Heart of God
Our Father Is Holy and Gracious
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
You have not come to a mountain that can be touched and that is burning with fire; to darkness, gloom and storm; 19 to a trumpet blast or to such a voice speaking words that those who heard it begged that no further word be spoken to them, 20 because they could not bear what was commanded: “If even an animal touches the mountain, it must be stoned to death.” 21 The sight was so terrifying that Moses said, “I am trembling with fear.” 22 But you have come to Mount Zion, to the city of the living God, the heavenly Jerusalem. You have come to thousands upon thousands of angels in joyful assembly, 23 to the church of the firstborn, whose names are written in heaven. You have come to God, the Judge of all, to the spirits of the righteous made perfect, 24 to Jesus the mediator of a new covenant, and to the sprinkled blood that speaks a better word than the blood of Abel.
Hebrews 12:18-24
If you and I were to think that God is only holy, only righteous, and only hates sin, and we know ourselves to be sinners, it would be like standing before a roaring fire with no protection—fearful of being consumed (Hebrews 12:29; Isaiah 33:14). We would shrink back from coming to Him out of dread. But we must come to Him and be united to Him to be free (John 8:36; Romans 6:22). This creates a dilemma within the soul of every human being who first considers coming to God to be rescued from their sin (Romans 7:24-25).
Some people only view God as the Old Testament far-away God, like a fortress surrounded by impenetrable walls, where no sinner is allowed to draw near (Exodus 19:12-13). The Old Covenant that was made with the nation of Israel reinforced this idea and terrified the people (Exodus 20:18-19). When God gave the Law on Mt. Sinai, He set limits around the mountain, commanding that no human being come near, and the leaders set a fence around the mountain (Exodus 19:16-17, 21-24). All that was like a warning sign saying, "Stay out! I am holy; you are sinful. Do not approach!" (Habakkuk 1:13).
Some people think God is ONLY that. But it’s like seeing only one side of a coin and assuming it’s all there is. We need to grow in our understanding of the gospel so that we are emboldened to come to God based on the removal of our sin at the cross (Colossians 1:20-22; 1 John 4:9-10). For Hebrews 12:18-24 tells us that we have not come to Mt. Sinai, but rather Mt. Zion through Mt. Calvary (Luke 23:33).
The contrast is striking. Mount Sinai was like a storm-filled sky, dark and ominous, with flashes of lightning that left the people trembling because they could not approach a holy God due to their sinfulness (Exodus 19:16-19; Deuteronomy 5:22-27). Even Moses, the mediator of the old covenant, was like a man standing on the edge of a cliff, gripped by the sheer terror of what lay before him (Hebrews 12:21). This scene reminds us that God’s holiness is unapproachable! (Isaiah 6:1-5).
But we have not come to Sinai; we’ve come to Mount Zion (Hebrews 12:22). Here, we encounter the Father heart of God in a new light. Zion is like a lush, welcoming meadow—a place of peace and invitation, fulfilled through Jesus Christ (John 14:27). At Mount Zion, we see Jesus as the bridge spanning a great chasm, suffering in our place, paying our debt, and restoring us to God (Romans 5:6-11). The heavenly Jerusalem welcomes us into a gathering of saints, angels, and the perfected spirits of the redeemed (Revelation 7:9-10; Hebrews 12:22-24). At its center is Jesus, whose sprinkled blood "speaks a better word" than Abel’s, declaring forgiveness and reconciliation instead of vengeance and retribution (Hebrews 12:24; Genesis 4:10). Jesus and the bloody cross are like a melody of grace, drowning out the cry for justice with the song of mercy (Ephesians 1:7-8; Romans 8:1-2).
A simple outline of Hebrews 12:18-24 might go something like this:

    The Peril of Sinai – The mountain represents fear and judgment, emphasizing the unapproachability of God under the law. Our Father is holy, righteous, and perfect.
    The Peace of Zion – Mount Zion symbolizes the grace and joy found in the city of the living God and the assembly of angels. Our Father is love and forgiveness, full of grace and truth.
    The Power of the Savior – Through Jesus, the mediator of the new covenant, believers receive a better word and access to God. Our Father came in the body of a human to die and draw us near to Him.

How have you viewed God as your Father in the past, and how does the gospel affect your current view?


Day 41: The Father Heart of God
Biblical Illustration: Mt. Sinai and Mt. Calvary
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
In Exodus 19-20, we see the dramatic scene at Mount Sinai. The mountain trembles as God’s presence descends with fire and smoke. The people stand far off, terrified by the display of His holiness. They cry out for Moses to mediate, unable to bear hearing God’s voice directly.
Contrast this with Luke 15:20, the parable of the prodigal son. The father runs to meet his son, embraces him, and celebrates his return. This image of a loving father, eager to reconcile, complements the welcome we receive at Mount Zion through the work Jesus did at Mt. Calvary. Through Christ, the holy God who once seemed unapproachable now calls us to draw near with confidence.
"Therefore, brothers and sisters, since we have confidence to enter the Most Holy Place by the blood of Jesus, 20 by a new and living way opened for us through the curtain, that is, his body, 21 and since we have a great priest over the house of God, 22 let us draw near to God with a sincere heart and with the full assurance that faith brings, having our hearts sprinkled to cleanse us from a guilty conscience and having our bodies washed with pure water." Hebrews 10:19-22
How does the scene at Mount Sinai differ from the father’s response in the parable of the prodigal son?
