Day 42: Helps for Doubters
Doubting Christians
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
Come now, let us reason together, says the Lord: though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool.
Isaiah 1:18
Christians are always partial doubters. We have been shown the holiness of God (Isaiah 6:3) and have seen the requirement to "be holy as I am holy" (Leviticus 11:44; 1 Peter 1:16), yet we see our own sin so clearly. It never seems to leave us. "My sin is ever before me," says the man after God's own heart (Psalm 51:3), and many of us know this to be true of ourselves as well.
Especially is this true when we constantly see the results of our sin (Proverbs 22:8). It's like planting weeds in a garden years ago (Hosea 10:13), and now all we can see are weeds choking out the flowers. Maybe we had a failed marriage or lost a job due to sinful influence, or maybe we now struggle with sin's hold over us in the area of food, alcohol, or drugs, because we opened the door to those areas years ago (Galatians 6:7-8).
Maybe we have children who have turned from the faith, and we feel condemned when we remember "wisdom is vindicated by her children" (Luke 7:35). It's like building a house only to see it crack and crumble despite all our efforts (Matthew 7:24-27). We don't feel vindicated when looking at ours!
Or maybe we see the fruit of the Spirit as listed in Galatians 5:22-23, but our own thoughts, if not words and actions, show that we have more of the works of the flesh (Galatians 5:19-21) than the fruit of the Spirit. We feel like we're holding up an empty basket where there should be a harvest (John 15:4-5, 16).
All of these things, and so many more, can plague our thoughts, discourage or crush our spirits (Proverbs 18:14), and leave us feeling hopeless and/or terrified for when we die as to what we might face (Hebrews 9:27). It's like standing before a judge knowing He has the evidence to pronounce a guilty verdict (Romans 3:19-20). All seems dark, discouraging, and foreboding.
God knows all this about His children. Thankfully, He has made a way for us to come to a right conclusion about the condition of our souls. He calls us to enter into dialogue with Him about this, to reason with Him specifically about our sin (Micah 6:2-3), and to come to a settled conclusion based on the evidence He presents (Hebrews 11:1).
"Come now, let us reason together, says the Lord: though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool" (Isaiah 1:18).
In Isaiah 1:18, God is specifically calling us to reason with Him about our sins. He acknowledges the horrendous nature of them, the deadly consequences of them (Romans 6:23), and that those sins are plainly seen in our lives as no one can miss scarlet and crimson. It's like a stain on a white cloth—unavoidable, glaring, impossible to ignore. We often feel the weight of our sins, like they are on top of us pressing us down right into hell (Psalm 38:4).
But the reasoning we are to do is to examine our sins in light of the cross (John 3:14-16). His promise that He will change the very nature of our sins (scarlet to white, crimson to wool) finds its fulfillment in the great exchange where Jesus becomes sin for us, and we become the righteousness of God in Him (2 Corinthians 5:21).
To examine the wounds and bruises of Jesus Christ (Isaiah 53:5) is to see Him bearing all the consequences of our sins. You are to look at the ledger of your life and see that every debt you owe is marked "paid in full" by His blood (Colossians 2:13-14). He was wounded for our transgressions and bruised for our iniquities (Isaiah 53:5). And if He bore them, then He bore them for us, and we are free from them eternally (John 8:36). Our crimson sins have been "whited out" at the cross. Like the Egyptians, our sins have all been drowned in the red sea of Jesus' blood!
Whenever you see your sin and doubts creep in, whenever you see bad fruit in your life and are reminded of your sin, let that be like a warning bell urging you to run to the cross (Hebrews 12:1-2). Take a full 3-D view of all that Jesus accomplished there (Philippians 3:7-9). Survey the wondrous cross (Galatians 6:14), reason there with God, and don't stop until your heart melts in contrition (Psalm 51:17), your soul breathes in new faith (Romans 10:17), and your mind and soul are convinced that He died for you and rose to justify you (Romans 4:25).
Let the sight you see there be like watching the sun rise after a long night (Psalm 30:5), reminding you that one day all the reminders of sin will be gone (Revelation 21:4), and your faith will be sight (2 Corinthians 5:7).
How does God help Christians face their sin and doubts through the message of the cross?

Day 42: Helps for Doubters
Biblical Illustration: Thomas
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Thomas, one of Jesus’ disciples, provides a compelling example of doubt being overcome by faith in the wounds of Jesus (John 20:24-29). After Jesus’ resurrection, Thomas struggled to believe the testimony of the other disciples. His doubt was tied to his inability to fully grasp the reality of Jesus’ victory over sin and death. He demanded tangible proof, saying, "Unless I see in His hands the mark of the nails, and place my finger into the mark of the nails, and place my hand into His side, I will never believe."
In His mercy, Jesus appeared to Thomas, inviting him to touch His wounds. This act was not only physical evidence of Jesus’ resurrection but also a powerful assurance that the scars of the cross had paid for Thomas' sin in full. Thomas’ response was immediate: "My Lord and my God!" His doubt turned into unwavering faith as he recognized the sufficiency of Christ’s sacrifice.
Thomas’ story shows us that when we bring our doubts to Jesus, He meets us with grace, reminding us that His work on the cross is complete. His scars are the proof that our sins are forgiven, and His resurrection is the guarantee of our salvation. Oh yes, God "abundantly pardons" all our sin (Isaiah 55:7), and freely justifies all who believe (Romans 3:24).
How do Jesus’ scars provide assurance of forgiveness and victory over sin?
What can Thomas’ transformation teach you about bringing your doubts to the cross?

Day 43: Praise Your Way to Victory
Praise Wins The Battle
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
But thou art holy, O thou that inhabitest the praises of Israel.
Psalm 22:3 KJV
As a pastor, I used to hear people praise God for the nice weather, sunshine, health, family, church, job, and their country. And while these things are good, and it is right to praise God for them, what happens when they are gone? It’s like admiring the frame of a masterpiece—good and beautiful, but not the painting itself. Does the frame still hold our focus if the painting is removed? Do we have nothing to praise God for if all the "good" things in our life are gone? (Matthew 6:19-21)
The content of all heavenly praise, and the proper primary and foundational subject of all our praises on earth, is that Jesus Christ died for us and rose again (Romans 5:8). In Revelation 5, we read expressions of extreme sadness in heaven because nobody is found worthy to open the scroll of life. It’s like the apostle John is standing before a vault that contains the treasure of eternal life, only to realize no one has the key. John weeps over the thought that the scroll remains sealed for lack of a worthy person to open it (Revelation 5:4).
But then this happens: "Then one of the elders said to me, ‘Do not weep! See, the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the Root of David, has triumphed. He is able to open the scroll and its seven seals’” (Revelation 5:5). Look at those words, "Do not weep! See, the Lion...has triumphed!" Jesus, the Lion, is like a victorious king returning from battle, His triumph proclaiming the end of all sin and sorrow (1 Corinthians 15:55-57). He was victorious in removing our sin and conquering death (1 Corinthians 15:54), and that alone is enough to stop our sadness, wipe our tears, and quiet our conscience and our hearts. “Do not weep! See, the Lion...has triumphed!”
When John looks, though, he does not see a Lion: "Then I saw a Lamb, looking as if it had been slain, standing at the center of the throne, encircled by the four living creatures and the elders" (Revelation 5:6). The Lamb, looking as if it had been slain, is like seeing a hero who bears scars—not signs of weakness but proof of victory. This is a direct reference to the scars of crucifixion (Matthew 27:35-36), which is heaven's focus. The slain Lamb and the victorious Lion make up the two parts of the gospel: Christ died for our sins (Romans 4:25); Christ rose again on the third day (1 Corinthians 15:1-5).
The true understanding of the suffering of the Lamb and the conquering of the Lion is what stops all weeping and starts all praising (Philippians 2:9-11). This glorious gospel is the compelling reason we have to praise God. It’s like discovering a wellspring of water in the desert—everlasting, life-giving, and enough to sustain praise forever (John 4:14).
And this, this praising of God for the provision of the cross is what brings real victory over daily sin!  "Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death? Thanks be to God through Jesus Christ our Lord!" (Romans 7:24-25).
"Through him then let us continually offer up a sacrifice of praise to God, that is, the fruit of lips that acknowledge his name" (Hebrews 13:15). 
How can you incorporate daily praising God for the gospel into your life?

Day 43: Praise Your Way to Victory
Biblical Illustration: Jehoshaphat and the Praise Team
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
In 2 Chronicles 20:21-22, we read the account of King Jehoshaphat of Judah facing an overwhelming enemy, and instead of panicking or using typical battle strategies, he appoints praisers to go ahead of the army. "Give thanks to the Lord, for his love endures forever" (v. 21). As they sing, God causes the enemy forces to defeat themselves.
This historical account is a powerful metaphor for our own spiritual battles. In life, we often feel surrounded by problems—temptations are many and varied, like an army marching toward us. But the gospel shows us that victory comes not through force, but through praise and the power of God’s love in Jesus Christ.
Like the praisers who led the army, we are called to lead into our battles with praise, as if God had already won the battle for us, which He did! When we fix our eyes on Jesus, the true victor, we realize our strength isn’t in fighting, but in trusting that He has already fought for us. The cross, a symbol of death, became the means of life because God, in His infinite love, turned defeat into victory.
In times of doubt and discouragement, let us praise. In times of weakness, let us praise! Praise declares God’s sovereignty over our lives, reminding us that we fight not for victory, but from it. The praisers in Judah knew this secret, and when they praised, the enemy was defeated before a sword was lifted.
This isn’t just a story of ancient battles—it’s a call to surrender to God's work and trust His plans. In life’s challenges, let’s be the praisers who go before, lifting Jesus’ name, the victorious Savior (1 Corinthians 15:57).
How can praising God in the midst of challenges help you trust His victory and surrender to His plans?

Day 43: Praise Your Way to Victory
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
The story of the praisers in 2 Chronicles reminds us that our victory in Christ is secured through praise, especially when we focus on the cross. 
In moments of struggle with sin, we can feel overwhelmed, like the enemy is too strong to overcome. But just as the praisers led the army with praise, we too can lead with gratitude for the cross. When we praise God for what Jesus has done—defeating sin and death on our behalf—we acknowledge His victory over the very things that try to control us. 
Praise shifts our focus from the weight of our struggles to the power of God’s love, reminding us that He has already won the battle. Let’s make it a habit to speak out in praise, sing praise, and pray praise, declaring that through Christ, sin no longer reigns, and the victory has already been won.
How can you intentionally use praise to shift your focus from struggles with sin to the victory Christ has already won for you

Day 44: Gratitude For The Gospel
Gratitude and Gross Sins
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key Passage
For although they knew God, they neither glorified him as God nor gave thanks to him, but their thinking became futile and their foolish hearts were darkened.
Romans 1:21
Teaching
It's amazing what happens to people when they don't live their lives dependent on God, not thankful for His grace, but rather being their own head (living lives as they see best). They're like a ship without a rudder, drifting wherever the current of the world takes it, unable to steer toward truth (Proverbs 14:12; Jeremiah 10:23), and ending up battered to pieces on the rocks of life.
Romans 1 gives a whole list of sin traps into which people plunge headlong. It's not just that they became foolish idolaters (Romans 1:23), bowing before lifeless statues like someone desperately trying to draw water from an empty well (Psalm 115:4-8; Isaiah 44:9-20), but also "sexual impurity" and "degrading of their bodies with one another" (Romans 1:24), "shameful lusts" (Romans 1:26), and "women exchanged natural sexual relations for unnatural ones" (Romans 1:26), "men also abandoned natural relations with women and were inflamed with lust for one another. Men committed shameful acts with other men, and received in themselves the due penalty for their error" (Romans 1:27) (Leviticus 18:22; 1 Corinthians 6:9-10; Jude 1:7).
And finally, "They have become filled with every kind of wickedness, evil, greed, and depravity"—like a tree whose roots have rotted so thoroughly that its fruit is nothing but poison (Matthew 7:17-18; Galatians 5:19-21). "They are full of envy, murder, strife, deceit and malice. They are gossips, slanderers, God-haters, insolent, arrogant and boastful; they invent ways of doing evil; they disobey their parents; they have no understanding, no fidelity, no love, no mercy" (Romans 1:29-31) (2 Timothy 3:2-5; Proverbs 6:16-19).
But reading through that list above leaves out one essential truth. How did these people sink that low in sin? How did they become like a candle that may have once burned bright but is now suffocated by its own melted wax (Ephesians 4:18-19)? How did they become so perverted and evil?
The answer is in Romans 1:21, "For although they knew God, they neither glorified him as God nor gave thanks to him..." It is very clear that there is a God based upon creation; His existence is as undeniable as the sun in the sky warming the earth, whether people acknowledge it or not (Psalm 19:1-4; Romans 1:20). People know Him through obvious acts of nature, through the stars and heavens declaring the glory of God, and through their own conscience declaring that He exists (Psalm 8:3-4; Ecclesiastes 3:11; Romans 2:14-15). But they chose not to glorify Him nor give thanks to Him.
So they become darkened in their understanding and perverted in their living. This lack of thinking of God and thanking God in a person's life is like turning off the lighthouse and wondering why ships crash against the rocks (John 3:19-20; 2 Thessalonians 2:10-12). God hands selfish, unthankful people over to a life of sin: "Therefore God gave them over in the sinful desires of their hearts to sexual impurity for the degrading of their bodies with one another" (Romans 1:24) (Psalm 81:11-12; Proverbs 1:28-31).
How about you? Are you thankful to God? Do you express it often? We not only have the witness of creation and conscience, we have the gospel and the Holy Spirit, telling us that God became man and out of love chose to take our sins on Himself and die in our place (Isaiah 53:5-6; 2 Corinthians 5:21; 1 Peter 2:24). Are you grateful for that act of sacrifice? Or do you treat it like a priceless gift left unopened on a shelf (John 1:11-12; Hebrews 2:3)? When was the last time you told Him, "Thank You for dying for me" (Luke 17:15-16; 1 Thessalonians 5:18)?
What is the primary reason, according to Romans 1:21, that people fall into deep sin and depravity?
They were not taught moral values.
They lacked access to religious texts.
 They neither glorified God nor gave thanks to Him.Correct
 
 Day 44: Gratitude For The Gospel
Biblical Illustration: The Ingratitude of the Israelites
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
The sun beat down on the weary travelers as they trudged through the wilderness. Their sandals kicked up dust, their throats felt parched, and their stomachs groaned. But instead of remembering how God saved them from slavery (Deuteronomy 5:6), the miraculous parting of the Red Sea (Exodus 14:21-22), the water from the rock (Exodus 17:6), or the manna that fell from heaven each morning (Exodus 16:14-15), their hearts were filled with grumbling.
"If only we had meat to eat! We remember the fish we ate in Egypt at no cost—also the cucumbers, melons, leeks, onions, and garlic. But now we have lost our appetite; we never see anything but this manna!" (Numbers 11:4-6).
Their ingratitude spread like a disease, poisoning their hearts against the very God who had saved them. They forgot that in Egypt, they were slaves, crying out under oppression (Exodus 2:23). They had exchanged gratitude for complaining, and trust for entitlement.
God heard their grumbling and sent quail—so much that it piled up around them (Numbers 11:31-32). But as they greedily stuffed themselves, the Lord’s anger burned against them, and a plague struck them down (Numbers 11:33-34). Their ingratitude led to judgment.
Centuries later, Paul warned believers not to follow their example: "And do not grumble, as some of them did—and were killed by the destroying angel" (1 Corinthians 10:10). Instead, he urged believers to "give thanks in all circumstances, for this is God’s will for you in Christ Jesus" (1 Thessalonians 5:18).
Like the Israelites, we are prone to forget God’s daily provisions, which point to the eternal provision of the gospel. But complaining darkens the heart, while thankfulness opens our eyes to God's faithfulness in our lives. Will we grumble in the wilderness of this life, or trust the God who provides?
What was the consequence of the Israelites' ingratitude and grumbling in the wilderness?
instead of being grateful to be FREE......

Sir Nicholas Winton organized the rescue and passage to Britain of about 669 mostly Jewish Czechoslovakian children destined for the Nazi death camps before World War II in an operation known as the Czech Kindertransport. Watch the "children" (who are now grown adults) surprise and thank Sir Winton. 
Should we not thank and praise and worship our God 100 times more, considering Sir Winton didn't die for those children but our God did?

One of the best things we can do is to thank God, sincerely from our hearts, for the sending of His Son, for the Son giving His life, and for the Spirit of God who makes that gift personal to us. To thank God for Jesus Christ is the language of heaven (Revelation 5:12) and the saints on earth's greatest acts (1 Thessalonians 5:18).
I've found one of the best ways we can do this is to start at the top of Jesus' head and go to the bottom of His feet, thanking Him for everything. Like this:
Gratitude for Jesus Christ and the Gospel
Lord Jesus, I bow in gratitude, beginning with the crown of thorns that pierced Your head. Thank You for bearing the curse of sin for me, that I might receive the crown of life (Matthew 27:29, James 1:12).
Thank You for Your eyes, filled with compassion even as You looked upon those who mocked You. "When He saw the crowds, He had compassion for them" (Matthew 9:36). You see me with that same love.
Thank You for Your lips, which spoke words of life, even from the cross: “Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do” (Luke 23:34).
Thank You for Your shoulders, bearing the weight of the cross and my sin. "Surely He has borne our griefs and carried our sorrows" (Isaiah 53:4).
Thank You for Your hands, nailed to a tree for my transgressions. "See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself" (Luke 24:39).
Thank You for Your feet, pierced so I might walk in newness of life (Romans 6:4). Thank You for the beauty of feet that bring good news! (Isaiah 52:7).
Finally, thank You for Your heart, broken so mine could be made whole. "By His wounds, we are healed" (Isaiah 53:5).
Jesus, I love You. Thank You for the gospel!

Day 45: Take Every Thought Captive
Take Captive Every Thought
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Key passage
The weapons we fight with are not the weapons of the world. On the contrary, they have divine power to demolish strongholds. 5 We demolish arguments and every pretension that sets itself up against the knowledge of God, and we take captive every thought to make it obedient to Christ.
2 Corinthians 10:4-5 
Teaching
In our life-long fight against sin, it is essential to learn how to demolish arguments against the knowledge of God and take our thoughts captive, making them obedient to Christ. This is not easy to do! It's like someone telling you, "Don't think about a pink elephant." 
In the early days of being released from sinful captivity, my mind was assaulted all the time with impure images—images of food, thoughts of getting drunk, porn-filled images, bitter thoughts of unforgiveness, etc. It was like a battlefield where flaming arrows of temptation rained down constantly, threatening to consume me. 
These thoughts can still rise up within me from time to time, as I'm sure they do for you, because we all still have flesh and a sneaky enemy lurking in the shadows, waiting for an unguarded moment.
But Christians have some things going for us that enable us to be successful in taking our thoughts captive.

    Our hearts have been circumcised (Romans 2:28-29). This is cutting out the old heart and replacing it with a new one. It’s a spiritual heart transplant—our diseased, sin-ridden heart has been removed, and in its place, we’ve received a heart that beats with the life of Christ. Why is this important? Because the heart is in control of the thoughts. “For from within, out of the heart of men, proceed evil thoughts…” (Mark 7:21-23). 

Christians have new hearts, new desires, and new thoughts, we just need to learn to live fulfilling the desires of our new hearts. Think of ways to gratify your new heart, ways to satisfy its appetite for Christ. Think of it as learning to feed a new appetite; just as a newborn craves milk, our new heart craves Christ (1 Peter 2:2).

    We have the mind of Christ (1 Corinthians 2:16). Through the gospel, we are given a transformed way of thinking. Our thoughts no longer have to be dominated by sin, because Christ’s wisdom and perspective now shape our minds. It’s like switching out an old, corrupted hard drive for a brand-new one, preloaded with the wisdom and truth of Christ. “Be transformed by the renewing of your mind” (Romans 12:2).
    We have the indwelling Holy Spirit (John 14:26). He teaches us truth and reminds us of Christ’s life, words, and sacrifice. It’s like having a personal guide on a treacherous mountain trail, constantly pointing us to the right path, to biblical thinking, and warning us of dangers ahead. We are not left to battle our thoughts alone. “Walk by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the flesh” (Galatians 5:16).
    We have the Word of God as our weapon (Ephesians 6:17). When Jesus was tempted, He responded with Scripture (Matthew 4:1-11). We do the same, wielding the sword of the Spirit to counter false thoughts. Learn to never trust your own thoughts, but rather have multiple Scriptures for every argument that seeks to bring you down.
    We have Christ’s victory (Colossians 2:15). On the cross, Jesus disarmed the powers of darkness. Our battle against sinful thoughts is fought from a place of victory. It’s like running a race knowing the trophy is already ours, like stepping onto the battlefield after the war has already been won. We fix our eyes on Jesus, who has overcome the world (John 16:33). We see Him dying and rising, we see His blood and sweat, we feel His wounds and His broken heart, and we think of His power in rising from the dead. We see Him as the triumphant King (Psalm 24:7-10), His scars telling the story of our redemption, His empty tomb declaring our freedom. 

Jesus has changed our hearts, renewed our minds, given us His Spirit, armed us with His Word, and won the battle for us!
What resources has God provided to help Christians take their thoughts captive and overcome temptation?

Day 45: Take Every Thought Captive
Biblical Illustration: Daniel
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
Biblical Illustration: Daniel—A Mind Captive to Christ
Daniel is a powerful example of someone who took his thoughts captive and made them obedient to God. As a young man, he was taken from his homeland and forced into the service of a pagan king in Babylon. He was surrounded by idolatry, immorality, and pressure to conform, yet he remained steadfast in his devotion to God.
1. A Heart Set Apart
Daniel’s ability to resist sinful thoughts and temptations started with his heart. “But Daniel purposed in his heart that he would not defile himself with the portion of the king’s delicacies” (Daniel 1:8). God had given him a new heart, and his heart was surrendered to God, which shaped his decisions and thoughts.
2. A Renewed Mind
When King Nebuchadnezzar issued a decree to execute all the wise men of Babylon, Daniel did not panic. Instead, he sought the Lord in prayer, trusting in God's wisdom rather than succumbing to fear (Daniel 2:17-18). His mind was transformed by his trust in God rather than shaped by the fear of man.
3. Walking by the Spirit
When forbidden to pray, Daniel continued to do so openly, showing that his thoughts were in submission to God, not man (Daniel 6:10). He chose to walk by the Spirit rather than gratify the desires of self-preservation.
4. Armed with the Word
Daniel knew and relied on God’s Word, even while living in a pagan culture. He prayed according to God’s promises (Daniel 9:2-3), demonstrating how Scripture guided his thoughts.
5. Victory in Christ
Despite persecution, Daniel stood firm, and God delivered him from the lions’ den (Daniel 6:22). His life points to Christ, who also overcame temptation, shut the mouth of Satan (the roaring lion), and secured victory for all who trust in Him.
Like Daniel, we take our thoughts captive by setting our hearts on Christ, renewing our minds, walking by the Spirit, and standing on God’s Word! This is how every believer down through history has won the battle of the mind. 
What does the life of Daniel teach you about taking your thoughts captive and make them obedient to Christ and His gospel?

Day 45: Take Every Thought Captive
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 4 months ago
How Do We Take Every Thought Captive?
Taking every thought captive and making it obedient to Christ is not a passive process—it requires intentional action. Here’s how:

    Recognize the Thought – When a sinful, fearful, or ungodly thought enters your mind, don’t ignore it. Identify it. Ask yourself, “Does this thought align with God’s truth?” (2 Corinthians 10:5). Be very careful not to think that every thought you have is your own, that it comes from you. 
    Replace it with Scripture – When Jesus was tempted, He responded with God’s Word (Matthew 4:4). If you struggle with fear, meditate on “God has not given us a spirit of fear” (2 Timothy 1:7). If you battle impure thoughts, dwell on “Blessed are the pure in heart” (Matthew 5:8). Memorize and declare Scripture that counters the lie. Fill your mind with what is true, noble, and pure (Philippians 4:8). 
    Pray Immediately – Turn every sinful or anxious thought into a prayer. “Cast all your anxiety on Him because He cares for you” (1 Peter 5:7).
    Meditate on the Gospel – Fix your mind on Christ crucified. Picture His broken body, His bloody wounds, His scars—all of it done in love for you (Isaiah 53:5). When tempted, remember that your sin nailed Him to the cross. His suffering reveals the horror of sin, making it repulsive rather than desirable. But His love also assures you of full forgiveness and power to walk in freedom (Romans 6:6-7).
    Walk in the Spirit – Rely on the Holy Spirit’s power daily (Galatians 5:16). The more you abide in Christ, the more your thoughts will reflect His.

By doing these things, we actively submit our minds to Christ and experience His victory over our thoughts!
How can you practically apply these principles the next time you experience a sinful thought?


ay 46: True Freedom is in Captivity
You're Free When You're a Slave
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
You have been set free from sin and have become slaves to righteousness.
Romans 6:18 
Teaching
There is a woman who sits beside a road that I drive on frequently. She is asking for money, holding up a sign that says "120 days sober" (and that number has been changing every day, of course). And while we would all agree that a sober drunk is better than a drunk drunk, is this woman truly free? 
Does freedom simply mean the stopping of certain behaviors? Absolutely not. Merely stopping an unwanted behavior is like trimming the leaves of a weed while leaving the roots intact—it may look different on the surface, but the problem remains. 
Without Christ, she may have left one form of slavery only to enter another—like stepping out of one prison cell only to find herself locked in another, whether it be dependence on willpower, human approval, or something else (Romans 6:16). Only Jesus sets captives truly free, replacing chains with righteousness, despair with hope, and brokenness with wholeness (Luke 4:18).

Notice how the Bible teaches that true freedom is found in slavery to Jesus and righteousness.
Romans 6:15 – "What then? Shall we sin because we are not under the law but under grace? By no means!" Some might think that grace gives us a free pass to sin, but Paul shuts that down immediately. Grace is not an excuse to return to the chains of sin; rather, it is the key that unlocks our freedom in Christ. Jesus’ death and resurrection did not set us free to indulge the flesh but to walk in newness of life. The cross was not a license for sin but a call to holiness, paid for in His blood.
Romans 6:16 – "Don't you know that when you offer yourselves to someone as obedient slaves, you are slaves of the one you obey—whether you are slaves to sin, which leads to death, or to obedience, which leads to righteousness?" Freedom is not found in autonomy but in servitude to the right master. We were all born in bondage to sin, slaves to our own desires, incapable of righteousness. But Jesus stepped in, purchasing us with His life's blood. Now, we have a choice: remain enslaved to sin, which leads to death, or surrender to Christ, whose yoke is easy and burden light (Matthew 11:30). The cross is the transfer point—from slavery in sin to joyful slavery to Christ.
Romans 6:17 – "But thanks be to God that, though you used to be slaves to sin, you have come to obey from your heart the pattern of teaching that has now claimed your allegiance." Salvation is not just about escaping hell; it is about a transformed heart. Before Christ, we could not obey God. Our chains kept us bound. But through the gospel, God changes us from the inside out. His grace reshapes our desires, and obedience becomes a response of love rather than obligation. Christ, through His death and resurrection, has rewritten the story of our hearts by changing the passions of our lives.
Romans 6:18 – "You have been set free from sin and have become slaves to righteousness." True freedom is not the absence of a master but the presence of the right one. Sin promised liberty but delivered death. Christ calls us to Himself, where we find real life. The gospel is the glorious exchange: our chains for His righteousness, our death for His life. When we surrender to Jesus, we don’t lose; we gain everything.
Have you truly embraced the freedom that comes from being a slave to Christ? 

Day 46: True Freedom is in Captivity
Biblical Illustration: Israelites
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
In the story of Israel's Exodus, the people were liberated from physical slavery under Pharaoh (Exodus 12:31-32), yet their journey to true freedom was far from immediate. They were freed from Egypt, but freedom was not just about leaving behind their chains; it was about learning to serve the right Master (Romans 6:18). Though they escaped slavery to Pharaoh, the Israelites often grumbled, longing for the comfort of Egypt (Exodus 16:3), even if it meant returning to oppression. They were free from Pharaoh's grasp, yet they had not yet learned to live under God's authority (Joshua 24:15).
In Exodus 16, after their miraculous crossing of the Red Sea (Exodus 14:21-22), the people grumbled about hunger, remembering the "good food" they had in Egypt (Exodus 16:3). God provided them manna from heaven to sustain them (Exodus 16:4), but the lesson was more than just provision—it was about trust and obedience (Deuteronomy 8:3). They were free from Pharaoh, but were they free from their sinful desires and fears? They still carried the chains of their hearts, bound by self-reliance, doubt, and double-mindedness (James 1:6-8)).
This is a picture of all who get "free" through worldly methods, step-groups, will-power, and self-effort. Only gospel freedom gives us such delightful freedom in such joyful slavery to Jesus (John 8:36, Romans 6:22).
What does the story of Israel’s Exodus teach us about true freedom in Christ?

Day 46: True Freedom is in Captivity
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
There are some practical applications to today's teaching:

    Submit to Christ’s Lordship: Freedom in Christ begins with recognizing Him as your true Master. You were bought with a price (1 Corinthians 6:20), so surrender daily to His authority over every aspect of your life. This might involve letting go of areas you want to control and trusting that He knows what is best (Romans 6:16).
    Renew Your Mind: Constantly fill your mind with God’s Word. Romans 12:2 encourages us to be transformed by the renewing of our minds. Replace lies with truth, and don’t allow the world to define freedom for you. The more we meditate on Scripture, the more we become aligned with God’s will, leading to true freedom in Him.
    Obey from the Heart: True freedom isn’t about following rules out of obligation; it’s about desiring to obey Christ because of the love and grace He’s shown us (Romans 6:17). Focus on cultivating a heart of obedience—not out of fear of punishment, but out of gratitude for what Christ has done for you.
    Identify a New Master: Remember, true freedom means being a servant of righteousness. Identify areas where you’re tempted to fall back into sin, and proactively choose to serve Christ instead. Remember we are set free from sin and have become "slaves to righteousness" (Romans 6:18).
    Live Out Your Freedom: Finally, remember that freedom in Christ isn't just for your benefit but for others. Share the gospel with those around you who are still enslaved to sin. Demonstrate the transforming power of Christ through your actions, words, and relationships (Luke 4:18).

Pick one of the five applications given and share what it looks like when you implement it in your life.

Day 47: We Don't Measure Up - Jesus Did
We Always Fall Short
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
"...for all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God, 24 and all are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus. 25 God presented Christ as a sacrifice of atonement, through the shedding of his blood —to be received by faith. He did this to demonstrate his righteousness, because in his forbearance he had left the sins committed beforehand unpunished — 26 he did it to demonstrate his righteousness at the present time, so as to be just and the one who justifies those who have faith in Jesus."
Romans 3:23-26
Teaching
I've asked people going through this devotional to suggest subjects they'd like to see addressed in this devotional, and one person requested that we discuss the biblical definition of sin.
The biblical definition of sin is any thought, word, or action contrary to God’s law and character. It is rebellion against God (1 John 3:4) and a failure to live up to His holy standard (Romans 3:23). Sin separates humanity from God and brings death (Romans 6:23).
Greek Word: ἁμαρτία (Hamartia)
The Greek word "hamartia" means "to miss the mark" and was originally used in archery to describe an arrow that failed to hit the target. In the New Testament scriptures, the word is repeatedly translated as sin or sins because it conveys the idea of failing to live up to God's perfect righteousness. Sin is not just breaking rules but falling short of God's glory (Romans 3:23), whether through deliberate rebellion or unintentional failure.
We Don't Measure Up – Jesus Did
Romans 3:23 – "For all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God."
No exceptions. No loopholes. Every person, from the most upright, altruistic citizen to the most hardened criminal, has missed the mark. God’s standard is perfect holiness, and we all fall short. We are that arrow that never reaches the target, landing in the dirt far from the bullseye. 
Sin has stained us, like ink spilled on a white garment, soaking deep into the fibers, leaving us unfit for God's presence. Notice that we "have sinned" past tense and that we "fall short" present tense. Trying to never fall short is like trying to fill a cracked jar with water—no matter how much is poured in, it always leaks out. We are always falling short.
But here’s the good news—the gospel isn’t about us reaching up to God, or about us trying hard to hit the mark. It’s about Jesus coming down to us and the cross is the bullseye for which He aimed.
Romans 3:24 – "And all are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came by Christ Jesus."
Though we’ve fallen short (Ecclesiastes 7:20; Romans 3:23), God doesn’t leave us in our failure. He justifies us—declares us righteous—not because we earned it, but because of His grace (Titus 3:7). This grace is free to us because Jesus paid the price to redeem us (1 Peter 1:18-19), buying us out of our slavery to sin with His own blood (Ephesians 1:7; Revelation 5:9). Grace means we didn’t work for it, and we don’t deserve it (Ephesians 2:8-9). Jesus did the work (John 19:30), and we receive the gift (Romans 6:23).
Romans 3:25 – "God presented Christ as a sacrifice of atonement, through the shedding of his blood—to be received by faith."
In the Old Testament, the high priest would sprinkle the blood of a sacrifice on the mercy seat to cover the sins of the people (Leviticus 16:15-16). That was a shadow of the real atonement—Jesus Himself would make! (Hebrews 9:11-12). His blood wasn’t sprinkled in a temple but poured out on the cross (Matthew 26:28). His sacrifice doesn’t just cover sin—it atones for it and removes it (Psalm 103:12; John 1:29). And how do we receive this cleansing? Not by effort but by faith (Habakkuk 2:4; Galatians 2:16).
Romans 3:26 – "He did this to demonstrate his righteousness at the present time, so as to be just and the one who justifies those who have faith in Jesus."
God is both just and merciful (Exodus 34:6-7; Psalm 85:10). He cannot ignore sin (Nahum 1:3), but in Jesus, He punishes sin and provides righteousness at the same time (2 Corinthians 5:21). The cross is where justice and mercy meet—God’s wrath was satisfied (Romans 5:9; 1 John 2:2), and His love was poured out (Romans 5:8). We don’t measure up, but Jesus did (Hebrews 4:15). And through Him, we stand before God not in our own failure, but in Christ’s perfect righteousness (Philippians 3:9).
So the conclusion is, that we must "finish our sentences." This means that yes, we sin daily (1 John 1:8), yes we fall short continually (Psalm 130:3). All day long. But let's not stop there, let's finish the sentence. We are justified freely by God's grace through believing in the cross (Romans 3:24-25). We are fully justified from all wrong and made fully righteous in Christ! (Isaiah 61:10; Colossians 2:13-14) Or to shorten it, we are always falling short in ourselves, we always fully measure up in Christ! (Romans 8:1; Hebrews 10:14)
Reflection: Have you been trying to measure up on your own? Rest in the finished work of Jesus. Trust in Him alone, and rejoice in the grace that justifies the undeserving and ungodly.
The Bible teaches us that sinning is falling short of the glory of God and that we all have done it. How does Jesus make us right with God?

ay 47: We Don't Measure Up - Jesus Did
Biblical Illustration: Jesus Loving His Own
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Loved in the World
In John 13:1, we read a profound truth: “Having loved His own who were in the world, He loved them to the end.” At first glance, this phrase—“in the world”—seems unnecessary. Where else would they be? But Scripture doesn’t waste words. The point is clear: Jesus loved His disciples where they were—not where they would be, not after they had measured up, but right there, in the world, in their weakness, failures, and struggles.
Consider the scene: Peter would soon deny Him, Thomas would doubt Him, and all would abandon Him in His darkest hour. Yet, Jesus didn’t withhold His love until they proved worthy. He loved them in their worldliness, their misunderstandings, and their sin. He bent down, took a towel, and washed their feet—dirt, filth, and all.
This is the love of Christ: He doesn’t wait for us to “hit the mark” before He embraces us. He meets us in our brokenness, in our failings, and loves us to the end. Just as He loved His disciples in the world, so He loves us—wherever we are, as we are, yet never leaving us there.


According to John 13:1, how does Jesus demonstrate His love for His disciples and ultimately us?
By waiting until they measured up to His standards.
By withholding His love during their moments of failure.
By loving them right where they were, in their weaknesses and sins, all the way to the cross and back.Correct
By loving them only after they proved themselves worthy.

Day 47: We Don't Measure Up - Jesus Did
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Loved as We Are
Since Jesus loved His disciples in the world, He loves us right where we are—sinful, struggling, and imperfect. We don’t have to clean ourselves up before coming to Him. He meets us in our failures, washes us in His grace, and loves us to the end.
So what should we do? First, come to Jesus daily—bring your sins, struggles, and burdens to Him in prayer, knowing He loves you as you are. Second, extend this same love to others—forgive freely, show grace, and love people where they’re at, just as Jesus loves you. Let His love shape how you live today.
When we fall short, we run to Him, not from Him. And just as He loves us in our weakness, we are called to love others the same way—meeting them where they are, pointing them to Christ.
Jesus' love is powerful. It receives us dirty and makes us clean, receives us broken and makes us whole, receives us wandering and brings us back, receives us as those who miss the mark and makes a bullseye with us!
How can you intentionally extend grace and love to someone in your life today, meeting them where they are just as Jesus meets you?

Day 48: Godly Sorrow
Change Laughing Into Mourning
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
Come near to God and he will come near to you. Wash your hands, you sinners, and purify your hearts, you double-minded. 9 Grieve, mourn and wail. Change your laughter to mourning and your joy to gloom. 10 Humble yourselves before the Lord, and he will lift you up.
James 4:8-10
One of the things that habitual sin can do to us is make us goofy and jokey, always trying to compensate for the continual shame we feel from disobeying God. Godly sorrow is the solution. 
Notice James 4:8-10:
James 4:8 – “Draw near to God, and He will draw near to you. Cleanse your hands, you sinners, and purify your hearts, you double-minded.”
The call to draw near to God is an invitation drenched in grace. We who were once far off—lost in sin and separated from Him—are now brought near through the blood of Christ (Ephesians 2:13). But drawing near means more than a casual nod toward heaven. We draw near, our hands stained with sin, and acknowledge our desperate need for washing. We draw near, our hearts divided between God and the world, and acknowledge our desperate need for purification. We draw near, not by our efforts, but by looking to Christ, the Lamb of God, who takes away our sin (John 1:29). His sacrifice cleanses us, His Spirit renews us, and His love draws us near.
James 4:9 – “Be wretched and mourn and weep. Let your laughter be turned to mourning and your joy to gloom.”
This verse seems harsh at first glance. Shouldn’t joy be the mark of a Christian? Absolutely. But there is a joy that comes from the world—a laughter that numbs us to sin, a fleeting happiness that ignores the weight of our rebellion against God. True joy begins with true sorrow. The gospel calls us to mourn over sin, to see it for what it is: an offense against a holy God and the reason Christ was crucified. This is not mere guilt; it is godly sorrow that leads to repentance (2 Corinthians 7:10). When we see our sin in the light of the cross, we cannot laugh it off—we mourn it. And in that mourning, we find mercy.
James 4:10 – “Humble yourselves before the Lord, and He will exalt you.”
Here is the beautiful end of godly sorrow: exaltation through Christ. The world tells us to lift ourselves up, to wash our own guilt by stopping certain behaviors, and to build our own worth. But the gospel teaches the opposite—humility before God is the path to glory. Jesus Himself humbled Himself to the point of death, even death on a cross (Philippians 2:8) because He loved you so much. Because of this, God exalted Him, and in Christ, we too will be lifted up—not by our works, but by His grace. When we lay ourselves low, mourning over our sins and confessing our need for Him, He lifts us up with forgiveness, fills us with His Spirit, and promises us eternal joy.
Let us then draw near with confidence, mourn our sin with sincerity, and humble ourselves before the One who humbled Himself for us. In Christ, sorrow turns to joy, and mourning is met with mercy.
According to James 4:8-10, what is the purpose of godly sorrow?
To make us feel constant shame and guilt.
To help us compensate for our habitual sin with laughter.
To encourage us to lift ourselves up by our own efforts.
To lead us to repentance and find mercy in Christ.Correct



Day 48: Godly Sorrow
Biblical Illustration: The Passover Lamb and Bitter Herbs
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
In the Old Testament, the Israelites were commanded to eat the Passover Lamb with bitter herbs (Exodus 12:8). The bitter herbs were not merely a side dish; they were a powerful symbol of the bitterness of slavery in Egypt. As the Israelites ate, they remembered their years of oppression under Pharaoh, the harshness of their bondage, and the deep sorrow that came with it. This was not a time for celebration but for solemn reflection on their bitterness in slavery and, at the time, their desperate need for deliverance.
Likewise, Christians are called to mourn over our sin, which enslaves us far more deeply than the Israelites were ever bound by Pharaoh. Our sin separated us from God, leaving a bitterness in our souls, and even as believers every time we give in to sin we experience some bitterness again. 
But as the Israelites ate the Lamb, looking back on God's deliverance, we too feed on the Lamb of God, Jesus Christ (John 1:29). Christ, our Passover Lamb, was slain for our sins (1 Corinthians 5:7), and through His sacrifice, we are set free from the bitter slavery of sin. 
Yet, as we partake of Him—remembering His suffering and sacrifice—we are reminded of the bitterness of our sin. It is a mourning that leads to joy, for just as the Israelites were delivered from Egypt, so we are delivered from sin and death through the Lamb of God. In our sorrow, we find true joy in the redemption He provides.
How do the bitter herbs in the Passover meal help us understand the bitterness of sin and the deliverance Jesus offers?

	
Day 48: Godly Sorrow
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Changing Our Laughter into Mourning
To change our laughter into mourning, we must first recognize the seriousness of our sin before a holy God. Jesus calls us to repentance, which begins with godly sorrow (Matthew 5:4). This mourning isn’t about wallowing in guilt, but about understanding the weight of what Christ bore on the cross for us. As we reflect on His sacrifice, we mourn the sin that caused His suffering. Yet, this sorrow is not without hope—it leads to transformation. Confess your sin, mourn it deeply, and embrace the joy of forgiveness and new life in Christ. Let this humble you to live differently.
What effect does reflecting on your sin and Christ's sacrifice have on your repentance and the way you live your life?

Day 49: Godly Sorrow Part 2
Godly Sorrow Produces Real Change
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
See what this godly sorrow has produced in you: what earnestness, what eagerness to clear yourselves, what indignation, what alarm, what longing, what concern, what readiness to see justice done. At every point you have proved yourselves to be innocent in this matter.
2 Corinthians 7:11
Teaching
Because godly sorrow is so important to freedom, and so many other areas in the Christian life, we will continue the topic today with a study of 2 Corinthians 7.
2 Corinthians 7:8
 "For even if I made you grieve with my letter, I do not regret it; though I did regret it, for I see that that letter grieved you, though only for a while."

Paul begins by acknowledging that his first letter to the Corinthians caused grief. But this wasn’t just emotional turmoil; it was a wake-up call that led to transformation. In the same way, the gospel confronts us with the weight of our sin, brings us sorrow, and causes us to mourn (Matthew 4:4)—but not to leave us there (Romans 6:23). Like a refiner's fire, godly sorrow purifies and brings us to repentance (Malachi 3:2-3, 2 Corinthians 7:10). Just as a wound may hurt when cleaned, but is necessary for healing. Jesus bore our sins on the cross so that when we grieve, we can turn to Him for healing, not despair (Matthew 11:28-30).
2 Corinthians 7:9
 "As it is, I rejoice, not because you were grieved, but because you were grieved into repenting. For you felt a godly grief, so that you suffered no loss through us."

Paul rejoices because the grief led to repentance—a change of heart and direction. The sorrow that the Corinthians felt wasn’t just sadness, but a turning back to God. The gospel leads us to a sorrow that drives us to repentance, not just remorse. In Christ, we experience a sorrow that moves us to abandon our sins and embrace His forgiveness. When we respond to the gospel with godly sorrow, we lose nothing; instead, we gain everything—new life in Christ.
A purposeful gazing at the cross, understanding He is bleeding and dying for my sins, is the best way to gain godly sorrow followed by unspeakable joy.
2 Corinthians 7:10
 "For godly grief produces a repentance that leads to salvation without regret, whereas worldly grief produces death."

Godly sorrow produces repentance, which leads to salvation. This isn’t just a temporary feeling but a life-altering change. Worldly grief, on the other hand, is only regret without transformation, leading to death. The gospel provides a lasting change—one that doesn’t just make us feel bad about our sin but empowers us to turn away from it and live for Christ. In Him, our sorrow becomes a gateway to eternal life, not something that drags us down.
2 Corinthians 7:11
 "For see what earnestness this godly grief has produced in you, but also what eagerness to clear yourselves, what indignation, what fear, what longing, what zeal, what punishment! At every point you have proved yourselves innocent in the matter."

The Corinthians’ godly sorrow bore fruit—it led to real action. Their repentance wasn’t passive; it was active, involving every part of their being. This is the kind of change the gospel calls for. True repentance brings a desire to make things right, to clear our name, and to live with a zeal for God. The gospel isn’t just about feeling sorry; it’s about responding with a transformed life that seeks to reflect Christ in all things.
Closing Thought:
Godly sorrow isn’t about the guilt that keeps us stuck; it’s the sorrow that leads to real change through the gospel of Jesus Christ. When we encounter our sins and turn to the cross, God doesn’t leave us in shame but transforms us into new creations, filled with joy and zeal to live for Him.
According to 2 Corinthians 7:8-11, how does godly sorrow differ from worldly grief?

Day 49: Godly Sorrow Part 2
Biblical Illustration: Peter
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
A powerful example of godly sorrow leading to real change is the Apostle Peter. 
After Jesus was arrested, Peter denied Him three times, as Jesus had predicted. In Luke 22:61-62, after Peter’s third denial, the rooster crowed, and Jesus turned and looked at him. The weight of that look caused Peter to feel deep sorrow—“And he went out and wept bitterly” (Luke 22:62). This was not just a moment of regret; it was a godly sorrow that pierced his heart and led him to repentance.
Peter’s grief was rooted in the realization of his failure and sin against Jesus, whom he had boldly claimed to follow. But this sorrow didn’t lead to despair. Instead, it was the beginning of a transformation. In John 21:15-17, after Jesus’ resurrection, He restores Peter by asking him three times, “Do you love me?” Each time, Peter responds affirmatively, and Jesus commissions him to feed His sheep. Peter’s sorrow led to repentance, which led to restoration and commissioning for service.
Peter's life illustrates the power of godly sorrow. His failure didn’t define him; rather, it was through his repentance and the grace of Jesus that he was radically changed. Peter went from denying Christ to boldly proclaiming the gospel, ultimately laying down his life for the faith. His story shows how godly sorrow produces real change—one that leads to restoration and a new purpose in Christ.
How did Peter's godly sorrow and Jesus' grace lead to Peter's transformation and restoration?
