Submitted 3 months ago
Godly sorrow leads to transformation, not just regret. When we recognize our sin, we must respond like David, Peter, and others—by turning to Jesus in repentance. 
Remember, true repentance isn’t just feeling bad; it’s a heart change that leads to action (Ezekiel 36:26-27). If we’ve wronged others, we seek to make things right, like Zacchaeus (Luke 19:8). If we’ve denied Christ, we, like Peter, come to Him for restoration (John 21:15-17). The gospel empowers this change—through Jesus' sacrifice, we are forgiven and made new (2 Corinthians 5:17).
If you've been flippant, shallow, and laughing all the time, rather than serious, earnest, and mindful, you might be missing the depth and purpose that come with a life grounded in real conviction and intention (James 4:8-10). Ask God to give you a heart of godly sorrow that leads to lasting transformation, reflecting Christ in all areas of your life (2 Corinthians 7:10).
How can you cultivate godly sorrow and true repentance in your own life, and allow the gospel to transform you into a new creation in Christ?

Day 50: Hope Has a Name
Jesus is our Hope
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
Praise be to the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ! In his great mercy he has given us new birth into a living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead.
1 Peter 1:3
Teaching
My sin led me to become absolutely hopeless (Job 17:15)! It was as if I were locked in a prison cell with no key and no way out. After decades of slavery to sin (John 8:34), I had become distraught, unable to think straight (Romans 1:21), like a man dragging heavy chains that grew tighter with each step (Lamentations 1:14). Years of pornography, gluttony, deception, and greed had left me in total despair.
Do you understand this too? Sin leads us to inevitable hopelessness (Ephesians 2:12), and to increasing despair, just as a prisoner eventually stops believing in freedom (Psalm 107:10-11). This is one reason for the exponential increase in drugs that the psychiatric community has renamed "meds". These mind-altering chemicals are advertised to help with despair but in reality, they lead to bondage (and sometimes to death Proverbs 14:12). At least they have been required to state "may lead to suicidal or homicidal ideations" on the label. They should just include John 10:10 on the label.
What happened to me is that Jesus Christ came to me right in the pit of despair (Psalm 40:2), lifted my head to look up at Him (Psalm 3:3), showed me the lengths of love that He went to for me when He exchanged places with me and died my death (2 Corinthians 5:21), then He led me to invite Him to reign in my life as King and Lord (Romans 10:9). This has made me "addicted" to the Scriptures (Jeremiah 15:16) and to happily become Jesus' captive (2 Corinthians 2:14).
And now I've discovered something: I am filled with hope! Even if I'm having a bad day, or if I'm reminded of my failures, or if I see evidence of my past life, I still have hope! The reason is that my hope is entirely in what God did for me, and that hope has a name: His Name is Jesus (1 Peter 1:3-4)!
The importance of this cannot be overstated, because without hope we are an easy target for Satan, low-hanging fruit for him to pick off and drag away. But as our hope becomes ever attached to Jesus Christ, we realize our hope will never die because death has no hold on Jesus. "For we know that since Christ was raised from the dead, he cannot die again; death no longer has mastery over him" (Romans 6:9).
We're going to study this topic for the next day or two.
How does hope, or lack of it, affect your response to temptations toward sin?

Day 50: Hope Has a Name
Biblical Illustration: Man From The Garasenes
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
One man who exemplifies this journey from hopelessness to hope in Christ is the demon-possessed man in the region of the Gerasenes (Mark 5:1-20). He was utterly lost, living among the tombs—an outcast, enslaved by a legion of demons, unable to be restrained even by chains (Psalm 107:10-11). His condition mirrors the grip of sin: isolated, tormented, and hopeless (Ephesians 2:12). He was beyond human help, his mind shattered, his conscience seared, his heart was darkened and his body scarred from self-destruction (Romans 1:21).
But then Jesus came (Luke 19:10). No chains could bind this man, yet at a single command from Christ, the demons fled (Mark 5:8). Jesus cast them into a herd of pigs, demonstrating His authority over evil (Colossians 2:15). The man, once naked and wild, was now clothed and in his right mind, sitting at the feet of Jesus (Psalm 40:2). His transformation was complete.
This is the power of the gospel! Just as Jesus freed this man from bondage, He rescues us from the chains of sin (John 8:36). His death and resurrection break the power of hopelessness (Hebrews 2:14-15), and His Spirit fills us with new life (1 Peter 1:3-4). Like the healed man, we are sent to proclaim, “Look what Jesus has done for me!”—because true hope has a name, and His name is Jesus (Romans 6:9)!
What does the account of the demon-possessed man in the region of the Gerasenes teach us about the hope that Jesus brings to those in despair?

Day 50: Hope Has a Name
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Freedom from sin comes with increasing hope. Hope that we're forgiven, hope that our past was nailed to a cross and buried in a tomb, hope that there is no condemnation for us, hope that Jesus is preparing a place for us, hope that He's coming soon to take us to be with Him. We must learn, and grow in the understanding that all our hope is wrapped up in Jesus and His loving work for us.
For the practical application of this lesson, please think through each passage below, and see how Jesus is  the hope of every believer.

    1 Timothy 1:1 – "Paul, an apostle of Christ Jesus by the command of God our Savior and of Christ Jesus our hope." Jesus is directly called our hope, making it clear that true hope is found in Him alone.

    Colossians 1:27 – "To them God has chosen to make known among the Gentiles the glorious riches of this mystery, which is Christ in you, the hope of glory." Jesus, living in believers, is our hope of glory, meaning all our hope is wrapped up in Him.

    Titus 2:13 – "While we wait for the blessed hope—the appearing of the glory of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ." Jesus is described as the blessed hope, showing that our future and eternal hope is in Him.

    Hebrews 6:19-20 – "We have this hope as an anchor for the soul, firm and secure. It enters the inner sanctuary behind the curtain, where our forerunner, Jesus, has entered on our behalf." Jesus is our anchor of hope, securing us eternally in God's presence.

    John 14:6 – "Jesus answered, ‘I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through me.’" Because Jesus is the way, the truth, and the life, He is the very definition of hope for those seeking salvation.

Each of these verses points to Jesus as the embodiment of hope, making it clear that Hope has a name, and His name is Jesus!

How can you embrace the hope found in Jesus Christ to overcome sin and live a life filled with joy and purpose?

Day 51: Hope Is Kept For You
Your Hope Is Stored Up In Heaven
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
Praise be to the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ! In his great mercy he has given us new birth into a living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead, 4 and into an inheritance that can never perish, spoil or fade. This inheritance is kept in heaven for you, 5 who through faith are shielded by God’s power until the coming of the salvation that is ready to be revealed in the last time. 6 In all this you greatly rejoice, though now for a little while you may have had to suffer grief in all kinds of trials.
1 Peter 1:3-6
We live in a world filled with temptation, discouragement, and the relentless pull of sin; plus, our flesh is so weak and the devil so subtle. Without the hope of Christ, we are like a ship tossed on the waves, driven by our desires and enslaved to our failures. But God, in His mercy, has given us a living hope—a sure and steady anchor that holds us firm. This hope is not a vague wish or an uncertain dream. Our hope has a name: Jesus Christ. 
Let's look at 1 Peter 1:3-6 in some depth to see how Jesus is our hope and how our hope will always live because He does.
1 Peter 1:3 – "Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ! According to his great mercy, he has caused us to be born again to a living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead."
Our hope is rooted in God’s mercy, not our merit. He has given us a living hope "according to His great mercy!" Left to ourselves, we are dead in sin (Ephesians 2:1), unable to break free from its grip. But God, through the resurrection of Jesus, has given us new life. Just as Jesus rose from the grave in victory, we are raised with Him, born again into a living hope—one that death cannot defeat and sin cannot extinguish. What great news!
1 Peter 1:4 – "To an inheritance that is imperishable, undefiled, and unfading, kept in heaven for you."
Everything in this world fades. Money, health, sex, relationships—none of them can give lasting security. But in Jesus, we have an eternal inheritance. His righteousness, His victory, and His kingdom belong to us because we belong to Him. Unlike earthly treasures that can be stolen or ruined, our salvation is imperishable—secured by the finished work of Jesus on the cross.
1 Peter 1:5 – "Who by God's power are being guarded through faith for a salvation ready to be revealed in the last time."
It is God’s power, not ours, that keeps us saved. If salvation depended on our strength, we would lose it in a moment. But the gospel is not about us holding onto God—it is about Jesus dying for us and God holding onto us (John 10:28-29). His power guards us until the day we stand before Him, fully redeemed.
1 Peter 1:6 – "In this you rejoice, though now for a little while, if necessary, you have been grieved by various trials."
Trials test our faith, but they cannot steal our hope. Why? Because our hope is not in circumstances, but in Jesus Himself. The One who conquered sin and death is alive, and He will bring us safely home. No matter what we face today, no matter our temptations, no matter our weak flesh; we can rejoice—because our hope has a name, and His name is Jesus. He died for us, so He will keep us to the end! You should have a living hope, a powerful hope, a lasting hope in Him!
Question: According to 1 Peter 1:3-6, what is the nature of the hope that believers have in Jesus Christ?
A temporary feeling based on our merit.
A living hope rooted in God's mercy and the resurrection of Jesus Christ.Correct
An inheritance that can be stolen or ruined.



Day 51: Hope Is Kept For You
Biblical Illustration: Saul to Paul
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
A perfect biblical illustration of this teaching is the Apostle Paul before and after his encounter with Jesus.
Paul (formerly Saul) was a man driven by religious zeal but utterly without true hope. He relied on his own righteousness, believing that by strict obedience to the law, he could earn favor with God. He persecuted Christians, thinking he was doing God’s will (Acts 9:1-2). But despite all his efforts, he was spiritually dead—enslaved to sin, blinded by pride, and unable to save himself.
Then Jesus appeared to him on the road to Damascus (Acts 9:3-6). In a moment, Paul’s entire life changed. His hope was no longer in his works but in the risen Christ. He was “born again to a living hope” (1 Peter 1:3), given an eternal inheritance (1 Peter 1:4), and kept by God's power through faith (1 Peter 1:5). Even though he suffered greatly—beatings, imprisonments, and trials—he could rejoice, because his hope was no longer in this world, but in Jesus (1 Peter 1:6).
Paul’s life proves that self-effort cannot save, but the gospel can. The hope of Jesus transforms enemies of God into beloved children, turning lost sinners into faithful saints who rejoice even in suffering. Hope has a name, and His name is Jesus.
How does the transformation of Paul illustrate the power of the gospel and the living hope found in Jesus Christ?

Day 51: Hope Is Kept For You
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Practically, here is how to anchor our hope to the Person and work of Jesus Christ:

    Repent and believe the gospel (Mark 1:15)—turn from self-reliance and trust in Jesus’ finished work.
    Remember your identity in Christ (2 Corinthians 5:17). You are no longer defined by your failures but by the righteousness of Jesus.
    Rejoice in your salvation daily (Psalm 51:12), knowing that your inheritance is secure.
    Preach the gospel to yourself daily (Romans 8:1). Remind yourself that there is no condemnation in Christ and that His grace is sufficient.
    Share this hope with others (Matthew 28:19-20), offering the same life-changing hope you’ve received.

Our hope, in Jesus, cannot fail.
How can you incorporate these practices into your daily life to anchor your hope in Jesus Christ and experience the transformative power of the gospel?

Day 52: Which Covenant Are You Under?
The Law Stirs Up Sin
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
The sting of death is sin, and the power of sin is the law. 57 But thanks be to God! He gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ.
1 Corinthians 15:56-57
Teaching
If you are trying to keep God's Old Covenant, Moral, 10 Commandment laws, to be right with God, or grow into Christ-likeness, you will live under the power of sin and remain in bondage! That's a hard statement, yet true. Notice what God's Word says in 1 Corinthians 15:56-58.
"The sting of death is sin, and the power of sin is the law." – 1 Corinthians 15:56
The law of God is holy, righteous, and good (Romans 7:12), yet here we see that it gives power to sin. How? The law commands perfect obedience, but our flesh rebels against it, so whatever God tells us not to do we want to do, and whatever He tells us to do we don't want to do (Romans 7:7-8). 
I used to travel as a pilot and one time I was laying over at a hotel that I had been to multiple times before. The backside of this hotel faced a small lake, and during my layover this time I saw something that I had not seen before: people were fishing off the outdoor balconies of their hotel rooms. Then I discovered why: management had posted a new sign that said, "No fishing off hotel room balconies." Apparently this "do not fish" sign made people want to fish!
The Law of God demands righteousness and obedience, but provides no power to attain it, and even stirs up sinful desires within us. The Law requires us to spiritually obey perfectly, but we are powerless to do so in our flesh (Romans 7:18).
I remember reading of a truck driver descending a mountain pass when all of a sudden his brakes went out. As his truck began picking up speed, He noticed a speed limit sign. That sign required him to slow down upon penalty of a fine, yet he could not do so (fortunately he could take a slight turn to the right onto a gravel road up a hill, designed to stop runaway trucks, which is what he did). That is the purpose of God's Law: it tells us what to do and not to do (like the speed limit sign for the trucker) and how we are powerless to obey (like the truck without brakes).
This is why we need Jesus—He alone fulfills the law’s demands (Matthew 5:17) and frees us from its condemnation (Romans 8:1). The bloody cross of Jesus, far from stirring up sin, actually crucifies it. 
"But thanks be to God, who gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ." – 1 Corinthians 15:57
If sin leads to death and the law gives sin its power, how can we be free? The answer is Jesus Christ! Through His death and resurrection, He took our punishment, fulfilled the law’s righteous requirements, and broke the power of sin. "For what the law was powerless to do because it was weakened by the flesh, God did by sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh to be a sin offering. And so he condemned sin in the flesh, 4 in order that the righteous requirement of the law might be fully met in us, who do not live according to the flesh but according to the Spirit" (Romans 8:3-4).
The victory we could never achieve on our own is given to us through Jesus. Notice Paul says "gives us the victory"—it’s a gift, not something we earn. We receive it by faith, trusting in the finished work of Jesus Christ. His resurrection is our assurance that our sins and our death have been defeated forever.
"Therefore, my beloved brothers, be steadfast, immovable, always abounding in the work of the Lord, knowing that in the Lord your labor is not in vain." – 1 Corinthians 15:58
The word "therefore" means "because of", as if to say "because of Jesus' death and resurrection, and our victory in Him", or "because of Jesus' victory over sin on the cross, and His victory over death through resurrection" we are called to stand firm. Don’t waver between law and grace, guilt and freedom. We are immovable in the gospel. Instead of striving to earn righteousness, we now abound in the work of the Lord—not to be saved, but because we are saved. Every act of faith, every moment of sharing the gospel, and every sacrifice made for Christ is never wasted. Jesus' resurrection guarantees that our labor has eternal significance.
Conclusion
The law exposes sin, but Jesus defeats it. The law brings condemnation, but Jesus brings justification. The law demands righteousness, but Jesus gives it. 
To run and work the law commands
but gives me neither feet nor hands.

But better news the gospel brings
It bids me fly and gives me wings
John Bunyan
Jesus Christ lives, and we are ultimately victorious because the One who overcame the grave overcomes sin in us. Let this truth anchor you today—stand firm in Christ, and live in the power of His resurrection.
How does Jesus free us from the power of sin and the condemnation of the law?
By teaching us how to perfectly obey the law
By fulfilling the law’s demands and taking our punishment through His death and resurrectionCorrect
By removing all moral standards so that sin no longer exists

Day 52: Which Covenant Are You Under?
Biblical Illustration: The Israelites
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
The Israelites at Mount Sinai are a perfect picture of how the law stirs up sin but Jesus brings victory. When God gave the Ten Commandments, the people initially responded with confidence: “All that the Lord has spoken we will do” (Exodus 19:8). Yet, before Moses even returned from the mountain, they were worshiping a golden calf (Exodus 32:1-6).
The law had exposed their hearts. Rather than producing righteousness, it revealed their rebellion. They had seen God's power, heard His voice, and agreed to obey, yet their sin still overcame them. The law condemned them, and 3,000 people died that day as a result (Exodus 32:28).
Now contrast this with Acts 2—another event at Mount Zion, where the gospel was preached at Pentecost. Instead of 3,000 people dying, 3,000 people were saved (Acts 2:41). What made the difference? Jesus Christ! His death and resurrection brought grace instead of judgment, and life instead of death. The law demanded holiness, but Christ fulfilled it for us.
Israel’s failure under the law shows our need for Christ’s victory. Where the law exposes sin, Jesus forgives it. Where the law condemns, Jesus saves. Through Him, we stand forgiven and victorious!
What does the contrast between Mount Sinai and Pentecost reveal about the law and Jesus?

Day 52: Which Covenant Are You Under?
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Practical Application: Standing Firm in Christ’s Victory
Since Jesus has given us victory over sin and death, we must stand firm in the gospel (1 Corinthians 15:58). This means:

    Be steadfast—hold fast to Christ’s finished work, not relying on your own efforts or law-keeping (Galatians 2:16). When doubts or challenges arise, remember that Jesus has already accomplished everything necessary for your salvation.
    Be immovable—refuse to be shaken by guilt or legalism, knowing Jesus has fulfilled the law (Romans 8:1-4). Don’t let past mistakes or the pressure to “measure up” lead you away from His grace.
    Abound in the Lord’s work—serve joyfully, share the gospel, and walk in love (Ephesians 2:10). The victory we have in Christ compels us to live out His love in action, bringing His light to the world around us.

How can you stand firm in the victory of Jesus in your daily life, trusting in His finished work and letting that truth guide your actions and attitudes?

Day 53: New Covenant Vs. Old Covenant
The Ministry That Brings Righteousness
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Key Passage
If the ministry that brought condemnation was glorious, how much more glorious is the ministry that brings righteousness!
2 Corinthians 3:9
Teaching
Condemnation and repeating sinful behaviors go hand in hand and are a vicious cycle (Romans 7:19, 24). One prompts the other. The only way to break this cycle is for condemnation to be removed from us (Romans 8:1). The only way this could happen is for Jesus to take our condemnation and die our death (2 Corinthians 5:21), and in so doing, He rescues us from the vicious cycle (Galatians 1:4).
When Jesus died, He initiated a New Covenant (Luke 22:20, Hebrews 9:15). This means the Old Covenant no longer exists because Jesus fulfilled the terms of it (Matthew 5:17, Hebrews 8:13). If you pay off the mortgage on your house, you are no longer bound by the terms of the contract—you no longer owe anything, and you are debt-free (Colossians 2:14). When Jesus died on the cross, He met the terms of the Old Covenant (perfect obedience) (Hebrews 4:15), He paid off our debt to God (1 Peter 2:24), and He set us free from our sin (John 8:36). Now we are under a New Covenant with better promises and different terms (Hebrews 8:6).
2 Corinthians 3 compares and contrasts the Old Covenant Law and the New Covenant Gospel. If you want to be free, you need to understand the difference between these two covenants and make sure you are not attempting to fulfill an already fulfilled agreement. Notice the truth that sets us free:
2 Corinthians 3:7 – "Now if the ministry of death, carved in letters on stone, came with such glory that the Israelites could not gaze at Moses’ face because of its glory, which was being brought to an end..."
The Old Covenant, represented by the 10 commandments Law carved on stone, was a ministry of death because it revealed sin but could not remove it. The glory of the Law was temporary, fading like the radiance on Moses’ face. This Law condemned us because we could never keep it perfectly. But Jesus did. He met the Law’s demands, bore its penalty, and introduced a New Covenant in His blood (Luke 22:20).
2 Corinthians 3:8 – "...will not the ministry of the Spirit have even more glory?"
The New Covenant is superior because it is a ministry of the Spirit. Instead of external laws that condemn, we now have the indwelling Holy Spirit who transforms us from within. The Spirit applies the finished work of Christ to our hearts, leading us in righteousness not through fear, but through love and grace (Romans 8:1-2). The glory of the New Covenant does not fade; it is eternal, for it is grounded in Christ’s finished work.
2 Corinthians 3:9 – "For if there was glory in the ministry of condemnation, the ministry of righteousness must far exceed it in glory."
The Old Covenant brought condemnation (2 Corinthians 3:9); the New Covenant brings righteousness (Romans 3:21-22). Under the Law, we stood guilty before God (James 2:10), like prisoners awaiting a just sentence (Galatians 3:22). Under Christ, we stand justified (Romans 5:1), the Judge having declared us innocent by paying for our crimes Himself (Isaiah 53:4-5)! His righteousness is credited to us by faith (Romans 4:5). No longer do we live under the shadow of failure and condemnation (Romans 8:1); we live in the light of Christ’s righteousness (John 8:12), which means we are fully accepted and eternally secure (Hebrews 10:14).
Through Jesus, we have been transferred from death to life (John 5:24), from condemnation to justification (Romans 8:33-34), from the Law to grace (Galatians 3:13). It's like being rescued from a sinking ship and placed on solid ground (Psalm 40:2). We are now under a New Covenant (Hebrews 8:6) where we are not only forgiven but transformed (2 Corinthians 5:17). This is the glory of the gospel! The glory of the New Covenant!
How is the New Covenant superior to the Old Covenant?


Day 53: New Covenant Vs. Old Covenant
Biblical Illustration: Woman Caught in Adultery
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
The woman caught in adultery (John 8:1-11) perfectly illustrates this teaching. She was condemned under the Law, with the religious leaders ready to stone her. Like all of us, she stood guilty, unable to justify herself. Imagine her shock and horror when she was "found out" and knew she was awaiting execution. 
But when she was brought before Jesus, He did not condemn her. Instead, He exposed the hypocrisy of her accusers and extended grace to her. "Neither do I condemn you; go and sin no more" (John 8:11).
This moment encapsulates the shift from the Old Covenant of condemnation to the New Covenant of grace. The Law could only pronounce her guilty, but Jesus, embodying the gospel, offered her righteousness and transformation. Just as she was freed from condemnation and given a new life, so are we when we come to Jesus in faith. He lifts us from guilt to grace, from judgment to justification, and calls us to live in the light of His righteousness.
What does Jesus' response to the woman caught in adultery illustrate?
 That sin has no consequences
That Jesus changed the Law to allow sin
The shift from condemnation under the Law to grace in the New CovenantCorrect

Day 53: New Covenant Vs. Old Covenant
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 3 months ago
Since we are under grace, we should walk in the freedom Jesus bought for us. 
First, we must daily remind ourselves of the gospel, meditating on Scripture (Colossians 3:16). This is essential, and it is how freedom comes to us personally.
Second, we should reject condemnation and trust in the righteousness of Christ (Romans 8:1). Let Scripture's "no condemnation for those in Christ Jesus" overcome your own voice of "I just don't measure up and continually fall short."
Third, we are called to extend grace to others, forgiving as we have been forgiven (Ephesians 4:32). Freedom means not only taking in grace but giving it out to others.
Finally, we must walk in holiness, empowered by the Spirit (Galatians 5:16), not attempting to fulfill the demands of the Old Covenant Law. As we walk by the Spirit, we reflect the glory of the New Covenant, living as transformed people who shine the light of Christ to the world (Matthew 5:16).
In what ways can you reject condemnation and embrace the righteousness of Christ today?


Day 54: The Boldness of New Covenant Faith
The New Covenant Makes You Bold
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 2 months ago
Key Passage
Therefore, since we have such a hope, we are very bold.
2 Corinthians 3:12
Teaching
Being under the Old Covenant law not only stirs up sin in our lives (Romans 7:5), but it makes us timid, cowardly, and fearful. It's like Adam and Eve hiding and trying to cover themselves because they feared God's presence (Genesis 3:7-10), almost like children covering their eyes and thinking they can't be seen. It's like Gideon, who hid from the Midianites (Judges 6:11) and had to ask for multiple signs from God before he believed (Judges 6:36-40). It's like Saul hiding among the baggage because of insecurity (1 Samuel 10:21-22), like a soldier shrinking back from battle, paralyzed by doubt and fear. Or like the ten spies who feared the giants in Canaan (Numbers 13:31-33), as if they were grasshoppers before giants, overwhelmed by their own smallness.
On the contrary, being under the New Covenant gospel makes us bold! "The righteous are bold as a lion" (Proverbs 28:1). "The members of the council were amazed when they saw the boldness of Peter and John, for they could see that they were ordinary men with no special training in the Scriptures. They also recognized them as men who had been with Jesus" (Acts 4:13). 
Notice this truth from 2 Corinthians 3:
2 Corinthians 3:10 – "For what was glorious has no glory now in comparison with the surpassing glory."
The law had a kind of glory, but it was a fading and condemning glory (2 Corinthians 3:7-9). It showed us our sin, like a mirror reflecting every flaw (James 1:23-25), making us self-conscious of our weaknesses, but provided no power to overcome it (Romans 8:3). It left us hopeless and despairing (Romans 7:24), it made us timid and fearful, like prisoners in chains (Galatians 3:23), aware of their captivity but powerless to break free.
However, the gospel of Jesus Christ has a surpassing glory—it does not fade and brings freedom! (2 Corinthians 3:10-11, 17). Under the New Covenant, we see the radiance of Christ’s finished work (Hebrews 1:3). Our sins are fully forgiven (Colossians 2:13-14), and our righteousness is complete in Him (2 Corinthians 5:21). We know ourselves to be like a beggar now clothed in royal robes (Zechariah 3:3-5), no longer defined by our rags.
Instead of looking at ourselves and despairing, we now fix our eyes on Jesus, the author and finisher of our faith (Hebrews 12:2). His gospel outshines the law like the sun outshines a candle—blazing, undeniable, and life-giving (John 1:4-5).
2 Corinthians 3:11 – "And if what was fading away came with glory, how much greater is the glory of that which lasts!"
The Old Covenant Law was temporary, like scaffolding that supports a building but is removed once the structure is complete (Hebrews 8:13). It was given to point us to Christ (Galatians 3:19, 24). But now, in the New Covenant, we have something permanent—the unbreakable promise of salvation by grace through faith in Jesus (Ephesians 2:8-9).
The cross is our victory over sin (Colossians 2:15), the battlefield where the enemy was defeated once and for all (1 Corinthians 15:54-57), and our Victor was bloodied to death for us but rose on the third day in great power (Romans 4:25). This lasting glory is not a heavy yoke of commandments (Acts 15:10), but a living, breathing relationship with our risen Savior (John 15:4-5). It is not our effort that sustains us but the indwelling power of the Holy Spirit (Galatians 5:25). He is like wind filling the sails of a ship (John 3:8), carrying it forward in strength and certainty, the Spirit of God enabling us to walk in holiness (Ezekiel 36:27) and live in boldness (2 Timothy 1:7).
2 Corinthians 3:12 – "Therefore, since we have such a hope, we are very bold."
Our confidence does not come from our own performance, which is like a house built on shifting sand, but from Christ’s perfect righteousness credited to us, which is like a firm foundation that cannot be shaken. This gives us boldness, confidence, assurance, and victory! Through faith in the gospel, we have:

    Boldness to overcome habitual sin, like a soldier charging into battle, confident in the victory already won (1 Corinthians 15:57).
    Boldness to trample on snakes of sin, like a warrior crushing serpents beneath his heel (Luke 10:19).
    Boldness to crucify our lusts, like a gardener killing every weed that chokes out new life (Galatians 5:24).
    Boldness to stamp out every visage of our past life, like a coroner closing the lid on a coffin (Romans 6:4).
    Boldness to proclaim the gospel without fear, like a herald running with good news to a weary, war-torn city (Ephesians 6:20).
    Boldness to enter God’s presence freely, like a child running into the open arms of a loving Father (Hebrews 4:16).

You see, the cross destroys fear and shame, replacing them with courage and confidence in the finished work of Christ and our standing with God.
Let us walk in the boldness of the gospel today, standing firm in our freedom, overcoming sin by the power of the cross, and proclaiming the Good News with courage!
What is one key difference between the Old Covenant and the New Covenant?
The Old Covenant gives power to overcome sin, while the New Covenant only reveals sin.
The Old Covenant and the New Covenant are the same in how they affect our relationship with God.
The Old Covenant was temporary and brought fear, while the New Covenant is permanent and brings boldness.Correct



Day 54: The Boldness of New Covenant Faith
Biblical Illustration: Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 2 months ago
Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego stood before King Nebuchadnezzar, facing the fiery furnace for refusing to bow to an idol (Daniel 3:16-18). They boldly declared, "Our God is able to deliver us, but even if He does not, we will not serve your gods." Their faith was not in their ability but in the power and promises of God.
This is the same boldness the gospel of Jesus Christ gives us today! Jesus faced death for us, conquering sin and the grave so that we might live free and fearless. Because of His victory, we, too, can stand firm in the face of temptation and trials, knowing that our true life is hidden with Christ in God (Colossians 3:3). Just as Jesus was with the three Hebrew boys in the fire (Daniel 3:25), Jesus is with us through every battle, empowering us to live boldly for Him!
How does knowing that Jesus is with us in every trial change the way we face challenges or stand firm in our faith?
it does not depend on what I do/did or what I am, but what He has done and is

    Pray daily for strength, knowing that Christ’s power is made perfect in our weakness (2 Corinthians 12:9).

(Colossians 3:16).

Day 55: Old Covenant Dullness Vs. New Covenant Freedom
The Old Covenant Dull Mind
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 22 days ago
Key Passage
But their minds were made dull, for to this day the same veil remains when the old covenant is read.
2 Corinthians 3:14
Those under the Old Covenant have spiritually dull minds. They can't see or perceive the truth, they can't understand the Scriptures, they can't live any differently. They are dull, powerless, and slow. They are in bondage, slaves to rules and laws, and incompetent in life. 
But oh my, the gospel corrects all this! It makes us bold and equipped, confident, renewed, empowered, and quick!
Notice how 2 Corinthians 3 teaches this:
Verse 13: "We are not like Moses, who put a veil over his face to prevent the Israelites from seeing the end of what was passing away."

Under the Old Covenant, Moses' face shone with the fading glory of the law (Exodus 34:29-30), like a candle flickering in the wind before going out (2 Corinthians 3:7). He covered his face so the people would not see its temporary nature (2 Corinthians 3:13). The law was never meant to be permanent or saving—it pointed to something greater (Galatians 3:24). That something is Jesus Christ, whose glory does not fade but increases in those who behold Him (2 Corinthians 3:18), like the dawn breaking into a brilliant sunrise (Proverbs 4:18).
Verse 14: "But their minds were made dull, for to this day the same veil remains when the old covenant is read. It has not been removed, because only in Christ is it taken away."

The law, apart from Christ, leaves people in spiritual dullness, like someone trying to read in the dark (2 Corinthians 3:14). The veil over their minds keeps them from seeing God's plan in its fullness (Isaiah 29:11-12), from experiencing God's power in its entirety (Romans 8:3), and from embracing the transformative grace He provides (John 1:17), leaving them blind to the depths of His love and purpose for them (2 Corinthians 4:4).
This is why legalism is lifeless—it binds rather than frees, like chains that hold a prisoner in a cell. But Jesus Christ tears the veil (Matthew 27:51) and brings life to those who turn to Him, like the sunrise breaking through a foggy morning (Malachi 4:2).
Verse 15: "Even to this day when Moses is read, a veil covers their hearts."

A heart veiled by the law remains hardened and unable to perceive grace, like a stone that cannot absorb water (Ezekiel 36:26). The law reveals sin, but only Jesus removes it (Romans 8:3-4). Without Christ, the Scriptures remain a closed book, full of rules but lacking redemption (1 Corinthians 2:14). The Bible becomes like a map with no key to understand its symbols, and people in this condition are in need of Jesus opening their minds so they can understand the Scriptures (Luke 24:45).
Verse 16: "But whenever anyone turns to the Lord, the veil is taken away."

Here is the glorious contrast! The moment a sinner turns to Jesus, the blindness is lifted, and they see clearly like a person removing sunglasses indoors and realizing the room is full of light (2 Corinthians 4:6). No longer trying to earn righteousness, they rest in Christ’s finished work (Romans 3:28). The gospel is revealed, and freedom floods in (John 8:36).
Verse 17: "Now the Lord is the Spirit, and where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is freedom."

The law binds, but the Spirit liberates (2 Corinthians 3:6). The Old Covenant required obedience to endless commands, yet it could not give life (Galatians 3:21). But in Christ, the Spirit empowers and transforms (Romans 8:11). The believer is free—not to sin, but to love and obey from a renewed heart (Ezekiel 36:26-27).
Verse 18: "And we all, who with unveiled faces contemplate the Lord’s glory, are being transformed into his image with ever-increasing glory, which comes from the Lord, who is the Spirit."

No more fading glory, no more hidden truth. In Christ, we behold God clearly and are transformed, like a rough diamond being polished into brilliance. The gospel is not a set of rules but a living power that changes us from one degree of glory to another, all by looking at Jesus! (2 Corinthians 3:18). Through Jesus, we go from dullness to dazzling light (Ephesians 5:8), from slavery to sonship (Galatians 4:7), from law to love (Romans 13:10), from uselessness to usefulness (2 Timothy 2:21), and from grace to glory (Romans 5:2).
Are you still trying to live under the law, or are you living in the freedom Christ gives?

Day 55: Old Covenant Dullness Vs. New Covenant Freedom
Biblical Illustration: Nicodemus
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 22 days ago
Consider the story of Nicodemus, a Pharisee and member of the Jewish ruling council, who came to Jesus at night (John 3:1-21). Nicodemus was a man deeply steeped in the law. He knew the Scriptures inside and out, and was "Israel's teacher" (John 3:10); but his mind remained dull, unable to grasp the spiritual truths Jesus was revealing. Though he was a teacher of Israel, he was blind to the new birth Jesus spoke of, thinking only in terms of the old covenant, bound by the rules and laws that could never bring new life. Like Moses’ veil, there was a covering over his understanding, preventing him from seeing the full glory of God’s plan in the gospel.
But Jesus, in His grace, revealed the solution: the gospel. He explained that unless one is born again, one cannot see the kingdom of God. He taught Nicodemus how to be born again, by looking at the suffering Savior uplifted on the cross, like the Israelites who were healed by looking at an uplifted serpent on a pole. 
This was a radical shift from the old covenant—a move from law to grace, from spiritual dullness to spiritual awakening. Jesus offered Nicodemus the living water of the Holy Spirit, which would remove the veil, giving him a new heart that could understand and embrace God’s transforming love.
Through Christ’s gospel work, the veil is taken away. Nicodemus, like all who turn to Jesus, would be empowered by the Spirit, no longer trapped by the letter of the law but freed to live in the glory of God's grace.
Have you looked, recently, at the uplifted suffering Savior and believed He was dying for you?

Day 55: Old Covenant Dullness Vs. New Covenant Freedom
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 22 days ago
Just as Nicodemus needed Jesus to remove the veil from his understanding, we, too, must turn to Christ for clarity and transformation. We come to Jesus the first time and we are saved. We come to Jesus daily and are sanctified.
To experience this, we can immerse ourselves in the gospel daily—reading Scripture through the lens of Christ’s finished work (John 5:39-40). Ask God to show you Jesus everywhere. He's like the earth in darkness, buried under the water and empty, but on the third day rising out of the grave and producing fruit (Genesis 1). He's like Adam placed into a deep sleep, having His side opened so that He might have a bride (Genesis 2:21-25 and Ephesians 5:25-33). He's the sacrifice that covers sinners, like when Adam and Eve were clothed in the skins of a sacrifice (Genesis 3:21). And on and on it goes, seeing Jesus clearly when we have the veil of the Law removed and the clarity of the gospel imparted to us.
We must pray for the Holy Spirit to open our hearts and minds (Ephesians 1:17-18), removing the dullness caused by legalism and self-effort. See Acts 16:14-15. 
When we embrace the gospel, we move from striving in our own strength to resting in Christ's power, living in freedom and continually being transformed into His image (2 Corinthians 3:18). This is the way to freedom, brothers and sisters.
Think of an area in your life where you've been striving in your own strength rather than resting in Christ's finished work. How might your approach and attitude change if you viewed your struggle through the lens of the gospel rather than through legalism or self-effort?

Day 56 The Power of God's Word
God's Word Powerfully Changes Us
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 20 days ago
Key Passage
 Jesus answered and said to them, "You are mistaken, not knowing the Scriptures nor the power of God."
Matthew 22:29
Teaching
For someone battling against sin that seeks to dominate them, there is one thing they must do: study God's Word. Read it like their life depends on it. Seek God in it, and find Jesus there. This is a non-negotiable, and will, over time, provide the power to set them free.
In Matthew 22:29, Jesus rebukes the Sadducees for their lack of knowing the Scripture and the power of God (Hosea 4:6), connecting these two concepts together. The Sadducees were questioning Jesus about the resurrection, yet they were blind to the fact that God's Word holds the ultimate authority (Psalm 119:89), revealing His power over all things, including life and death (Deuteronomy 32:39, 1 Samuel 2:6). 
Of course, you know that it is a spiritual battle to be studying in God's Word. If we were to commit to seeking God daily and earnestly, all hell seems to break out against us, and suddenly we become extremely busy, or very distracted, unable to concentrate, as if we had spiritual ADHD. 
Satan knows that God's Word is not just informational but transformational and that it always accomplishes what God sends it out to do (Isaiah 55:10-11). When we engage with it, we are tapping into the life-changing power that reshapes us from the inside out, from the heart to the actions (Ezekiel 36:26-27), and as we immerse ourselves in it we become just like clay that the Potter molds on the wheel (Jeremiah 18:6). God's Word and spiritual power are intimately connected, as inseparable as the sun and its warmth, bringing light and life wherever it shines (Malachi 4:2).
The Bible consistently teaches that God's Word is alive and powerful. Hebrews 4:12 tells us, "For the word of God is living and powerful, and sharper than any two-edged sword, piercing even to the division of soul and spirit, and of joints and marrow, and is a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart." This verse describes the Word of God as both a sword and a surgical instrument—able to cut through the surface and reveal the deepest parts of our being (Psalm 139:23-24, Proverbs 20:27). It exposes sin and enlightens our spiritual eyes (Psalm 19:7-8), it heals wounds (Psalm 107:20), and transforms our hearts (Ezekiel 11:19-20). When we surrender to its authority, the Word changes us, bringing us closer to Jesus and setting us free from sin (John 17:17).
One of the most powerful ways God's Word works in our lives is by setting us free from sins that we were previously in bondage to (1 Corinthians 6:9-11). His Word is the source of truth that frees us from the chains of sin, shame, and fear (Isaiah 61:1, Psalm 34:4-5). 
The gospel of Jesus Christ states that we are sinners saved by grace through His sacrifice on the cross, and this is the foundation of not only our salvation but also our freedom. Romans 6:18 declares, "And having been set free from sin, you became slaves of righteousness." The Word reveals God's plan of salvation through the suffering Savior (Genesis 3:15, Isaiah 53:10-11), and by believing it, we are liberated from spiritual death (Ezekiel 37:5-6, Daniel 12:2, John 5:24-25). But it also reveals the plan of sanctification and maturity through the same suffering Savior, and by believing it, we are liberated from spiritual slavery (Romans 6:6-7).
Oh how the world does miss this concept in their defining sin as a "disease", or "addiction", something acquired entirely apart from the will and choices of the person. This "disease" model is designed by Satan to keep the person enslaved because only the truth sets us free.
The power of God's Word empowers believers to live differently and mature in our walk with God. Romans 12:1-2 says, "And do not be conformed to this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind, that you may prove what is that good and acceptable and perfect will of God." As we meditate on God's Word, it renews our minds (Joshua 1:8, Psalm 1:2-3), shaping our actions, attitudes, and desires to align with His will. It changes us because God's Word is living, active, and eternal (Jeremiah 23:29, Isaiah 40:8).
The enemy knows the power of God's Word and trembles at it. Martin Luther captured this truth beautifully in his hymn, A Mighty Fortress Is Our God. He wrote:
 
"The prince of darkness grim,
We tremble not for him,
His rage we can endure,
One little word will fell him."
The "little word" is the powerful Word of God, the message of the cross. With it, we can stand firm against the forces of darkness and walk in the freedom Jesus purchased. Let us cling to God's Word, feed on it often, listen to it carefully, and obey it fully, for it is powerful enough to set us free and change our lives forever.
How does understanding the power of God's Word change your approach to Bible study, especially when facing distractions or spiritual resistance?

Day 56 The Power of God's Word
Biblical Illustration: Creation
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 20 days ago
In the beginning, there was nothing but darkness—formless, empty, lifeless. Then, suddenly, “God said, ‘Let there be light’; and there was light” (Genesis 1:3). At the mere speaking of His Word, light shattered the darkness, illuminating the void. God did not struggle, sweat, or labor—He simply spoke, and reality obeyed.
This pattern continued: “God said... and it was so” (Genesis 1:6-7, 9, 11). The waters separated at His command. Dry land appeared, lush with vegetation. The sun, moon, and stars took their place, shining in obedience. The oceans teemed with life, the skies filled with birds, and the earth was alive with creatures, all because God spoke.
God’s Word does more than create; it transforms. Just as the world was once dark and empty, so too are human hearts without Christ. Yet when God speaks through His Word, light pierces the darkness, bringing life where there was death, and order where there was chaos. "If anyone is in Christ, he is a new creation" (2 Corinthians 5:17).
This same Word that created the universe is the Word that sets captives free. When God speaks, everything changes. Darkness flees. Life begins. The power of His Word is unstoppable.
Give one example of how you have seen God's powerful Word at work in your life.

Practical Application
