Since God's Word creates, transforms, and sets free, we must immerse ourselves in it daily:

    Read it (Psalm 119:105)
    Meditate on it (Joshua 1:8)
    Believe it (John 8:31-32)
    Proclaim it (2 Timothy 4:2)

Most importantly, trust in the gospel—Jesus, the Living Word (John 1:1-14), who died and rose to free us from sin. This Word of the cross is our weapon against temptation (Matthew 4:4), our strength in weakness (Isaiah 40:8), and our light in darkness (Psalm 119:130).
Let’s not be like the Pharisees who "were in error because they did not know the Scriptures or the power of God" (Matthew 22:29). We must overcome the "sudden busyness", the "multiple distractions", and the "lack of focus" to be in God's Word daily.
What is one specific distraction keeping you from God's Word, and what practical step will you take today to overcome it?

Day 57: The Awesome Power of God's Word
God's Word Accomplishes Everything Needed
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 17 days ago
Key Passage
The law of the Lord is perfect, reviving the soul!
Psalm 19:7
Teaching
If you struggle with sin, as we all do, you need the Word of God to overcome your own thoughts, the whisperings of the devil, the busyness of life, and all the pull and temptation of sin. The Word of God studied, meditated on, prayed through, and applied will free you! Notice all that it does in our lives:
The Word Revives the Soul
"The law of the Lord is perfect, reviving the soul" (Psalm 19:7a).
God’s Word is not only true—it is perfect. Unlike human wisdom, behavioral psychology, recovery programs, etc. which change and falters, Scripture is flawless and complete. And what does it do? It revives the soul. Before we came to Christ, our souls were dead in sin (Ephesians 2:1), but through the gospel, we are made alive. Jesus said, “The words that I have spoken to you are spirit and life” (John 6:63). His Word gives new life to the weary, restoring hope and joy to those who believe the Word and trust in it. This is how the application of the Word sets us free because guilt is removed and hope is restored, we are revived and able to fight sin!
The Word Makes the Simple Wise
"The testimony of the Lord is sure, making wise the simple" (Psalm 19:7b).
During my years of sinful bondage, I was "simple", meaning I had a lack of discernment, lack of wisdom, and lack of spiritual power. God’s testimonies—His revealed truth—are sure, meaning they are firm and unchanging. The world seeks wisdom in philosophies, science, experience, and step-groups, but true wisdom is found in Christ. "Christ, in whom are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge" (Colossians 2:3). The gospel takes those who are simple, weak, and lost and makes them wise unto salvation (2 Timothy 3:15).
The Word Rejoices the Heart
"The precepts of the Lord are right, rejoicing the heart" (Psalm 19:8a).
Sin brings pleasure and makes us happy, temporarily, until our hearts become so burdened with guilt, bitterness, sorrow, loss and death that we lose all the fun. But God's Word rejoices the heart, delights the heart, and causes the heart to sing out praises to God! 
The commands of God are not burdensome but right, leading to joy that is "unspeakable and full of glory" (1 Peter 1:8). Obedience to Christ is not a dull duty—it is a delight. Jesus Himself said, "These things I have spoken to you, that my joy may be in you, and that your joy may be full" (John 15:11). When we walk in His Word, we experience deep and abiding and powerful joy, a joy that remains even in trials, even severe trials.
The Word Enlightens the Eyes
"The commandment of the Lord is pure, enlightening the eyes" (Psalm 19:8b).
Sin darkens our understanding (2 Corinthians 4:4), but God’s Word brings light. When Jesus came, He declared, “I am the light of the world. Whoever follows me will not walk in darkness, but will have the light of life” (John 8:12). Through the gospel, our spiritual eyes are opened, and we see the glory and beauty of Christ, and sin becomes grotesque and ugly to us.
The Word Endures Forever
"The fear of the Lord is clean, enduring forever" (Psalm 19:9a).
Unlike earthly things that fade, unlike the pleasure of sin that is temporary, God’s truth endures. Jesus said, “Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away” (Matthew 24:35). The gospel is eternal (Revelation 14:6), and those who believe in Jesus receive eternal life (John 3:16) and lasting freedom (John 8:32).
The Word is Righteous Altogether
"The rules of the Lord are true, and righteous altogether" (Psalm 19:9b).
God’s Word is true and righteous, revealing our need for Christ, the only righteous One, to make us righteous through His sacrifice, not our works. "For our sake he made him to be sin who knew no sin, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God" (2 Corinthians 5:21). The gospel is the ultimate expression of God's truth and righteousness, it is "righteous altogether".
Final Thought
The power of God's Word is seen in its ability to transform lives. It revives, makes wise, gives joy, enlightens, endures, and leads to righteousness. In Christ, we see the fullness of God’s Word made flesh (John 1:14). May we treasure it, trust it, and be transformed by it daily.
How does understanding the specific benefits of God's Word (reviving, making wise, bringing joy, etc.) change your view of daily Bible reading/study?

Day 57: The Awesome Power of God's Word
Biblical Illustration: The Ethiopian Eunuch
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 17 days ago
The Ethiopian eunuch in Acts 8:26-39 is a powerful example of the truths in Psalm 19:7-9 and how the gospel of Jesus Christ is the solution.
This man was a high-ranking official, yet he lacked true wisdom. He was reading the Scriptures but did not understand their meaning. His soul was searching, but he had no lasting joy. His eyes were blind to the truth, and he needed someone to guide him. He represents all of us before we come to Christ—seeking, but without understanding; religious, but without salvation.
Then God sent Philip, who explained that the passage he was reading (Isaiah 53:7-8) was about Jesus. As Philip shared the gospel, the eunuch’s eyes were enlightened. The Word of God revived his soul, making him wise unto salvation. Joy flooded his heart, and after believing, he eagerly requested baptism.
When he came up from the water, he "went on his way rejoicing" (Acts 8:39), just as Psalm 19:8 declares. The power of God's Word had transformed him. What he had once read without understanding now made sense in light of Christ and the powerful cross. Jesus was the answer he had been searching for, and through the gospel, he found life, wisdom, and joy.
What does the Ethiopian eunuch's transformation primarily illustrate?
The importance of having a Bible teacher
How God's Word transforms when understood through ChristCorrect
The value of reading Scripture repeatedly

Day 57: The Awesome Power of God's Word
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 17 days ago
Like the Ethiopian eunuch, we must approach Scripture with open hearts, seeking Christ in every passage (Luke 24:27). Here’s how we can live out this truth:

    Read the Word Daily – Let Scripture revive your soul (Psalm 119:25).
    Seek Understanding Through Christ – Ask the Holy Spirit for wisdom (John 16:13).
    Rejoice in the Gospel – Meditate on Christ’s finished work (Romans 5:1-2).
    Walk in Obedience – Live as doers of the Word (James 1:22).
    Share the Gospel – Help others see Jesus in Scripture (Acts 8:35).

God’s Word leads us to Christ—our wisdom, joy, and salvation.
How will you seek to approach the Scriptures differently this week?

Day 58: God's Word Has All We Need
The Sufficiency of God's Word
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 10 days ago
Key Passage
His divine power has given us everything we need for a godly life through our knowledge of him who called us by his own glory and goodness.
2 Peter 1:3
When I was first desiring to leave my sin behind, I read the Bible a lot. But I also thought I needed more: some wisdom from a psychologist to understand "why" I did these things or some insight from groups that focused on helping me "recover" from my "addiction." But these things were like trying to patch a leaky roof with a towel, thinking it could fix the problem—soaking up a little water but doing nothing to stop the storm.
And while we can learn something from everybody, God has left nothing out of His Word that we need "for life (physical) and godliness (spiritual)." Like a treasure chest that holds everything we could ever need, like a pantry containing every food available, the Bible has the complete solution that we need. It’s like a well-stocked storehouse in the middle of a famine, offering nourishment when everything else runs dry.
Notice how He teaches this truth in His Word:
2 Peter 1:3 – "His divine power has given us everything we need for a godly life through our knowledge of Him who called us by His own glory and goodness."

In 2 Peter 1:3, Peter makes a powerful declaration: God's divine power has given us everything we need for a godly life. The term divine power refers to the mighty, transcendent strength of God, emphasizing that His power is not limited by human weakness or circumstance (Ephesians 1:19-20). This power is sufficient—it provides everything we need (Philippians 4:19).
One of the churches that I pastored was called in to an addiction/recovery center to have a church service every Saturday for those who wanted to attend. I really enjoyed teaching God's Word there, but I noticed an interesting fact. Some of those people had been to addiction/recovery centers multiple times. I think the most someone had been to was 13 times! And he sure had a lot of information, a lot of knowledge, just no spiritual power (2 Timothy 3:7).
It's not mere information that we need to overcome sinful bondage, but truth applied (John 8:32). It's not simple teaching of common sense or the makeup of bodily chemicals that we need, it's spiritual power (1 Corinthians 2:4-5)! And the gospel in God's Word, as applied by God's Spirit, is the provision that contains the power (Romans 1:16).
This provision comes through our knowledge of Him—knowing Jesus Christ personally (John 17:3). Knowledge here (Greek: epignosis) refers not just to intellectual understanding but a deep, relational knowledge that transforms us (Colossians 1:9-10). God has called us to Himself, not by our own merit but by His glory and goodness—the perfect expression of who God is.
The gospel is at the heart of this calling: it is through the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus that we are drawn to God (John 12:32). Through His glory and goodness, we are empowered to live godly lives (Titus 2:11-12). The Word of God, centered on Christ and the gospel, is how we grow in that relationship and are equipped to reflect His nature in our lives (2 Corinthians 3:18).
2 Peter 1:4 – "Through these he has given us his very great and precious promises, so that through them you may participate in the divine nature, having escaped the corruption in the world caused by evil desires."

In this verse, Peter continues by emphasizing that God has given us His very great and precious promises (2 Peter 1:4). The Greek word for precious (Greek: timios) means something rare, valuable, and highly esteemed—like a priceless gem hidden in a field, waiting to be discovered (Matthew 13:44). These promises are not just comforting words, but powerful, life-changing declarations from God Who declares that He gave His own Son up for our forgiveness (Romans 8:32).
Through these promises, we participate in the divine nature—this is the ultimate transformation (2 Peter 1:4). The term participate (Greek: koinonia) suggests fellowship or sharing, like being invited into the inner circle of God's heart, where we share in the very nature of God (John 15:5), not becoming divine, but reflecting His character by being joined with Him through Christ (Romans 8:29). Through Christ and His promises, we are being made holy, just as He is holy, because we are in Him and He is in us—like branches connected to a vine, drawing nourishment from the source (John 15:4-5; 1 Peter 1:16).
Peter concludes by reminding us that these promises help us escape the corruption in the world caused by evil desires (2 Peter 1:4). The word corruption (Greek: phthora) refers to decay, destruction, and the moral degradation brought about by sin; it’s like a rusted car slowly falling apart, no longer functional (Romans 8:21). What the world calls "addiction" is like a trap that holds us captive (2 Timothy 2:26), but through the gospel of Jesus Christ, we are set free from the power of sin and are being transformed into the image of God, who is pure and righteous—like a tarnished mirror being cleaned and restored to reflect His perfect image (2 Corinthians 3:18).
Conclusion
The gospel of Jesus Christ is the source of both power and sufficiency for every believer. God's divine power gives us everything we need for a godly life, and through His great and precious promises, we partake in His nature, escaping the corruption of sin. The Word of God, rooted in the gospel, is not only powerful but sufficient to lead us to victory over sin and death, and into life with Christ, now and forever.
How does the sufficiency of God's Word, as described in 2 Peter 1:3-4, address the need for both spiritual and practical transformation in the believer's life?

Day 58: God's Word Has All We Need
Biblical Illustration: The Provision of Manna
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 10 days ago
In the wilderness, the Israelites faced extreme hunger and uncertainty after being freed from slavery in Egypt. God, in His mercy and divine power, provided them with manna—a miraculous food that appeared each morning (Exodus 16). This divine provision was not just for their physical survival; it symbolized God's sufficiency to meet their needs in every way. Each morning, they were to gather only what they needed for the day, trusting that God’s provision would be renewed daily. This was a direct picture of God’s divine power supplying everything they needed for their journey.
However, despite God’s consistent provision, many Israelites grumbled and doubted, allowing their evil desires to lead them toward discontent and distrust. They failed to recognize the sufficiency of God's grace and the power of His provision, so they turned from the Manna, desiring something else.
The gospel of Jesus Christ mirrors this divine provision of Manna but in a far greater way. Just as God provided the manna, Jesus is the bread of life (John 6:35), the ultimate provision for both spiritual and physical needs. Through His death and resurrection, He offers us the great and precious promises—that we may partake in His divine nature, escaping the corruption of sin. Just as God supplied the Israelites’ needs, Jesus, through the gospel, provides everything we need for life and godliness.
How does God's provision of manna in the wilderness point forward to the sufficiency of Jesus as the "bread of life" in meeting both our physical and spiritual needs?

Day 58: God's Word Has All We Need
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Submitted 10 days ago
Just as the Israelites had to trust God daily for their needs, we are called to trust Jesus daily for both our spiritual and practical needs. The gospel teaches us to rely on God’s sufficiency in the sacrifice of Jesus for our righteousness. 
Knowing that through Christ, we have everything we need for life and godliness. We can:

    Seek Christ daily through prayer and Scripture, relying on His power to transform us.
    Live by God’s promises, trusting in His provision, even when circumstances are tough or temptation hits.
    Fellowship with others in faith, encouraging one another in the gospel, as we share in God’s divine nature.

In all, the gospel is our constant source of strength and sufficiency, it is all we need for life and godliness, and we are encouraged not to desire other sources for provision and freedom.
What are some ways you can rely on Christ daily for your spiritual and practical needs and also encourage others to do the same?

Day 59: Satan Loves God's Word
Satan Loves God's Word - to Destroy You With It
Key Passage
Now the serpent was more crafty than any of the wild animals the LORD God had made. He said to the woman, “Did God really say, ‘You must not eat from any tree in the garden’? ”
Genesis 3:1 
Teaching
Christians are sensitive people. We have tender consciences like David evidenced in 2 Samuel 24:10. We feel as though we dare not sin against our Father who loves us, Jesus who died for us, or the Spirit who lives in us; and we are extremely careful to keep a clear conscience. We tremble at every word of God. We want to live to honor the gospel, and we often wonder if we are doing so.
Satan can use this makeup of the Christian in a very condemning and destructive way. His special tactic is to use Scripture to accuse us or attempt to destroy us. Of course, this is exactly what He did with Jesus in Matthew 4 and Luke 4. 
Example: a while back I got to feeling very ashamed of my past life, my life of sin, all my failures in parenting, all the wasted years. I was ashamed. During the early morning hours, this verse began coming to my mind: Romans 10:10-11: "As Scripture says, 'Anyone who believes in him will never be put to shame.'” 
The more I dwelled on that verse, the worse I felt. I began thinking I surely must not be a believer, I am fully ashamed of my past life but Scripture promises those who believe will never be ashamed. 
It's during these times when Scripture seems to condemn us, it is important to understand the Scripture in question in its entirety and within the context of the whole Bible. Fortunately, my wife Jody showed me the context of that passage and pointed out that it is regarding salvation, that no believer will ultimately be put to shame eternally in hell. She brought out that the very next verse says this: "For there is no difference between Jew and Gentile —the same Lord is Lord of all and richly blesses all who call on him, for, “Everyone who calls on the name of the Lord will be saved" (Romans 10:12-13).
But the point remains, we must know that our enemy loves the Bible if he can use it to accuse us or destroy us. He's very good at it and is very subtle with it. 
Notice how he does this:
Genesis 3:1-6
1. “Now the serpent was more crafty than any other beast of the field that the Lord God had made. He said to the woman, ‘Did God actually say, “You shall not eat of any tree in the garden”?’”
From the beginning, Satan’s strategy has been to use God's Word against people; he does this by twisting it and distorting it, like a master forger altering a priceless document just enough to deceive. 
When initiating a discussion with Eve he didn’t outright deny God's command but distorted it, sowing doubt. His goal was to make Eve question God’s goodness, which is always his goal. Today, he does the same—misquoting Scripture, removing its context, or adding just enough falsehood to make lies seem true (Matthew 4:5-6). You can see this happening and know Satan is using God's Word when the Scriptures cause you to question the goodness of God, feel guilty or condemned for your sin, or think that there is no way out (Revelation 12:10; Romans 8:1).
But we have the gospel! In John 10:10, Jesus contrasts Himself with the thief: “The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy. I came that they may have life and have it abundantly.” Where Satan distorts and deceives to destroy (2 Corinthians 11:3), Christ speaks truth to give life (John 6:63).
2. “And the woman said to the serpent, ‘We may eat the fruit of the trees in the garden, but God said, “You shall not eat of the fruit of the tree that is in the midst of the garden, neither shall you touch it, lest you die.”’”
Eve misquoted God’s command, adding, “Neither shall you touch it.” Satan’s deception had already begun working—like a crack in a dam that starts small but eventually causes a flood. Distorting God's Word led to misunderstanding it.
This is why we need the gospel, which corrects error and leads us to truth (John 14:6). Jesus never misquotes or misapplies Scripture. He is the Word, full of grace and truth. Contrary to Satan Jesus uses His Word is a flawless mirror reflecting exactly what is real, with no distortion or deception, always showing the believer what He has accomplished for them out of love. 
4. “But the serpent said to the woman, ‘You will not surely die.’”
Here is Satan’s outright denial of God’s Word. The same lie continues today, often wrapped in religious language. He whispers, “Sin isn’t really that serious,” or, “God wouldn’t judge you”, or worse yet, "Remember the cross, you're already forgiven for all sin, so go ahead". But the gospel confronts these lies with Romans 6:23: “For the wages of sin is death, but the free gift of God is eternal life in Christ Jesus our Lord.” Satan tells half-truths to bring death, but Jesus speaks full truth to bring eternal life.
5-6. “For God knows that when you eat of it your eyes will be opened, and you will be like God, knowing good and evil.”
Satan promised divine wisdom but delivered spiritual death (Genesis 3:4-5; Romans 6:23). Sin never gives what it advertises (James 1:14-15). The first Adam believed lies and died (Genesis 3:6; Romans 5:12). Yet Jesus, the last Adam, resisted Satan’s twisted words (Matthew 4:1-11) and crushed him at the cross (Colossians 2:15). The gospel redeems us from Adam’s fall (1 Corinthians 15:22), restoring life where Satan sought to bring ruin (John 10:10).
So you should know what source is bringing Scripture to mind: either Satan, whispering condemnation (Revelation 12:10) and leaving you trapped in a dark, locked room with no key (2 Corinthians 4:4), or God, who always points to the accomplishment of the cross (Galatians 2:20)—like an open door flooding light into the darkness (John 8:12)—so that you might have hope and be free (John 8:36).
Today, let’s stand firm in Christ (Ephesians 6:13-14), knowing that the Word of God, rightly understood, always leads us to Him (John 5:39)—our Rescuer (Psalm 34:17), Redeemer (Isaiah 44:22), and the Truth that sets us free (John 8:32)!
What are some ways Satan distorts Scripture to accuse or deceive, and how can we stand firm in the truth of the gospel?

Day 59: Satan Loves God's Word
Biblical Illustration: Elijah
Consider Elijah after his great victory over the prophets of Baal on Mount Carmel (1 Kings 18). Fire fell from heaven, proving that the Lord alone is God, and showing His power to the world. Yet, shortly after, Elijah found himself in the wilderness, under a broom tree, despairing of life (1 Kings 19:4).
How did he get there? Jezebel's threat struck fear in his heart, and Satan used it to distort the truth. Elijah had just seen God's power firsthand, but suddenly, his perspective shifted. He felt alone, abandoned, and as if all his work had been in vain. "I have been very zealous for the Lord," he cried, "but I am the only one left, and now they seek my life" (1 Kings 19:10).
Satan's tactic was to isolate Elijah, whispering lies of failure and condemnation. But God did not rebuke Elijah harshly. Instead, He sent an angel to feed him, let him rest, and then spoke in a gentle whisper, reminding him that he was not alone—there were still 7,000 in Israel who had not bowed to Baal (1 Kings 19:18).
The gospel answers Satan’s distortions with truth. Just as God came to Elijah with grace and nourishment, Jesus comes to us in our moments of despair, giving us the Bread of Life (John 6:35) and the living water that revives our souls (John 4:14). Satan may try to use Scripture to condemn us, but Jesus—the Living Word—always leads us to restoration and hope. This ongoing work of restoring hope sure makes me love Him, how about you?
What does Elijah’s story reveal about spiritual battles?
 Fear and despair prove a lack of true faith.
Satan isolates and distorts, but God restores with grace.
Victory in faith means we will never struggle again.

Day 59: Satan Loves God's Word
Practical Application
When Satan distorts Scripture to condemn us, we must respond as Jesus did—by standing on God’s truth and applying the gospel. 
First, know the Word rightly (2 Timothy 2:15); read it in context, with the gospel as the lens. 
Second, rest in Christ’s finished work (Romans 8:1); our failures don’t define us—His righteousness does. 
Third, seek godly counsel (Proverbs 11:14); like Jody helped me with Romans 10, wise believers help clarify the truth. 
Fourth, preach the gospel to yourself daily (Psalm 42:5), remembering that Jesus, not Satan, has the final word. The gospel silences the accuser, fills us with life-giving truth, and restores spiritual energy! 
Fifth, rely on the Holy Spirit’s guidance (John 14:26); He brings Scripture to mind and strengthens us against deception. 
Sixth, engage in worship and thanksgiving (Philippians 4:6-7); praising God shifts our focus from lies to His unchanging goodness.
How can you actively apply God’s truth to counter Satan’s lies in your daily life?

Day 60 Antidotes for Discouragement
The Christian and Discouragement
Key Passage
Therefore we do not lose heart. Though outwardly we are wasting away, yet inwardly we are being renewed day by day.
2 Corinthians 4:16
Teaching
In our fallen world, we struggle with disaster, doubt, discouragement, and depression. Every one of us faces a culture that is plagued by spiritual darkness. It is so very easy to become discouraged, isn't it? Discouragement can be ignited by many sources—habitual sin, giving in to the flesh, dwelling on past failures, opposition from others, or the weariness of life’s trials. This discouragement can set us on a course toward giving in to some cherished sin, as we just desire an escape from our thoughts, some relief for our pain, or some distraction to quiet our consciences.
In 2 Corinthians chapter 4, the apostle Paul gives us a gospel-centered antidote for discouragement, a way to handle crippling sadness, and a path through our pain. Let’s walk through it together.
Verse 7: “But we have this treasure in jars of clay to show that this all-surpassing power is from God and not from us.”

We are weak, fragile, and sinful—like clay pots that are easily chipped and cracked (Isaiah 64:8; Psalm 103:14). But the treasure within every believer is Christ Himself (Colossians 1:27; Galatians 2:20)! It’s like a feeble lantern with a brilliant light inside—the vessel may be fragile, but what matters is the flame that shines through (Matthew 5:14-16). Discouragement comes when we put our eyes on ourselves; when we look at our cracks and failures (Hebrews 12:1-2; Romans 7:24-25). But Paul reminds us here that the power we need to get through our discouragement is from God, not from us (2 Corinthians 12:9-10; Zechariah 4:6; Ephesians 6:10). In fact, Christ’s strength is made perfect in our weakness (2 Corinthians 12:9).
Verses 8-9: “We are hard pressed on every side, but not crushed; perplexed, but not in despair; persecuted, but not abandoned; struck down, but not destroyed.”

Life brings pressure, confusion, persecution, and failure, and all of it can greatly discourage us. But because of Christ, no Christian is ever without hope. Our lives are like a ship battered by waves, yet we are kept secure by an anchor (Hebrews 6:19). The gospel assures us that Jesus was crushed for us (Isaiah 53:5) so that we are never crushed under sin. He was forsaken so that we are never abandoned (Matthew 27:46). This glorious thought brings tears to my eyes right now, seeing the love of Jesus so clearly. How about you?
Verses 10-11: “We always carry around in our body the death of Jesus, so that the life of Jesus may also be revealed in our body.”

The Christian life is one of dying to self—dying to sin, to fleshly desires, to carnal reasoning, to self-reliance (Luke 9:23; Romans 6:6-7). It reminds me of pruning a vine: when dead branches are cut away, new growth appears (John 15:1-2). When we cut away our own thoughts, our turning to substances, food or impurity, our self-focus, etc., we experience Christ’s resurrection power and we experience new life and begin to grow again (Romans 8:13; Colossians 3:5; Philippians 3:10). The gospel reminds you that your failures are not final because Christ owned them as His own (Isaiah 53:4-6), and He is now alive in you (Ephesians 2:4-5; Colossians 1:27).
Verses 16-17: “Therefore we do not lose heart. Though outwardly we are wasting away, yet inwardly we are being renewed day by day. For our light and momentary troubles are achieving for us an eternal glory that far outweighs them all.”

We may feel weary, our bodies may weaken, and trials may multiply. But the gospel renews us daily. It’s like the sun setting at night, only to rise again with fresh light each morning. Jesus has secured for us an eternal glory that far surpasses any suffering here. Our discouragement is temporary—our hope is eternal.
Verse 18: “So we fix our eyes not on what is seen, but on what is unseen, since what is seen is temporary, but what is unseen is eternal.”

Looking at ourselves leads to despair; fixing our eyes on Jesus leads to hope (Hebrews 12:2). It’s like focusing on the road ahead rather than staring at the cracks in the pavement. He bore our sins, conquered death, and secured our future. This is the antidote to all discouragement: Christ in us, the hope of glory (Colossians 1:27).
Conclusion:
If you feel discouraged today, turn your eyes away from your failures, the world’s troubles, and the enemy’s lies. Fix them on Jesus. He died for you and rose for you, and He is the treasure within you, the power sustaining you, and the glory awaiting you.
What changes when you focus on Jesus instead of your struggles?

Day 60 Antidotes for Discouragement
Biblical Illustration: Hannah
Hannah’s Discouragement and God’s Faithfulness
Hannah knew the pain of deep discouragement. She longed for a child but remained barren, while her husband’s other wife, Peninnah, provoked her relentlessly (1 Samuel 1:6-7). Year after year, she wept and sometimes could not eat, overwhelmed by sorrow. Even in worship, she found no relief—Eli the priest mistook her silent prayers for drunkenness (1 Samuel 1:12-14).
But Hannah turned to the Lord. Instead of giving in to despair, she poured out her soul before Him (1 Samuel 1:15). God heard her prayer and, in His perfect timing, gave her a son, Samuel (1 Samuel 1:20). More than that, He gave her peace before she even saw the answer, her discouragement was lifted and she could eat (1 Samuel 1:18).
Like Hannah, we may feel discouraged by requests denied, unfulfilled desires, or the sting of others’ words. Christians have a multitude of things that discourage us, hurt us and increase our sorrow. But the gospel tells us that Jesus carried our sorrows (Isaiah 53:4) and invites us to cast our cares on Him (1 Peter 5:7). In Jesus, we find peace that surpasses understanding (Philippians 4:6-7) and assurance that God is working all things for our good (Romans 8:28), even when we cannot yet see the outcome.
What can we learn from Hannah’s response to discouragement?
We should take matters into our own hands so that we can get what we want.
It is okay to take revenge against those who hurt us.
If we turn to the Lord, pouring out our soul in prayer to Him, our hearts will be relieved..

Day 60 Antidotes for Discouragement
Practical Application
When discouragement overwhelms you, as it does me so often, whether from prayers that appear unanswered, unmet desires, or the hurtful words of others, follow Hannah’s example. Pour your heart out to God in honest prayer (1 Samuel 1:15; Philippians 4:6-7), trusting that He hears you. Surrender your burdens to Him, knowing that Jesus carried your sorrows (Isaiah 53:4) and invites you to cast your cares on Him (1 Peter 5:7). Instead of focusing on what you lack, fix your eyes on Jesus (Hebrews 12:2) and rest in His peace (John 14:27). 
Like Hannah, we may not see immediate answers, but faith trusts God’s timing over our own. Waiting is not wasted when it deepens our dependence on Him (Lamentations 3:25-26). Even before your circumstances change, God’s presence and promises will renew your strength (Isaiah 40:31).
What steps can you take to fix your eyes on Jesus and trust in His peace when you feel overwhelmed by discouragement?

    My sin takes on a different feeling now. I don’t want the suffering Jesus went through for me to be for nothing, with Jesus and what he did for me I can fight my flesh and win. 

    I am so grateful for this course at Setting Captives Free and have told anyone who will listen about this. No diet plan will work and hasn’t worked but as soon as I took it to the cross it was instant, I could feel this is exactly what I needed. I felt God saying to me that this is your answer that you asked for. 

    Thank you Jesus for taking these chains and breaking them for me!

Here is where to give to bring freedom to many more people: donate!

Day 61: Get Serious!
Stop Playing With Sin, Crucify It!
Awaiting feedback
Key Passage
So you also must consider yourselves dead to sin and alive to God in Christ Jesus.
Romans 6:11
Teaching
I'm going to speak forthrightly to you because sin is such a deceptive thing that I fear it may be deceiving you as it has me so many times (2 Corinthians 11:3, Hebrews 3:13). It's time to get serious about defeating it (Romans 8:13).
If my tone seems overly direct today, please know it is only because I want what is best for you (Proverbs 27:6, Galatians 4:16). The truth is we should not be entering into dialogue with temptation (Genesis 3:1-6, Matthew 4:1-11), we should not coddle sin any more than one would coddle a rattlesnake (Psalm 97:10, Romans 6:12), we should not play with it or joke about it (Ephesians 5:3-4, Proverbs 14:9), we should kill it! Get serious! (Colossians 3:5, Romans 13:14).
Get Serious! Kill Sin Before It Kills You
Romans 6:11-14
“So you also must consider yourselves dead to sin and alive to God in Christ Jesus.” (Romans 6:11)
If you walked past a graveyard and saw someone sitting up in their coffin, you’d run the other way! Dead people have no life in them, they can't sit up in their coffins (Ecclesiastes 9:5). Yet sin keeps trying to resurrect us so we can play with it. Paul says, consider yourself dead to sin—reckon it, declare it, believe it (Romans 6:11). Your old self was nailed to the cross with Jesus (Galatians 2:20), buried with Him in the tomb (Romans 6:4). Stop giving CPR to the corpse! Stop playing nice with the sin Christ died to destroy (Colossians 3:5).
“Let not sin therefore reign in your mortal body, to make you obey its passions.” (Romans 6:12)
Picture your heart as a throne. Who’s sitting on it? Sin wants to reign, barking orders, making demands—but it’s an illegitimate ruler (1 Corinthians 6:19-20). Jesus, the true King, bought that throne with His own blood (Acts 20:28, Revelation 5:9). Evict sin. Drag it outside. Nail the door shut (Ephesians 4:22-24). You don’t negotiate with a terrorist organization; you destroy its power. Christ has broken sin’s rule—now live like it! (Romans 6:14, 1 John 3:8).
“Do not present your members to sin as instruments for unrighteousness, but present yourselves to God as those who have been brought from death to life.” (Romans 6:13a)
Your hands, feet, eyes, and mind are weapons in a war. Will you use them to fight for holiness or sin? If you put a sword in an enemy’s hand, don’t be surprised when he stabs you! You put a sword in his hand by perusing porn, fixating on food, savoring sinful substances, etc. and these things want to hunt you down and snuff out your life (Proverbs 6:25-26). 
But through the gospel, your old self that did those things has been crucified (Galatians 2:20, Romans 6:6), and your new self that avoids them has been raised to life (Romans 6:4-5). Jesus bought you at the cost of His suffering—now offer your body, your mind, your desires to Him, not to the enemy (1 Corinthians 6:19-20, Romans 12:1).
“…and your members to God as instruments for righteousness.” (Romans 6:13b)
Trade your old tools of sin for the armor of Christ (Ephesians 6:10-18). Use your mouth for worship, not speaking ill of others (James 3:9-10, Ephesians 4:29). Your eyes for purity, not lust (Matthew 5:28, Psalm 101:3). Your hands for service, not selfishness (Galatians 5:13, Philippians 2:3-4). God didn’t save you to sit on the sidelines—He enlisted you in the fight! (2 Timothy 2:3-4). So stop giving in and stand up, stand firm,  and fight! (Ephesians 6:13). Do something different! Have some holy boldness and some spiritual strength, act like a man, and defeat your enemies! (1 Corinthians 16:13, 2 Samuel 10:12).
The definition of insanity is doing the same thing over and over while expecting a different outcome. If you're giving in you must make some changes. Real changes that will result in victory rather than ongoing defeat. Plead with God for a heart change, and ask God to make you thirsty for Jesus. Stop turning to worldly diets and man-made plans. Get up off of David's couch (2 Samuel 11:2), stop napping on Delilah's lap (Judges 16:19), quit pitching your tent near Sodom (Genesis 13:12), stop hiding things devoted to destruction (Joshua 7:11-12). Do something different, change something, fix the chinks in your armor, offer your members to God, and learn to fight to win!
“For sin will have no dominion over you, since you are not under law but under grace.” (Romans 6:14)
The law says, Do better, try harder, but grace says, It is finished (John 19:30, Romans 10:4). Sin’s dominion is broken because Christ purchased you out from under the Law and put you under grace. He reigns victorious in His people (Romans 6:14, Colossians 1:13). He didn’t just forgive you—He freed you! (Romans 8:2, Galatians 5:1). So stop flirting with sin. Let there be no room in your heart for sin, just like there was no room in the inn for Jesus. Flee from it like Joseph fled from Potiphar's wife! (James 4:7-8, 1 Corinthians 6:18). Get serious. Get ruthless. Bury sin in the grave where Christ put it (Romans 6:4-6, Colossians 3:5), and live free in the victory He purchased (Romans 8:37, 1 Corinthians 15:57).
Prayer: Lord, help me to get serious about holiness. Help me nail my sin to the cross, bury it in the grave, and rise to new life in Christ. Amen.
What is one action you can take today to stop allowing sin to reign in your life and start living in the victory Christ has won for you?

Day 61: Get Serious!
Biblical Illustration: David and Goliath
Awaiting feedback
Slaying the Giant: No Mercy for Sin
1 Samuel 17:45-51
David stood before Goliath, a shepherd boy facing a warrior. Goliath towered over him, mocking him, taunting him. Sin does the same to us: boasting that it will never be defeated, daring us to engage with it. But David didn’t cower. He didn’t negotiate. He didn’t try to reason with the giant. He got serious.
“You come to me with a sword and with a spear and with a javelin, but I come to you in the name of the Lord of hosts.” (1 Samuel 17:45)
David’s confidence wasn’t in his slingshot—it was in his God. When we fight sin, we don’t rely on willpower or self-discipline alone. We come in the name of the Lord, in the power of the cross, where Christ already won the victory. We come with Scripture, with the power of the Holy Spirit. We come with the courage we got from watching Jesus fight Satan and stomp on his head at the cross, and with the power that rose Him from the dead on the third day.
“The battle is the Lord’s, and He will give you into our hand.” (1 Samuel 17:47)
David didn’t waver. He didn’t hesitate. He ran toward Goliath, slung the stone, and struck the giant in the forehead. One decisive blow—and Goliath fell, face down in the dirt. That’s how we must deal with sin. No lingering, no hesitation. Kill it before it kills you.
But David didn’t stop there. “Then David ran and stood over the Philistine and took his sword and drew it out…and cut off his head with it.” (1 Samuel 17:51)
Why cut off his head? Because dead giants don’t come back. David didn’t just wound Goliath—he finished him off. 
This whole story of David and Goliath pointed forward to what Jesus did to Satan. He didn’t just weaken the enemy, He disarmed him when He died for you, crushing his power at the cross (Colossians 2:15). Jesus, the greater David, went into battle alone, faced the giant of sin and death, and struck the final blow on the cross and the empty tomb is a big exclamation point!
Now, because of Christ, we don’t have to bow to sin anymore. The enemy is defeated. The victory is won. So pick up your sword (God’s Word) and get serious. No mercy for sin. No second chances for temptation. Cut off its head, bury it in the grave, and walk in the freedom Jesus purchased!
How can you apply David's example of confronting Goliath to your own battle with sin?

Day 61: Get Serious!
Practical Application
Awaiting feedback
Friend, I have experienced Satan's craftiness too often in my life and so felt the need to warn you. I've seen too many people that I love lured into temptation and then had their souls gutted by Satan as they are left wounded and bleeding on the battlefield. It was time to try to jolt you into a fighting stance if you weren't already.

Here is what God's Word teaches us:

    Kill Sin with the Word – Just as David’s stone struck Goliath, and he cut off the giant's head with a sword, use Scripture to strike down temptation. “It is written…” was Jesus’ weapon against Satan (Matthew 4:4-10). Meditate on specific verses, and write down ones that will help you wield them in battle.
    Cut Off Sin’s Head – Remove access to sinful habits. If your phone tempts you, get a blocker. If pride rises, humble yourself in prayer, and make no provision for the flesh (Romans 13:14).  
    Stand in Christ’s Victory – Jesus already defeated Satan at the cross (Colossians 2:15). Live in His power. Sin is dead—so stop giving it a voice in your ear! 

How can you actively apply God's Word to resist temptation and remove access to sinful habits in your own life?

Day 62: Cultivate Thirst For Jesus!
Cultivate Thirst For Jesus
Key passage
On the last day of the feast, the great day, Jesus stood up and cried out, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink.”
John 7:37 
Teaching
Get Thirsty for Jesus - Isaiah 55:1-3
Everyone is born thirsting, longing, craving, yearning for something. But it is this earth that we long for, craving sinful pleasures, temporary things. 

    They're desperately thirsty, always craving more. But for what? Not for Christ or heaven. For the things of this world. They never feel they have enough until death silences them with a shovelful of dirt from their own grave. What a torturous existence they must lead, constantly yearning for more, yet unable to satisfy their insatiable greed.  The only way—if people would just believe it—to quench this worldly thirst is to ignite a greater thirst for Christ and heaven.  Develop a deep, passionate longing for these, and the other desires will fade away, just as a feverish thirst subsides when the body recovers. - William Gurnall, The Christian in Complete Armor, Page 33-34 Kindle edition.

They say you can lead a horse to water, but you can't make it drink. But that's not true. You can put salt in their oats (Matthew 5:6). This makes the horse thirsty. The way to put salt in our oats to make us thirsty for Jesus is by contemplative reading of Scripture (Psalm 1:2; Colossians 3:16) and praying through each verse as we read (John 15:7). This stirs up a thirst for Jesus as we encounter Him (John 7:37-38), for more of the Word as it changes us (Romans 12:2; Hebrews 4:12), for the work of the Holy Spirit in our lives as He quenches our thirst (Isaiah 44:3; John 4:14).

    The appetite for the Word grows in those who feed on it.  — Charles Spurgeon

Let's see this in God's Word:

Isaiah 55:1 – “Come, everyone who thirsts, come to the waters; and he who has no money, come, buy and eat! Come, buy wine and milk without money and without price.”

God’s invitation is open to everyone who thirsts (Revelation 22:17). The world tells us to earn, to strive, to qualify (Ephesians 2:8-9). But God says, come with empty hands (Matthew 5:3; Luke 18:13-14). Come thirsty!

    Nothing in my hands I bring
    Simply to Your cross I cling

One of my favorite pastors, Colin Smith, has written a wonderful treatise on this subject here! 
If you have no money, you are welcome (Romans 3:24). If you are broken, you are invited (Psalm 34:18; Luke 14:21). The living water of Jesus Christ is not for sale—it’s a free gift of grace (John 4:10; Romans 6:23). Like a traveler lost in the desert, desperate for a drink, our souls long for something more (Psalm 42:1-2). Jesus is the fountain of life (Psalm 36:9), the only One who truly satisfies (John 6:35).
Isaiah 55:2 – “Why do you spend your money for that which is not bread, and your labor for that which does not satisfy? Listen diligently to me, and eat what is good, and delight yourselves in rich food.”

People waste their lives chasing after things that do not fill them, as we did (Ecclesiastes 1:2). Wealth, pleasure, success—all of it is like eating air (Ecclesiastes 1:17). It looks good but leaves us starving (Proverbs 27:20). God calls us to a better feast, the rich nourishment of His Word (Matthew 4:4, Jeremiah 15:16).
How do we drink deeply of Jesus? By listening diligently to Him (Isaiah 55:2). The Bible is the banquet table, and every page is filled with soul-satisfying truth (Psalm 19:7-10, Psalm 119:103). When we open Scripture, we are not just reading words; we are feasting on Christ (John 5:39).
Are you hungry? Then don't try to have a banquet in the grave, rather listen to God's Word (Isaiah 55:3). Take in every bite of His promises and be filled (Psalm 107:9). Are you thirsty? Then drink deeply at the well of salvation, and be quenched (Isaiah 12:3, Revelation 21:6).
Isaiah 55:3 – “Incline your ear, and come to me; hear, that your soul may live; and I will make with you an everlasting covenant, my steadfast, sure love for David.”

Listening to God is how we come to Him (Romans 10:17, John 6:45). His voice in Scripture is our pathway to life (Psalm 119:105, John 5:24).
We are not just invited to drink and be filled but to enter into an everlasting covenant (Isaiah 55:3)—a relationship with the living God based upon the blood Jesus shed to make this New Covenant (Luke 22:20, Hebrews 9:15), which secures our forgiveness (Ephesians 1:7). This is not a temporary satisfaction but an eternal promise, secured in Jesus Christ (2 Corinthians 1:20, Hebrews 13:20-21).
He is the fulfillment of God’s love for David (Acts 13:34), the King who reigns forever (Luke 1:32-33, Revelation 11:15). When we incline our ears, when we listen and believe, our souls come alive (John 5:25, Ephesians 2:4-5).
Are You Thirsty?
We were made for more than this world’s empty wells. Jesus stands before us, offering water, wine, and milk—abundant, free, and overflowing. But we must come. We must listen. We must feast on His Word.
Are you thirsty? Then open your Bible. Listen diligently. Drink deeply. And be satisfied daily.
What are some ways you can cultivate a deeper thirst for Jesus and His Word in your life?

Day 62: Cultivate Thirst For Jesus!
Biblical Illustration: Woman at the Well
The Samaritan woman at the well (John 4:7-30) perfectly embodies Isaiah 55’s message. She came to Jacob’s well, seeking physical water, but her soul was parched. She had tried to quench her thirst with relationships—five husbands and a man who was not her husband—but was left empty.
Then Jesus met her. He offered her living water, something far greater than what she came for. But how did she drink? By listening. As Jesus spoke, He revealed her heart, her need, and ultimately, Himself as the Messiah. She believed. She drank deeply of His words, and her thirst was satisfied. The result? She left her water jar behind and ran to tell others. The well that once enslaved her was no longer her focus—her soul had been flooded with Christ’s living water.
Like the Samaritan woman, we come to Jesus not by works, but by hearing and believing. The gospel is free, the water is abundant, and Christ is enough. Are you thirsty? Then drink deeply of Him today.
What did the Samaritan woman do to receive the living water Jesus offered her?
She worked hard to earn it.
She believed and listened to Jesus.
She tried to find a better well.

Day 62: Cultivate Thirst For Jesus!
Practical Application
To drink deeply of Christ, we must listen diligently to Him. 

    Start your day in Scripture, meditating on God’s promises. Pray with an open heart, asking Jesus to satisfy your soul. 
    Make time to reflect on His goodness throughout the day, keeping your focus on His presence rather than the distractions around you. 
    Gather with other believers to hear the gospel preached and encourage one another. Share the living water by telling others about Christ. 
    Just as the Samaritan woman left her water jar behind, leave behind worldly distractions and seek Jesus above all. 
    The more we feast on His Word, the more our thirst for Him grows—and the more we are truly satisfied in Him.

How can you intentionally "drink deeply" of Christ and make Him the focus of your day?

Day 63: Drink Deeply of Jesus
Come to Jesus Daily and Drink
Key Passage
On the last day of the feast, the great day, Jesus stood up and cried out, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink.
John 7:37 
One of the things I do in my daily life is to read a Bible chapter, then do some physical exercise while meditating on what I just read, and then do some of my other chores. The next hour I repeat this, and so on each hour of the day. By the end of the day, I'm so filled with Scripture and the Spirit of God that I am about to burst. And yet all that does is wet my appetite for more of Jesus! 
You can do something similar. You might have a job where you need to focus, but you can slip away to read Scripture during a break, at lunchtime, or during your commute. Ask God to give you a real thirst for Jesus, then come to Him and drink. This satisfies our hearts and keeps us from turning to sin (Matthew 17:17).
John 7:37 – On the last day of the feast, the great day, Jesus stood up and cried out, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink.” 
The festival was ending. The people had feasted, laughed, and celebrated. Their bellies were full, but their souls remained parched. This is the story of the world and of our own lives: we drink deeply from broken wells (lust and lying, greed and gluttony, etc.) yet still wake up dry. 
Jesus stood, not sitting idly by, not whispering His invitation but crying out, as if to shake slumbering souls. Are you thirsty? Then come. Not to religion. Not to sinful sources. Not to self-help. Come to Me. He alone is the living water that never runs dry.
John 7:38 – “Whoever believes in me, as the Scripture has said, ‘Out of his heart will flow rivers of living water.’”
Water doesn’t trickle out—it flows (John 7:38). A river isn’t a stagnant pond; it moves, bringing life everywhere it goes (Ezekiel 47:9). Jesus transforms our parched souls into fountains of life, overflowing with the grace of the gospel (Isaiah 58:11). The world drinks from poisoned streams—pleasure, success, sinful bondage which are "broken cisterns" (Jeremiah 2:13)—but believers become conduits of something better. 
Picture a cracked desert suddenly bursting with a fresh spring (Isaiah 35:6-7). That’s what happens when Jesus fills a soul (John 4:14). Are you dry? Come (Isaiah 55:1). Drink deeply. Then let the river run from you to others (Proverbs 11:25).
John 7:39 – “Now this he said about the Spirit, whom those who believed in him were to receive, for as yet the Spirit had not been given, because Jesus was not yet glorified.”
The source of this living water is the Holy Spirit (John 7:39). Jesus first had to be glorified; that is, lifted up on the cross (John 12:32), raised from the grave (1 Corinthians 15:3-4), and enthroned in heaven (Hebrews 1:3). Then, He poured out the Spirit like rain on dry ground (Acts 2:33). The Spirit is the divine irrigation system, continually filling us with Christ’s presence (Ezekiel 36:27). No longer do we scrape by spiritually, rationing drops of joy. The Spirit floods our hearts with the assurance of God’s love (Romans 5:5), the power to overcome sin (Galatians 5:16), and the deep satisfaction of knowing Jesus (John 16:14).
Let me put it very plainly. Each day we have a choice: we can either ignore God's Word (Proverbs 1:24-25, Hosea 4:6) and live in constant thoughts of regret, guilt, sorrow over our past, condemning thoughts, etc. (Psalm 32:3-4), OR we can renew our minds in God's Word (Romans 12:2, Ephesians 4:23) and experience the flooding of thoughts about Jesus and His accomplishment on Calvary (1 Corinthians 1:18, Colossians 2:13-14) and His wonderful work in our hearts and lives (Philippians 1:6, Hebrews 13:20-21).
Are You Thirsty?
Everyone is thirsty for something. I used to chase money, drink in entertainment, pursue sinful relationships and substances, impurity, and gambling. But every earthly drink left me thirstier than before.
Like the woman at the well I was seeking
For things that could not satisfy;
And then I heard my Savior speaking:
"Draw from my well that never shall run dry".
George Beverly Shea
Jesus invites us to a different kind of thirst, one that He alone can quench (John 4:13-14). The more we drink of Him, the more we want (Psalm 63:1-5). His love is better than wine (Song of Solomon 1:2), His mercy richer than the purest spring (Lamentations 3:22-23). The world offers saltwater that dehydrates; Jesus offers living water that overflows (John 7:37-39).
Come to Him (Matthew 11:28). Drink deeply (Isaiah 55:1). Be satisfied forever (John 6:35).
Which of the following is the best way to stay spiritually refreshed throughout the day?
 Relying on past spiritual experiences to quench our thirst.
Waiting until Sunday to seek God at church.
Consuming distractions to avoid feeling empty.
Taking intentional moments to read Scripture, pray, and meditate on Jesus.

Day 63: Drink Deeply of Jesus
Biblical Illustration: The Rock That Was Struck
In the wilderness, the Israelites grew thirsty, and once again, they grumbled against Moses. God commanded Moses to strike a rock, and when he did, water gushed out, satisfying the people’s thirst (Exodus 17:6). This miraculous provision was a moment of grace, where God provided exactly what they needed. Yet, the water from that rock was temporary, and the people would soon thirst again.
However, in 1 Corinthians 10:4, Paul reveals the true meaning of that event in the wilderness: "And all drank the same spiritual drink. For they drank from the spiritual Rock that followed them, and the Rock was Christ." Jesus Himself is the Rock, the true source of living water. 
But here is the critically important part of this passage: just as Moses struck the rock and water flowed for the Israelites, Jesus was struck on the cross, His body broken, and His blood poured out, to provide us with the ultimate satisfaction: the Holy Spirit.
Out of Jesus’ death, as He was glorified on the cross, flowed the Holy Spirit, the living water that continually satisfies our spiritual thirst. Through the Gospel, the Holy Spirit fills believers, renewing us with the power to overcome sin and bringing us into an intimate, ongoing relationship with God. In the wilderness the rock had to be struck in order to pour out the water. Jesus, our Rock, was crucified, struck down in the prime of life for our salvation, and now provides us with thirst-quenching life through the Spirit.
If you're thirsty, come to the cross, and watch! Listen to your Savior say, "I thirst", as He took on your sin as your Substitute. He was parched and dehydrated on the cross, He thirsted to quench your thirst. Oh, what love!
How does Jesus, the true Rock, satisfy your spiritual thirst in a way that nothing else can?

Day 63: Drink Deeply of Jesus
Practical Application
Just as the Israelites had to come to the rock to receive water, we must come to Jesus, our Rock, for spiritual nourishment. Through His death, He made the Holy Spirit available to us. This coming to the cross should not be a one-time event but a daily coming. Just as our bodies require water to survive, our souls need the continual refreshment of the message of the cross.
To practically apply this, we must first acknowledge our need for Him daily. Spend time in prayer, seeking the Holy Spirit’s enlightenment. Read and meditate on Scripture, asking the Spirit to fill you with the truth of the Gospel (John 16:14). Beyond personal devotion, we must also allow the Spirit to work through us, letting His love and truth flow to others in our words and actions. The more we drink from Christ, the more we become a source of His living water to those around us.
As we humble ourselves, we experience the overflowing joy, peace, and power that comes from living in the Spirit, abiding in Christ, being deeply rooted in the gospel.
How can you intentionally rely on Jesus, your Rock, today to receive the spiritual nourishment you need?

Day 64: Turning From God Leads to Slavery
Drinking From the Wrong Source = Slavery
Key Passage

My people have committed two sins: They have forsaken me, the spring of living water, and have dug their own cisterns,
broken cisterns that cannot hold water.
Jeremiah 2:13
Teaching
Jeremiah 2:11-14
For the past few days, we've been discussing the importance of quenching our spiritual thirst in Jesus. There is a very important reason for this: if we turn to the wrong source to quench our thirst, we end up in slavery. That is, if we turn from the Lord and try to have our longings fulfilled, our cravings met, and our desires (thirst) satisfied anywhere else, the end result is spiritual slavery.
Let's notice this from Scripture:
Verse 11 – Trading Living Water for Empty Cisterns
"Has a nation changed its gods, even though they are no gods? But my people have changed their glory for that which does not profit."
Imagine someone trading a chest of gold for a handful of gravel—madness! Yet, Israel abandoned the true and living God for lifeless idols (Jeremiah 2:11), even as we have traded life-giving water of the Spirit for a handful of food gravel or alcohol gravel or smoking gravel, etc. It wasn’t just a bad trade; it was a catastrophic loss. The glory of God, His presence and power, was exchanged for what could never satisfy (Psalm 106:20; Romans 1:23). This is the same exchange we see today when people trade Jesus—the fountain of living water (John 4:14; Jeremiah 17:13)—for the broken wells of greedy wealth (1 Timothy 6:10; Proverbs 11:28), sexual pleasure (Proverbs 5:3-5; Ephesians 4:19), false identities of trans-ideology (Genesis 1:27; Deuteronomy 22:5; Romans 1:26-27), or self-reliance (Proverbs 3:5-6; Revelation 3:17).
Verse 12 – The Shocking Reality of Sin
"Be appalled, O heavens, at this; be shocked, be utterly desolate, declares the LORD."
Even the heavens are horrified when people turn from the Lord to satisfy their own desires (Jeremiah 2:12; Deuteronomy 32:17). This is no small mistake but a rebellion against the Creator (Isaiah 1:2; Romans 1:21-22). Picture a man walking away from an oasis in the desert, choosing instead to dig in dry sand, hoping to find water (Jeremiah 2:13). Our sin is not just poor judgment—it’s insanity (Ecclesiastes 9:3). The cross of Christ exposes the weight of this tragedy; He was forsaken so that we might be restored to the true source of life (Matthew 27:46; 2 Corinthians 5:21) and brought back from the dead to quench our thirst in the Living Water of the Spirit (John 7:37-38; Revelation 22:17).
Verse 13 – Broken Cisterns and Living Water
"For my people have committed two evils: they have forsaken me, the fountain of living waters, and hewed out cisterns for themselves, broken cisterns that can hold no water."
A broken cistern is a cracked jar—whatever you pour in leaks out (Jeremiah 2:13). This is the tragedy of sin: it promises life but leaves us empty (Proverbs 14:12; Romans 6:21). Whether it’s so-called "addiction" (John 8:34; Titus 3:3), success (Ecclesiastes 2:11; Mark 8:36), or religion without Christ (Matthew 23:27-28; Galatians 2:16), all man-made systems are broken cisterns. Jesus stood in the temple and cried, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink” (John 7:37). He alone fills our souls with living water that never runs dry (Isaiah 55:1; Revelation 21:6), but we must come to Him daily and drink.
The problem is clear: turning away from the Lord to something else leads to slavery (Romans 6:16; Galatians 4:8-9). I was a slave to my fleshly desires (Romans 7:18; Ephesians 2:3), my sinful cravings (James 1:14-15), my false gods and idols (Ezekiel 14:3; 1 Corinthians 10:14), and that for many years.
Verse 14 – Slavery Through Idolatry
"Is Israel a slave? Is he a homeborn servant? Why then has he become a prey?"
By turning from God, Israel didn’t find freedom; they found slavery (Jeremiah 2:14; Judges 2:19) and became prey for other nations. Idolatry always enslaves (Psalm 106:36; Romans 1:25). The Israelites, longing for other gods, were dragged into captivity (2 Kings 17:7-8; Nehemiah 9:26-27). Likewise, sin is a cruel master (John 8:34; Romans 6:16), forcing us to gratify our cravings (Ephesians 2:1-3). 
But Jesus came to break our chains: “If the Son sets you free, you will be free indeed” (John 8:36). The only way to escape slavery is to return to the true Master (Romans 6:22), the One who hung in agony on the cross out of love for us (Galatians 2:20), and who calls us sons and daughters (2 Corinthians 6:18).
Conclusion: Freedom in Christ
The Israelites turned from the fountain of life and found themselves in captivity (Jeremiah 17:13; Lamentations 1:18). But here’s the hope: turning back to God brings freedom (Isaiah 55:7; Joel 2:12-13). The Bible calls us to forsake the dry wells of this world (Psalm 63:1; Revelation 18:4) and drink deeply from Christ (John 6:35; Isaiah 12:3). His cross is the bridge back to living water (1 Peter 3:18). In Him, we are no longer slaves but free (Galatians 5:1), no longer thirsty but satisfied forever (Revelation 7:16-17).
Will you turn back today? The fountain is flowing, and Jesus invites you to drink (Revelation 22:17).
How does turning to worldly sources for fulfillment result in spiritual slavery, and what is the only path to true freedom?
It leads to temporary satisfaction, and the only path to freedom is self-discipline.
It leads to empty promises and enslavement to sin, with true freedom found in returning to God through Christ.
It brings inner peace, and true freedom is attained by following societal norms and traditions.

Day 64: Turning From God Leads to Slavery
Biblical Illustration: The Prodigal Son
A great illustration of this teaching is found in the prodigal son (Luke 15:11-24). I've used the prodigal son to illustrate other truths, but notice how this parable perfectly illustrates this truth of turning from God the Living Water to broken cisterns.
In Jesus' parable, He describes a young man blessed with a loving father and a secure home, but he decided to forsake his source of life and chase after his own desires. He demanded his inheritance early and ran off to a far country, drinking deeply from the world's broken cisterns—pleasure, wealth, and reckless living. For a time, it seemed satisfying, but soon, the wells dried up. His money vanished, famine struck, and he found himself feeding pigs—a powerful picture of slavery to sin. He was starving, empty, and alone, just as Israel was when they turned from God.
Then, in his brokenness, he "came to himself" (Luke 15:17). He realized that even his father’s servants had more than he did. In humility, he returned home, ready to beg for mercy. But before he could utter a word, his father ran to him, embraced him, and restored him as a son.
This parable illustrates the gospel! We, like the prodigal, have abandoned the fountain of living water, but when we turn back to the Father through Jesus Christ, we find not judgment, but grace. We find that the Father, Himself, has paid the price for our rebellion out of love by sending His Son to die in our place. The robe, the ring, and the feast remind us that in Christ, we are fully restored, free, and satisfied.
How does the parable of the prodigal son illustrate the consequences of turning from God to broken cisterns?

Day 64: Turning From God Leads to Slavery
Practical Application
If turning from God leads to slavery, then turning back to Him brings freedom. How do we return?

    Repent and confess – Acknowledge the empty wells you've been drinking from (1 John 1:9).
    Drink from Christ daily – Spend time in His Word, where the living water flows (John 7:37-38).
    Pray for renewal – Ask God to restore your soul and satisfy you in Him (Psalm 51:10-12).
    Stay in fellowship – Walk with believers who encourage faithfulness (Hebrews 10:24-25).

Like the prodigal, come home! The Father runs to embrace all who turn back to Christ.
Think about a time you felt distant from God. Which of these four steps to return (repent, read Scripture, pray, or fellowship) is hardest for you? What specific action will you take this week to grow in that area?"

Day 65: Steps to Captivity
The Accident Chain
Key Passage
Lot looked around and saw that the whole plain of the Jordan toward Zoar was well watered, like the garden of the LORD, like the land of Egypt. This was before the LORD destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah.
Genesis 13:10 
Teaching
In the courses at Setting Captives Free, we have lessons on the steps to captivity, and I wanted to revisit that today, to remind us of the need to break a link in the accident chain.
Steps in the Accident Chain
 Genesis 13
We have learned that in aviation, accidents rarely happen due to a single cause. Instead, they result from a sequence of seemingly small decisions and circumstances—a concept known as The Accident Chain. Perhaps a pilot didn’t sleep well the night before, he was then running late to the airport, distracted by a phone call, rushed through his pre-flight checklist, and then made a crucial error during takeoff. Each step built upon the last until, suddenly, disaster struck.
The same principle applies to sin. It rarely overtakes us in a single moment. Instead, we take small steps down a dangerous path, each one leading us closer to captivity. Lot’s life is a vivid illustration of this process:

    Lot saw the Jordan Valley and Sodom. “And Lot lifted up his eyes and saw that the Jordan Valley was well watered everywhere like the garden of the Lord, like the land of Egypt...” (Genesis 13:10). His eyes were drawn to the beauty, but like Eve looking a the forbidden fruit, he failed to see the danger around the corner.
    Lot set out toward Sodom. “So Lot chose for himself all the Jordan Valley, and Lot journeyed east. Thus they separated from each other” (Genesis 13:11). What began as a glance became a decision to move in that direction.
    Lot pitched his tent near Sodom. “Lot settled among the cities of the valley and pitched his tent toward Sodom” (Genesis 13:12). Now he was living close to wickedness, just outside the city, flirting with temptation. He may have reasoned, "I may be close, but at least I'm not living in Sodom."
    Lot was living in Sodom. “They also took Lot, the son of Abram’s brother, who was dwelling in Sodom, and his possessions, and went their way” (Genesis 14:12). He had moved in, got a Sodom address, fully enmeshing himself in the city life.
    Lot was taken captive. “They also took Lot, the son of Abram’s brother, who was dwelling in Sodom, and his possessions, and went their way" (Genesis 14:12). He lost his freedom, dignity, and possessions, as he was dragged away in chains.

Like an accident chain, Lot’s captivity didn’t happen suddenly—it was a series of small compromises (Genesis 13:10-12; 14:12). If he had broken any link in the chain—if he had refused to move toward Sodom, refused to live there—his captivity could have been avoided (Proverbs 4:14-15).
Sin follows the same pattern. A glance, a lingering thought, a small compromise, and before we know it, we are ensnared (James 1:14-15). But because the Holy Spirit lives in all believers we can break the chain. If a pilot recognizes danger and changes course, breaks one link, and takes one proactive step, disaster can be avoided (1 Corinthians 10:13). If we recognize the steps leading to sin and turn back, we escape captivity (2 Timothy 2:22).
Where are your feet leading today? Are you making any small compromises that are setting the stage for greater consequences? 

Day 65: Steps to Captivity
Biblical Illustration: Peter
Peter’s journey to denying Jesus is a clear example of the accident chain. It didn’t happen in an instant but through a series of steps:

    Peter was confident in himself. “Peter answered him, ‘Though they all fall away because of you, I will never fall away.’” (Matthew 26:33). He believed he was too strong to fail.
    Peter followed at a distance. “And Peter was following him at a distance, as far as the courtyard of the high priest…” (Matthew 26:58). Instead of standing boldly, he lingered on the edge of danger.
    Peter sat with the enemies of Christ. “Then they seized him and led him away, bringing him into the high priest’s house, and Peter was following at a distance. And when they had kindled a fire in the middle of the courtyard and sat down together, Peter sat down among them.” (Luke 22:54-55). He placed himself among those who hated Jesus.
    Peter denied knowing Jesus. “But he denied it before them all, saying, ‘I do not know what you mean.’” (Matthew 26:70). Fear took hold, and he verbally disowned Christ.
    Peter wept bitterly. “And Peter remembered the saying of Jesus, ‘Before the rooster crows, you will deny me three times.’ And he went out and wept bitterly.” (Matthew 26:75). He realized too late that he had fallen into sin’s trap.

But the gospel of Jesus Christ restores fallen sinners. After His resurrection, Jesus sought out Peter, forgave him, and restored him (John 21:15-17). Peter became a bold preacher of the gospel, proving that while sin ensnares, Jesus sets captives free. If we find ourselves on a dangerous path, we must turn back to Christ, who alone can break every chain and restore our souls.
Which of the following best describes the steps that led to Peter’s denial of Jesus?
He was overconfident, followed at a distance, sat with Jesus' enemies, and eventually denied Him.
He was forced by the soldiers to deny Jesus.
He never actually denied Jesus but was misunderstood.

Day 65: Steps to Captivity
Practical Application
The opposite of an accident chain is an advancement chain. This is where each thing we do advances us in our faith, propels us in our walk with Jesus and secures our soul against sin. For example, notice 2 Timothy 2:22 – “So flee youthful passions and pursue righteousness, faith, love, and peace, along with those who call on the Lord from a pure heart.” 
What are some links in the advancement chain in this passage?
To break the chain of sin, we must be vigilant. Guard your heart and eyes (Proverbs 4:23). Flee from temptation (2 Timothy 2:22). Surround yourself with godly influences (Hebrews 10:24-25). Confess sin quickly and seek Christ’s forgiveness (1 John 1:9). Keep your focus on Jesus, who alone can rescue and restore (Hebrews 12:2). Just as Peter was restored, we too can be renewed by the grace of Jesus. The gospel is the power to break every chain—cling to it and walk in the freedom of His forgiveness.
What steps can you take today to guard your heart, avoid temptation, and keep your focus on Jesus to break the chain of sin in your life?

Day 66: The Spiritual Stupor of Lot
Lot's Spiritual Apathy and Dullness
The Spiritual Stupor of Lot
Lot’s life is a tragic cautionary tale of spiritual dullness leading to devastating consequences, as we saw in yesterday's lesson. He lived in a spiritual stupor, blinded to the dangers around him until he finally stumbled into a drunken stupor. His slow drift into sin warns us against complacency. Let’s examine five points from his life and learn how to do the opposite.
1. A Slow Slide into Sodom
Lot’s downfall didn’t happen overnight. It began with a look: “Lot looked around and saw that the whole plain of the Jordan toward Zoar was well watered, like the garden of the Lord” (Genesis 13:10). Then, he “set out toward” Sodom (Genesis 13:11). Next, he “pitched his tent near Sodom” (Genesis 13:12). Finally, he was “living in Sodom” (Genesis 14:12). What a progression! Sin often lures us gradually, like a riptide pulling a swimmer farther from shore. 
This is one area that every Christian can grow in: learning to spot this slide into sin, recognize it, and reject it outright. Instead of flirting with temptation, and "sliding into Sodom", we should flee it as Joseph did (Genesis 39:12). 
2. Offering His Daughters to Evil Men
When the wicked men of Sodom surrounded his house, Lot offered them his daughters to protect the visitors (angels) who had come under his roof: “Look, I have two daughters who have never slept with a man. Let me bring them out to you, and you can do what you like with them” (Genesis 19:8). This shocking moment reveals his moral blindness. Sin dulls our discernment, making even unthinkable actions seem reasonable. 
Wake up! Instead of compromising with evil, we must stand firm like Daniel, who refused to defile himself (Daniel 1:8). The admonition, “Wake up, sleeper, rise from the dead, and Christ will shine on you” (Ephesians 5:14) is then explained, as to how to do it, in the next verses:
15 Be very careful, then, how you live —not as unwise but as wise, 16 making the most of every opportunity, because the days are evil. 17 Therefore do not be foolish, but understand what the Lord’s will is. 18 Do not get drunk on wine, which leads to debauchery. Instead, be filled with the Spirit, 19 speaking to one another with psalms, hymns, and songs from the Spirit. (Ephesians 5:15-19).
3. Hesitating to Leave Sodom
Even after being warned of Sodom’s destruction, Lot lingered: “When he hesitated, the men grasped his hand and the hands of his wife and two daughters and led them safely out” (Genesis 19:16). Like a sleepwalker refusing to wake up, Lot was too attached to let go. A spiritual stupor makes us sluggish in responding to God. 
If you want to grow in the Lord, learn not to hesitate in obeying His Word. We must be like Peter, who obeyed immediately when Jesus said, “Come” (Matthew 14:29).
4. Not Wanting to Get Far from Sodom
When Lot's rescuers commanded him to flee far away from Sodom to the mountains Lot pleaded with the angels: “I cannot flee to the mountains... Look, here is a town near enough to run to” (Genesis 19:19-20). He still wanted a taste of Sodom’s lifestyle. Many people try to keep a foot in both worlds, but Jesus said, “No one can serve two masters” (Matthew 6:24). True repentance means forsaking sin, not clinging to its edges. 
One of the greatest ways to grow in our walk with the Lord is to try to get as far away from our previous sin habits as possible (2 Corinthians 7:1, Ephesians 4:22), not leaving any room for the flesh (Romans 13:14).
5. Drunkenness and Incest
Lot’s final fall was tragic: “They got their father to drink wine that night... and he was not aware of it” (Genesis 19:33). His slow spiritual dullness ended in literal intoxication, leading to unspeakable sin (Genesis 19:33-35). Sin always blinds and enslaves. 
Instead of numbing ourselves, we must be “alert and of sober mind” (1 Peter 5:8). This is because the devil prowls around seeking whom he may devour (1 Peter 5:8). We are to "Resist him, standing firm in the faith because you know that the family of believers throughout the world is undergoing the same kind of sufferings" (1 Peter 5:9). 
Lot’s life warns us: Wake up! Don't drift into spiritual sleep. Don't be asleep on the mast (Proverbs 23:31-34), when you should be watching. Walk in the light of Christ before the darkness overtakes you (John 12:35).
But I do want to close with this thought. In all of Lot's spiritual dullness, apathy, and sin, he was a believer, a righteous man. "and if he rescued Lot, a righteous man, who was distressed by the depraved conduct of the lawless 8 (for that righteous man, living among them day after day, was tormented in his righteous soul by the lawless deeds he saw and heard)" (2 Peter 2:7-8). 
The point is, in all of our stumblings and falls, it is faith in Jesus Christ that saves us.
How does Lot’s gradual descent into sin serve as a warning against spiritual complacency?

Day 66: The Spiritual Stupor of Lot
Biblical Illustration: Samson
Samson's Spiritual Stupor
Samson’s life closely parallels Lot’s descent into spiritual dullness. Blessed with supernatural strength, Samson squandered his calling through gradual compromise. He first looked at a Philistine woman and demanded her as a wife (Judges 14:2). He set out toward the Philistines, forming ungodly alliances. He pitched his tent near sin, visiting a prostitute in Gaza (Judges 16:1). Finally, he was living in sin, entangled with Delilah, who ultimately betrayed him (Judges 16:19). Like Lot, he lingered too long until he was physically captured and blinded.
"These things happened to them as examples and were written down as warnings for us, on whom the culmination of the ages has come. 12 So, if you think you are standing firm, be careful that you don’t fall!" (1 Corinthians 10:11-12).
The gospel of Jesus Christ is the solution to spiritual stupor. Where Lot lingered and Samson stumbled, Jesus remained steadfast, resisting temptation and sin clear to the shedding of His blood for us. He never compromised with sin but stood firm, even unto death, even death on a cross. Through His sacrifice and by His Spirit He opens blind eyes, restores fallen sinners, and leads them out of darkness into His marvelous light (1 Peter 2:9). 
The call is clear: wake up, repent, and follow Christ wholeheartedly!
How did Samson’s gradual compromise with sin lead to his downfall?Day 66: The Spiritual Stupor of Lot

Samson's Spiritual Stupor

Like Lot, he lingered too long until he was physically captured and blinded.
"These things happened to them as examples and were written down as warnings for us, on whom the culmination of the ages has come. 12 So, if you think you are standing firm, be careful that you don’t fall!" (1 Corinthians 10:11-12).
