How did Samson’s gradual compromise with sin lead to his downfall?

Day 66: The Spiritual Stupor of Lot
Practical Application
To avoid spiritual stupor, we must stay alert in Christ. This means at least the following:

    Fix your eyes on Jesus, not the world (Hebrews 12:2), not that which is tempting you, not how to gratify your flesh. 
    Flee temptation and pursue holiness (2 Timothy 2:22). 
    Do not compromise with sin but resist the devil (James 4:7). 
    Remain in prayer (1 Thessalonians 5:17) and in God’s Word (Psalm 119:105). 
    Surround yourself with godly influences (Hebrews 10:24-25). 
    Above all, trust in Christ’s finished work on the cross. He alone rescues us from darkness and keeps us steadfast in the faith (Colossians 1:13). 

Wake up, stand firm, and walk in the light of Christ (Ephesians 5:14).
What specific steps will you take in your daily life to stay spiritually alert and resist compromise with sin?

Day 67: Jesus Rescues Us Externally
Jesus Rescues Us
Key Passage
He recovered all the goods and brought back his relative Lot and his possessions, together with the women and the other people.
Genesis 14:16
Teaching
Jesus Rescues Us
Genesis 14:1-16
Lot’s journey to Sodom didn’t happen overnight, but ended in captivity, as does our journey to sin (John 8:34). In Genesis 13:10-12, Lot saw the well-watered Jordan Valley, moved near Sodom, then lived in Sodom—and eventually, he was taken captive when war came (Genesis 14:12). This is how sin works. At first, it looks harmless, even attractive, like a delicious piece of fruit (Genesis 3:6). But step by step, we edge closer, finally partaking of it, and one day we find ourselves trapped in sin, bound in chains we never meant to wear (Proverbs 5:22).
In Genesis 14:14-16, Abraham hears that Lot has been taken captive. He doesn’t hesitate as he wants to rescue his nephew (Proverbs 24:11). With 318 trained men, he sets out, tracks the enemy down, and launches a surprise attack—under the cover of darkness. Victory is swift, and Lot is rescued, bringing him out of the darkness and breaking his chains (Psalm 107:14).
Abraham's rescue of Lot points to Jesus' rescue of us sinners. Can you see it? Like Lot, we too were captives—bound by sin, trapped in the enemy’s camp, powerless to free ourselves. But Jesus came for us. And just as Abraham’s rescue took place under the cover of darkness, so did ours. As Jesus hung on the cross, “darkness came over the whole land” (Matthew 27:45). It was there, when it was midnight at midday, that He won our freedom and rescued us from captivity to sin and Satan. “For He has rescued us from the dominion of darkness and brought us into the kingdom of the Son He loves” (Colossians 1:13).
Sin is like quicksand; it lures us with the illusion of firm ground, but the moment we step in, we start sinking (Psalm 69:2). Struggling only makes it worse (Romans 7:18-19). We need someone outside of the pit to pull us free (Psalm 40:2). Jesus is that Rescuer. He doesn’t just throw us a rope and tell us to climb out. He steps into the pit Himself (Philippians 2:7-8), lifts us onto His shoulders (Luke 15:5), and rescues us from the darkness (Colossians 1:13).
Maybe today you feel the weight of chains, habits you can’t break, or regrets you can’t escape or forget (Isaiah 43:18-19). The good news is, you don’t have to rescue yourself (Ephesians 2:8-9). Jesus has already stormed the enemy’s camp, shattered the chains (Isaiah 61:1), and carried you out (John 8:36). And what you can't forget, God can't remember (Hebrews 8:12).
The question is: will you walk in that freedom? Lot would have been foolish to want to stay a captive in the darkness, he needed to walk with Abraham away from the danger. Will you, like Lot, drift toward Sodom (Hebrews 2:1) or learn to walk by the Spirit, walk with Jesus?
Come to Jesus. The Rescuer is here (Matthew 11:28).
Prayer:
Lord, I confess that I have wandered toward sin and found myself captive. Thank You for coming into the darkness to rescue me. Help me walk in the freedom You have given me. Amen.
How can you walk in the freedom that Jesus has won for you instead of drifting back toward the captivity of sin?

Day 67: Jesus Rescues Us Externally
Biblical Illustration: David
David Rescues His Family
In 1 Samuel 30 David and his men returned to Ziklag to find their city burned to the ground, their wives and children taken captive by the Amalekites. Their hearts sank, and they wept until they had no strength left. David’s men, in their grief, even talked of stoning him. But David turned to the Lord for strength.
With God’s guidance, David and his men pursued the enemy. They found an abandoned Egyptian servant who led them to the Amalekites. Again, under the cover of night (like Abraham rescuing Lot), David launched a surprise attack and “recovered everything the Amalekites had taken” (1 Samuel 30:18). Not a single person was lost—wives, sons, daughters—all were rescued.
This is an illustration of the gospel. Like the people of Ziklag, we were powerless, captive to sin, the enemy had plundered our souls. But Jesus, the greater David, pursued us. At the cross, He stormed the enemy’s camp, defeated sin and death, and reclaimed us for His kingdom. Every last one of us.
Jesus doesn’t leave captives behind. He has come to rescue you, even if it's for the 490th time (Matthew 18:22). Turn to Him, and walk in the freedom He has won for you.
What does David’s rescue of the captives in Ziklag teach us about Jesus’ mission?
Jesus leaves some captives behind if they have sinned too much.
 Jesus, like David, pursues and rescues those held captive by sin.
Only the strongest believers can be rescued by Jesus.

Day 67: Jesus Rescues Us Externally
Practical Application
Like Lot and the people of Ziklag, we can drift into spiritual captivity (Hebrews 2:1). But Jesus, our Rescuer, calls us to walk in freedom. How?

    Cry out to the Lord – Like David (1 Samuel 30:6), strengthen yourself in God through prayer and His Word.
    Repent and turn back – If sin has pulled you in, confess and turn to Christ (1 John 1:9).
    Walk in the light – Stay close to Jesus, rejecting sin’s lure and remembering how He rescued you by going to death and back (Colossians 1:13).
    Surround yourself with godly influences – Walking in freedom isn’t meant to be done alone. Stay in fellowship with other believers who will encourage and strengthen you (Hebrews 10:24-25).
    Live with purpose – Jesus didn’t just rescue you from sin; He rescued you for His kingdom. Step forward in obedience, using your freedom to serve and glorify Him (Galatians 5:13).

The gospel isn’t just about rescue—it’s about living free. Jesus has delivered you; now walk in His victory!
Of the five applications given, which one do you need to focus on today?  Please share.

Day 68: Jesus Rescues Us Internally
External Rescue, Internal Change
Key Passage
A person is not a Jew who is one only outwardly, nor is circumcision merely outward and physical. 29 No, a person is a Jew who is one inwardly; and circumcision is circumcision of the heart, by the Spirit, not by the written code. Such a person’s praise is not from other people, but from God.
Romans 2:28-29
Teaching
In yesterday's devotional we reveled in the fact that, after sin traps us and makes us captives, like Lot in Genesis 14, Jesus Christ rescues us from our sin and sets us free, like Abraham did Lot. This is the same thing Paul rejoiced in. After he was discouraged over his struggles with his flesh, admitting his slavery to sin (Romans 7:14), he asked "who will rescue me from this body of death?" (Romans 7:24) and immediately provided the answer in the next verse, "Thanks be to God, who delivers me through Jesus Christ our Lord!" (Romans 7:25).
But think about this: if all Jesus did was rescue us externally from sin traps, like a firefighter rescuing someone out of a burning building, what would keep us from going right back into another sin trap (Proverbs 26:11)? Then Jesus would rescue us again, then we'd return to the trap, He'd rescue us again, and on and on it would go.
But this is not how it works. Along with the external rescue out of our sin trap (Psalm 25:15), comes an internal rescue that involves crucifying our old nature (Galatians 2:20), changing our heart (Ezekiel 36:26), circumcising our flesh (Colossians 2:11), so that we might begin to hate sin (Romans 12:9) and learn to walk in holiness (1 Peter 1:15-16).
The Cross Circumcises Us
Colossians 2:11-12
"In him you were also circumcised with a circumcision not performed by human hands. Your whole self ruled by the flesh was put off when you were circumcised by Christ." (Colossians 2:11)
Before Christ, our hearts were nothing but flesh, resistant to God in every area. JI Packer says:
In Christ, a spiritual circumcision takes place—not a physical cutting of flesh, but a divine surgery on the heart (Romans 2:29). The old, rebellious nature is removed like a stone blocking a well (Ezekiel 11:19), allowing the living water of the Spirit to flow freely within us (John 7:38). This is not our doing, nor can any religious effort achieve it (Ephesians 2:8-9). It is a work done "not by human hands" (Colossians 2:11) but by Christ Himself, through the power of His cross (Galatians 6:14) and the Spirit of God.
Think of it like a prisoner, locked in a dark dungeon, whose chains are not just on his wrists but woven into his very nature (John 8:34). A mere unlocking of the door would not suffice; he would remain a slave to his old ways and old chains. But Jesus does something greater: He breaks the chains of sin at their source (Isaiah 61:1). Through His cross, He cuts away our sinful nature (Titus 3:5), performing a heart transplant (Ezekiel 36:26) so that we no longer live for the flesh but for Him (2 Corinthians 5:15).
"Having been buried with him in baptism, in which you were also raised with him through your faith in the working of God, who raised him from the dead." (Colossians 2:12)
Baptism is a picture of our burial with Christ (Romans 6:4). Imagine a seed being planted in the ground—it must be buried before it can sprout to new life (John 12:24). In the same way, through faith, we are buried with Christ in death (Colossians 2:12), our old selves laid to rest in the grave (Galatians 2:20). But the story does not end there. Just as Christ rose from the dead (1 Corinthians 15:20), so we rise with Him, no longer bound by sin but walking in newness of life (Acts 5:20).
This is the internal rescue we desperately need. The cross is not merely a lifeboat pulling us out of sin’s raging sea; it is also the storm that drowns our old nature (Romans 6:6), so that we may be raised as new creations (2 Corinthians 5:17). Jesus does not just rescue us from sin’s traps—He transforms us so that we no longer desire them (Ezekiel 36:26-27). He lovingly performs both an internal and an external rescue!
Let us then walk in the reality of our new life (Ephesians 4:24), remembering that the old self has been crucified (Galatians 5:24). We no longer belong to sin (Romans 6:14). We belong to Christ, who has circumcised our hearts (Colossians 2:11) and raised us to live for Him (Romans 14:8).
How does Jesus' internal rescue change the way we live and respond to sin?

Day 68: Jesus Rescues Us Internally
Biblical Illustration: Zaccheus
The story of Zacchaeus in Luke 19:1-10 provides a vivid picture of an internal rescue, a real transformation. Zacchaeus was a chief tax collector whose heart was tangled in the thorns of greed and dishonesty. He had spent years building his life on sinful gain, much like that field overrun with weeds that I talked about in the teaching. But when Jesus came to Jericho, everything changed.
Zacchaeus climbed a sycamore tree to catch a glimpse of Christ, unaware that Jesus was about to cut away his old nature. "Zacchaeus, come down immediately. I must stay at your house today" (Luke 19:5). With those words, Jesus invited Himself into Zacchaeus’ life; not just into his home, but into his very heart. 
The encounter was transformative, an internal rescue. Zacchaeus, once ruled by the flesh, was now circumcised in heart. He stood up and declared, "Look, Lord! Here and now I give half of my possessions to the poor, and if I have cheated anybody out of anything, I will pay back four times the amount" (Luke 19:8).
This was not external reform but internal rescue. The cross had come to Zacchaeus, cutting away his old self and raising him to a new life. Jesus summed it up perfectly: "Today salvation has come to this house" (Luke 19:9). This is what Jesus does for all who believe: He doesn't just call us down from our trees of curiosity; He transforms our very hearts and minds, desires and cravings.
What was the primary change in Zacchaeus after his encounter with Jesus?
He was forced by Jesus to give away his possessions.
 He became wealthier and more respected in his community.
He experienced an internal transformation, leading to repentance and generosity.

Day 68: Jesus Rescues Us Internally
Practical Application
Since Christ has circumcised our hearts, let us walk in newness of life, having experienced both the internal and external rescues. 
We do this by living in His Word (John 15:5), renewing our minds through Scripture (Romans 12:2), and putting to death sinful desires by the Spirit’s power (Romans 8:13). 
Like Zacchaeus, we respond to Jesus with repentance and generosity, living out the gospel daily. As we abide in Christ, our lives become a testimony of His grace, bearing fruit that reflects His transforming work. When we stumble, we do not lose heart but run back to the Father, who lovingly restores us (1 John 1:9).
Let us pray for a heart continually transformed by Christ (Psalm 51:10), fix our eyes on Him (Hebrews 12:2), and seek to love and serve others as He has loved us at the cross (1 John 3:16), and loves us every day by His Spirit (Romans 5:5).
How can you intentionally abide in Christ today, allowing His Spirit to shape your thoughts, actions, and interactions with others?

Day 68: Jesus Rescues Us Internally
Practical Application
Since Christ has circumcised our hearts, let us walk in newness of life, having experienced both the internal and external rescues. 
We do this by living in His Word (John 15:5), renewing our minds through Scripture (Romans 12:2), and putting to death sinful desires by the Spirit’s power (Romans 8:13). 
Like Zacchaeus, we respond to Jesus with repentance and generosity, living out the gospel daily. As we abide in Christ, our lives become a testimony of His grace, bearing fruit that reflects His transforming work. When we stumble, we do not lose heart but run back to the Father, who lovingly restores us (1 John 1:9).
Let us pray for a heart continually transformed by Christ (Psalm 51:10), fix our eyes on Him (Hebrews 12:2), and seek to love and serve others as He has loved us at the cross (1 John 3:16), and loves us every day by His Spirit (Romans 5:5).
How can you intentionally abide in Christ today, allowing His Spirit to shape your thoughts, actions, and interactions with others?

Day 69: Dead in Sin to Alive in Christ
Circumcised And Made Alive
Key Passage
When you were dead in your sins and in the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made you alive with Christ. He forgave us all our sins, 14 having canceled the charge of our legal indebtedness, which stood against us and condemned us; he has taken it away, nailing it to the cross.
Colossians 2:13-14
Teaching
From Dead in Sin to Alive in Christ
To be spiritually "uncircumcised" is to be alive and responsive to sin but spiritually dead and unresponsive to God (Ephesians 2:1; Colossians 2:13). This is why I lived so many years bound in the chains of substance abuse, greed, porn, and impurity, gluttony and laziness (Romans 6:16-21; Galatians 5:19-21).
When God circumcises us, He makes us alive and responsive to Himself (Deuteronomy 30:6; Romans 2:29). While Jesus' work on the cross saves us from the penalty of sin (Romans 5:9), His work in us saves us from the power of sin (Titus 2:11-12). The first is an external rescue, the second is an internal rescue and both complete the work of salvation for us (Philippians 1:6).
Today let's examine Colossians 2:13-15.
Colossians 2:13 – "When you were dead in your sins and in the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made you alive with Christ. He forgave us all our sins."
As I've noted before, sin is like quicksand: the more we struggle, the deeper we sink (Psalm 69:2; Romans 7:18-19). Dead in our sins, we could no more raise ourselves to life than a corpse could rise from a grave (Ephesians 2:1).
But God, in His mercy, performed a resurrection in us! (Ephesians 2:4-5; Titus 3:5) Just as Jesus stood outside Lazarus’ tomb and called, "Come out!" (John 11:43), so He calls us to life in Him (John 5:24-25), forgiving every sin that once kept us in the grave of spiritual death (Psalm 103:12; Colossians 2:13-14).
Colossians 2:14 – "Having canceled the charge of our legal indebtedness, which stood against us and condemned us; he has taken it away, nailing it to the cross."
Imagine a courtroom (an analogy I like to use) where you stand guilty, and the evidence against you is overwhelming (Romans 3:19-20; James 2:10). The law pronounces its verdict: "Guilty!" (Romans 3:23; Galatians 3:10). But then, Jesus steps forward. He does not argue your innocence; He acknowledges your guilt (1 John 1:8), but then takes the sentence upon Himself (Isaiah 53:5; 2 Corinthians 5:21).
The record of debt that condemned us was nailed to the cross, driven through Jesus' hands and feet (Colossians 2:14; Psalm 22:16). Our rap sheet is wiped clean, and we walk out of that courtroom free (Romans 8:1). All legal requirements were met (Matthew 5:17; Romans 10:4), all penalties and fines paid (1 Peter 2:24), all justice satisfied (Romans 3:25-26), all wrath poured out on Jesus (Romans 3:24), and all mercy purchased for us (Ephesians 1:7; Titus 3:5-7).
Colossians 2:15 – "And having disarmed the powers and authorities, he made a public spectacle of them, triumphing over them by the cross."
Satan and his demons thought they had won when Jesus was crucified, but the cross was not their victory, it was their defeat (Hebrews 2:14). Like a victorious king leading a defeated army through the streets, Jesus made a spectacle of His enemies (Colossians 2:15). What once held power over us—sin, shame, and fear—was paraded as powerless before all, as Jesus' death destroyed death (2 Timothy 1:10). The cross, which seemed like a place of defeat, became the throne of Christ’s triumph and a display of His true worth (Philippians 2:8-11; Revelation 5:9-10).
Because of Christ, we are no longer enslaved but free (John 8:36; Galatians 5:1), no longer condemned but forgiven (Romans 8:1; Ephesians 1:7), no longer dead but alive (Ephesians 2:5-6; Colossians 3:1). Shame has no place in us anymore, for Jesus owned it as His own (Hebrews 12:2). So if you are living under shame, you are like a thief, trying to steal what belonged to Jesus!
Let us live as those who have been raised from the dead (Romans 6:4, 11), responsive to God (Romans 12:1-2), free from the chains of sin (Romans 6:18), and walking in the victory of Jesus Christ! (1 Corinthians 15:57).
What does Colossians 2:13-15 reveal about Christ’s victory over sin and its impact on believers?

Day 69: Dead in Sin to Alive in Christ
Biblical Illustration: Israelites at Gilgal
When the Israelites crossed the Jordan River into the Promised Land, God commanded Joshua to circumcise all the males who were not previously circumcised. This happened at Gilgal (Joshua 5:2-9). For years, they had wandered in the wilderness, uncircumcised, unresponsive to God, and carrying the shame and reproach of Egypt. But at Gilgal, they were set apart, marked as God's people, and prepared to enter His promised land.
Gilgal, known later as Golgotha, is where Jesus bore our reproach and cut away the sin that enslaved us. Just as the Israelites needed this circumcision to "roll away" their shame, we need the circumcision of the heart that happened at Golgotha and is personalized in us by the Spirit. The gospel does what human effort cannot; it makes us alive in Him, removes the shame of our past, and brings us into the fullness of God's promise. At Golgotha, Jesus completed the work begun at Gilgal, cutting away the sin that kept us from true life.
What is the significance of Gilgal and Golgotha in illustrating God's work of removing shame and setting His people apart? (Click the box for each answer that is correct) )
Gilgal represents the Israelites' physical journey, while Golgotha represents Jesus' spiritual victory over sin and shame. 
Both places symbolize God’s act of cutting away sin and marking His people as His own.
Gilgal and Golgotha show that human effort is necessary to remove sin and shame.
The events at Gilgal and Golgotha are unrelated to each other.

Day 69: Dead in Sin to Alive in Christ
Practical Application
Because of Christ’s work on the cross, and the Spirit's work within us, we are called to walk in newness of life (Romans 6:4). This means daily turning to Jesus in repentance (1 John 1:9), renewing our minds with Scripture (Romans 12:2), and seeking God in prayer (Philippians 4:6). 
We no longer live in bondage but in freedom, and each one of us should be serving others in love (Galatians 5:13). Just as the Israelites left Egypt behind, we must leave sin behind, trusting in Christ’s finished work. This requires an active, daily surrender of our desires to God, relying on His strength, and walking in obedience to His Word.
Let us fix our eyes on Jesus (Hebrews 12:2), resting in His grace and walking boldly in His victory.
How can you intentionally renew your mind with Scripture and prayer this week to walk in the freedom Christ has given you?

Day 70: Transformation By Seeing
Transformation Not By Doing But By Seeing
Key Passage
So all of us who have had that veil removed can see and reflect the glory of the Lord. And the Lord—who is the Spirit—makes us more and more like him as we are changed into his glorious image.
2 Corinthians 3:18
Teaching
The Christian life is unlike anything man could ever invent. If man invented a religion it would be him doing the work to make himself right with God and man, and him changing his behavior, and he would get the credit and the glory.
In the Christian life, change comes not by doing but by seeing. We become what we focus on. We change by seeing the glory of God, by contemplating it, by meditating on it. 
And where do we see the glory of God? What should we focus on and "see" to truly experience change? The Bible is so clear on this point, let's see it from Hebrews 1:3:
Hebrews 1:3 – The Son is the radiance of God's glory and the exact representation of His being, sustaining all things by His powerful word. After He had provided purification for sins, He sat down at the right hand of the Majesty in heaven.

    The Son is the radiance of God’s glory.
    The sun is too bright for us to look at directly, yet its light fills the sky, giving warmth and life. Jesus is the radiant light of God’s glory—God made visible. To see Him is to see the Father (John 14:9). Transformation happens when His light shines into our darkness, exposing what is hidden and igniting our hearts with divine fire, and as He redirects our attention to the gospel as the solution. As Moses’ face glowed after being with God, so we are changed by beholding Christ’s brilliance (2 Corinthians 3:18).
    The Son is the exact representation of God’s being.
    If you press a signet ring into wax, the impression is an exact match. Jesus is the imprint of God—everything God is, Jesus is. Want to know God's heart? Look at Jesus healing the sick, welcoming sinners, and speaking the truth. The clearer we see Him, the more we are shaped into His image, just as a mirror reflects whatever it faces.
    The Son provided purification from sin when He died on the cross.
    The gospel is how God shows His glory in this world. Jesus purified you when He died, taking your sin away from you, and then sending His Spirit to live in you. This is purification. Imagine a stained garment plunged into and washed by pure water—what was once filthy becomes white as snow. Jesus' blood cleanses every stain of sin (Isaiah 1:18). The more we gaze at the cross, the more we grasp the depth of His love, and love transforms us. When we see how He suffered for us, our selfishness dissolves, replaced by gratitude and devotion.
    The Son rose from the dead.
    A seed buried in the ground seems dead, but in time, it bursts forth in new life (John 12:24). Jesus conquered death (1 Corinthians 15:55–57), proving that the grave is not the end (Acts 2:24). If we see Him as the risen King (Revelation 1:18), we live differently—fear is abolished along with death (2 Timothy 1:10), hope grows (1 Peter 1:3), and the chains of sin lose their grip (Romans 6:6). His resurrection power pulses in us (Ephesians 1:19–20), renewing and reshaping us (2 Corinthians 5:17).
    The Son sat down at the right hand of God.
    A finished masterpiece is framed and displayed, not left unfinished. Jesus sat down because His work was complete. Seated in victory, He reigns. When we see Him enthroned, we stop striving and rest in the grace He won for us. Transformation is not self-effort—it’s looking to Him, the author and finisher of our faith (Hebrews 12:2).

The point is this: to be transformed we must see (2 Corinthians 3:18). We must see Jesus (Hebrews 12:2). We must see His purifying work on the cross for our sins (Hebrews 9:14). We must see His resurrection from the dead as He defeated death forever for us (Romans 6:9–10). We must see Him seated in heaven, reigning on the throne (Hebrews 1:3), worshiped as God (Revelation 5:12), and interceding with God (Romans 8:34).
Summary: Unlike man's religion which has man doing the work and wanting the credit (Romans 4:4), the glory of God is in the gospel of His Son (2 Corinthians 4:6)! Jesus did the work for us (John 17:4), Jesus suffered death for us (Isaiah 53:5), Jesus rose in power for us (Romans 4:25), Jesus is interceding daily for us (Hebrews 7:25), Jesus is building a home for us (John 14:2–3), Jesus will soon return for us (Revelation 22:12). We must see Jesus' day of death and resurrection (Luke 24:46), and in seeing it we will be transformed (Romans 12:2).
Look to Christ, behold His glory, and be transformed.
According to 2 Corinthians 3:18, how does true transformation occur in the Christian life?

Day 70: Transformation By Seeing
Biblical Illustration: Abraham
Abraham Saw Jesus’ Day
Genesis 22:1-14
Jesus said, "Your father Abraham rejoiced at the thought of seeing my day; he saw it and was glad.” (John 8:56). But when did Abraham see Jesus' day? The answer is found in Genesis 22, when God tested Abraham by telling him to sacrifice his son, Isaac. In this remarkable event, Abraham saw a picture of the gospel.

    The Son Was Under a Death Sentence for Three Days (Genesis 22:2-4)
    When God commanded Abraham to sacrifice Isaac, the son was as good as dead in Abraham’s mind. For three days Isaac was under this death sentence, as they traveled to the place God designated, just as Jesus was in the grave for three days. Abraham journeyed to Moriah with the weight of loss pressing upon him, and he could do it because he believed in resurrection (Hebrews 11:19).
    The Father Put the Wood on the Back of His Son (Genesis 22:6)
    As they climbed the hill of Mt. Moriah, Abraham laid the wood on Isaac’s back. In the same way, Jesus bore His cross up Calvary’s hill (John 19:17) as the Father gave His Son for the world. Isaac, the beloved son, carried the very instrument of his sacrifice, just as Jesus did centuries later.
    A Substitute Was Found—A Ram with a Crown of Thorns (Genesis 22:13)
    Just as Abraham was about to sacrifice Isaac, God provided a substitute—a ram whose horns were caught in a thicket (a grouping of thorns). The ram’s head was wrapped in thorns, just as Jesus, our Substitute, wore a crown of thorns (Matthew 27:29). Isaac was spared, but Jesus, the true Lamb of God, was not. Jesus' day was the day of the cross when He became your Substitute, crowned with thorns that you might receive the crown of life.
    The Father Received His Son Back as From the Dead (Genesis 22:4, Hebrews 11:19)
    On the third day, Isaac was restored to his father, just as Jesus rose from the dead on the third day. Abraham had trusted that God could raise the dead, and in a sense, he did receive Isaac back from death.
    Abraham Pointed to the Future Provision on Calvary (Genesis 22:14)
    Abraham named the place "The Lord Will Provide," declaring that on this mountain, God would provide the ultimate sacrifice. Centuries later, on this very ridge, Jesus was crucified, fulfilling Abraham’s prophetic words.

Abraham saw Jesus’ day in the offering up of his son Isaac, his "only son whom he loved", and through this story, we see the gospel clearly. God provided the Lamb so that we might live. Look to the Lamb, and see the glory of Christ! And be transformed!
According to John 8:56 and Genesis 22, how did Abraham "see" Jesus' day?
Through a vision God gave him in a dream.
In God commanding the sacrifice of Isaac, which foreshadowed the gospel.
By witnessing Jesus' miracles in the future.

Day 70: Transformation By Seeing
Practical Application
Seeing God's Glory Transforms Us
Just as Abraham saw Jesus’ day and was changed, we too must fix our eyes on Christ. How do we see His glory?

    Behold Christ in Scripture – Read the Bible daily, and ask God to show you the gospel of Jesus Christ for it testifies of Him (John 5:39). As you do this, your understanding of Christ and His gospel deepens, and your heart is shaped by His truth.
    Meditate on the Cross – Fix your thoughts on Jesus’ sacrifice, where God's love and justice meet (Galatians 2:20). Let the reality of His suffering and victory over sin lead you to gratitude and a renewed desire to walk in obedience.
    Worship and Pray – Ask God to open your eyes to His glory (2 Corinthians 4:6) and declare God's worth when you see Him. When we worship, we align our hearts with heaven and experience the presence of God in a way that changes us from the inside out.
    Proclaim the Gospel – Sharing Christ deepens our own vision of Him (Philemon 1:6). As we speak of His goodness, our faith is strengthened, and others are invited to see His glory as well.

Look to Jesus, and be transformed!
How can you intentionally fix your eyes on Jesus today so that His glory transforms you?

Day 71: The Glory of God in the Death of Christ
Transformed by the Cross
As we saw yesterday, to be transformed, that is, to be set free and made like Jesus, we must see/contemplate the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ. The glory of God is seen in the gospel. God is sovereign in who He reveals the glory of His gospel (Matthew 16:17), so we must seek Him actively to be one who sees!
If you were to take a look at three Psalms, one right after another, we would see this truth of the glory of the gospel illustrated. Let's summarize them this way: Psalm 22: the death of Jesus. Psalm 23: Jesus' burial (in the valley of the shadow of death). Psalm 24: the resurrection and ascension of King Jesus.
Today let's focus on Psalm 22, the death of Jesus. Remember to ask God to show you His glory, to reveal Himself to you as you study this Psalm. If you see His glory, you'll be transformed: sin will lose its grip, holiness will be attractive to you, and you will crave more of Jesus.
Psalm 22: The Suffering, the Supplication, and the Salvation
1. The Suffering of the Savior (Psalm 22:1-21)

    Desertion – “My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?” (Psalm 22:1) 

These very words came from Jesus' bleeding lips as His loud cry on the cross (Matthew 27:46). The weight of sin separating the sinless Son from the Father is very evident in this verse. You can see Him there suffering for your sin, experiencing the wrath of God. God deserted His own Son so that you might be rescued, saved from sin, and restored to God.

    Derision – “All who see Me mock Me” (Psalm 22:7)

The ridicule at Calvary couldn't have been worse as evil men hurled their insults at the pure Son of God (Luke 23:35-39) and trampled on the innocent Lamb. The rejection by men was evident here (Isaiah 53:3), all according to the plan of God that the rejection of the Son would be the acceptance of many sons and daughters.

    Distress – “I am poured out like water… My heart is like wax” (Psalm 22:14). "Dogs surround me, a pack of villains encircles me; they pierce my hands and my feet" (Psalm 22:16).

The agony of crucifixion and the physical, emotional, and spiritual torment Jesus underwent here is beyond words; sometimes we must just sit and watch, mouths agape at His love for us that His heart would turn to wax that our hearts of stone would become hearts of flesh. Watch Him there, as He is pierced for our transgressions, nails in His hands and feet, and "...one of the soldiers pierced Jesus’ side with a spear, bringing a sudden flow of blood and water" (John 19:34).

2. The Supplication of the Sufferer (Psalm 22:19-21)

    Plea for Proximity – “But You, O Lord, do not be far off!” (Psalm 22:19) 

Jesus’ cry for the Father’s presence was intense like a drowning man gasping for air as Jesus drowned in our sins. He knew that far worse than the nails, the whip, and the spear was the deep, aching void of God's absence. The promise of God’s nearness to the brokenhearted (Psalm 34:18) went unfulfilled with Jesus, as Father and Son were torn apart by our sin so that Jesus could bridge the chasm and bring a lost world back to the Father. Because the Father was far off from His Son on the cross He now promises to be near to you, a refuge that never fails, and He will never leave you nor forsake you.

    Petition for Power – “O You my help, come quickly to my aid!” (Psalm 22:19)

Christ’s dependence on the Father was made very clear in his loud cry and prayer here. He wanted His Father more than His own physical life. His craving here is a model for our own prayers in suffering, to cry out for His help and His aid.

    Protection from Peril – “Deliver my soul from the sword” (Psalm 22:20-21)

Jesus entrusted Himself to the Father (Luke 23:46) and prayed for deliverance. He was heard and answered with His ultimate victory over death in resurrection. 

3. The Salvation of the Saints (Psalm 22:22-31)

    Proclamation – “I will tell of Your name to my brothers” (Psalm 22:22)The suffering Savior is looking into the future when He will be the risen Christ declaring victory (Hebrews 2:12). His death will not be the end, He will rise to declare the name, nature, and character of God to His brothers.

    Praise – “All the ends of the earth shall remember and turn to the Lord” (Psalm 22:27)

The spread of the gospel to all nations (Matthew 28:19–20) is seen here, and prophesied that every ear shall hear the good news (Mark 13:10) and every knee shall bow to King Jesus (Philippians 2:10–11), and every tongue confess Him as Lord (Romans 14:11). Praise will swell from the East to the West (Malachi 1:11), and all nations will be declaring the glory of King Jesus (Psalm 86:9), who ransomed them through death (Revelation 5:9), who redeemed them in love (Isaiah 43:1), who rescued them in power (Colossians 1:13–14)!

    Promise – “It shall be told of the Lord to the coming generation” (Psalm 22:30)

The gospel will endure for all generations, and you and I are to tell the coming generation of the good news. This is a call to proclaim Christ’s finished work as the full solution to sin's problem. 

Conclusion: From the Cross to the Crown
Psalm 22 begins with suffering but ends in salvation—just like the gospel. Christ endured desertion, derision, and distress, made supplication to the Father, and secured salvation for all who trust in Him. The cross was not the end—resurrection and redemption were the plan all along!
Do you see how the Old Testament reveals the glory of God in the gospel of His Son? This is what we need to see, over and over, to be loved out of our sins and drawn powerfully to Christ!
How can contemplating the glory of God in the death, burial, and resurrection of Jesus Christ transform our lives and lead us to greater holiness?

Day 71: The Glory of God in the Death of Christ
Biblical Illustration: The Thief on the Cross
Hanging beside Jesus, a condemned criminal gasped for breath, his body wracked with pain. At first, he joined the crowd in mocking Jesus (Matthew 27:44). His heart was hardened, his guilt undeniable. But as time passed, something changed. He saw the innocence of Christ, heard His prayer for His enemies, and beheld His silent suffering. The weight of the thief's sin pressed upon him, and he realized the One beside him was no ordinary victim—He was the King.
With desperation and faith, the thief turned to Jesus and said, “Jesus, remember me when You come into Your kingdom” (Luke 23:42). In that moment, the gospel transformed him. He acknowledged his guilt, confessed Jesus as Lord, and placed his hope in Christ alone.
Jesus responded with the words of salvation: “Truly, I say to you, today you will be with Me in paradise” (Luke 23:43). The thief, once rebellious and lost, was now redeemed and destined for glory.
Like the thief, all sinners deserve judgment, but through Christ’s suffering, supplication, and salvation, we receive eternal life. His finished work on the cross secures our place in His kingdom—not by merit, but by grace.
Jesus, thank you for not leaving my guilt on me any more than you left it on the thief who cried out to you. Thank you for the assurance that you died for me and I will be with you in paradise forever. Thank you for drawing me to You and away from sin. In Jesus' name, amen
What did the thief on the cross see about Jesus that led to his transformation?

Day 71: The Glory of God in the Death of Christ
Practical Application
Like the thief on the cross, we are transformed by beholding Christ (2 Corinthians 3:18). To see His glory:

    Fix Your Eyes on Jesus – Daily meditate on His suffering and salvation in Scripture (Hebrews 12:2). Take time each day to reflect on His sacrifice, allowing the weight of His love to soften your heart and draw you closer to Him.
    Pray for Spiritual Sight – Ask God to open your heart to His glory (Ephesians 1:18), just like He did the first time. Praying for clarity will help you see beyond your circumstances and focus on the eternal hope found in Christ.
    Worship with Gratitude – Proclaim His name in praise, just as the psalmist did (Psalm 22:22). True worship flows from an understanding of His sacrifice, reminding us of His greatness and deep love.
    Share the Gospel – Tell others of Christ’s finished work (Psalm 22:30-31). Let your life reflect the hope and transformation Christ offers, and actively share this life-changing message with those around you. 

Transformation comes not by effort but by faith in Christ crucified and risen.
How can you actively fix your eyes on Jesus and invite His transformation into your life today?
